### [1] [MV2: Multi-View Multi-Vehicle Driving Dataset for Novel View Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12442)
*Sanjay Bhargav Dharavath,Hanvitha Saraswathi Mukkamala,Faizan Farooq Khan,Ioannis Kakogeorgiou,Aditya Arun,C V Jawahar,Zakaria Laskar*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces MV2 dataset for evaluating neural view synthesis under large viewpoint changes in dynamic urban scenes, using synchronized multi-vehicle captures and 50 scenes (~12k images); shows NVS degrades with viewpoint disparity and that feed-forward pose estimators lag optimization-based methods; provides benchmark resources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fill the gap in evaluating differentiable rendering for driving with sparse viewpoints and dynamic objects by providing a multi-vehicle, synchronized, ground-truth-registered benchmark to stress-test NVS across large viewpoint changes.

Method: Collect synchronized camera streams from three vehicles (car, scooter, drone) following distinct but synchronized trajectories; register world using Structure-from-Motion; verify camera poses with manual pixel-level correspondences; compile 50 scenes (~12,000 images); evaluation protocol trains on one vehicle’s stream and tests on others, benchmarking recent NVS and pose estimation methods.

Result: NVS performance declines as viewpoint disparity increases; feed-forward pose estimators lag optimization-based approaches; MV2 demonstrates its utility as a rigorous driving NVS testbed; dataset and resources are publicly available.

Conclusion: MV2 provides a robust, real-world benchmark for differentiable rendering in driving with substantial viewpoint changes, enabling development and fair comparison of NVS and pose estimation methods in dynamic urban settings.

Abstract: Differentiable rendering has advanced novel view synthesis (NVS), yet applying it to real-world driving remains difficult due to sparse capture viewpoints, dynamic objects, and limited multi-trajectory data. We introduce the Multi-View Multi-Vehicle (MV2) dataset and benchmark for evaluating NVS models under large viewpoint changes in dynamic urban scenes. MV2 features synchronized captures from a car, scooter, and drone, each following distinct yet synchronized trajectories. Training NVS methods on one vehicle's camera stream and testing on another enables evaluation under substantially larger viewpoint variations than existing single-trajectory datasets. All sequences are registered via Structure-from-Motion and camera poses verified using manual pixel-level correspondence annotations, yielding 50 high-quality scenes with 12000 images. Benchmarking recent NVS and camera pose estimation methods shows that NVS performance degrades with increasing viewpoint disparity, and that feed-forward pose estimators notably lag behind optimization-based approaches, highlighting MV2 as a rigorous testbed for NVS in driving. The dataset, benchmark protocol, and project resources are available at https://mv2-dataset.github.io/.

</details>


### [2] [HIMEC: Directional Change Representation and Fixed-Interface Decoding for Remote Sensing Image Change Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12502)
*Aysha Ashraf,Shaina Ashraf,Wafaa I. M. Hussin,Ali Haider,Zhi Lu,Zhenming Peng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: HIMEC uses Directional Change Representation and fixed-interface decoding to improve RSICC, achieving CIDEr gains on LEVIR-CC and SECOND-CC while preserving decoder interface across training/inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the lack of explicit intermediate change structure and fragile decoder-interface consistency in fusion-based RSICC approaches by introducing structured change streams and a stable memory-based decoder interface.

Method: Introduce Directional Change Representation (DCR) splitting signed changes into appearance-, disappearance-, and shared-context streams before fusion; convert fused representation into visually conditioned change-query tokens via a learned-query encoder; employ a training-only auxiliary phrase decoder for supervision; fix the scene decoder input to zero to keep a constant interface during training and inference; evaluate a local-to-scene cascade with teacher-forced local states during training and autoregressive states at inference.

Result: On LEVIR-CC, CIDEr improves to 142.81±0.60 from 139.51±3.40 for direct fused-feature memory; on SECOND-CC, fixed-zero and regime-matched conditioning reach 75.67 and 76.99 CIDEr respectively versus 60.77 for mismatched cascade; a mean cosine distance of 0.69 is observed for the local-to-scene cascade states; source code will be released.

Conclusion: Fixed-interface decoding paired with structured change representations yields measurable gains for RSICC and preserves interface stability, though conclusions are demonstrated within the evaluated cascade and broader generalization remains to be tested.

Abstract: Remote sensing image change captioning (RSICC) converts bitemporal imagery into a sentence describing semantic changes. Most RSICC methods condition caption decoders directly on fused visual features, leaving intermediate change structure and decoder-interface consistency less studied. We present HIMEC, combining Directional Change Representation (DCR) with fixed-interface decoding. DCR separates signed differences into appearance-oriented, disappearance-oriented, and shared-context streams before fusion. A learned-query encoder converts the fused representation into visually conditioned change-query tokens that form the scene decoder's only sample-dependent memory. A training-only auxiliary phrase decoder supplies caption-derived supervision. With a fixed zero input, the scene decoder maintains the same interface during training and inference. Separately, we evaluate a local-to-scene cascade conditioned on teacher-forced local states during training and autoregressive states at inference. On changed LEVIR-CC validation pairs, these states have a mean cosine distance of 0.69. Regime-matched conditioning recovers most of the associated deficit, whereas permuting state correspondence causes no detectable penalty. These findings are limited to the evaluated cascade. In a matched three-seed comparison, HIMEC reaches a Consensus-based Image Description Evaluation (CIDEr) score of $142.81\pm0.60$ on LEVIR-CC, versus $139.51\pm3.40$ for direct fused-feature memory. On SECOND-CC, fixed-zero and regime-matched diagnostic conditioning reach 75.67 and 76.99 CIDEr, respectively, versus 60.77 for the mismatched cascade. The source code will be made publicly available at https://github.com/ayshaashra/HIMEC upon publication.

</details>


### [3] [Can Vision-Language Models Assess Proxemic Risk from Egocentric Robot Images?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12515)
*Vladyslava Rudas,Dmytro Kuzmenko*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Three open-source VLMs (InternVL, Qwen-VL, SmolVLM) were evaluated for egocentric proxemic danger classification with three prompts and two rounds of QLoRA fine-tuning; results show modest gains from fine-tuning, with Qwen-VL + advanced prompt achieving higher high-danger recall; localization analysis reveals danger labeling can be decoupled from spatial grounding; overall, VLMs struggle with fine-grained proxemic reasoning, though prompting and selective fine-tuning help in high-risk cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess the capability of current vision-language models to perform fine-grained proxemic danger assessment from egocentric robot imagery, a key component for safe embodied navigation in human environments.

Method: Evaluate three open-source VLMs (InternVL, Qwen-VL, SmolVLM) on classifying egocentric robot images into four danger levels. Compare three prompting strategies and two rounds of QLoRA fine-tuning against a stratified random baseline. Also analyze person localization to study spatial grounding versus danger labeling.

Result: Without fine-tuning, models perform near the baseline. Fine-tuning yields only modest overall improvements. Qwen-VL with an advanced prompt achieves substantially higher recall for high-danger cases. Localization analysis shows correct danger labels do not necessarily correspond to better spatial grounding.

Conclusion: Current VLMs are limited in fine-grained proxemic reasoning and spatial grounding. Targeted prompting and fine-tuning can improve high-danger detection in selected models, but robust proxemic understanding remains challenging.

Abstract: Assessing proxemic danger from a robot's egocentric perspective is critical for safe embodied navigation in human environments and requires both visual and contextual reasoning. We evaluate three opensource vision-language models (VLMs) (\textit{InternVL}, \textit{Qwen-VL}, and \textit{SmolVLM}) on the classification of egocentric robot images into four danger levels, comparing three prompting strategies and two rounds of QLoRA fine-tuning against a stratified random baseline. Without fine-tuning, all models perform near the baseline, while fine-tuning yields only modest overall improvements. However, \textit{Qwen-VL} with an advanced prompt achieves substantially higher recall for high-danger cases than the other models. An analysis of person localization further shows that correct danger classification does not correspond to better spatial grounding, indicating that a model may produce a useful safety label without attending to the relevant region of the scene. These results show that current VLMs remain limited in fine-grained proxemic reasoning and spatial grounding, although targeted prompting and fine-tuning can improve high-danger detection in selected models.

</details>


### [4] [Surface-to-Skeleton 3D Cephalometry: Estimating Hidden Skeletal Landmarks from CT-Derived External Soft-Tissue Surfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12537)
*Tomoki Abe,Taiki Kanaya,Kazuki Saita,Mao Noda,Chie Tachiki,Yasushi Nishii,Hideo Saito*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Inferring internal skeletal landmarks from external soft-tissue geometry is feasible with mm-level accuracy using a coordinate-consistent surface-to-skeleton framework; achieved 2.97 mm radial error for skeletal landmarks and 3.03 mm for deep/invisible landmarks in held-out data; evidence of patient-specific signals and coverage effects; optical inference remains unresolved.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address whether CT-defined internal skeletal landmarks can be inferred from external soft-tissue geometry, enabling hidden landmark inference and improving cross-modality or registration robustness.

Method: Use a coordinate-consistent surface-to-skeleton task with same-acquisition CT-derived surfaces, separating estimation from optical-to-CT registration and scanner/acquisition effects. Build a locked retrospective protocol from 240 clinical CT scans across two hospitals, pairing external soft-tissue point clouds with 21 skeletal landmarks and 3 visible soft-tissue landmarks. Train an integrated hierarchical point-cloud model to predict skeletal landmarks.

Result: Mean radial error of 2.97 mm for skeletal landmarks and 3.03 mm for deep/surface-invisible landmarks on 40 held-out patients. Patient-mismatch controls indicate patient-specific signal beyond population-level patterns. Coverage ablations show dependence on non-anterior geometry. Optical-transfer diagnostics reveal coverage-related and global-configuration components; deployable optical inference remains unresolved.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates controlled feasibility of hidden skeletal landmark inference from external geometry and provides a basis for future development of architectures that infer internal landmarks from CT-derived surfaces.

Abstract: Existing 3D facial-landmark methods localize points on visible skin, but whether CT-defined internal skeletal landmarks can be inferred from external soft-tissue geometry remains unclear. We formulate a coordinate-consistent surface-to-skeleton task using same-acquisition CT-derived surfaces, separating estimation from optical-to-CT registration, scanner-domain, and acquisition-state effects, with coverage analyzed separately. From 240 clinical CT scans from two hospitals, we construct a locked retrospective protocol pairing CT-derived external soft-tissue point clouds with 21 skeletal landmarks and three visible soft-tissue landmarks. An integrated hierarchical point-cloud model achieves 2.97 mm mean radial error on skeletal landmarks and 3.03 mm on deep or surface-invisible landmarks in 40 held-out patients. Patient-mismatch controls support patient-specific signal beyond a fixed population configuration or global similarity alone, while coverage ablations indicate dependence on non-anterior geometry. Optical-transfer diagnostics reveal substantial coverage-related and global-configuration components, although deployable optical inference remains unresolved. These results answer the controlled feasibility question affirmatively and provide a basis for hidden skeletal landmark inference.

</details>


### [5] [StrAD: A Streaming Method and Benchmark for Audio Description Generation for Long-form Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12549)
*Julian Spravil,Sebastian Houben,Sven Behnke*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: StrAD introduces a long-form, streaming benchmark for automatic descriptive narration (AD) in full-length videos, reframing AD generation as streaming dense video captioning. It evaluates a fine-tuned model (StrAD-FT) and a zero-shot baseline (StrAD-Zero), achieving state-of-the-art results on segment-level CMD-AD, establishing a reference on StrAD, and showing modest gains in a full-video streaming setting, with notable limitations in localization and coherence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address accessibility gaps for blind/low-vision audiences by moving beyond clip-based AD generation and expensive manual annotation. Current methods rely on short clips and extra cues; there is a need for long-form, streaming AD generation without ground-truth timestamps to scale coverage across diverse content.

Method: Propose StrAD benchmark for long-form AD generation. Reformulate as streaming dense video captioning. Use a sliding-window approach to process full-length videos, inserting ADs into transcripts without ground-truth timestamps. Evaluate both fine-tuned models (StrAD-FT) and zero-shot vision-language prompting (StrAD-Zero). Benchmark includes CMD-AD, StrAD, and MAD-Eval; compare segment-level and full-video streaming tasks.

Result: Segment-level: StrAD-FT achieves 36.3 CIDEr on CMD-AD ( +10.0 over Shot-by-shot ), 51.0 CIDEr on StrAD, and 24.9 CIDEr on MAD-Eval. Full-video streaming: StrAD-FT reaches SODA 2.4, StrAD-Zero 1.1. Both show limitations in temporal localization and narrative coherence. First streaming, offline-less AD generation; establishes a measurable progress toward scalable accessibility.

Conclusion: StrAD is the first streaming solution for full-video AD generation without ground-truth timestamps, yielding measurable gains and a new benchmark for long-form AD. It demonstrates feasibility and provides a reference point for future improvements, though temporal precision and narrative coherence require advancement.

Abstract: Visual content is the dominant medium of communication, yet without audio descriptions (ADs), it remains inaccessible to blind and low-vision people. ADs narrate context-relevant visual events during natural audio pauses. Manually creating ADs is expensive, limiting coverage to a small fraction of available content. Most existing automatic AD generation methods frame the task as video clip captioning, requiring ground-truth timestamps and additional context cues such as character databases. Current benchmarks reinforce this framing, consisting of short video segments paired with automatic or task-mismatched annotations. We introduce StrAD, a benchmark for long-form AD generation on full-length videos spanning diverse genres such as movies, documentaries, short films, performances, and video games. We reformulate AD generation as streaming dense video captioning. Our approach processes full-length videos with a sliding window, inserting ADs into existing transcripts without ground-truth timestamps, and supports both fine-tuned models and zero-shot prompting of vision-language models. On the segment-level task with given timestamps, our fine-tuned StrAD-FT sets the state of the art on CMD-AD with 36.3 CIDEr (+10.0 over Shot-by-shot), establishes a reference point on StrAD (51.0 CIDEr), and remains competitive on MAD-Eval at 24.9 CIDEr. On the full-video streaming task, StrAD-FT reaches a SODA score of 2.4 against 1.1 for our zero-shot baseline StrAD-Zero, though both exhibit limitations in temporal localization and narrative coherence. While prior work has tackled full-video AD generation in an offline, multi-stage fashion, ours is the first streaming approach, generating ADs on the fly without ground-truth timestamps. StrAD makes progress on full-video AD generation measurable, a prerequisite for scaling accessibility.

</details>


### [6] [Attribute-Conditioned Multimodal Slot Factorization for Controllable Fashion Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12570)
*Najmeh Forouzandehmehr,Topojoy Biswas,Evren Korpeoglu,Kannan Achan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MM-slotgate introduces a four-slot multimodal encoder to factorize Fashion-CLIP embeddings into color, pattern, category, and demographic slots with individual gates, achieving interpretable, controllable retrieval and improved color performance on H&M.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome monolithic embeddings and opaque item-level codes, enabling attribute-specific control in fashion retrieval by balancing image-driven vs text-driven signals per attribute.

Method: Proposes MM-slotgate, four named slots with per-slot text-image gates; combines slot-similarity and slot-logit retrieval scores; evaluates on H&M with linear probes and quantized slot codes for intervention; reports breakdown by color, etc.

Result: Macro ConstraintSatisfied@10: 0.7566 (vs baselines 0.7142 and 0.4755); color performance rise from 0.321 to 0.889; color gate 57.4% image evidence; gates interpretable; no leakage; 15.3x lift for color using quantized slots.

Conclusion: Typed, attribute-conditioned multimodal slots outperform monolithic or item-level methods; retrieval becomes more controllable and interpretable.

Abstract: Fashion retrieval often requires satisfying multiple attributes at once, such as category, color, pattern, and demographic. Monolithic embeddings mix these signals into a single vector, making attribute-specific control difficult at retrieval time. Many existing semantic-ID methods provide discrete item codes, but these codes are typically optimized as item-level or residual addresses and do not expose named, independently controllable attribute slots.
  We introduce MM-slotgate, a multimodal slot encoder that factorizes Fashion-CLIP text and image embeddings into four named attribute slots. Each slot learns its own text-image gate, so visually grounded attributes such as color and pattern can rely more on image evidence, while taxonomy-oriented attributes such as category and demographic can remain more text-driven.
  On H&M, using a combined slot-similarity and slot-logit retrieval score, MM-slotgate achieves 0.7566 macro ConstraintSatisfied@10, outperforming equal-weight multimodal fusion (0.7142) and fCLIP text-only retrieval (0.4755). The largest gain is on color, which improves from 0.321 to 0.889 (+0.568 absolute), as the learned color gate assigns 57.4% weight to image evidence. The learned gates are interpretable without modality supervision: color is image-leaning, category is text-leaning, and pattern and demographic lie near the middle.
  The resulting slots also remain controllable: linear probes show no measured excess leakage beyond the label-correlation baseline, and quantized slot codes support targeted intervention, including a 15.3x lift for color. These results suggest that controllable fashion retrieval benefits from typed, attribute-conditioned multimodal slots rather than either a single global embedding or opaque item-level semantic IDs.

</details>


### [7] [PseudoMapLabeler: Confidence-Aware Pseudo-Label Generation for Semi-Supervised Online Mapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12600)
*Chikao Tsuchiya,Dhaval Bhanderi,David Ilstrup,Hsinmin Cheng,Christopher Ostafew*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A semi-supervised teacher-student framework for online HD map construction uses Beta-based confidence maps and a spatial clipping mechanism to refine pseudo-labels, improving map prediction under low-label data regimes (+6.1 mAP on nuScenes).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: HD map construction suffers from limited labeled data, hindering model generalization across diverse environments. A data-efficient SSL approach can leverage unlabeled data to improve performance without extensive annotations.

Method: 1) Train a teacher on limited labeled data. 2) Use Beta-distribution-based confidence maps to assess reliability of predicted map elements across temporal observations. 3) Apply spatial clipping to retain high-confidence regions while removing unreliable segments, forming refined map priors. 4) Use refined predictions to re-train the teacher on unlabeled data (second pass). 5) Generate pseudo-labels from enhanced predictions. 6) Train a student model from scratch with pseudo-labels and fine-tune on the original labeled data.

Result: Experimental results on nuScenes show a +6.1 mAP improvement under a low-label regime compared to training solely on labeled data.

Conclusion: The proposed confidence-guided refinement and teacher-student framework provides a practical, data-efficient solution to labeled-data scarcity in online HD map construction, though calibration, domain shift, and computation remain considerations.

Abstract: A critical challenge in deploying online HD map construction systems to real-world scenarios is the scarcity of labeled training data, which limits model generalization in diverse environments. To address this limitation, we propose a teacher-student semi-supervised learning (SSL) framework that generates high-quality pseudo-labels from unlabeled data through confidence-aware map refinement. Our approach first trains a teacher model on limited labeled data, then leverages Beta-distribution-based confidence maps to assess the reliability of predicted map elements across temporal observations. Unlike conventional filtering methods that discard entire elements, we introduce a spatial clipping technique that selectively preserves high-confidence regions while removing unreliable segments. The refined map elements serve as map priors that improve the teacher model's prediction accuracy on unlabeled data in a second pass. These enhanced predictions become pseudo-labels for training a student model from scratch, followed by fine-tuning on the original labeled data. Experimental results on the nuScenes dataset demonstrate that our teacher-student framework with refined pseudo-labels improves performance by +6.1 mAP under a low-label regime compared to training on labeled data alone, offering a practical solution to the labeled data scarcity problem in online HD map construction.

</details>


### [8] [From Visual Widgets to UI Code: Efficient Tool-Grounded Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12611)
*Houston H. Zhang,Tao Zhang,Li Gu,Linfeng Ye,Yuanhao Yu,Xinxin Zuo,Yang Wang,Zhixiang Chi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: WidgetGen grounds observable evidence to guide direct widget-to-code generation, improving fidelity without fixed UI schemas.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Screen-to-code tasks face a trade-off: direct multimodal generation can hallucinate details, while structured pipelines constrain outputs to predefined templates and representations. A middle ground seeks better fidelity and flexibility.

Method: Propose WidgetGen, a lightweight tool-grounded framework that extracts observable text and color evidence, performs high-level layout and optional chart reasoning, and directly generates executable JSX. Evaluates across six multimodal models on 1,000 held-out widgets.

Result: WidgetGen outperforms direct prompting and the Widget2Code pipeline on most visual reconstruction metrics (area, legibility, style) and yields consistent gains. Reconstruction-derived image-code pairs further improve six Qwen-family open-weight models via supervised fine-tuning across all reported metrics.

Conclusion: Selective evidence grounding offers a strong, lightweight alternative to extensive representation constraints, achieving favorable fidelity–efficiency trade-offs and establishing WidgetGen as a robust baseline.

Abstract: Existing screenshot-to-code systems face a trade-off between flexibility and controllability. Direct multimodal generation can hallucinate visible details, whereas structured pipelines reduce such errors through component-wise decomposition, predefined templates, and customized intermediate representations. These structures, however, introduce additional generative orchestration and restrict outputs to designs covered by the representation. We investigate whether selective tool grounding can improve the fidelity--efficiency trade-off of direct widget-to-code generation. We introduce \textbf{WidgetGen}, a lightweight tool-grounded framework that extracts observable text and color evidence, performs high-level layout and optional chart reasoning, and directly generates executable JavaScript XML (\emph{JSX}). This design reduces reliance on component-wise generation while avoiding a fixed UI schema. Across six multimodal models and \(1{,}000\) held-out widgets, WidgetGen outperforms direct prompting and the structured Widget2Code pipeline on most visual reconstruction metrics, with consistent gains in area, legibility, and style. Finally, reconstruction-derived image-code pairs improve six Qwen-family open-weight models across every reported metric through supervised fine-tuning. These results establish WidgetGen as a strong lightweight baseline and show that selective evidence grounding offers an effective alternative to extensive representation constraints.

</details>


### [9] [EgoCITE: Context-Augmented Indexing and Time-Aware Retrieval for Long-Horizon Egocentric Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12627)
*Le Zhang,Ke Sun*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: EgoCITE is a three-component framework that augments egocentric memory for long-horizon QA by creating context-rich memory indices and time-aware evidence retrieval, achieving higher accuracy at lower cost across EgoLifeQA, EgoMem, and EgoR1-Bench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two bottlenecks limit current egocentric memory systems: context-poor captions hinder agentic search, and retrieval ignores a user's temporal intent. A memory system should produce self-contained indices and align evidence with temporal question intent.

Method: EgoScheme converts fragmentary video captions and transcripts into atomic memory indices using local multimodal context; EgoIndex builds multi-view indices (action, activity, utterance, conversation) at multiple granularities; EgoRetrv performs semantic search with question-conditioned temporal relevance scoring and evidence curation.

Result: Evaluation on EgoLifeQA, EgoMem, and EgoR1-Bench shows EgoCITE improves answer accuracy by 4.4–14.2 percentage points over agentic-memory baselines and reduces inference cost by ~36x compared with long-context LLM agents.

Conclusion: The framework effectively addresses the two bottlenecks, enabling reliable long-horizon, temporally aligned egocentric QA with substantial cost savings; its modular design supports scalable, multi-granularity memory and time-aware evidence retrieval.

Abstract: Long-horizon egocentric memory transforms continuous first-person video and audio into a searchable record of past experiences. We demonstrate two bottlenecks in existing systems: indices built from context-poor captions are unreliable for agentic search, while retrieval ignores a question's temporal intent. To address both bottlenecks, we introduce EgoCITE (Egocentric Context-augmented Indexing and Time-aware Evidence retrieval), a long-horizon agentic memory framework for egocentric QA. EgoCITE comprises three components. EgoScheme uses local multimodal context to turn fragmentary video captions and speech transcripts into self-contained atomic memory indices. EgoIndex organizes complementary action, activity, utterance, and conversation representations into searchable multi-view memory indices at multiple granularities. EgoRetrv combines semantic search with question-conditioned temporal relevance scoring and curation of retrieved evidence. We evaluate EgoCITE on EgoLifeQA, EgoMem, and EgoR1-Bench in terms of answer accuracy and target-event retrieval alignment. EgoCITE improves accuracy over agentic memory baselines by at least 4.4--14.2\% while achieving 36$\times$ lower cost than long-context LLM agents.

</details>


### [10] [Inference-Time Orthogonal Seeding Enables Geometry-Aligned 3D Organ Segmentation for Slice-Propagation Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12658)
*Md Rakibul Haque,Tushar Kataria,Shireen Y. Elhabian*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Multi-axis seed propagation improves voxel-level 3D segmentation; training strategy has limited impact while inference-time orthogonal seed geometry dominates performance gains, outperforming single-axis propagation by large margins on multi-organ CT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dense voxel-level annotations for 3D medical images are expensive; single-slice propagation like Sli2Vol suffers from error accumulation due to axial-only propagation and underutilizes 3D information. The work investigates how training and inference choices, especially seed geometry, affect this propagation.

Method: Systematic comparison of training vs inference choices for slice-propagation models: single-axis vs multi-axis label-free registration; single-seed vs multi-seed propagation; and orthogonal seed configurations. Proposes using three orthogonal seeds (axial, coronal, sagittal) and fusing their propagated labels with a simple label-free rule. Evaluation on a multi-organ CT cohort against single-axis baseline.

Result: Training paradigm showed limited impact; an axially trained network applied to off-axis seeds recaptures almost all gains, with explicit three-axis training adding little. Inference-time seed geometry, particularly seed orthogonality, drove performance; a budget-matched three-axial control offered no benefit and could degrade results. Quantitatively, orthogonal seeding with the axial Sli2Vol backbone yielded Dice +21.9%, Normalized Surface Dice +25.5%, and Average Hausdorff Distance −53.5% vs single-axis baseline.

Conclusion: Inference-time seed geometry is the dominant factor in multi-seed, label-free propagation for voxel-level segmentation. Orthogonal (three-axis) seeding provides substantial gains without requiring three-axis training, suggesting practical, efficient improvements for Sli2Vol-like methods. Future work could explore more sophisticated fusion strategies or robustness across modalities and anatomies.

Abstract: Dense voxel-level annotation remains a major bottleneck in 3D medical image segmentation. Single-slice propagation methods such as Sli2Vol reduce this burden by propagating one annotated seed slice through a volume using label-free registration. However, axial-only propagation accumulates errors with distance from the seed, especially in surface-distance metrics, because it ignores coronal and sagittal evidence and therefore underuses the 3D information available in CT/MRI volumes. To better leverage volumetric geometry, we study how key training and inference choices affect slice-propagation models, including single-axis versus multi-axis label-free registration, single-seed versus multi-seed propagation, and orthogonal seed configurations. Instead of propagating from a single axial seed, we use three orthogonal seeds---one axial, one coronal, and one sagittal---and fuse their propagated labels with a simple label-free rule. Our results show that the training paradigm has limited impact: an axially trained network applied to off-axis seeds captures nearly all the improvement, while explicit three-axis training adds little. Instead, performance is driven by inference-time seed geometry, especially orthogonality rather than the number of annotated slices, as a budget-matched three-axial control provides no benefit and can even degrade performance. On a multi-organ CT cohort, orthogonal seeding with the axial Sli2Vol backbone improves Dice by 21.9%, Normalized Surface Dice by 25.5%, and reduces Average Hausdorff Distance by 53.5% over the single-axis baseline.

</details>


### [11] [Mr3D-VL: A generalist vision language foundation model for Multiparametric 3D Magnetic Resonance Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12689)
*Zhi Qiao,Xintong Wu,Yichu He,Feng Shi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A 4B-parameter 3D visual-language foundation model (Mr3D-VL) for multi-parametric MRI that jointly models 3D space and multiple imaging modalities, using unsupervised pre-training, 4D rotational embeddings, and a multi-resolution cross-modal projection to improve report generation and QA in mpMRI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current mpMRI AI models lack natural language interaction and robust cross-modal, cross-slice reasoning; 3D spatial perception and multi-modality integration are essential for accurate glioma assessment but are inadequately addressed by 2D/VLMs or single-modality 3D models.

Method: Unsupervised pre-training of a shared 3D encoder; 4D rotational positional embeddings for dual modality-spatial alignment; cross-modal projection with a multi-resolution feature implantation strategy; designed for multi-parametric 3D MRI across modalities.

Result: Significant performance gains over 4B/7B/30B domain-specific and general models on text generation and comprehension tasks: BERTScore 0.856 for report generation, QA accuracy 0.713, MCQ accuracy 0.912.

Conclusion: Mr3D-VL demonstrates effective collaborative inference across mpMRI modalities and 3D space, achieving strong text generation and QA capabilities and indicating potential clinical utility, while inviting further evaluation on broader datasets and robustness.

Abstract: Multi-parametric magnetic resonance imaging (mpMRI) is a cornerstone for brain tumor diagnosis and treatment, yet current AI models face critical limitations: their lack of natural language interaction and interpretability impedes spatial information integration and cross-modal reasoning required clinically. Key challenges arise from significant physical meaning differences across modalities, spatial misalignment due to scan intervals, and the need for complex multi-feature interpretation in tasks like glioma grading. While visual-language models (VLMs) show promise in cross-modal understanding, existing methods focus mainly on 2D image modeling, neglecting direct perception of 3D volumetric space. Although 3D VLMs have been proposed for report generation and feature alignment in 3D CT imaging, mpMRI applications demand collaborative inference across multiple imaging modalities-a requirement unmet by current solutions. To address this, we introduce Mr3D-VL, a dedicated visual-language foundation model for multi-parametric 3D MRI. With 4 billion parameters, it employs an unsupervised pre-trained shared 3D encoder and 4D rotational positional embedding for dual modality-spatial integration. Its cross-modal projection layer uses a multi-resolution feature implantation strategy to enhance feature perception across resolutions. Experimental results show significant improvements over existing 4B/7B/30B domain-specific and general-purpose models in text generation tasks, achieving a BERTScore of 0.856 for report generation, with question-answering accuracy at 0.713 and multiple-choice accuracy at 0.912.

</details>


### [12] [Class Geometry as Supervision for Sample-Efficient Open-World Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12698)
*Akash Rao,Zhou Chen,Revanth Reddy Palem,Udhav Ramachandran,Ruth Scimeca,Sathyanarayanan N. Aakur*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CGS proposes a dissimilarity-preserving objective that enforces a target geometry among class representations, improving open-world and few-shot detection by maintaining relational class structure without sacrificing standard task loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-world object detection must recognize known classes, reject unknowns, and learn new classes, but in scarce-data regimes class relationships matter. Prototype-based detectors treat prototypes independently, missing relational structure among classes.

Method: Introduce class-geometry supervision (CGS) that aligns pairwise distances of learned class representations with a target class-geometry matrix, while keeping the original detection loss. The objective is instantiated across multiple detection setups (prototype recognition, few-shot biomedical detection, open-set/novel-class insertion, OWOD on COCO).

Result: CGS improves sample efficiency and unknown recall, strengthens novel-class insertion, and preserves known-class detection on COCO. Ablations show visual geometry yields the most reliable gains; random geometry helps novelty separation but is less consistent for few-shot tasks.

Conclusion: Relational class geometry is an effective supervisory signal for calibrated, extensible open-world detectors under limited supervision.

Abstract: Open-world object detection requires models to recognize known categories, reject unfamiliar objects, and incorporate new classes over time. This is especially challenging in scarce-data settings such as biomedical and scientific imaging, where rare categories may have only a few annotated examples and fine-grained classes differ by subtle morphology. Prototype-based detectors are natural for this regime, but they typically learn class prototypes as independent anchors, ignoring relational structure among classes. We propose class-geometry supervision (CGS), a general framework that constrains learned prototype or class-representation spaces to preserve visual or semantic class dissimilarities estimated from training data. CGS introduces a dissimilarity-preserving objective that aligns pairwise distances among learned class representations with a target class-geometry matrix while retaining the standard task loss. We instantiate the same objective across prototype recognition, few-shot biomedical object detection, open-set detection, novel-class insertion, and OWOD adaptation on COCO. Experiments show that CGS improves sample efficiency in recognition and ova detection, substantially strengthens novel-class insertion, and improves unknown recall on COCO while retaining much of the known-class detection performance. Ablations show that meaningful visual geometry provides the most reliable gains, while random geometry can help novel separation but is less consistent for few-shot detection. These results suggest that relational class geometry is an effective supervisory signal for building calibrated and extensible open-world detectors under limited supervision.

</details>


### [13] [Towards Sparsely Annotated Open-World Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12714)
*HeeJu Han,AJeong Kim,Jinsun Park*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DPOD is a unified framework for sparsely annotated open-world object detection (SA-OWOD) that jointly addresses unlabeled known and unknown objects via two modules, achieving improved unknown object detection over prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world detection involves ambiguous supervision where unlabeled regions may be either missing annotations of known objects or genuinely unknown categories. SAOD and OWOD address these issues separately, but their co-occurrence remains unresolved. SA-OWOD introduces a task that combines sparse supervision with unseen categories, motivating methods that can recover supervision for known objects while reliably discovering unknowns.

Method: DPOD combines two complementary components: (1) Known Target Recovery Module (KTRM) that recovers supervision for unlabeled known instances and regularizes the feature space to separate known vs unknown representations; (2) Dual-Disagreement Target Generator (DDTG) that uses cross-view semantic inconsistency to identify reliable unknown candidates. The integration resolves contradictory signals from ambiguous unlabeled regions, stabilizing decision boundaries and differentiating known from unknown objects.

Result: Experiments on sparsely annotated open-world benchmarks show that DPOD outperforms existing open-world detection methods, with particular gains in detecting unknown objects.

Conclusion: DPOD effectively tackles the SA-OWOD problem by resolving supervision contradictions and enforcing clearer boundaries between known and unknown categories, leading to improved unknown-object detection in real-world, sparsely annotated settings.

Abstract: Real-world object detection operates under ambiguous supervision, where unlabeled regions may correspond to missing annotations of known objects or genuinely unknown categories. These challenges have been addressed separately in Sparsely Annotated Object Detection (SAOD) and Open-World Object Detection (OWOD). In practice, their co-occurrence remains an open problem. To address this problem, we introduce Sparsely Annotated Open-World Object Detection (SA-OWOD), a new task that jointly considers sparse supervision and the presence of unseen categories. We propose Dual-Perspective Object Discovery (DPOD), a unified framework that jointly models unlabeled known and unknown instances via two complementary mechanisms. The Known Target Recovery Module (KTRM) recovers supervision for unlabeled known instances and explicitly regularizes the feature space to separate known and unknown representations. Complementarily, the Dual-Disagreement Target Generator (DDTG) identifies reliable unknown candidates through cross-view semantic inconsistency. By integrating these modules, DPOD resolves contradictory supervision signals caused by ambiguous unlabeled regions. As a result, it prevents misclassification between known and unknown objects and stabilizes the decision boundaries. Experimental results on sparsely annotated open-world benchmarks demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms existing open-world detection methods, particularly in detecting unknown objects.

</details>


### [14] [VOS-Agent: The 1st Place Solution for the 8th LSVOS Challenge (MOSEv2 Track)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12721)
*Canyang Wu,Jinrong Zhang,Xusheng He,Ce Bian,Xianjing Han,Jianlong Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A modular VOS framework (VOS-Agent) routes targets to specialized agents (tiny, semantic, regular) while leveraging SAM3 for dense segmentation, achieving state-of-the-art performance on MOSEv2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address robust video object segmentation under severe occlusion, disappearance, and reappearance, where a single inference path is unreliable for tiny or semantics-dominated targets.

Method: A Target Perception and Routing Agent classifies sequences into three routes (regular, tiny, semantic-dominated). Tiny targets are handled by a Visual Tracking Agent using confidence-aware box prompts; semantic-dominated targets are handled by an MLLM-based Semantic Agent performing description-guided localization and candidate verification; SAM3 serves as the shared dense segmentation backbone. The approach conditionally activates specialized agents per target characteristics.

Result: On the MOSEv2 test set, achieve 69.82% on the official J&F metric and rank first in the MOSEv2 Track of the 8th LSVOS Challenge at ECCV 2026.

Conclusion: Conditional activation of targeted, specialized agents integrated with a shared segmentation backbone improves VOS performance for challenging targets (tiny and semantics-driven), achieving state-of-the-art results on MOSEv2.

Abstract: Complex video object segmentation requires robust target propagation under severe occlusion, disappearance and reappearance. Although SAM3 provides strong promptable mask propagation, a uniform inference path remains unreliable for tiny targets with insufficient visual evidence and semantic-dominated targets whose identities depend on explicit attributes. To this end, we present VOS-Agent, a collaborative framework that retains SAM3 as the shared dense segmentation module and conditionally activates specialized agents according to target characteristics. A Target Perception and Routing Agent assigns each sequence to a regular, tiny, or semantic-dominated route. Tiny targets are supported by a Visual Tracking Agent through confidence-aware box prompts, while semantic-dominated targets are handled by an MLLM-based Semantic Agent through description-guided localization and candidate verification. On the MOSEv2 test set, VOS-Agent achieves 69.82% on the official $\mathcal{J}\&\dot{\mathcal{F}}$ metric and ranks first in the MOSEv2 Track of the 8th LSVOS Challenge at ECCV 2026.

</details>


### [15] [A Generative Approach for Improving Multi-Label Defect Classification in Photovoltaic Modules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12725)
*Abdul Mueez,Yogesh S. Rawat,Shruti Vyas*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GDI uses LaMa inpainting with FFT convolutions to synthesize single-defect EL PV cell images, enabling targeted training for multi-label defect classification and yielding large gains in low-data regimes, especially for rare defects, while reducing errors from co-occurring defects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address learning ambiguity in multi-label PV defect classification caused by co-occurring defects and data scarcity for individual defect types; improve data efficiency through realistic single-defect samples.

Method: Generative Defect Isolation (GDI) pipeline: identify defects to remove, apply LaMa inpainting with Fast Fourier Convolutions to fill removed regions and generate single-defect training samples; evaluate across ViT-S, ViT-L, and EfficientNetV2-L architectures on EL PV cell images.

Result: GDI significantly outperforms baselines; largest gains in low-data settings; rare defect classes see up to 63.6% F1-score improvement; 26% reduction in co-occurring defect misclassification; sets new performance benchmark for multi-label classification in this domain.

Conclusion: GDI effectively leverages existing segmentation datasets to maximize data utility, enabling more accurate and data-efficient multi-label defect classification in PV EL images, with notable improvements for rare defects and reduced ambiguity from co-occurring defects.

Abstract: This paper addresses the challenge of multi-label defect classification in electroluminescence (EL) images of photovoltaic (PV) cells. Training models on images where multiple defects co-occur creates learning ambiguity, making it difficult to disentangle visual features for specific defect types, a problem compounded by the scarcity of examples for individual classes. To tackle this, we introduce Generative Defect Isolation (GDI), utilizing the LaMa inpainting model with Fast Fourier Convolutions to remove selected defects and generate realistic, single-defect training samples. Extensive experiments on Vision Transformer (ViT-S, ViT-L) and EfficientNetV2-L architectures demonstrate that GDI significantly outperforms baselines. The performance gains are most pronounced in low-data scenarios; class-wise analysis shows substantial improvements, boosting the F1-Score for rare defect classes by up to 63.6%. Furthermore, GDI effectively resolves learning ambiguity from co-occurring defects, yielding a 26% reduction in such co-occurring classification errors. Our work establishes GDI as an effective method for maximizing the value of existing segmentation datasets and sets a new performance benchmark for multi-label classification in this domain.

</details>


### [16] [Dual-Manifold Geometry Guided Representation Learning: Adaptive Coupling between Kernel and Data Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12737)
*Wencong Zhang,Yue Zhang,Meiyan Huang,Wei Yang,Qianjin Feng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A dual-manifold framework (kernel and data manifolds) guides feature learning via a kernel-guided transform that reshapes feature covariance, with depth-aware modes; applicable to CNNs and transformers and improves tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep representation learning has largely ignored the structured geometry encoded in network parameters. The paper proposes a dual-manifold view where a Kernel Manifold (filters) and a Data Manifold (intermediate features) share the channel space, enabling geometric information from the kernel to guide feature evolution.

Method: Introduce Kernel-Guided Feature Transform (KGFT): derive a geometric guidance matrix from the kernel Gram matrix and use it to transform the covariance of feature representations. Besides conventional attention, KGFT reshapes feature relationships by transferring geometry from the kernel to the data manifold. Include Exploit and Explore modes with depth-aware scheduling and a learnable guidance strength to adaptively control geometric transformation, promoting shallow-layer alignment and deeper-layer diversity. Provide theoretical analysis of the transformation and its impact on feature covariance.

Result: Experiments across CNN- and Transformer-based architectures (ResNet, ViT, LLaMA-7B) show consistent improvements on image classification and arithmetic reasoning tasks. The approach demonstrates generality across architectures, with theoretical backing for the transformation and its covariance effects. Code will be publicly available.

Conclusion: Kernel-guided dual-manifold representation learning offers a general, effective mechanism to incorporate parameter geometry into representation learning, yielding improved performance while maintaining flexibility across architectures and tasks.

Abstract: Deep representation learning has primarily focused on how features evolve across network layers, while largely overlooking the structured geometry embedded in network parameters. We introduce a dual-manifold perspective in which each convolutional layer contains two coupled geometric spaces: a Kernel Manifold induced by convolutional filters and a Data Manifold characterized by intermediate feature representations. Because these manifolds share the same channel space, parameter geometry can provide complementary structural information to guide feature evolution. Based on this insight, we propose Kernel-Guided Feature Transform (KGFT), a lightweight module that derives a geometric guidance matrix from the kernel Gram matrix and uses it to transform the covariance structure of feature representations. Unlike conventional attention mechanisms that reweight feature responses, KGFT explicitly reshapes feature relationships by transferring geometric information from the kernel manifold to the data manifold. To accommodate network hierarchy, we further introduce Exploit and Explore modes with a depth-aware scheduling strategy and a learnable guidance strength that adaptively controls the contribution of geometric transformation. This design promotes geometric alignment in shallow layers while encouraging feature diversity in deeper layers, without imposing excessive constraints on representation learning. Theoretical analysis establishes the validity of the proposed transformation and characterizes its effect on feature covariance. Extensive experiments across CNN- and Transformer-based architectures, including ResNet, ViT, and LLaMA-7B, demonstrate consistent improvements on image classification and arithmetic reasoning tasks, validating the generality and effectiveness of kernel-guided dual-manifold representation learning. Code will be publicly available.

</details>


### [17] [Dual-Stream Cross-Anchor Correction Grounding Long-Form Captions and the Domain Limits of Object-Level Anchors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12746)
*LingKai Bu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DSCC injects object-level visual anchors into fine-tuned multimodal LMs to curb object hallucination, outperforming standard SFT on long captions under a unified evaluation, while showing domain-conditional improvements and synergy between perception and cognition streams.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Object hallucination arises when language priors and co-occurrence bias overpower visual evidence; existing remedies are inefficient or fail on longer captions. A unified approach to anchor visuals in the LM aims to align object mentions with image content throughout generation.

Method: Introduce Dual-Stream Cross-Anchor Correction (DSCC): a perception stream aligns object-level hidden states at an intermediate LM layer to frozen text anchors via a bidirectional contrastive objective; a cognition stream enables deeper layers to query these anchors through cross-attention at every generation step; a two-stage curriculum gate couples both streams to enforce evidence retrieval as a structural constraint at each autoregressive step during fine-tuning.

Result: With a single backbone and scoring protocol, DSCC achieves longer captions with higher factual precision: captions are ~1.9× baseline length and 88.19% precision per object mention, the highest across density-independent settings; outperforms vanilla SFT on the same corpus/schedule. Ablations show the perception stream alone reduces precision but benefits when stacked with the cognition stream; domain-generalization tests reveal domain-conditionality with no universal superiority; performance gains depend on the semantic domain of the anchors and can break under charts/optical illusions.

Conclusion: Injecting object-level anchors into LM tuning via DSCC yields substantial improvements in long-caption formatting and object-mention fidelity but exhibits domain-conditional behavior and synergy-dependent effects; anchors must align semantically with the target domain for consistent gains.

Abstract: Object hallucination in multimodal large language models arises when language priors and corpus co-occurrence bias outweigh the visual evidence, with nothing tying an individual object mention to what the image shows. Most remedies intervene at decoding time without training, yet under a unified protocol their benefit is confined to short captions;supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on a detail- rich corpus lengthens captions, but over forty percent still name absent objects. This paper proposes Dual-Stream Cross-Anchor Correction (DSCC). Unlike work that post-processes decoding, DSCC is the first to inject object-level visual anchors into the language model itself during fine- tuning: a perception stream aligns object-level hidden states at an intermediate layer to frozen text anchors by a bidirectional contrastive objective; a cognition stream lets deeper layers query those anchors by cross-attention at every generation step; and a two-stage curriculum gate couplesthem, making evidence retrieval a structural constraint at each autoregressive step. Under one backbone and one scoring protocol, experiments span long-caption hallucination, object-existence discrimination and cross-domain generalisation, with vanilla SFT on the same corpus and schedule as a length- and density-matched control, so gains are attributed layer by layer. DSCC is the only method reaching the long-caption, low-hallucination region: captions roughly 1.9 times the baseline length at 88.19% precision per object mention, the highest under a density-independent criterion. Ablations expose a synergy: the perception stream alone degrades precision yet reverses sign when stacked on the cognition stream. No universal superiority is claimed: three out-of- domain benchmarks yield a predictable, falsifiable domain-conditionality, the synergy being bound to the anchors' semantic domain and breaking on charts and optical illusions.

</details>


### [18] [Scaling Representation Diversity: Modulated Attention and Reconstructive Regularization for Visual Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12748)
*Junyi Hu,Tian Bai,Fengyi Wu,Yian Huang,Wei Wen,Zaoli Li,Junli Lin,Xingchen Li,Zhenming Peng,Yi Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Unified open-vocabulary REC via data-model co-design; introduces Modulated Attention-Contrastive Head (mACH) and text-conditioned JEPA auxiliary stream; adds Objects365-Caption for large-scale language supervision; provides theory on complementary gradient subspaces; achieves competitive, cross-dataset generalization with a single checkpoint.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: REC models typically rely on dataset-specific fine-tuning, causing poor cross-dataset generalization and reduced representation diversity. A single generalist model with robust, diverse representations is desirable for open-vocabulary grounding.

Method: Architectural: Modulated Attention-Contrastive Head (mACH) enables efficient token-level vision-language alignment; a text-conditioned JEPA auxiliary stream provides supportive gradients to preserve alignment-active representations without adding inference cost. Data: Objects365-Caption enriches Objects365 with context-aware referring expressions for large-scale language supervision. Theory: analysis showing complementary gradient subspaces preserve alignment capacity, enabling scalable representation diversity.

Result: The single-checkpoint framework delivers competitive performance on standard REC benchmarks and shows strong generalization across heterogeneous grounding datasets without dataset-specific adaptation.

Conclusion: Holistic data-model co-design preserves representation diversity and alignment capacity, enabling scalable open-vocabulary grounding with a single generalist model.

Abstract: Referring Expression Comprehension (REC) is commonly studied under dataset-specific fine-tuning, resulting in specialist models with limited cross-dataset generalization. In this work, we revisit REC from the perspective of unified open-vocabulary grounding and identify representation degeneration as a key obstacle to scaling a single generalist model. To preserve representation diversity, we propose a holistic data-model co-design framework. Architecturally, we introduce the Modulated Attention-Contrastive Head (mACH) for efficient token-level vision-language alignment and a text-conditioned JEPA auxiliary stream that provides complementary gradient support to preserve alignment-active representations without inference overhead. On the data side, we introduce Objects365-Caption, enriching Objects365 with context-aware referring expressions for large-scale language supervision. We further provide a theoretical analysis showing that complementary gradient subspaces preserve alignment capacity and thereby scale representation diversity. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our single-checkpoint framework achieves highly competitive performance on standard REC benchmarks while exhibiting strong generalization across heterogeneous grounding datasets without benchmark-specific adaptation.

</details>


### [19] [PatchGen: Learning Soft Intra-Image Predictive Subsets for Visual Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12766)
*Zhaorui Tan,Weimiao Yu,Xi Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PatchGen introduces a sample-dependent predictive-subset mask for image patches, enabling prediction based on an oracle subset per image. This preserves Bayes risk while reducing complexity, improving generalization under data shifts, target shifts, and their combinations, and is competitive with text-supervised methods without using text.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current domain-generalization approaches often aim for domain invariance but ignore intra-image predictive sufficiency. The hypothesis is that each image contains a sample-adaptive oracle subset of patches sufficient for label prediction, with remaining patches forming non-essential context that can introduce spurious correlations. Exploiting this structure can improve generalization and reduce model complexity under distribution shifts.

Method: Propose PatchGen, a text-free module that learns a sample-dependent soft predictive-subset mask as a task-driven proxy for the unobserved oracle subset. Theoretical analysis shows that restricting prediction to the oracle subset preserves Bayes risk while yielding a complexity bound that tightens with the subset size. Empirically, PatchGen uses a soft mask to select patches, with histopathology visualizations showing higher scores for tumor-consistent regions.

Result: Extensive experiments on natural and histopathological image benchmarks under data shifts, target shifts, and their combinations show PatchGen improves average accuracy over matched-backbone baselines in most configurations, enhances generalization to unseen classes, and remains competitive with vision-language methods without text supervision.

Conclusion: The structural hypothesis of intra-image predictive sufficiency is supported by theory and experiments. PatchGen provides a practical, text-free mechanism to learn an adaptive predictive-subset mask that preserves Bayes risk and enhances shift robustness, offering strong performance while avoiding text supervision.

Abstract: Visual classifiers are expected to generalize under data shifts, target shifts, and their combinations, yet most existing methods focus on domain invariance while failing to address intra-image predictive sufficiency. We investigate the structural hypothesis that each image contains a sample-adaptive oracle intra-image predictive subset sufficient for label prediction, while the remaining patches form non-essential complementary context that may correlate with the label. The theoretical analysis shows that restricting prediction to this oracle subset preserves the Bayes risk achievable by the full-patch representation while admitting a complexity bound that tightens with the oracle-subset size. Based on this view, we propose PatchGen, a text-free module that learns a sample-dependent soft predictive-subset mask as a task-driven proxy for the unobserved oracle subset mask. Specifically, histopathology visualizations suggest that PatchGen assigns higher scores to tumor-consistent regions than to some frequently co-occurring inflammatory context. Extensive experiments on natural and histopathological image benchmarks spanning all three shift settings show that PatchGen improves average performance over matched-backbone baselines in most evaluated configurations, enhances generalization to unknown classes, and remains competitive with vision-language methods without text supervision.

</details>


### [20] [CW-BASS v2: Saturation-Aware Pseudo-Label Selection for Semi-Supervised Segmentation under Foundation-Model Teachers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12773)
*Ebenezer Tarubinga*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CW-BASS v2 is a saturation-aware pseudo-label selection gate for semi-supervised semantic segmentation that adapts between strict filtering and an adaptive floor using held-out calibration (pi_kept = Pr[correct | c >= tau]) and per-class noise estimates, removing reliance on a fixed rule and aligning with the confidence regime of self-supervised DINOv2 teachers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In self-supervised contexts, teacher confidence can saturate, making fixed-threshold or single-rule pseudo-label selection ineffective or harmful. A principled method is needed to decide when to strictly filter or when to fall back to a floor based on empirical reliability.

Method: Form a one-pass gate combining held-out calibration with an unbiased per-class noise estimate and a self-adaptive confidence floor. Compute pi_kept on a held-out slice and compare to tau. If pi_kept >= tau, apply strict filtering at the existing operating threshold; otherwise, use the adaptive floor to bound retention away from 1. The boundary, not mIoU-tuned, remains the operating threshold. Validate across six DINOv2 teachers.

Result: CW-BASS v2 recovers the UniMatch V2 operating point on saturated benchmarks (e.g., Pascal VOC and Cityscapes) and yields improvements where the confident set is unreliable (ADE20K, +1.5 mIoU, single seed). It produces the correct strict-vs-floor call across six teachers, and highlights that dynamic confidence ranges (e.g., 98% of Pascal pixels >= 0.95) can cause simple adaptive cutoffs to flood retention and induce confirmation bias if not tempered.

Conclusion: A saturation-aware gating strategy that measures teacher reliability on held-out data provides a robust, principled alternative to fixed-rule pseudo-label selection in semi-supervised semantic segmentation with self-supervised teachers.

Abstract: Semi-supervised semantic segmentation has long turned on one question, which pseudo-labels to trust, and a generation of selection rules, dynamic thresholds, per-class curricula, soft confidence weights, answered it for the noisy, under-confident ResNet teachers of their day. Self-supervised foundation encoders change the regime: with a DINOv2 teacher, confidence saturates, so the filtering that helped a weak teacher can hurt a strong one. We propose CW-BASS v2, a saturation-aware pseudo-label selection method that reads the teacher's confidence regime rather than committing to one rule. It pairs held-out calibration, an unbiased per-class noise estimate, with a self-adaptive confidence floor that provably bounds retention away from 1, and combines them in a one-pass gate: measure the reliability of the teacher's confident set, pi_kept = Pr[correct | c >= tau], on a held-out slice, and filter strictly when it meets the confidence demanded (pi_kept >= tau), falling back to the adaptive floor otherwise. The boundary is the pre-existing operating threshold, not a value tuned to mIoU, and across six DINOv2 teachers it makes the correct strict-vs-floor call blind. CW-BASS v2 thus recovers the UniMatch V2 operating point on the saturated benchmarks by selecting strict (Pascal VOC 1/8 87.4 against its reported 87.9; Cityscapes within 0.5), and improves on it where the confident set is unreliable (pi_kept ~ 89%, ADE20K), where the floor edges ahead (+1.5 mIoU, single seed). The gate is principled because the failure it avoids is measured, not assumed: on a reliable, saturated teacher the confidence distribution's dynamic range collapses (98% of Pascal pixels >= 0.95), so an adaptive cutoff floods the retention mask and self-training decays into confirmation bias.

</details>


### [21] [SCOPE: Subspace Clustering with Online Per-Head Top-K Estimation for Sparse Video Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12780)
*Qi Zhao,Qirui Li,Hanlin Tang,Yiduo Li,Zhen Guo,Cuifeng Shen,Chao Xu,Zhaosheng Chi,Xiaojin Lu,Kan Liu,Tao Lan,Lin Qu,Xi Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SCOPE enables training-free sparse attention for diffusion-video transformers by clustering 3D-RoPE-aligned key subspaces and adaptively estimating per-head Top-k, delivering significant speedups with preserved fidelity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion Transformers suffer from quadratic self-attention on spatiotemporal tokens. Training-free sparse methods rely on coarse proxy scores that can miss important keys or overly concentrate distributions; a per-head, data-adaptive sparsity scheme is needed.

Method: SCOPE partitions RoPE-aligned keys into temporal, height, and width subspaces, clusters each subspace independently, and aggregates centroid scores via lookup tables to obtain per-key proxy scores per query cluster. It derives a head-specific Top-k online by averaging the initial retained-key counts per head (weighted by query cluster size) and adds keys only for clusters whose count falls below this value. Sparse attention is computed over the selected original keys/values, improving efficiency.

Result: Across six model–task configurations, SCOPE outperforms training-free baselines in both fidelity and latency, achieving up to 1.99× end-to-end speedup on 720p HunyuanVideo with 28.46 dB PSNR relative to dense attention.

Conclusion: Training-free sparse attention with per-head adaptive Top-k via 3D subspace clustering improves inference efficiency for video-DiT without sacrificing performance, demonstrating robust gains across multiple configurations.

Abstract: Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) incur quadratic self-attention cost over spatiotemporal tokens. Existing training-free sparse attention methods often construct sparse masks from block-level or cluster-level proxy scores, which can obscure fine-grained differences among keys and miss high contribution keys under aggressive sparsity. Moreover, such proxy scores may yield overly concentrated softmax distributions, causing Top-$p$ to retain too few keys for some query clusters. Although a fixed Top-$k$ minimum alleviates this failure mode, a shared value cannot adapt to variations across heads and inputs. To address both limitations, we propose SCOPE, a training-free sparse attention framework that combines 3D-RoPE-aligned key subspace clustering with online per-head Top-$k$ estimation for efficient video-DiT inference. SCOPE partitions post-RoPE keys into temporal, height, and width subspaces, clusters them independently, and aggregates the corresponding centroid scores through lookup tables to obtain per key proxy scores for each query cluster. Building on existing hybrid Top-$p$/fixed Top-$k$ selection, SCOPE derives a head-specific Top-$k$ value online by averaging the initial retained key counts within each head, weighted by query cluster size, and selects additional keys only for query clusters whose initial retained key counts fall below this value. Sparse attention is then computed over the selected original keys and values. Across six model--task configurations, SCOPE consistently outperforms existing training-free baselines in both fidelity and latency, achieving up to a $1.99\times$ end-to-end speedup on 720p HunyuanVideo with $28.46$ dB PSNR relative to dense attention.

</details>


### [22] [Beyond Correctness: Benchmarking and Aligning Response Behaviors in Hybrid-Thinking MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12781)
*Xinming Wang,Weinong Wang,Hongming Yang,Yansong Lin,Zheng Ruan,Shangpin Peng,Qiming Peng,Nan Qiao,Fengyuan Lu,Guoqing Ma,Marito Li,Songyang Zhang,Saiyong Yang,Han Hu,Yonglong Tian,Xu-Yao Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A diagnostic benchmark (PatternEval) and RL-based penalties (PatternRL) mitigate misalignment between thinking/non-thinking interfaces in hybrid multimodal LLMs, exposing failures and reducing them with minor performance cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To ensure consistent user-visible behavior across deliberative (thinking) and latency-efficient (non-thinking) interfaces, addressing widespread response-pattern failures when switching modes.

Method: Introduce PatternEval, a failure-enriched diagnostic benchmark with 2,415 multimodal prompts across perception, grounding, comprehension, and reasoning; test four failure types (chain-of-thought leakage, repetition, contradiction, performative reasoning); develop PatternRM (a response-level reward model) and PatternRL (mode-aware penalties in RL); evaluate on Qwen3-VL-4B and Qwen3-VL-8B.

Result: PatternRL with pattern-specific penalties mitigates cross-mode misalignment with only a marginal cost to task performance; response-pattern failures are prevalent, especially in non-thinking mode.

Conclusion: PatternEval + PatternRL provide an evaluation-and-training framework to align user-visible response patterns across hybrid-thinking interfaces.

Abstract: Hybrid-thinking multimodal large language models (MLLMs) allow a single model to alternate between deliberative thinking and latency-efficient non-thinking inference. Although these modes differ in reasoning budget, their delivered responses should satisfy the same user-facing standard. Correctness alone may not characterize this response quality; we therefore evaluate task accuracy and response-pattern failures as complementary outcomes. We study this gap through \textbf{response-pattern alignment}: whether thinking and non-thinking interfaces preserve acceptable final-response behavior. We introduce \textbf{PatternEval}, a failure-enriched diagnostic benchmark comprising 2,415 multimodal prompts spanning visual perception and grounding, structured image understanding, and multimodal knowledge reasoning. PatternEval tests four recurrent failures: chain-of-thought leakage, response repetition, logical contradiction, and performative reasoning. Response-pattern failures are widespread across models from different providers, with non-thinking inference exhibiting substantially higher failure rates and thereby creating systematic misalignment between thinking and non-thinking interfaces. Motivated by this diagnosis, we develop \textbf{PatternRM}, a response-level reward model, and \textbf{PatternRL}, which introduces pattern-specific penalties during reinforcement learning. Experiments on Qwen3-VL-4B and Qwen3-VL-8B show that incorporating pattern-specific penalties into reinforcement learning can mitigate cross-mode misalignment while incurring a marginal task performance trade-off. Together, PatternEval and PatternRL provide an evaluation-and-training framework for aligning user-visible response patterns across hybrid-thinking interfaces.

</details>


### [23] [Erase but Preserve: Controllable Removal of Copyrighted Animation Characters via Optimized Semantic Anchors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12806)
*Qiao Li,Xiaomeng Fu,Wangjia Yu,Runze He,Baisen Wang,Jiao Dai,Jizhong Han*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A controllable method to erase target animation characters in text-to-image diffusion models by optimizing a surrogate anchor embedding in the model’s textual space and replacing target embeddings with an adaptive, structure-aware anchor. This achieves strong erasure with preserved image fidelity and supports flexible, multi-target, and transferable erasure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The unauthorized reproduction of animation characters in diffusion-generated content raises copyright concerns. Existing concept-erasure methods are inadequate for highly distinctive characters and lack fine-grained control, hindering real-world deployment.

Method: The approach optimizes a structurally informed anchor embedding as a character surrogate. It imposes structural and detailed constraints to capture the character and then uses a structure-aware adaptive strategy to replace target-related embeddings with the anchor during generation, operating directly in the model's continuous textual representation. It additionally enables controllable erasure degree, multi-target removal, and model transferability; anchors are plug-and-play with existing baselines.

Result: Experiments show state-of-the-art erasure effectiveness and preservation of image fidelity, with support for controllable erasure strength, multiple-target removal, and cross-model transferability; optimized anchors can improve other model-modification approaches.

Conclusion: The proposed anchor-based, structure-aware erasure in the model’s textual space offers effective, flexible, and transferable character erasure, addressing limitations of prior methods and enabling practical deployment.

Abstract: The exceptional generation capabilities of text-to-image diffusion models have raised copyright concerns, particularly the unauthorized reproduction of animation characters. Existing concept erasure methods fall short for animation character erasure: model modification methods struggle to identify suitable anchors for diverse, highly distinctive characters; prompt-based steering methods lack fine-grained control for precise intervention. These approaches often yield incomplete erasure and degraded image fidelity, hindering real-world deployment. In this paper, we propose a controllable method operating on the model's continuous textual representation to erase target characters during generation. We optimizes an anchor embedding via structural and detailed constraints to serve as a character surrogate, then replaces target-related embeddings with the anchor via a structure-aware adaptive strategy. Experiments show that our method achieves state-of-the-art erasure effectiveness and image fidelity preservation, while supporting controllable erasure degree, multi-target removal, and model transferability. Moreover, our optimized anchors are plug-and-play with current model modification baselines to improve their erasure performance.

</details>


### [24] [Structured Local Differential Modeling for AI-Generated Image Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12811)
*Jiazhen Yang,Ruijin Jin,Junjun Zheng,Xiangheng Kong,Zunlei Feng,Jie Lei*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RippleNet detects AI-generated images by exploiting low-SNR forensic cues in local statistics, using adaptive forgery-sensitive regions, multi-directional multi-scale local differential representations, and differential-attention in the local space; achieves competitive results across benchmarks and cross-generator tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Detectors biased toward semantically salient, high-SNR regions neglect valuable low-level statistical traces of generation mechanisms. An information-theoretic view suggests strengthening low-SNR signals in local statistics to improve detection.

Method: RippleNet constructs local differential representations in neighborhoods, identifies forgery-sensitive regions adaptively, builds multi-directional, multi-scale differential features, and refines attention to operate in the local differential space to capture pixel-level traces beyond conventional CNNs or patch-level attention.

Result: Extensive experiments on public benchmarks and cross-generator evaluation show consistent competitive performance.

Conclusion: A locally-focused differential-signal framework can effectively reveal subtle forensics traces in AI-generated images, addressing limitations of globally-focused detectors and enabling finer-grained statistical dependencies; results are promising but further improvements across diverse generators may be sought.

Abstract: The rapid advancement of AI-generated content has made the reliable detection of generated images an increasingly critical challenge. Existing detection methods are often dominated during training by semantically salient components with high signal-to-noise ratios (SNRs), thereby suppressing subtler forensic cues associated with the underlying generation mechanisms and embedded in low-level statistical structures. From an information-theoretic perspective, we present a key insight: effective detection in the low-level statistical space requires mitigating the dominance of semantic components while emphasizing and amplifying responses to low-SNR forgery traces. Building on this insight, we propose RippleNet, an AI-generated image detection framework based on local differential signals.
