Method: Introduce Structured Physical Chain-of-Thought (SP-CoT): a per-subdiscipline schema (six steps: scene identification, force/process analysis, governing laws, synthesis) with fidelity rules; symbolic math typing; used as dense training supervision and as a structured prompt. Build a 4.3M-image physics dataset (115,037 expert-annotated) and a unified multimodal backbone. Create VeriphyT2IBench, deriving item-specific binary questions from each diagram's annotations to decompose model scoring. Evaluate on the physics subset of GenExam and VeriphyT2IBench.

Result: Explicit physics-structured supervision yields improved physical faithfulness of generated diagrams on held-out physics diagrams.

Conclusion: Structured supervision via SP-CoT, a large annotated dataset, and the VeriphyT2IBench benchmark enable more reliable physics-faithful scientific diagram generation.

Abstract: Text-to-image generation has reached photorealistic quality, yet state-of-the-art systems remain unreliable at producing scientific diagrams, whose value depends not on appearance but on physical faithfulness: correct force directions, valid coordinate systems, consistent thermodynamic states, and equations matching the depicted scenario. Trained on web imagery with physically shallow captions, generic models produce diagrams that look plausible but are physically wrong, harmful in education and scientific communication. We present Princigram, a physics-faithful scientific-diagram generator, and its data pipeline. Our central advance is Structured Physical Chain-of-Thought (SP-CoT): a per-subdiscipline schema that decomposes a physics diagram into an explicit multi-step reasoning chain across six subdisciplines, from scene identification through force or process analysis to governing laws and synthesis. Unlike free-form chain-of-thought, SP-CoT follows a fixed schema with strict fidelity rules that separate visually grounded facts from physically inferred reasoning and type all mathematics symbolically; it serves both as dense training supervision and, at inference, as a structured "thinking" prompt. With it we curate and structurally annotate 4.3 million physics images, of which 115,037 carry expert-level annotation, and adapt a unified multimodal backbone. We further introduce VeriphyT2IBench, whose questions are derived from each held-out diagram's own structured annotation: each diagram becomes an item-specific bank of binary questions about its objects, forces, and states, so a judge model's score decomposes into named physical facts rather than one holistic number. On the physics subset of GenExam and on VeriphyT2IBench, Princigram shows that explicit physics-structured supervision improves the physical faithfulness of generated scientific diagrams.

</details>


### [51] [EgoMonth: A Month-Level Egocentric Video Benchmark for Long-Term Spatiotemporal Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13113)
*Weitao Chen,Hu Jiaxin,Xie Tianyidan,Yang Li,Yuyi Qian,Banghao Xu,Ziheng Tang,Shenyi Wang,Mingyue Yu,Duo Li,Jiacheng Shi,Gao Wang,Zhan Xu,Zhicheng Qiu,Xuanfu Li,Jian Yang,Lanjun Wang,Zili Yi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: EgoMonth is the first month-long egocentric video benchmark with 300+ hours from 20 participants over 20–120 days, paired with 1,443 QA pairs across 14 tasks. It reveals that contemporary MLLMs lag human memory, with the best model at 71.8% macro accuracy vs 94.2% human baseline, highlighting the need for long-term spatiotemporal memory architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the lack of long-range, cross-day continuity in existing video benchmarks and evaluate whether MLLMs can maintain consistent, long-term memory over real-world daily-life experiences.

Method: Data: EgoMonth dataset with 300+ hours of first-person daily-life videos from 20 participants across 20–120 days; Questions: 1,443 human-crafted multiple-choice Q&A pairs. Evaluation framework: 14 tasks organized into three cognitive levels—Schema Consolidation, Episodic Indexing, and Cascading Reasoning. Models: state-of-the-art open-source and closed-source MLLMs (e.g., Gemini 2.5 Pro) evaluated on macro-average accuracy.

Result: Best model achieves 71.8% macro-average accuracy; human baseline corrected at 94.2%, a 22.4-point gap. Some tasks (Route Reasoning, Cross-view Spatial Reasoning, Direction Judgement) near or at random-chance (~25%). Overall, models function more as lossy summarizers than faithful memorizers of long-term spatiotemporal information.

Conclusion: Current MLLMs struggle with long-term, cross-day spatiotemporal memory. There is a clear need for architectures that support genuine long-term memory (e.g., memory-augmented or retrieval-based mechanisms) to achieve human-level performance on real-world egocentric video tasks.

Abstract: Recent advances in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have led to substantial progress in video understanding, accompanied by a growing number of long video benchmarks. However, existing benchmarks rely predominantly on web-sourced videos that lack inter-clip spatiotemporal continuity, making it difficult to assess whether models can maintain consistent memory across days or weeks of real-world experience. We introduce EgoMonth, the first month-level egocentric video understanding benchmark. EgoMonth comprises over 300 hours of first-person daily-life recordings from 20 participants spanning 20 to 120 days, paired with 1,443 human-crafted multiple-choice question-answer pairs. We design a cognitively grounded 14-task evaluation framework organized into three hierarchical cognitive levels: Schema Consolidation, Episodic Indexing, and Cascading Reasoning. Evaluation of state-of-the-art open-source and closed-source MLLMs reveals that even the best-performing model, Gemini 2.5 Pro, achieves only 71.8% macro-average accuracy, remaining 22.4 percentage points below the corrected human baseline of 94.2%. Several models perform near or below the 25% chance level on tasks such as Route Reasoning, Cross-view Spatial Reasoning, and Direction Judgement, while even the strongest closed-source model remains substantially below human performance. These results indicate that current MLLMs function as lossy summarizers rather than faithful memorizers, highlighting the need for architectures with genuine long-term spatiotemporal memory.

</details>


### [52] [Fast Iterative Five point Relative Pose Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13114)
*Johan Hedborg,Michael Felsberg*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Iterative Dog Leg-based solver for relative pose from five points; matches precision of Nister's method but is ~2x faster; extendable to more points and useful as a refinement step; validated on ground-truth datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Speed and efficiency in robust relative pose estimation (RANSAC) without sacrificing precision; enable extension to larger point sets and use as a refinement stage.

Method: An iterative optimization using Powell's Dog Leg algorithm applied to the five-point relative pose problem; compatible with adding more than five points and maintaining fast, efficient error metrics within a robust estimation framework.

Result: Achieves similar precision to Nister's five-point solver while being approximately twice as fast; scalable to more than five points; demonstrated as effective refinement; evaluated on three dataset types with ground truth.

Conclusion: The Dog Leg-based iterative approach offers a competitive alternative to the canonical five-point solver, delivering speed gains and scalability while preserving accuracy and applicability as a refinement step in SfM pipelines.

Abstract: Robust estimation of the relative pose between two cameras is a fundamental part of Structure and Motion methods. For calibrated cameras, the five point method together with a robust estimator such as RANSAC gives the best result in most cases. The current state-of-the-art method for solving the relative pose problem from five points is due to Nister [9], because it is faster than other methods and in the RANSAC scheme one can improve precision by increasing the number of iterations. In this paper, we propose a new iterative method, which is based on Powell's Dog Leg algorithm. The new method has the same precision and is approximately twice as fast as Nister's algorithm. The proposed method is easily extended to more than five points while retaining a efficient error metrics. This makes it also very suitable as an refinement step. The proposed algorithm is systematically evaluated on three types of datasets with known ground truth.

</details>


### [53] [QuISE: Defense against Typographic Attacks on VLMs via Query-Irrelevant Semantic Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13119)
*Shubin Lu,Jiaqi Yin,Yihao Huang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: QuISE: a model-agnostic, training-free black-box defense against typographic attacks on vision-language models. It uses influence-aware text localization to identify text regions affecting the query, replaces them with two semantically distinct, query- and image-irrelevant texts, and decides the final answer by consistency across edited images. It achieves 67.9–75.0% recovery with 0.5–1.1% harm across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Typographic attacks can mislead VLMs by injecting misleading text; existing defenses require model-specific access or retraining, limiting applicability to closed-source VLMs. A training-free, model-agnostic, black-box defense is needed.

Method: Identify query-influencing text regions via influence-aware localization; generate two replacement texts that are semantically distinct and irrelevant to both the query and image; edit the image text regions accordingly; derive final answer from consistency across edited variants.

Result: Extensive experiments on three typographic-attack benchmarks, four attack settings, and four VLMs show consistent defense improvement. Recovery rate 67.9–75.0%, harm rate 0.5–1.1%.

Conclusion: QuISE offers a practical, training-free, model-agnostic defense for typographic attacks in VLMs, suitable for closed-source models, with strong empirical recovery and low harm.

Abstract: Typographic attacks pose a critical threat to vision-language models (VLMs) by injecting misleading text into images and causing models to rely on adversarial textual cues rather than visual evidence. Existing defenses often require model-specific modifications, additional training, or access to internal model components, limiting their applicability to modern closed-source VLMs. In this paper, we propose QuISE, a model-agnostic, training-free black-box defense based on query-irrelevant semantic editing. QuISE first identifies text regions likely to affect the current query through influence-aware text localization. QuISE then replaces these regions with two semantically distinct replacement texts that are irrelevant to both the query and the image. The final answer is determined by answer consistency across the edited images. Extensive experiments on three typographic-attack benchmarks, four attack settings, and four VLMs show that QuISE consistently improves defended accuracy. QuISE achieves a recovery rate of 67.9-75.0% with a harm rate of 0.5-1.1%.

</details>


### [54] [Predicting Signed Distance Functions for Visual Instance Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13135)
*Emil Brissman,Joakim Johnander,Michael Felsberg*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes learning directional distance maps per pixel to approximate the signed distance function for segmentation, offering a flexible alternative to anchor-based methods; achieves higher foreground IoU than YOLACT on COCO, but full instance mapping from distance maps remains unresolved.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Anchor-based and fixed-shape representations struggle with unconstrained object shapes; need a representation that captures diverse contours such as long slender objects.

Method: Train a neural network to predict, at each pixel, the distance to the nearest object contour along multiple directions; pool these maps to approximate an SDF; threshold to obtain foreground-background segmentation; compare with YOLACT on COCO.

Result: Foreground IoU surpasses YOLACT on COCO; distance maps retain some instance-specific information but translating to full instance masks is nontrivial.

Conclusion: Directional distance maps are a promising direction for resolving shape variability in instance segmentation; future work to integrate with full instance segmentation pipelines.

Abstract: Visual instance segmentation is a challenging problem and becomes even more difficult if objects of interest varies unconstrained in shape. Some objects are well described by a rectangle, however, this is hardly always the case. Consider for instance long, slender objects such as ropes. Anchor-based approaches classify predefined bounding boxes as either negative or positive and thus provide a limited set of shapes that can be handled. Defining anchor-boxes that fit well to all possible shapes leads to an infeasible number of prior boxes. We explore a different approach and propose to train a neural network to compute distance maps along different directions. The network is trained at each pixel to predict the distance to the closest object contour in a given direction. By pooling the distance maps we obtain an approximation to the signed distance function (SDF). The SDF may then be thresholded in order to obtain a foreground-background segmentation. We compare this segmentation to foreground segmentations obtained from the state-of-the-art instance segmentation method YOLACT. On the COCO dataset, our segmentation yields a higher performance in terms of foreground intersection over union (IoU). However, while the distance maps contain information on the individual instances, it is not straightforward to map them to the full instance segmentation. We still believe that this idea is a promising research direction for instance segmentation, as it better captures the different shapes found in the real world.

</details>


### [55] [MergeOver: Post-Training Token Merging for Recursive Vision Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13141)
*Junseo Kim,Uraz Odyurt,Amirreza Yousefzadeh*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MergeOver is a post-training method that fuses Token Merging (ToMe) with a recursively weight-shared Sliced Recursive Transformer (SReT) using an Unmerge tracking stack, constraint-safe merge-rate adjustment, and synchronized token-mass tracking across permutations. It employs a stage-wise single-shot schedule to reduce tokens early while keeping sequence length fixed, achieving notable memory and latency benefits with a small accuracy drop on ImageNet-1K, without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision Transformers (ViTs) suffer from large parameter counts and quadratic compute. While ToMe reduces tokens and SReT reduces parameters, integrating them without retraining is challenging. MergeOver aims to combine token merging with recursive weight-sharing in a post-training fashion to improve efficiency for edge deployment.

Method: Introduce an Unmerge tracking stack to recover spatial and merging constraints, constraint-safe merge-rate adjustments, and synchronized token-mass tracking across spatial permutations. Apply a stage-wise single-shot schedule that reduces tokens at the first block of each stage and maintains a fixed sequence length through subsequent recursive iterations. Evaluate on ImageNet-1K with a selected configuration.

Result: On ImageNet-1K, top-1 accuracy drops by 1.47 percentage points. On GPU, peak activation memory decreases by 37.3% (batch 1) and 38.4% (batch 16); throughput changes by -21.7% (batch 1) and +21.7% (batch 16). On Raspberry Pi 5, latency reduces by 2.4% (batch 1) and 17.6% (batch 16). Overall, MergeOver recovers a meaningful portion of the throughput and memory costs of recursive weight-sharing without retraining and provides a baseline for combining token merging with hierarchical recursive transformers.

Conclusion: MergeOver demonstrates that post-training integration of ToMe with hierarchical, recursively weight-shared transformers is feasible and beneficial. It yields nontrivial memory and latency benefits with a modest accuracy loss, offering a baseline for further exploration of token merging in conjunction with recursive transformer architectures.

Abstract: Vision Transformers (ViTs) demonstrate exceptional performance in computer vision but suffer from large parameter counts and quadratic computational complexity, severely limiting their deployment on resource-constrained edge hardware. While recursive weight-sharing reduces parameter counts and token merging mitigates computational and memory bottlenecks, integrating these two paradigms without costly retraining is non-trivial, leaving this intersection largely unexplored. We propose MergeOver, a post-training approach that integrates Token Merging (ToMe) into the recursively weight-shared Sliced Recursive Transformer (SReT). Through an Unmerge tracking stack, constraint-safe merge-rate adjustment, and synchronised token-mass tracking across spatial permutations, MergeOver resolves the spatial and merging constraints of this integration. We further employ a stage-wise single-shot schedule that performs token reduction at the first block of each stage and maintains a fixed sequence length throughout its subsequent recursive iterations. Benchmarked on ImageNet-1K, our selected configuration reduces top-1 accuracy by 1.47 percentage points. On the GPU, it reduces peak activation memory by 37.3% and 38.4% at batch sizes 1 and 16, while throughput decreases by 21.7% at batch size 1 but increases by 21.7% at batch size 16. On a Raspberry Pi 5 (ARM CPU), it reduces latency by 2.4% and 17.6% at batch sizes 1 and 16. These results show that MergeOver can recover a meaningful part of the throughput and memory cost that recursive weight-sharing introduces, without retraining, and provides a baseline for combining token merging with hierarchical recursive transformers.

</details>


### [56] [Geometry-Grounded Unified 3D Perception for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13147)
*Longfei Xu,Xiaohui Wang,Zehao Huang,Han Li,Ya Yang,Naiyan Wang,Si Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GeoUP unifies 3D perception for multi-camera driving with a geometry-grounded latent; it adapts a reconstruction-oriented latent to calibrated streaming scenes, using self/temporal/view attention and calibration-aware raymap encodings to support metric depth, 3D object detection, and semantic occupancy; trained jointly on multiple datasets, achieving SOTA across tasks and datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Preserve explicit metric 3D geometry in multi-camera driving perception. Conventional image-based pipelines rely on semantic backbones and downstream geometric modules, which can distort metric structure. A geometry-grounded latent aims to maintain accurate 3D geometry across tasks.

Method: Factorizes cross-image interaction into self, temporal, and view attention. Injects calibration-aware raymap encodings to provide metric scale and camera geometry. Decodes a geometry-grounded latent into metric depth, 3D object detection, and semantic occupancy (surface-, instance-, and volume-level readouts). Trains jointly on multiple datasets to leverage heterogeneous annotations and generalize across sensor configurations and ranges. Adapts the VGGT reconstruction-oriented latent to calibrated, streaming multi-camera driving scenes.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance across depth estimation, 3D object detection, and semantic occupancy on nuScenes, Argoverse 2, Waymo, KITTI, and DDAD, demonstrating strong generalization across sensor setups and perception ranges.

Conclusion: Geometry-grounded representations enable a unified, metric-consistent 3D perception framework for autonomous driving, with GeoUP delivering superior cross-task and cross-dataset performance and robust generalization.

Abstract: Camera-based autonomous driving perception requires a shared representation that preserves metric 3D structure across synchronized multi-camera streams. However, existing image-based frameworks often rely on backbones pretrained for semantic recognition, and introduce 3D geometry through downstream task-specific modules. As a result, their shared representations may fail to preserve explicit metric geometry and consistent 3D scene structure. In this paper, we present a Geometry-grounded Unified 3D Perception (GeoUP) framework that adapts the reconstruction-oriented latent of VGGT to calibrated, streaming multi-camera driving scenes. GeoUP factorizes cross-image interaction into self, temporal, and view attention to capture structurally distinct temporal and cross-view correspondences. It further injects calibration-aware raymap encodings to provide metric scale and camera geometry. The resulting geometry-grounded latent is decoded for metric depth estimation, 3D object detection, and semantic occupancy prediction, corresponding to surface-, instance-, and volume-level readouts of the same 3D scene. Through joint multi-task and multi-dataset training, GeoUP effectively leverages heterogeneous annotations and generalizes across diverse sensor configurations and perception ranges. Extensive experiments on nuScenes, Argoverse 2, Waymo, KITTI, and DDAD demonstrate that GeoUP achieves SOTA performance across detection, occupancy, and depth estimation. These results validate the effectiveness of geometry-grounded representations for unified 3D driving perception.

</details>


### [57] [Less Annotation, More Interpretation: Prior-Guided Concept Bottleneck Models for Interpretable Cancer Imaging Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13148)
*Baoqiang Ma,Kenneth Gilhuijs*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A prior-guided hybrid CBM improves concept detection with limited annotations by combining class-conditional concept distribution matching and prior initialization of the concept-to-diagnosis head, achieving higher concept AUC with minimal annotation while preserving diagnostic accuracy; ablations show prior initialization is crucial; zero-shot VLMs fail for fine-grained predictions; suggests structured priors reduce annotation burden in cancer imaging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome reliance on full instance-level concept annotations in concept bottleneck models (CBMs) for interpretable cancer imaging, enabling training with sparse annotation.

Method: Develop a hybrid CBM using (i) limited concept annotations, (ii) class-conditional concept distribution matching on unannotated patients, and (iii) prior initialization of the concept-to-diagnosis head. Evaluate on CBIS-DDSM (masses and calcifications) and LIDC-IDRI nodules across 0-100% annotation; perform ablations to identify key components.

Result: In the 0-20% annotation regime, the hybrid CBM consistently outperforms a matched standard CBM in mean concept AUC while maintaining diagnostic performance near black-box models. At 10% annotation: masses 0.619→0.741, calcifications 0.650→0.787, nodules 0.597→0.642. Ablation shows prior initialization as the main contributor to improved concept detection, likely by stabilizing the concept-to-diagnosis head. Zero-shot VLMs remain insufficient for reliable fine-grained tumor-level concept prediction.

Conclusion: Structured priors, particularly prior initialization, can substantially reduce the annotation burden for interpretable cancer imaging CBMs without sacrificing diagnostic accuracy.

Abstract: Concept bottleneck models (CBMs) can improve the transparency of cancer image diagnostic prediction by expressing predictions through radiological concepts. However, their dependence on instance-level concept annotations limits practical applicability. We propose a prior-guided hybrid CBM that integrates limited concept annotations, class-conditional concept distribution matching on unannotated patients, and prior initialization of the concept-to-diagnosis head. We evaluate the method on CBIS-DDSM mammographic masses and calcifications and LIDC-IDRI pulmonary nodules across 0-100% concept annotation. In the clinically relevant 0-20% annotation regime, the hybrid CBM consistently improves mean concept AUC over a matched standard CBM, while maintaining diagnostic performance close to black-box models. At 10% annotation specifically, concept AUC increases from 0.619 to 0.741 for masses, from 0.650 to 0.787 for calcifications, and from 0.597 to 0.642 for pulmonary nodules. Ablation experiments identify prior initialization as the main component contributing to improved concept detection, likely by stabilizing the concept-to-diagnosis head. Zero-shot VLMs remain insufficient for reliable fine-grained tumor-level concept prediction. These findings suggest that structured priors can substantially reduce the annotation burden of interpretable cancer imaging models.

</details>


### [58] [Splat-based Metal Artifact Reduction in Cone-Beam CT via Polychromatic Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13159)
*Kiseok Choi,Inchul Kim,Jaemin Cho,Hyeongjun Cho,Min H. Kim*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A physics-inspired, self-calibrating metal artifact reduction method for CBCT using Gaussian Splatting, jointly optimizing reconstruction and X-ray spectrum to mitigate beam hardening without metal masks; introduces a synthetic data pipeline and shows state-of-the-art improvements on synthetic and real data; claims first splat-based approach for beam hardening in CBCT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Metal artifacts from beam hardening limit CBCT quality in high-attenuation materials. Polychromatic X-ray models and system response are essential, yet existing neural methods are costly and often impractical. A practical, physics-informed, self-calibrating solution is needed.

Method: Integrates a polychromatic X-ray projection model, material-dependent attenuation, and system response into a Gaussian Splatting framework. Eliminates manual masks and strong priors, and jointly optimizes reconstruction parameters and X-ray spectral characteristics during training. Also introduces a high-fidelity synthetic CBCT data-generation pipeline validated with Monte-Carlo x-ray simulations.

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art artifact suppression and reconstruction accuracy on both synthetic and real-world CBCT datasets. Claims to be the first splat-based method for reducing beam hardening in CBCT.

Conclusion: Provides an efficient, self-calibrating CBCT reconstruction framework with improved artifact suppression and generalization, complemented by a publicly released synthetic dataset pipeline to support the community.

Abstract: Cone-beam computed tomography (CBCT) enables volumetric reconstruction from X-ray projections, but suffers from severe artifacts--especially beam hardening--when imaging materials with high attenuation such as metals. These artifacts arise from the polychromatic nature of X-rays and are not properly addressed by conventional monochromatic reconstruction algorithms. While recent neural representation-based methods offer improved reconstruction quality, they are computationally expensive and often impractical for deployment. We propose a novel physics-inspired, self-calibrating metal artifact reduction method that efficiently reconstructs 3D CBCT volumes while correcting beam hardening artifacts. Our method integrates a polychromatic X-ray projection model, material-dependent attenuation profiles, and system response modeling into a Gaussian Splatting framework. Unlike prior work, we eliminate the need for manual metal masks or strong prior assumptions, and we optimize both reconstruction parameters and X-ray spectral characteristics jointly during training. We further introduce a high-fidelity synthetic CBCT dataset generation pipeline validated on Monte-Carlo x-ray simulation toolbox and release new datasets with severe metal-induced artifacts to support the community. This is the first splat-based method for reducing beam hardening in CBCT. Extensive experiments on both synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate that our method outperforms state-of-the-art approaches in artifact suppression and reconstruction accuracy.

</details>


### [59] [TRAPSBench: Vision-Language Models Encode but Fail to Express Epistemic Restraint](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13167)
*Fnu Pramono,John Cai,Sourabh Kulkarni*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VLMs internally signal when abstention is needed but fail to express it; introduces TRAPSBench and PECS to assess abstention; results show poor spontaneous restraint and a bottleneck in expression; latent signals can be manipulated to change abstention; needs output-stage interventions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify and address the gap between internal detectability of uncertain/undetermined outcomes and the explicit abstention/output behavior of Vision-Language Models under occluded or chaotic visual evidence.

Method: TRAPSBench: procedurally generated video benchmark with 1,404 matched physics pairs where a single targeted change makes the outcome undeterminable from visual evidence. PECS (Penalized Epistemic Calibration Score): a robust metric requiring correct answers when knowable and abstention when not. Evaluation across 16 VLMs from five families; linear probes decode answerability from hidden states (AUROC up to 0.91 across physics domains); a single-layer void-direction can causally induce or suppress abstention. Replicated across open-weight families (Qwen, Gemma, LLaVA).

Result: Best PECS ~0.292 despite diverse models; bottleneck lies in expression rather than perception. Hidden representations contain answerability signals decodable by linear probes (AUROC up to 0.91). Abstention can be steered via a latent single-layer void direction. Findings replicate across Qwen, Gemma, LLaVA. Visual uncertainty is harder (abstention textually detectable ~4× more readily than missing visual evidence).

Conclusion: Closing the representation-output gap likely requires output-stage interventions; improving abstention may demand architectural, prompting, or calibration-level changes rather than purely perceptual improvements.

Abstract: When visual evidence is occluded or chaotic, models should abstain. In this paper, we show that Vision-Language Models (VLMs) can internally distinguish when abstention is required, but fail to express it anyway. We introduce TRAPSBench, a procedurally generated video benchmark of 1,404 matched physics pairs in which a single targeted change renders the outcome undeterminable from the visual evidence. Furthermore, we introduce Penalized Epistemic Calibration Score (PECS), a new robust metric that requires models to both answer correctly when the outcome is knowable, and abstain when the outcome is not. Across 16 VLMs spanning five families, spontaneous restraint is poor: the best PECS is 0.292. The bottleneck is expression, not perception: linear probes decode answerability from hidden states at up to 0.91 AUROC across physics domains; steering a single-layer void direction causally induces or suppresses abstention. Our results replicate across three open-weight families (Qwen, Gemma, LLaVA). The failure is also more pronounced in visual than textual uncertainty: models detect textual impossibility about 4x more readily than missing visual evidence. Closing this representation--output gap likely requires output-stage interventions.

</details>


### [60] [A Controlled Study of Self-Supervised Image and Video Pretraining under Limited Resources](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13183)
*Brunó B. Englert,Gijs Dubbelman*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Under resource constraints, DINOv2-style pretraining is strongest; combining image and video SSL (DINOv2 + VideoMAE) improves image tasks but harms video tasks, indicating a semantic vs geometric representations tradeoff and a need for balanced SSL designs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how self-supervised learning objectives behave under resource constraints. The study compares multiple SSL families (contrastive, reconstruction, feature-prediction, diffusion) and evaluates both standalone and jointly trained image-video SSL under matched data, architecture, and compute budgets across downstream image and video tasks.

Method: Controlled empirical study with matched data, architecture, and compute budgets. Implement and evaluate diverse SSL objectives (contrastive, reconstruction, feature-prediction, diffusion) for image-only, video-only, and joint image-video SSL. Assess on downstream tasks including image classification, semantic segmentation, video tracking, and camera pose estimation to understand performance and tradeoffs.

Result: DINOv2-style pretraining yields the strongest overall performance under limited resources. Combining DINOv2 with video SSL (VideoMAE) substantially improves image classification and segmentation but degrades video tracking and camera pose estimation, revealing a semantic vs geometric/temporal representation tradeoff. The results suggest that jointly optimizing image and video SSL can be beneficial in resource-constrained settings, but there is a need for methods that better balance semantic, temporal, and geometric supervision.

Conclusion: In resource-efficient regimes, the choice and combination of SSL objectives shape both semantic and geometric/temporal capabilities. While image-focused DINOv2 dominates overall, integrating image and video objectives can boost image tasks at the cost of video tasks, highlighting the need for balanced, multi-objective SSL methods that harmonize semantic, temporal, and geometric signals.

Abstract: Visual foundation models are a cornerstone of image and video understanding but typically require large amounts of data and computation. The current scale required for pretraining visual foundation models may be unsustainable or unnecessary, and significant benefits arise when effective models can be obtained with fewer resources. To better understand how self-supervised learning (SSL) objectives behave under resource constraints, we conduct a controlled study of image and video SSL objectives under matched data, architecture, and compute budgets. We compare contrastive, reconstruction, feature-prediction, and diffusion objectives and evaluate both standalone and jointly trained image-video SSL formulations across a diverse set of image and video understanding tasks. Our results show that DINOv2-style pretraining consistently provides the strongest overall performance under limited resources. Furthermore, combining DINOv2 with video SSL objectives such as VideoMAE substantially improves image classification and segmentation performance, but degrades video tracking and camera-pose estimation performance, revealing an important tradeoff between semantic and geometric representation learning. These findings suggest that combining image and video SSL objectives can be beneficial in resource-limited settings, while highlighting the need for improved methods that better balance semantic, temporal, and geometric supervision.

</details>


### [61] [SketchSense: Learning to Interpret Imperfect Sketch Guidance for Image Inpainting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13186)
*Zian Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SketchSense enables robust sketch-guided inpainting by co-denoising RGB and structure streams with bidirectional attention fusion, phrase-level alignment, and sketch-aware spatial regulation; yielding improved restoration quality and structural fidelity over prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imperfect user sketches mix reliable global intent with locally noisy, displaced, or unconventional strokes. Prior approaches either fix the sketch as a constant condition, propagating local errors, or clean the structure before refining appearance, delaying semantic grounding.

Method: A two-stream design that denoises RGB and structure concurrently. Key components include Bidirectional Attention Fusion to couple appearance and structure, a phrase-level objective aligning semantics across streams, Sketch-Aware Spatial Regulation that modulates attention and fusion based on local generation state, and an optional signed prior to inject preserve-vs-correct intent into features and attention.

Result: Experiments on natural and structurally complex imagery show substantial gains over existing methods in restoration quality and structural fidelity.

Conclusion: SketchSense demonstrates robust interpretation of imperfect sketch guidance, improving both appearance and structure in sketch-guided inpainting and enabling more reliable, interactive editing with unreliable sketches.

Abstract: Sketch-guided image inpainting provides intuitive structural control, yet real sketches often mix reliable global intent with locally crowded, displaced, incomplete, or deliberately unconventional strokes. Existing approaches typically either retain the input sketch as a fixed condition throughout denoising or refine it into a clean structure before RGB synthesis. The former assumes uniformly reliable strokes and can propagate local errors throughout generation; the latter must resolve ambiguous structure before emerging appearance and semantic context become available. We propose SketchSense, a framework that interprets imperfect sketch guidance by synchronously denoising interacting RGB and structure streams. Bidirectional Attention Fusion couples appearance generation with structural recovery, producing a refined structure that exposes the model's evolving sketch interpretation. A phrase-level objective aligns the semantic grounding of the two streams. Sketch-Aware Spatial Regulation further adapts sketch use to local generation states by modulating attention and the fusion process, while an optional signed prior injects preserve-versus-correct intent into feature representations and attention behavior. Experiments on natural and structurally complex imagery show substantial gains over existing methods in both restoration quality and structural fidelity.

</details>


### [62] [Fidelity-Constrained Anchoring for Black-Box Denoisers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13194)
*Masaki Satoh*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A fidelity-constrained anchoring framework for black-box denoisers blends the denoised image with the input to satisfy a local fidelity constraint, enabling control via PSNR or SSIM without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve controllable denoising fidelity for black-box denoisers while preserving naturalness, without incurring retraining or heavy computation.

Method: Linearly blend denoised output with input. Find maximum blending factor that satisfies a local fidelity constraint. PSNR control has a closed-form under a local constant-blending assumption. SSIM control uses an inverse-SSIM formulation with the same assumption and solved via iterative root finding.

Result: Anchoring provides effective fidelity control and balances denoising strength with statistical naturalness (via excess kurtosis of residuals). SSIM-based anchoring yields more consistent behavior across noise levels than PSNR-based anchoring.

Conclusion: The proposed anchoring framework enables practical fidelity control for black-box denoisers with minimal compute and no retraining, with SSIM-based anchoring offering better stability across conditions.

Abstract: We propose a fidelity-constrained framework that anchors the output of a black-box denoiser to its input without retraining and with little additional computation. The method linearly blends the denoised image with the input and selects the maximum blending factor that satisfies a prescribed local fidelity constraint using Peak Signal-to-Noise Ratio (PSNR) or Structural Similarity Index (SSIM). For PSNR control, a closed-form solution is obtained under a local constant-blending assumption. For SSIM control, we derive a tractable formulation based on inverse SSIM under the same assumption and solve it efficiently using iterative root finding. Experiments on DIV2K images with synthetic Gaussian noise and outputs from Real-ESRGAN and a non-local means denoiser show that the proposed anchoring strategy provides effective fidelity control while balancing denoising performance and statistical naturalness, as measured by the excess kurtosis of residual noise. In particular, SSIM-based anchoring yields more consistent behavior across noise levels than PSNR-based anchoring.

</details>


### [63] [HPSD: Hybrid-Policy Self-Distillation for Text-Image-to-Video Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13205)
*Jiazi Bu,Pengyang Ling,Yujie Zhou,Yibin Wang,Yuhang Zang,Xuanlang Dai,Shengyuan Ding,Tianyi Wei,Xiaohang Zhan,Jiaqi Wang,Tong Wu,Dahua Lin,Xingang Pan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Hybrid-Policy Self-Distillation (HPSD) for TI2V models that teaches a single model to perform both T2V and TI2V by using the teacher's privileged TI2V cues as anchors and velocity-level supervision for the student’s own policy, improving base generation and TI2V performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To internalize privileged TI2V capabilities into the model's base generation ability while avoiding the mismatches of off-policy supervised fine-tuning and on-policy distillation.

Method: A single TI2V model acts as both teacher and student under different conditions: (1) teacher operates in TI2V mode with a high-quality first frame and enhanced prompt; (2) student runs in base T2V mode with a vanilla prompt. The student inherits off-policy teacher trajectory points as anchors, locally refines them toward its own policy, and finally receives velocity-level supervision on self-generated roll-outs.

Result: Extensive experiments show that HPSD significantly improves T2V performance and provides notable TI2V gains, effectively strengthening the model's base generation ability.

Conclusion: HPSD successfully internalizes the teacher's privileged prior into the model's base policy while preserving precise policy corrections, yielding improvements in both T2V and TI2V tasks.

Abstract: Text-Image-to-Video (TI2V) models are an emerging unified architecture, where a single model simultaneously supports text-to-video (T2V) and image-to-video (I2V) generation. Given a high-quality first frame or a detailed textual prompt, TI2V models unlock substantially better visual quality than their T2V mode, raising a natural question: can the capability elicited by such privileged conditions be internalized into the model's own base generation ability? A common approach toward this goal is model self-distillation. However, the most straightforward solution, supervised fine-tuning, follows an off-policy strategy: its supervision is confined to teacher-generated endpoints from a fixed offline distribution rather than student-visited states, lacking precise correction tailored to the evolving policy. Recent on-policy distillation methods instead suffer from condition-state mismatch, where supervision is steered toward the given first frame instead of the student's actual content, misleading the correction. To achieve self-distillation that absorbs the teacher's privileged prior while retaining precise policy correction, in this work, we propose Hybrid-Policy Self-Distillation (HPSD), a novel self-distillation framework where a single TI2V model acts as both teacher and student under different conditions: the teacher operates in TI2V mode with a high-quality first frame and an enhanced prompt, while the student runs in the base T2V mode with only the vanilla prompt. Specifically, the student inherits off-policy teacher trajectory points as anchors, locally refines them toward its own policy, and finally receives velocity-level supervision on these self-generated roll-outs. Extensive experiments demonstrate that HPSD significantly improves T2V performance while also delivering notable TI2V gains, effectively strengthening the model's base generation ability.

</details>


### [64] [NARU: A Benchmark for NARrative Evolution and Cultural Nuance Understanding in Japanese Extreme Long Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13210)
*Yuheng Huang,Jianlang Chen,Jiayang Song,Hua Qi,Aza Kai,Vincent Markert,Edison Marrese-Taylor,Jianjun Zhao,Lei Ma*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: NARU is a Japanese long-form video benchmark for Narrative evolution and Reasoning on cultural Understanding, containing 1,481 questions across 155 videos (146.8 hours) and spanning four narrative and five cultural dimensions, built via a hierarchical memory-based annotation pipeline and validated by native speakers; eight-model evaluations reveal gaps in long-range narrative integration and culturally grounded reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-form video understanding requires tracking evolving narratives and interpreting implicit social/cultural meaning, which existing benchmarks rarely evaluate jointly, especially for high-context, non-English media. NARU targets this gap by providing a scalable, culturally grounded benchmark in Japanese.

Method: A hierarchical memory-based annotation pipeline converts raw video into structured event, narrative, and cultural annotations. Questions are generated through task-oriented synthesis with iterative shortcut removal. The dataset includes two native-speaker verification stages with 68 annotators. Stats: 1,481 questions, 155 videos, 146.8 hours, four narrative and five cultural dimensions. Evaluations cover eight model configurations.

Result: Across eight model configurations, models show substantial limitations in long-range narrative integration and culturally grounded reasoning, highlighting persistent gaps in current multimodal understanding for long-form, high-context content.

Conclusion: NARU provides a systematic testing ground for developing multilingual/multicultural LLMs capable of reliably interpreting long-form, high-context video, guiding future benchmarks and model improvements.

Abstract: Long-form video understanding encompasses tasks that go beyond retrieving isolated events, including tracking an evolving narrative and interpreting social meaning that may remain implicit. However, existing benchmarks rarely evaluate these capabilities jointly, particularly in high-context, non-English media. To address this gap, we introduce NARU, a benchmark designed to evaluate Narrative evolution and Reasoning on cultural Understanding in Japanese long-form video. NARU consists of 1,481 questions grounded in 155 videos totaling 146.8 hours, spanning four narrative and five cultural dimensions. To construct the benchmark at this scale, we propose a hierarchical memory-based annotation pipeline that transforms raw video into structured event, narrative, and cultural annotations, then generates questions via task-oriented synthesis and iterative shortcut removal. The construction process includes two native-speaker verification stages involving 68 annotators. Evaluations across eight model configurations reveal substantial limitations in both long-range narrative integration and culturally grounded reasoning. By exposing these persistent gaps, NARU offers a systematic testing ground for developing MLLMs capable of reliably interpreting long-form, high-context video.

</details>


### [65] [UniCon-Former: Unified Convolution Transformer is All You Need for Hand Gesture Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13217)
*Mallika Garg,Debashis Ghosh,Pyari Mohan Pradhan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A unified CNN-Transformer model (UniCon-Former) for dynamic hand gesture recognition that fuses local CNN projections with global self-attention in a pyramidal architecture to achieve state-of-the-art results with fewer parameters and MACs on NVGesture and Briareo.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CNNs excel at local feature extraction but have limited receptive fields; transformers capture global dependencies but are computationally heavy and somewhat redundant. A hybrid approach aims to combine strengths for robust and efficient dynamic hand gesture recognition.

Method: UniCon-Former incorporates convolution projections at the start of each transformer stage to downsample inputs, forming a pyramidal transformer architecture. This hybrid design enables learning of both local and global features, supports multi-scale and high-resolution representations, and reduces resource usage relative to vanilla transformers.

Result: Empirical evaluation on NVGesture and Briareo datasets shows state-of-the-art performance with fewer parameters and MACs compared to baseline transformers.

Conclusion: The unified CNN-Transformer framework effectively marries local and global representations for dynamic hand gesture recognition, delivering robust performance with improved efficiency and scalability across multi-scale, high-resolution features.

Abstract: Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) capture local features efficiently but struggle with global context due to their limited receptive field. On the other hand, transformers effectively capture global dependencies through self-attention but suffer from high redundancy and computational costs. Thus, to leverage the advantages of both CNNs and transformers, we propose a unified model (UniCon-Former) that aims to provide robust and efficient performance on dynamic hand gesture recognition. The unified approach helps the model to learn both local and global features. At the beginning of each transformer stage, the convolution projections help in decreasing the dimension of the input vectors of the transformer block. This creates a pyramidal structure at each transformer stage. These features enable the UniCon-Former to reduce resource usage than vanilla transformers, making it flexible for learning multi-scale and high-resolution features, which is required in hand gesture recognition. We have performed experiments with NVGesture and Briareo datasets and achieved state-of-the-art results with fewer parameters and MACs.

</details>


### [66] [Reliability analysis for BraTS-GoAT segmentation: a controlled robustness study of deep-ensemble uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13223)
*Riya Deepak Shet,Le Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Uncertainty estimation for brain-tumor segmentation under distribution shift: ensembles improve calibration and error detection; inter-model disagreement serves as a robust shift indicator; however models still miss hard lesions; no single method dominates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess how well segmentation models calibrate and detect errors under domain shift to inform safe clinical deployment.

Method: 5-fold cross-validated nnU-Net baseline and 3-seed deep ensemble; per-region mask evaluation; calibration and error detection metrics; synthetic corruptions to simulate shift; analysis of per-case and per-voxel behavior; leaderboard evaluation.

Result: Under IID, ensemble modestly improves calibration. Under corruption shift, single model confidence stays flat while accuracy and calibration degrade; inter-member disagreement rises significantly. Whole-tumor Dice on official validation: 0.87. Generalization gap concentrates on harder regions; small satellite lesions commonly missed on unseen cohorts. 3-seed disagreement is a sensitive shift indicator; per-voxel localization degrades with severity.

Conclusion: Provides a rigorous, honest reliability comparison; demonstrates that ensemble disagreement offers valuable shift detection but no single method dominates; emphasizes the need for reliability-focused evaluation in medical SOTA.

Abstract: Deep networks segment brain tumours accurately in-distribution, but can fail silently when the input differs from their training data. That risk is central to clinical deployment and is the premise of the BraTS-GoAT generalizability task. We ask not only how well a model segments, but whether its uncertainty knows when it is wrong. On BraTS-GoAT (Task 3) we train a 5-fold cross-validated nnU-Net baseline (one held-out prediction per case) and a 3-seed deep ensemble. Both are evaluated for calibration and error detection on a per-region relevant mask, aggregated per case. In-distribution the 3-seed ensemble improves modestly over the already strong single model on the same held-out split, with the clearest gain in calibration. The separation appears under shift. In a controlled robustness study using graded synthetic corruptions as a proxy for acquisition shift, the single model's confidence stays flat while its accuracy and calibration degrade. Inter-member disagreement instead rises steeply, about a quarter to a third above the clean condition, several times the single model's response. On the official validation leaderboard the 5-fold ensemble of those folds attains whole-tumour Dice 0.87. The generalization gap is concentrated on the harder regions, with a characteristic failure of missing small, satellite lesions on unseen cohorts. In the synthetic study, disagreement among the 3-seed members is a more sensitive case-level indicator of acquisition shift than single-model confidence. Its per-voxel error localisation weakens as severity grows. The contribution is a rigorous, honest reliability comparison rather than a claim that any one uncertainty method dominates.

</details>


### [67] [CoverPrune: Coverage-Driven Token Pruning for 3D VLMs via Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13226)
*Peng Ling,Yingda Yin,Lingting Zhu,Weikai Chen,Shengju Qian,Zeyu Hu,Xin Wang,Wenming Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes CoverPrune: an OT-based, training-free token pruning method for 3D VLMs that favors visual evidence coverage over diversity to preserve spatial reasoning under aggressive pruning; includes CoverPrune and fast CoverPrune-Lite variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 3D VLMs suffer from massive visual token counts causing inference bottlenecks; diversity-based pruning can discard prototype tokens, harming multi-view consistency and geometric structure essential for spatial reasoning.

Method: Formulates token pruning as an Optimal Transport problem; introduces Feature-Spatial-Temporal (FST) transport cost and a target capacity; develops Spatial-Guided Greedy Selection (SGS) to approximate OT; proposes CoverPrune-Lite with spatially structured local matching for lower overhead; training-free.

Result: Extensive experiments on several 3D visual-spatial reasoning benchmarks show state-of-the-art token efficiency while preserving robust reasoning performance under aggressive pruning; project website provided.

Conclusion: Shifts the paradigm from diversity-based pruning to coverage-based pruning for 3D VLMs, enabling efficient inference without retraining and maintaining spatial reasoning capabilities.

Abstract: While 3D Vision-Language Models (3D VLMs) have demonstrated remarkable spatial reasoning capabilities, they suffer from massive visual token counts that create severe computational bottlenecks during inference. Existing token pruning methods primarily rely on diversity-based selection, discarding similar tokens to maximize dispersion. However, in 3D environments, this approach frequently drops representative prototype tokens in favor of outliers, breaking the multi-view consistencies and geometric structures essential for spatial reasoning. In this paper, we propose a paradigm shift for 3D VLM token pruning: from maximizing diversity to preserving visual evidence coverage. We introduce CoverPrune, a training-free framework that formulates inference-time token pruning as an Optimal Transport (OT) problem. To overcome the intractable combinatorial subset selection inherent in this formulation, we design the Feature-Spatial-Temporal (FST) transport cost and target capacity, along with an efficient Spatial-Guided Greedy Selection (SGS) algorithm to approximate the OT objective. Furthermore, we propose CoverPrune-Lite, an accelerated variant utilizing spatially structured local matching for minimal overhead. Extensive experiments across multiple 3D visual-spatial reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that our methods achieve state-of-the-art token efficiency, maintaining robust reasoning performance even under highly aggressive pruning budgets. Visit our project website at https://github.com/Brucess/CoverPrune.

</details>


### [68] [Reasoning for Social Audio-Visual Question Answering: Where Do We Stand?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13239)
*Koen P. de Vries,Xavier Alameda-Pineda,Estefanía Talavera,Stéphane Lathuilière*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: IntentBench is noisy; a simple Vanilla SFT baseline matches or outperforms complex reasoning on multimodal social understanding, and textual priors (via captions) can rival video inputs. IntentBench-Prime and Vanilla SFT provide stronger baselines; results suggest limitations in current MLLMs' social understanding and the value of robust, low-cost baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate the efficacy of chain-of-thought reasoning and existing benchmarks for audio-visual social understanding, and to establish stronger baselines and cleaner datasets for multimodal LLMs.

Method: Analyze the IntentBench benchmark, propose IntentBench-Prime to remove noisy/trivial questions, compare reasoning methods with Vanilla SFT across three benchmarks, and assess the impact of text-only priors and video vs caption inputs.

Result: IntentBench is noisy (~7% broken, ~23% trivially answerable without video). Vanilla SFT matches or outperforms existing reasoning methods at lower cost. Substantial priors can be learned from text; using a textual caption can yield performance on par with Vanilla SFT. IntentBench-Prime, Vanilla SFT model, and code are publicly available.

Conclusion: Current multimodal LLMs have notable limitations in social understanding; robust, cost-effective baselines (Vanilla SFT) and dataset refinements (IntentBench-Prime) are crucial. Textual priors can dominate when video input adds limited value; benchmark quality and evaluation of reasoning are essential for progress.

Abstract: Training Multimodal Large Language Models for audio-visual social understanding is a crucial step toward embodied social intelligence. Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning has become the dominant approach, with HumanOmniV2 and its IntentBench benchmark as a prominent reference point. In this context, we report three findings. First, IntentBench is highly noisy: $\sim$7% of questions are broken and $\sim$23% are trivially answerable without the video input. We remove the affected questions and release Intentbench-Prime. Second, current reasoning approaches are expensive and surprisingly ineffective. A simple Vanilla SFT baseline matches or outperforms existing reasoning methods across three benchmarks at a fraction of the cost, establishing it as an essential baseline for evaluating novel fine-tuning techniques. Third, our analysis reveals that substantial priors can be learned solely from the text modality and that using a textual caption instead of the video yields performance on par with Vanilla SFT. These surprising findings reveal the limitations of current MLLMs when it comes to social understanding. IntentBench-Prime, Vanilla SFT model, and code are publicly available.

</details>


### [69] [GeoCache: Training-Free Acceleration of Multi-View Texture Diffusion via Geometric Delta Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13255)
*Haotang Li,Zhenyu Qi,Shaohan Henry Wang,Kebin Peng,Yutong Zhao,Zi Wang,Bo Liu,Huanrui Yang,Sen He*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GC-View: A training-free cross-view geometry plugin that reduces denoising cost in geometry-conditioned multi-view diffusion by transferring per-step updates from a rotating subset of anchor views to other views; achieves 2x+ speedups with competitive fidelity across Hunyuan3D-2.1, SyncMVD, MVPainter.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce the high computational cost of per-view denoising in geometry-conditioned multi-view diffusion for 3D texture, addressing the degradation from skipping steps due to loss of cross-view interaction in existing training-free accelerators.

Method: Introduce GC, a training-free plugin that samples a rotating subset of anchor views and transports their geometry-aligned per-step updates to the remaining views via xz updates. Periodic full-view computation bounds accumulated error, while sampler-consistent reconstruction preserves the denoising trajectory. Uses position maps available in geometry-conditioned texturing pipelines; requires no retraining or architectural changes.

Result: Across Hunyuan3D-2.1, SyncMVD, and MVPainter, GC achieves stronger speed–fidelity trade-offs than temporal caches and step reduction at operating points above 2x. On Hunyuan3D-2.1, it delivers a 2.21× denoiser-loop speedup with MV-LPIPS of 0.0293 and MV-PSNR of 33.60 dB, the best fidelity among accelerated methods above 2×. The transferred configuration yields the highest speedup and lowest FLOPs on SyncMVD, and the lowest FLOPs with best fidelity among accelerated methods on MVPainter.

Conclusion: Cross-view geometry constitutes an effective acceleration axis for multi-view texture diffusion. GC provides a practical, training-free, architecture-agnostic approach to improve speed without retraining, preserving fidelity through geometry-aligned cross-view updates.

Abstract: Geometry-conditioned multi-view diffusion enables high-quality 3D texture generation, but its repeated per-view denoiser evaluations introduce substantial computational cost. Existing training-free accelerators primarily exploit temporal redundancy by reusing computation across denoising steps. In multi-view texturing, however, skipping a step also removes the cross-view interaction that continually aligns different observations of the same surface, leading to rapidly degraded consistency and fidelity. Our analysis identifies a complementary source of redundancy: although intermediate features remain view-specific, geometrically corresponding surface points exhibit transferable evolution in their predicted clean signals. Based on this observation, we introduce \gc{}, a training-free plugin that evaluates a rotating subset of anchor views and transports their geometry-aligned per-step $\xz$ updates to the remaining views. Periodic full-view computation controls accumulated error, while sampler-consistent reconstruction preserves the denoising trajectory. \gc{} requires neither retraining nor architectural modification and uses the position maps already available in geometry-conditioned texturing pipelines. Across Hunyuan3D-2.1, SyncMVD, and MVPainter, \gc{} achieves a stronger speed--fidelity trade-off than temporal caches and step reduction at operating points above $2\times$. On Hunyuan3D-2.1, it delivers a $2.21\times$ denoiser-loop speedup with an MV-LPIPS of 0.0293 and an MV-PSNR of 33.60 dB, providing the best fidelity among all tested methods above $2\times$. The same transferred configuration reaches the highest speedup and lowest FLOPs on SyncMVD, while \gc{} achieves the lowest FLOPs and best fidelity among the accelerated methods on MVPainter. These results establish cross-view geometry as an effective acceleration axis for multi-view texture diffusion.

</details>


### [70] [How Good are Foundation Models in Longitudinal MRI Disease Progression Reasoning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13309)
*Wafa Al Ghallabi,Ritesh Thawkar,Sara Ghaboura,Omkar Thawakar,Numan Saeed,Dana Al Nuaimi,Ajnas Alkatheeri,Salman Khan,Fahad Shahbaz Khan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A Time-Aware Multi-View MRI Benchmark for longitudinal MRI interpretation with multi-view inputs and explicit temporal reasoning; evaluates progression tracking, interval change localization, and temporal ordering using 3,920 QA pairs across 890 patients and seven cohorts. Shows moderate temporal alignment but poor change-direction and volumetric quantification, with multi-view aiding localization yet harming temporal reasoning in compact models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Radiology requires integrated temporal-spatial reasoning across multiple views and timepoints, which current vision-language benchmarks do not capture. There is a need for standardized evaluation of progression tracking and interval change localization in MRI.

Method: Assemble a benchmark with longitudinal, multi-view MRI data across seven clinical cohorts, generating 3,920 expert-verified QA pairs in open-ended, multiple-choice, and binary formats. Include structured localization guidance (boundaries, imaging features, confounders). Evaluate 16 vision-language models on tasks of detecting maximal change regions, characterizing progression across sequences/views, and providing structured localization.

Result: Models show moderate temporal alignment but systematic failures in determining change direction and performing volumetric quantification. Multi-view inputs improve spatial localization but degrade temporal reasoning in compact architectures.

Conclusion: The benchmark provides a systematic framework to evaluate progression tracking, interval change localization, and temporal ordering in MRI, supporting clinical deployment. Code, split definitions, and data are publicly available.

Abstract: Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) interpretation is fundamental to clinical decision-making, requiring radiologists to integrate multi-view anatomical planes across sequential timepoints while precisely localizing interval changes. However, existing vision-language benchmarks remain confined to single-timepoint, single-view interpretation, failing to capture the temporal-spatial reasoning essential to radiologic practice. We introduce the Time-Aware Multi-View MRI Benchmark, an evaluation framework unifying multi-view anatomical input, temporal reasoning across longitudinal scans, and structured localization guidance. The benchmark comprises 3,920 expert-verified question-answer pairs derived from 890 patients across over 3,200 longitudinal MRI timepoints, drawn from seven clinical cohorts covering glioblastoma, neurodegeneration, vestibular schwannoma, and brain metastases, in open-ended, multiple-choice, and binary formats, requiring models to identify anatomical regions of maximal change, characterize progression across sequences and views, and provide structured guidance specifying boundaries, imaging features, and confounders. Experiments across 16 vision-language models reveal moderate temporal alignment but systematic failure on change direction recognition and volumetric quantification, while multi-view inputs improve spatial localization yet degrade temporal reasoning in compact architectures. Our benchmark provides a systematic framework for evaluating progression tracking, interval change localization, and temporal ordering, which are essential for clinical deployment. Code, evaluation splits, and the dataset are available at: https://github.com/wafaAlghallabi/Time-Aware-MRI.

</details>


### [71] [AmalthAI: An Open-Source Computer Vision Platform for Cultural Heritage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13343)
*Christos Chatzisavvas,Stelios Alvanos,Efstratios Politis,Panagiotis Rigas,Thomas Pappas,Ioannis Giannoukos,Nikolaos Mitianoudis,Agata Ulanowska,Katarzyna Żebrowska,Nazarij Buławka,Christina Margariti,George Pavlidis,Chairi Kiourt,Anestis Koutsoudis,Vassilis Katsouros,George Ioannakis*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: AmalthAI is an open-source, self-hosted computer vision platform for archaeology that enables non-expert CH researchers to manage datasets, train/infer ML models (classification, segmentation, object detection) with scalable training via Kubeflow/Katib, interpret predictions with Grad-CAM and a vision-language model, while keeping data within institutional custody; demonstrated on clay textile imprints with CH experts training/validating segmentation and classification models; code available at GitHub.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the accessibility gap between CV/ML tooling and cultural heritage domain experts who lack deep ML expertise, while preserving data sovereignty and enabling interpretable results for archaeologically meaningful findings.

Method: End-to-end platform integrating dataset management, training, and inference for CV tasks; uses Kubeflow and Katib for scalable training and hyperparameter search; Grad-CAM for localization of predictions; a vision-language model to generate textual descriptions for expert review; self-hosted deployment to keep sensitive data on premises; evaluated on a custom archaeological dataset of clay textile imprints with CH experts performing model training and validation.

Result: Feasibility demonstrated: CH experts could train and validate segmentation and classification models on the specialized dataset for hypothesis testing; platform is open-source and demonstrated in a relevant CH use case.

Conclusion: AmalthAI addresses accessibility, reproducibility, and governance challenges in applying CV/ML to cultural heritage data by providing an end-to-end, self-hosted, interpretable platform, enabling archaeologists to derive and validate findings without requiring ML expertise.

Abstract: Computer vision (CV) and machine learning (ML) offer new tools for cultural heritage (CH) artifact analysis, but the CV/ML pipeline remains largely inaccessible to CH domain experts, who lack the background to configure, train, or assess models. We present AmalthAI, an open-source CV platform that bridges this gap, enabling non-ML CH experts to independently produce and validate archaeologically meaningful findings. The interface covers dataset management, training, and inference for classification, segmentation, and object detection, with Kubeflow and Katib handling scalable training and hyperparameter search. Grad-CAM localizes the image region behind a prediction, and a vision-language model (VLM) adds a text description of it for expert review. Since archaeological data is often state-owned or rights-encumbered and cannot leave institutional custody, AmalthAI's self-hostable deployment ensures sensitive data is kept within premises. We test the platform on an archaeological use case built on a custom dataset of clay textile imprints, where CH experts trained and validated segmentation, and classification models for hypothesis testing. We provide the implementation code at https://github.com/TEXTaiLES/AmalthAI.

</details>


### [72] [Sign Language Video Synthesis via Loss-Guided Multi-Expert GANs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13368)
*Dingzhan Nong,Zhihao Ren,Ziqi Li,Tim Lo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A loss-guided, multi-expert GAN for sign-language video synthesis with three region-focused discriminators (global, hand, head) guiding corresponding generator branches; stabilizes training via United Loss consensus; uses a dual-pathway conv-transformer with AdaptiveFeatureFusion; three-mode training on a large dataset; achieves ~30 dB PSNR with 0.2B-1.3B params and deployable VRAM footprints; ablations ongoing; showcased publicly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enhance sign-language communication by producing high-fidelity sign-language videos with region-specific detail while ensuring stable training and deployment on consumer hardware.

Method: A GAN with three specialized discriminators guiding three expert branches in the generator (global, hand, head). Introduces United Loss consensus to regularize discriminators toward the ensemble average (weight 0.1). Each branch uses a dual-pathway convolutional-transformer with learnable AdaptiveFeatureFusion. Training follows an alternating three-mode schedule: discriminate, holistic generation, branch-specialized generation. Training on a custom 156GB dataset; ablations pending due to long training cycles.

Result: 0.2B-parameter variant attains 29.8 PSNR; 1.3B-parameter variant attains 30.7 PSNR; inference VRAM 1.5 GB and 8 GB respectively; deployment on consumer hardware feasible; full ablations still ongoing (2–3 months on a single GPU).

Conclusion: Demonstrates that region-specific supervision and ensemble-consensus stabilizers enable plausible sign-language video synthesis with limited inference memory, though comprehensive ablations and perceptual evaluations remain to be completed.

Abstract: This preliminary technical report presents a framework for sign language video synthesis using a loss-guided multi-expert Generative Adversarial Network (GAN) to enhance communication for individuals with hearing impairments. Three specialized discriminators -- global, hand, and head -- each guide a corresponding expert branch in the generator toward a distinct visual region, enabling implicit feature specialization without explicit diversity losses. To stabilize this multi-discriminator system, whose early-phase training otherwise exhibits chaotic dynamics, we introduce a United Loss consensus mechanism that regularizes each discriminator toward the ensemble average at a 10% weight. Each branch further adopts a dual-pathway convolutional-transformer design with learnable AdaptiveFeatureFusion, balancing the stability of convolutions against the detail of windowed self-attention. The generator is trained using an alternating three-mode schedule (discriminator, holistic generation, branch-specialized generation). On a custom 156GB dataset with a filtered test set that removes easy and repetitive samples, our 0.2B-parameter variant achieves 29.8 PSNR and the 1.3B-parameter variant achieves 30.7 PSNR, with inference VRAM footprints of 1.5 GB and 8 GB respectively, enabling deployment on consumer-grade hardware. Full ablation studies remain ongoing due to the 2-3 month training cycle on a single GPU. The system was showcased at the 2025 Hong Kong Frontier Technology Summit.

</details>


### [73] [Reconstructing Historical Manuscripts through MSI: The Potential of Contrast in Assessing Image Quality and Legibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13381)
*Anna Breger*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Contrast-based IQA metrics correlate with expert readability in reconstructed manuscript images; potential contrast aligns best with expert ratings; contrast-to-noise ratio aligns with full-reference measures; overall, contrast-based metrics outperform general IQA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess restoration quality of historical manuscript images where readability is subjective and expert annotations are scarce; develop objective, reliable metrics that reflect legibility.

Method: Two experiments on publicly available multi-spectral manuscript datasets; obtain expert quality scores for reconstructed images; compute full-reference image quality measures; evaluate correlations between objective metrics and expert scores and between metrics and full-reference scores.

Result: Potential contrast yields the highest correlation with expert ratings; contrast-to-noise ratio shows the strongest agreement with full-reference quality measures; contrast-based metrics outperform general image quality measures.

Conclusion: Contrast-based measures are effective objective indicators of manuscript legibility and reconstruction quality and have potential to guide restoration pipelines when expert labels are limited.

Abstract: Digital restoration of historical manuscript images aims to improve readability while preserving the authenticity of cultural heritage documents. However, evaluating quality of restored manuscripts remains challenging, where readability is often subjective and expert annotations are scarce. This study investigates the suitability of contrast-based image quality measures to assess quality and legibility of reconstructed manuscript images from multi-spectral imaging. Two experiments were conducted with publicly-available data sets, facilitating manual quality scores by experts and full-reference image quality measures as reference evaluations. The results show that potential contrast achieves the highest correlation with expert ratings, while contrast-to-noise ratio demonstrates the strongest agreement with full-reference quality measures. Overall, contrast-based measures consistently outperform general image quality measures, demonstrating their potential as objective indicators of manuscript legibility and reconstruction quality.

</details>


### [74] [When Is a Task Vector Enough? An Empirical Theory of Implicit Multimodal ICL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13385)
*Jiaqian Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes the Selection–Realization Hypothesis for implicit multimodal in-context learning (M-ICL): demonstrations induce a compact family of internal changes from which the query selects, with the model constraining how these changes are implemented. Empirically validated on controlled multimodal tasks and VQA benchmarks, revealing when a static task vector suffices versus when more expressive interventions are needed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reconcile diverse implicit interventions (static task vectors, query-conditioned transforms, attention routing) and determine the minimal additional complexity required for a given task without changing primitives or prompts.

Method: Propose the Selection–Realization Hypothesis; evaluate with controlled multimodal tasks where query dependence varies but task primitives and prompts stay constant; compare correct demonstrations to matched counterfactuals to reveal explicit M-ICL structure; predict intervention behavior; extend analyses to natural VQA benchmarks and consider cost-aware method selection.

Result: Static task vectors perform well when demonstration-induced changes are largely shared across queries; when explicit M-ICL contains query-specific or distributed structure, additional intervention complexity (beyond a local additive shift) is useful; findings generalize to natural VQA benchmarks.

Conclusion: Offers a unified empirical theory for when demonstrations can be compressed into a task vector and when a more expressive intervention is warranted, enabling cost-aware method selection without access to test performance and extending to natural VQA benchmarks.

Abstract: Implicit multimodal in-context learning compresses demonstrations into internal interventions, ranging from static task vectors to query-conditioned transformations and attention routing. Despite their common goal, these methods differ substantially in how the intervention depends on the query and where it modifies the model, leaving unclear which additional complexity is necessary for a given task. We propose the Selection--Realization Hypothesis. It views demonstrations as inducing a compact family of internal changes from which the query selects, while the model's computation constrains how the selected change can be implemented. We evaluate this account using controlled multimodal tasks in which query dependence varies without changing the underlying task primitives or prompt format. By contrasting correct demonstrations with matched counterfactuals, we measure the structure of explicit M-ICL and test whether it predicts intervention behavior. We find that the success of a static task vector is closely tied to how much of the demonstration-induced change is shared across queries.
