LBH was evaluated with random forest, support vector machine, and multilayer perceptron classifiers under EEG-only and EEG+EMG settings, and compared with the dataset hypnogram (DH) and best-scorer hypnogram (BSH). LBH consistently improved overall performance. The best results were obtained with random forest and EEG+EMG, reaching 86.07% accuracy, 85.46% precision, and 85.29% F1-score on DOD-H, and 86.04% accuracy, 85.21% precision, and 84.70% F1-score on DOD-O. These findings suggest that personalized scorer modeling can improve reference hypnogram construction without discarding information from individual experts.

</details>


### [99] [Geometric and Behavioral Stratification in Transformer Residual Streams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12447)
*Nelson Guda*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The prediction direction in transformer residual streams is a content-defined privileged anchor that shapes the geometry and readout of predictions, a property that holds across 18 models with a narrow near-prediction interface and a broader, scale-dependent distal region.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To identify and characterize privileged coordinate axes (anchors) in transformer representations, focusing on the unembedding/prediction direction and its role in enabling linear readout from high-dimensional computation.

Method: Empirical analysis across eighteen models (dense and mixture-of-experts, 7B–120B, base and instruction-tuned). Measure residual-stream variance relative to the prediction (unembedding) direction, assess geometric stratification by distance to the prediction, examine readout relevance, and perturb variance directions to observe effects on task behavior; compare with principal axes and analyze how scaling affects distal structure.

Result: The prediction direction acts as a content-defined anchor; residual-stream variation is geometrically and behaviorally stratified by proximity to the prediction. Prediction-proximal regions are highly structured and cluster related prompts; the distal complement expands with model scale. A narrow, scale-invariant prediction interface concentrates readout-relevant structure; perturbing directions near the prediction causes immediate divergence and framing shifts, while perturbing farther directions delays divergence. The distal region is causally and temporally load-bearing and drives behavior despite weaker per-direction readout alignment.

Conclusion: The prediction direction is a distinct, privileged anchor from previously described coordinate axes, providing a geometric account of how high-dimensional computation coexists with linear readout and for why models exhibit structured, scale-dependent stratification around the prediction direction.

Abstract: Trained transformer models develop privileged bases: coordinate axes whose statistics differ from the rest of the residual stream. But what kind of direction does such a basis select? We investigate the prediction direction, the unembedding direction of the token a model currently predicts, and find that it functions as a content-defined privileged anchor. Measured with respect to this anchor, residual-stream variation is geometrically and behaviorally stratified by proximity to the prediction.
  The stratification holds in all eighteen models tested (dense and mixture-of-experts, 7B-120B, base and instruction-tuned). A narrow, scale-invariant prediction interface concentrates readout-relevant structure, while the vast prediction-distal complement expands with model scale. Because the prediction direction sits nearly orthogonal to the principal variance axes, variance-based analyses recover this organization only partly, and the shortfall grows with prompt heterogeneity.
  Anchoring reveals a steep geometric gradient: prediction-proximal regions are highly structured and cluster related prompts, while the complement is flatter and anti-discriminates among prompt groups. The interface is a narrow slice but functionally decisive. Disrupting the variance directions closest to the prediction causes immediate divergence and frequent task-frame shifts; disrupting the next level down delays divergence and preserves framing. The complement is weakly readout-aligned per direction yet causally and temporally load-bearing, and behavior is driven by direction rather than magnitude.
  These results establish the prediction direction as a privileged anchor distinct from previously described coordinate axes, and give a geometric account of how high-dimensional computation coexists with linear readout.

</details>


### [100] [Exemplar-based objective classification of gust-induced loads across multiple flight conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12448)
*Paolo Olivucci,Kowshik Srivatsan,David E. Rival*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A data-driven, exemplar-based classification of gust-induced load observations that yields nine recurring response types across flight attitudes, enabling interpretable summaries and guiding further experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To obtain an objective, interpretable criterion for organizing gust-induced loads across many flight conditions, beyond coarse labels like flight attitude.

Method: Learn a machine-learned representation of a large dataset of gust-induced pressure-load measurements (from 3480 observations) and apply a summarization step to extract a minimal set of highly significant exemplars that define a similarity-based classification of observations.

Result: Identification of nine fundamental response types that recur across multiple attitudes. Analyzing the transient of each type provides physical intuition into the underlying fluid mechanics; exemplars are more convenient for expert inspection and experimental planning.

Conclusion: The approach delivers an interpretable, objective classification framework for gust-induced loads and demonstrates it on a sizable real dataset, potentially guiding targeted experiments and deeper fluid-mechanics insights.

Abstract: Is it possible to find an objective classification criterion that organizes the complexity of gust-induced loads across many flight conditions? And one that remains as interpretable as a labelling based on coarse parameters, such as the flight attitude? Our approach encodes a large number of experimental observations through a machine-learned representation and applies a summarization procedure to select a minimal subset of highly significant exemplars. The exemplars provide a similarity-based objective classification criterion of all the observations, they can be more conveniently inspected by experts and can become subject of more refined experiments. We demonstrate the approach on a database of 3480 pressure-load measurements induced by random gusts on a flying-wing model across six flight attitudes. We find nine fundamental response types that recur across multiple attitudes; analysis of a type's transient response enables physical intuition into the underlying fluid mechanics.

</details>


### [101] [Learning Under Treatment-Induced Label Indeterminacy with Expert Annotations of Counterfactual Outcomes: A Case Study in Neurological Prognostication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12477)
*Xiaobin Shen,Chloe Y. H. Huang,Jonathan Elmer,George H. Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Treat outcome observability being affected by treatment decisions; split evaluation into certain vs uncertain outcomes; propose a neural model leveraging labels from both groups; reveal tradeoffs hidden by standard metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address bias and mis-evaluation in prognostic models when outcomes can be censored by treatment decisions; case study on post-cardiac-arrest neurological prognostication with many indeterminate outcomes.

Method: Introduce a framework that separately evaluates certain and uncertain cases; obtain uncertain-case labels via independent experts' counterfactuals; train neural and tabular models using both label types with a tunable trade-off; compare models on AUROC for certain cases, Brier score, and probability calibration for uncertain cases; analyze how alignment with uncertain labels affects certain-case performance.

Result: Models with similar certain-case AUROC show substantial differences in certain-case Brier scores and uncertain-case probability estimates; improving alignment with uncertain labels often reduces certain-case accuracy; conventional metrics can conceal critical failure modes in the presence of treatment-driven observability constraints.

Conclusion: Evaluation must account for how treatment decisions affect outcome observability; standard metrics can miss important failure modes; explicit tradeoffs between certain and uncertain cases should guide model development and assessment to improve prognostic support.

Abstract: Clinical prediction models are often developed as if the outcome of interest were cleanly observed for every patient. This assumption fails when treatment decisions make the clinically relevant outcome permanently unobservable. As a case study of this problem, we consider post-cardiac-arrest neurological prognostication using a cohort of 2,497 patients, including 1,429 patients whose outcomes were rendered indeterminate by treatment decisions. These patients with indeterminate outcomes were reviewed by independent clinical experts, who provided their guesses of counterfactual outcomes about what would have happened to the patients. We refer to these patients as uncertain cases. We also have patients for whom we observe their clinically relevant outcomes; we refer to these patients as certain cases. We propose a framework for evaluating prediction models that explicitly splits the evaluation between certain and uncertain cases. Here, we cannot easily evaluate both types of cases in a uniform manner as the available target labels differ. We then propose a simple prediction model that uses target labels from both certain and uncertain cases in a manner that allows us to trade off between them. Across the proposed neural model and a collection of tabular baselines, models with similar certain-case AUROC can nevertheless differ substantially in both certain-case Brier score and their probability estimates for uncertain cases. Improving alignment with target labels of uncertain cases for our proposed model generally comes at the cost of worse accuracy on certain cases, highlighting an explicit tradeoff that standard evaluation conceals. These results show that when treatment decisions determine whether clinically meaningful outcomes remain observable, conventional evaluation metrics can miss important failure modes in the very patients for whom prognostic support matters most.

</details>


### [102] [When Can You Trust Offline Evaluation of Equal-Cost Top-k Allocation? A Controlled, Reproducible Benchmark and Practitioner's Guide](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12489)
*Binshuang Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Off-policy evaluation for deterministic top-k targeting rules: six estimators evaluated across multiple datasets reveal overlap is driven by logger-target action alignment, cross-fitting does not fix optimizer's curse, and propensity estimation error dominates degradation; a synthetic/semi-synthetic benchmark is released for public use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Provide reliable pre-deployment evaluation of targeting rules under budget constraints, focusing on deterministic policies where standard averaging over actions is invalid and overlap is critical.

Method: Benchmark six estimators on five datasets with two known-effect sweeps, validate against a non-simulated paired reference, synthesize logs with bounded propensities (floored at 0.02), compare cross-fitting and honest policy-level splitting, analyze overlap, reuse bias, and propensity errors across environments.

Result: Weak overlap is governed by logger-target action alignment; action-level disagreement collapses overlap. Effective sample size is not reliable for ranking within a single log. Cross-fitting nuisance does not fix reuse bias; honest policy-level splitting alters the estimand. Propensity estimation error is the largest degradation; out-of-fold IPS drops more than others and can invert overlap diagnostics. Four distinct estimators remain when floors and hybrids are applied. The benchmark is released with synthetic/semi-synthetic data; public dataset only.

Conclusion: Deterministic top-k OPE faces pronounced overlap and propensity- estimation challenges; overlap diagnostics depend on logger-target alignment; evaluation strategy must address reuse bias via policy-level splitting; propensity errors dominate performance, necessitating robust propensity modeling and careful benchmark design; the authors provide a publicly available benchmark.

Abstract: Organizations decide whom to treat under a budget and want to know what a targeting rule would have earned before deploying it. Off-policy evaluation promises this from logged data, but the deployable rule is a deterministic top-k policy: it removes all averaging over actions, so weak overlap hits the estimate directly. We benchmark six estimators across five datasets and two known-effect sweeps, and validate the mechanisms against a non-simulated paired reference. First, weak overlap is governed by logger-target action alignment, not by logging sharpness alone: what governs support is the logger's probability of the target's actions. Sharpening a logger built from the target's own score barely moves overlap over the tested range; action-level disagreement collapses it. Effective sample size ranks this risk across logging environments, but is weak at ranking candidates within the single log a practitioner holds, and its cut point does not transfer. Second, the optimizer's curse is not fixed by cross-fitting the outcome nuisance. When the rule is fit on the data used to evaluate it, cross-fitting the nuisance alone leaves the reuse bias in place and makes it worse. Honest policy-level splitting avoids the reuse by targeting the learning procedure's value -- a change of estimand, not a de-biasing of the full-sample policy. Third, propensity-estimation error is the largest degradation we measure: an out-of-fold estimate hurts IPS more than any other stress we apply, leaves doubly-robust estimation almost unchanged, and can invert the overlap diagnostic itself. Logging is synthesized and propensities floored at 0.02, so every failure occurs with bounded weights; the floor also reduces the two tuned hybrids to their untuned parents, leaving four practically distinct estimators, and all exact-value surfaces are synthetic or semi-synthetic. We release the benchmark; public data only.

</details>


### [103] [Exploring Oversmoothing with Householder Matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12514)
*Bhaskar Karol*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HouseGNN uses Householder reflectors to update node embeddings via a reflection direction estimated from neighborhood messages, followed by GroupSort, creating piecewise orthogonal layers that preserve Euclidean norms at every node and depth, aiming to mitigate oversmoothing in deep GNNs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Oversmoothing in deep GNNs arises from repeated normalized graph propagation, leading to collapse of node representations; need for depth-stable architectures that preserve information.

Method: Aggregate neighborhood messages to estimate a reflection direction; apply a Householder reflector to update the node embedding, followed by GroupSort to form a piecewise orthogonal layer; ensures per-node Euclidean-norm preservation at each depth.

Result: Theoretical guarantees: (i) node-wise Euclidean norm preserved in every internal layer; (ii) Householder reflector is scale- and sign-invariant with respect to the message; (iii) pairwise distances between nodes can change due to mismatch between node-wise orthogonal operators.

Conclusion: HouseGNN provides a norm-preserving, depth-stable GNN architecture that mitigates oversmoothing by leveraging orthogonal-like Householder transformations, supported by theoretical properties and the combination with GroupSort.

Abstract: Deep graph neural networks(GNNs) suffer from oversmoothing- a progressive collapse of node representation towards a low information subspace as network depth increases because the normalized graph propagation operator is repeatedly applied directly to the hidden representations. In this work we study Householder Graph Neural Network (HouseGNN). Rather than updating the hidden state like standard GCN, HouseGNN uses the aggregated neighbourhood message solely to estimate a reflection direction; the node embedding is then updated by a Householder reflector followed by GroupSort, yielding a piecewise orthogonal layer that preserves Euclidean norm at every node and at every depth. We prove three core properties: (i) every internal layer preserves the node-wise Euclidean norm; (ii) the Householder reflector is scale scale and sign-invariant in the message; and (iii) pairwise distance between nodes can change through mismatch between node-wise orthogonal operators.

</details>


### [104] [GENADA: efficient generative time series adversarial attack framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12535)
*Michael Baronov,Denis Vorobev,Margarita Rusanova,Petr Sokerin,Alexey Zaytsev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GENADA: a generative model that crafts time-series adversarial perturbations in a single forward pass, with single-step and iterative variants; achieves comparable attack quality to strong baselines while reducing perturbation-generation time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce computational burden of iterative gradient-based adversarial attacks on time-series models, enabling faster generation of deceptive perturbations suitable for real-time or large-scale scenarios.

Method: Train a neural generator that maps inputs to perturbations, enabling direct perturbation in one forward pass. The approach includes single-step and iterative variants and a training procedure to optimize perturbations for effective attacks (likely using a surrogate model and an adversarial objective).

Result: Empirical evaluation shows GENADA attains comparable attack quality to strong baselines across several neural models and time-series datasets in a controlled, low-dimensional setting, with significantly faster perturbation generation during inference.

Conclusion: Generative-time-series adversarial attacks can match the effectiveness of gradient-based methods while offering substantial efficiency gains; this highlights a need for robust defenses and further exploration of transferability and generalization to higher-dimensional/real-world time-series data.

Abstract: Deep learning models are widely used for time series analysis in domains such as healthcare, finance, energy systems, and environmental monitoring. However, these models remain vulnerable to adversarial attacks, where small input perturbations cause severe degradation in predictive performance. Commonly used gradient-based attacks, iterative first-order methods, are computationally burdensome, as they repeatedly backpropagate through the victim model to compute input gradients during a number of iterative refinement steps. We propose a GENerative ADversarial Attack (GENADA) that learns a generative model to produce deceptive perturbations directly in a single forward pass and a procedure to train it. Variants include single-step and iterative generative attack schemes. The validation considers attacks on several neural models and datasets in the time-series domain, a controlled, low-dimensional setting. Empirically, GENADA achieves comparable attack quality to strong baselines while requiring less time to generate perturbations during inference.

</details>


### [105] [Scaling Automatic Research Agents via World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12564)
*Xiyuan Yang,Sheikh Sarwar,Jingru Cheng,Zhan Shi,Duanshun Li,Huiyuan Chen,Haiyang Zhang,Chenlei Guo,Jingrui He,Zhenyu Liao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Replace environment execution in AutoResearch RL with a world model (WMRL) to decouple disparate scaling costs, add Online Debiasing and Inverse-Variance Denoising to handle biased/noisy rewards, and achieve substantial speedups and strong performance comparable to much larger models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AutoResearch trajectories have two scaling components: agent generation (batched compute) and environment execution (sandbox/real-time). Environment execution becomes the bottleneck as trajectories grow, hindering scaling of RL-based automated research.

Method: Introduce World Model RL (WMRL) that substitutes environment execution with a world model. Equip WMRL with Online Debiasing to offset reward bias and Inverse-Variance Denoising to reduce reward noise. Provide theoretical convergence guarantees and empirically validate across tasks and agent scales, including post-training 4B and 9B agents; demonstrate transfer to embodied VLA policies.

Result: WMRL achieves 3-4x training speedups across tasks and scales, surpasses standard RL baselines, and 4B/9B agents outperform much larger open-weight agents (48B/120B) on held-out benchmarks. Demonstrates transfer of benefits to embodied VLA policies beyond AutoResearch.

Conclusion: Decoupling environment execution via a world model and applying debiasing/denoising mitigations yields faster convergence and stronger performance, with broad generalizability to AutoResearch and embodied policies.

Abstract: Automating empirical research is a long-standing direction of AI. Recent automatic research (AutoResearch) agents bring this goal within reach, as modern LLMs show the capability to independently implement solutions and learn from the execution outcomes. Behind these gains, post-training (especially RL) plays a central role. In this paper, we identify a fundamental tension when scaling RL for these agents: the two components of every AutoResearch trajectory (agent generation and environment execution) scale in very different manners, since all generation shares compute through batching, while each execution occupies its exclusive sandbox and real machine time. As a result, the environment execution dominates the training cost and becomes the bottleneck as trajectories grow. To resolve this tension, we propose World Model RL (WMRL), which replaces environment execution with a world model to remove this bottleneck. Additionally, the world model can be imperfect, as its rewards are corrupted by bias and noise. Therefore, we further equip WMRL with two mitigations, Online Debiasing and Inverse-Variance Denoising, which offset the bias and suppress the noise respectively. Theoretically, we prove that both mitigations of WMRL strictly improve the convergence guarantee. Empirically, WMRL accelerates training by 3-4x on various tasks at different agent scales, while exceeding the performance of standard RL baselines. Moreover, our post-trained 4B and 9B agents outperform much larger open-weight agents of 48B and 120B on held-out benchmarks. Beyond AutoResearch, WMRL also transfers to post-training embodied VLA policies, which demonstrates the generalizability of our method.

</details>


### [106] [Prof-K: Probabilistic One-Pass Filtering for Efficient Top-k Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12573)
*Tadeusz Dziarmaga,Witold Sikora,Łukasz Struski,Jacek Tabor,Marcin Mazur*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Prof-K is a fast, distribution-agnostic top-k algorithm with probabilistic correctness guarantees. It uses a single-pass sampling-based threshold estimate, streams N elements into a compact buffer, and runs an exact top-k on the buffer to recover the true top-k with probability at least 1-ε. It provides high-probability guarantees for correctness and buffer size, an approximately optimal sample size, and speedups (1.5x–10x) over PyTorch topk and RadiK, especially for large-scale, small-to-moderate k inputs. It is robust to adversarial/heavy-tailed inputs and supports accuracy-speed trade-offs by relaxing recall. It also demonstrates impact on training BatchTopK Sparse Autoencoders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As data sizes grow, exact top-k methods incur high memory/compute, while approximate heuristics are brittle and can fail under adversarial or heavy-tailed inputs. A robust, scalable, distribution-agnostic solution with probabilistic guarantees is needed for modern workloads.

Method: Single-pass: draw a small random sample to estimate an adaptive threshold. Stream N items into a compact buffer and perform an exact top-k on the buffer. The true top-k is recovered with probability at least 1-ε. Derive high-probability correctness and buffer-size guarantees, and compute an approximately optimal sample size that minimizes overhead as a function of N and k.

Result: Empirical results show Prof-K achieves 1.5x–10x speedups over optimized PyTorch topk and RadiK, especially in large-scale, small-to-moderate-k regimes. Guarantees hold independently of input distribution. Relaxing recall (e.g., 95% of true top-k) yields a principled accuracy-speed trade-off. Demonstrated applicability to training BatchTopK Sparse Autoencoders, where top-k selection is a major cost.

Conclusion: Prof-K provides a robust, scalable, and distribution-agnostic top-k primitive with probabilistic correctness guarantees, offering substantial speedups and a tunable accuracy-speed trade-off, suitable for large-scale ML workloads and SAEs.

Abstract: Top-k selection is a fundamental computational primitive with applications spanning databases, information retrieval, signal processing, and modern machine learning workloads, including sparse activations and attention pruning. As data sizes grow, existing approaches become inefficient: exact methods incur high memory and compute overhead, while approximate methods often rely on brittle heuristics that degrade under adversarial or heavy-tailed inputs. In this paper, we introduce Prof-K, a fast, scalable, and distribution-agnostic top-k algorithm with probabilistic correctness guarantees. Prof-K performs a single-pass filtering procedure: a small random sample estimates an adaptive threshold, the N input elements are streamed once into a compact buffer, and an exact top-k routine on this buffer recovers the true top-k elements with probability at least 1 - $ε$, where $ε$ > 0 is user specified. We derive high-probability guarantees for correctness and buffer size, together with an approximately optimal sample size that minimizes overhead as a function of N and k. Empirically, Prof-K achieves 1.5x-10x speedups over the highly optimized PyTorch topk and recent RadiK implementations, with the largest gains in the large-scale, small-to-moderate-k regime where prior methods struggle most. Unlike previous approaches, these guarantees hold independently of the input distribution, ensuring robustness to adversarial settings. By relaxing the recall target (e.g., recovering 95% of the true top-k values), Prof-K additionally provides a principled accuracy-speed trade-off. We further demonstrate its impact on training BatchTopK Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs), where top-k selection constitutes a significant portion of the training cost.

</details>


### [107] [Represent, Then Generate: Multimodal-Conditioned Time-Series Generation under Irregular Missingness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12592)
*Haochen Zhang,Jiaheng Guo,Yu-Chao Huang,Nicholas Knoz,Tianlong Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ReCoGen is a two-stage multimodal conditional generation framework for synthesizing missing physiological signals. It decouples conditioning from generation: Stage I uses per-modality masked autoencoders to produce compact tokens; Stage II uses a flow-matching generator to fuse tokens with static covariates and synthesize the target. It achieves state-of-the-art downstream utility across multiple benchmarks (AI-READI, MIMIC-III, MIMIC-IV) on 16 dataset-task-metric settings, often matching or exceeding utility of real signals. Ablations emphasize the conditioning path (learnable cross-attention over frozen encoders and a dual token-plus-AdaLN route).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical time series are often incomplete, with informative signals being invasive or unavailable. Existing generators typically condition on a single modality and struggle with heterogeneous, irregular missingness. A multimodal, robust conditioning strategy is needed to synthesize target signals from available co-recorded data and static covariates.

Method: Stage I: train one masked autoencoder per modality to distill time-variant conditions into compact, missingness-tolerant token sequences. Stage II: train a flow-matching generator that fuses these tokens with static conditions via learnable cross-attention to synthesize the target signal. Conditioning path includes cross-attention over frozen per-modality encoders and a dual token-plus-AdaLN route for static covariates.

Result: Across three physiological benchmarks (including AI-READI for continuous glucose monitoring and MIMIC-III/MIMIC-IV for arterial blood pressure), ReCoGen achieves the best downstream utility in all 16 dataset-task-metric settings, surpassing six representative conditional generators. In 13 settings, its utility matches or exceeds the utility of the real signal. Ablations confirm the conditioning path as a key contributor to gains.

Conclusion: ReCoGen enables reliable synthesis of informative surrogates for invasive or unavailable signals using routinely collected data, moving toward less invasive, lower-cost continuous clinical monitoring.

Abstract: Continuous physiological time series underpin modern clinical monitoring, yet many of the most informative signals are invasive, expensive, or simply unavailable for a given patient. Conditional generation offers a remedy: an absent signal can be synthesized from co-recorded signals and routine clinical variables. Existing generators, however, are built around a single conditioning modality and degrade when forced to handle the heterogeneous, irregularly missing mix of time-variant signals and static covariates seen in practice. We propose ReCoGen (Represent Conditions, then Generate), a two-stage framework that decouples multimodal condition representation from target generation. Stage I trains one masked autoencoder per modality, distilling each time-variant condition into a compact and missingness-tolerant token sequence. Stage II trains a flow-matching generator that fuses these tokens with static conditions to synthesize the target signal. Across three physiological benchmarks, including continuous glucose monitoring on AI-READI and arterial blood pressure generation on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV, ReCoGen attains the best downstream utility on all sixteen (dataset, task, metric) settings, surpassing six representative conditional generators; on thirteen of them its utility also reaches or exceeds the utility measured on the real signal, a reference we read as an approximate anchor rather than a ceiling. Ablations trace the gains to the conditioning path: learnable cross-attention over the frozen per-modality encoders, and a dual token-plus-AdaLN route for the static conditions. ReCoGen thus turns routinely collected signals into informative surrogates for invasive or unavailable ones, a step toward less invasive, lower-cost continuous clinical monitoring.

</details>


### [108] [Predicting When Random Low-Dimensional Reparameterizations Train Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12597)
*Andrew Cheng,Ali Eslamian,Jie Cheng,Mehdi Zargham,Qiang Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Predicts the latent-dimension required for random low-dimensional reparameterizations to reach low loss, via a conic reformulation and an orientation-resolved quadratic master formula; introduces memory-efficient Random Mapping Networks (RaMaN) and validates them with theory and neural-curvature experiments across images and language tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pragmatic need to know how large a latent search space must be for effective training via random low-dimensional reparameterization; to connect accessibility transitions with geometry, reduce memory for random maps, and enable scalable optimization.

Method: Reformulate the problem as a conic feasibility task centered on the polar cone and statistical dimension; derive an orientation-resolved quadratic master formula that links the random-slice residual to curvature spectrum and displacement profile; obtain a self-consistent isotropic predictor and recover Gaussian-width bounds in a special case; instantiate the predicted latent dimension with structured Hadamard or seed-regenerated Gaussian maps (RaMaN) to avoid dense map storage; develop matrix-free curvature approximations and a sweep-free approach to dimension selection; perform controlled quadratic and neural-curvature experiments plus end-to-end training on image and language models.

Result: The orientation-resolved predictor tracks transition locations closely and outperforms orientation-agnostic bounds when displacement direction matters; RaMaN achieves significant memory savings (O(d) vs O(P)); curvature approximations and dimension-selection are effective; end-to-end runs show sharp protocol-dependent transitions in both vision and language models.

Conclusion: Orientation-aware, conic-geometry analysis yields accurate latent-dimension predictions for random low-dimensional reparameterizations; RaMaN provides memory-efficient, scalable implementations; the framework generalizes across tasks and modalities, with clear transition phenomena during training.

Abstract: Neural networks can often be trained or fine-tuned through random low-dimensional reparameterization, where a small latent vector is mapped into a full parameter update by a frozen random map. This raises a practical question: how large must the latent search space be to reach a low-loss region? We first express the known accessibility transition in an equivalent conic form, centered for compact convex targets at the statistical dimension of the polar cone. Our main theoretical contribution is an orientation-resolved quadratic master formula that predicts the random-slice residual from both the curvature spectrum and the reference-to-solution displacement profile. It yields a self-consistent isotropic-orientation predictor and, in a conservative radius-only specialization, recovers the earlier Gaussian-width quadratic bound. Building on this analysis, we introduce Random Mapping Networks (RaMaN), which instantiate the predicted latent dimension using structured Hadamard or seed-regenerated Gaussian maps. These constructions avoid the O(dP) storage of dense random maps and reduce optimizer-state memory from O(P) to O(d). We also develop matrix-free curvature approximations and sweep-free dimension selection. Across controlled quadratic and neural-curvature experiments, the orientation-resolved predictor closely tracks measured transition locations and outperforms orientation-agnostic approximations when displacement direction matters. End-to-end experiments further show sharp, protocol-dependent training transitions across image and language models.

</details>


### [109] [The Boolean Power of ReLU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12617)
*Pablo Barceló,Floris Geerts,Matthias Lanzinger,Klara Pakhomenko,Jan Van den Bussche*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ReLU MPLang strictly more expressive than Σ-MPLang for Boolean queries on graphs with a single Boolean feature; resolves an open problem and implies ReLU-GNNs outperform TrReLU-id-GNNs on such queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Determine the comparative expressive power of graph query languages/models endowed with different activation functions in a precise Boolean-query setting.

Method: Prove Σ-MPLang ⊆ ReLU-MPLang and establish strictness by constructing a separating Boolean query; consider arbitrary real coefficients and any collection Σ of eventually constant activations.

Result: Boolean queries expressible in Σ-MPLang form a strict subset of those expressible in ReLU-MPLang. Concludes ReLU-GNNs are strictly more expressive than {TrReLU,id}-GNNs for Boolean-featured graphs.

Conclusion: Activation-with-ReLU yields strictly greater expressive power for Boolean queries on graphs than Σ-activations; settles the comparative question between ReLU-MPLang and trReLU-MPLang.

Abstract: We prove that, on finite simple undirected graphs equipped with a single Boolean node feature, the Boolean queries expressible in $Σ$-MPLang, for any collection $Σ$ of eventually constant activation functions and with arbitrary real coefficients, form a strict subclass of the Boolean queries expressible in ReLU-MPLang. We thereby settle a recently posed open problem: whether ReLU-MPLang is more powerful than trReLU-MPLang when it comes to Boolean queries. In particular, this implies that ReLU-GNNs are strictly more expressive than {TrReLU,id}-GNNs with respect to Boolean queries on Boolean-featured graphs.

</details>


### [110] [Structure-preserving uncertainty quantification for GENERIC dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12624)
*Zequn He,Celia Reina*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Structure-preserving UQ for constrained scientific models via S-PENNs: attach lightweight epistemic nets to preserve admissibility, applied to GENERIC dynamics, with split conformal prediction for finite-sample calibrated intervals; yields thermodynamically consistent realizations, computationally cheaper than deep ensembles, and broadly extendable to hard/soft constraints in computational mechanics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uncertainty quantification (UQ) for models with hard architectural constraints is challenging because standard UQ methods can violate admissibility, require architecture changes, or be computationally expensive. The work proposes a framework (S-PENNs) that preserves physical structure while enabling UQ.

Method: Develop Structure-Preserving Epistemic Neural Networks (S-PENNs) by attaching lightweight epinets to the constrained components of a pretrained model, ensuring admissible realizations. Instantiate for GENERIC dynamics. Combine with split conformal prediction for post-hoc calibration to achieve finite-sample coverage. Validate on three ODE/PDE benchmarks (harmonic oscillator with heat bath, chemical motor, 1D viscoplastic PDE).

Result: S-PENNs produce thermodynamically consistent stochastic realizations and well-calibrated prediction intervals. Computational cost reduced by ~1–3 orders of magnitude relative to deep ensembles, while maintaining physical admissibility and coverage guarantees.

Conclusion: S-PENNs offer a general approach to UQ in science-informed ML with hard constraints, extendable beyond GENERIC to other constrained computational mechanics models (hard or soft constraints).

Abstract: Structure-preserving machine learning embeds physical structure directly into model architectures, yet uncertainty quantification (UQ) for such hard-constrained models remains limited because standard UQ methods may violate the encoded admissibility conditions, require architectural modifications, or impose substantial computational costs. In this work, we propose Structure-Preserving Epistemic Neural Networks (S-PENNs), a general framework for UQ in scientific machine learning models with hard architectural constraints, and instantiate it for GENERIC (General Equation for Non-Equilibrium Reversible-Irreversible Coupling) dynamics. S-PENNs preserve the structural constraints of a pretrained model by attaching lightweight epinets to its constrained components, ensuring that every sampled realization remains physically admissible by construction. When applied to GENERIC dynamics, such a proposed framework yields thermodynamically consistent rollouts that preserve the first and second laws. Furthermore, we combine S-PENNs with split conformal prediction as a post-hoc calibration method to produce prediction intervals with finite-sample marginal coverage guarantees. We validate S-PENNs on three numerical examples: a harmonic oscillator coupled to a heat bath and an idealized chemical motor, both governed by ODEs, and a one-dimensional viscoplastic model governed by PDEs. Across all three examples, S-PENNs produce thermodynamically consistent stochastic realizations and well-calibrated prediction intervals while reducing the computational cost by about one to three orders of magnitude compared to deep ensembles. Although the present study focuses on GENERIC dynamics, S-PENNs can be extended more broadly to scientific machine learning models in computational mechanics with either hard or soft constraints.

</details>


### [111] [CAKE: Compiler-Agent Co-Design for Frontier Kernel Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12629)
*Zihao Ye,Yingyi Huang,Hongyi Jin,Bohan Hou,Junru Shao,Zhongming Yu,Jinqi Chen,Meghan Cowan,Shiyi Cao,Shanli Xing,Hanfeng Chen,Vinod Grover,Tianqi Chen,Luis Ceze*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CAKE enables compiler–agent co-design for GPU kernels via a typed, hardware-explicit IR that reveals scheduling decisions and improves performance and verifiability over existing CUDA/DSL approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome reproducibility and optimization gaps in expert GPU kernels caused by treating compilers as opaque black boxes and by DSLs that obscure or complicate scheduling.”

Method: Introduce CAKE IR, a typed, hardware-explicit schedule representation exposing warp roles, memory movement, synchronization, and pipelines; support verification, cost modeling, and localized diagnostics; create a self-evolving harness where recurring failures become verifier rules, IR primitives, model calibrations, and reusable optimization tactics; evaluate on NVIDIA GPUs (Ampere–Blackwell) with matched implementation-hidden benchmarks (Flash-KMeans, FlashML) and downstream tasks (KNN, KMeans, Delta Attention).

Result: CAKE yields significant improvements: best IR on 80M tokens achieves 1.144x the tuned FlashML baseline vs 0.928x for direct CUDA/PTX; agent-generated Kimi Delta Attention attains 2.05x geometric-mean speedup over official FlashKDA and passes end-to-end serving validation; dispatcher-backed KNN/KMeans improve 1.42x–2.12x across 400+ shapes, with four kernel changes released upstream; CAKE targets NVIDIA GPUs from Ampere through Blackwell and decouples single-shape evolution from library generalization and dispatch.

Conclusion: CAKE demonstrates that compiler–agent co-design with a typed, hardware-explicit IR can unlock substantial performance gains, enhanced verification capability, and upstreamable optimizations, while enabling shape-specific evolution and broader generalization for GPU kernels.

Abstract: GPU kernel agents and GPU programming languages have advanced separately, leaving expert kernels difficult to reproduce. Agents usually treat the compiler as a fixed black box and receive only errors, correctness outcomes, and timing, while existing DSLs either hide critical scheduling decisions or expose them through difficult layout abstractions. We present CAKE, a compiler-agent co-design in which agents author CAKE IR, a typed, hardware-explicit schedule representation. CAKE exposes warp roles, memory movement, synchronization, and pipelines while supporting verification, cost modeling, and localized diagnostics. The harness itself evolves: recurring failures become verifier rules, IR primitives, model calibrations, and reusable optimization tactics. In matched implementation-hidden Flash-KMeans clean starts on B200, the best CAKE IR candidate at an 80-million-token budget runs at 1.144x the tuned FlashML baseline, compared with 0.928x for direct CUDA/PTX. Beyond this benchmark, agent-generated Kimi Delta Attention achieves a 2.05x geometric-mean speedup over official FlashKDA and passes end-to-end serving validation. Dispatcher-backed KNN and KMeans improve performance by 1.42x to 2.12x across more than 400 shapes, and four kernel changes are available as upstream PRs. CAKE targets NVIDIA GPUs from Ampere through Blackwell and separates single-shape evolution from library generalization and dispatch.

</details>


### [112] [Interpretable Causal Discovery via Causal-Effect Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12640)
*Cixuan Zhang,Guy Van den Broeck,Benjie Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Bayesian rare-event approach for conditional causal discovery estimating posterior over graphs/parameters given a constraint, scalable to small posterior mass; uses particle-based rare-event sampling; validated on synthetic graphs and Sachs dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interpretability and explanation in causal discovery require conditioning on events like causal-effect constraints; standard Bayesian methods struggle with events of small posterior probability.

Method: Adapts rare-event estimation to the joint graph-parameter space; uses a population of particles that are gradually guided toward the constrained region, preserving samples that approximate the conditional posterior.

Result: Synthetic-graph experiments show accuracy across small and large scales; Sachs dataset case study demonstrates usefulness for pathway-level summaries.

Conclusion: The proposed rare-event conditioned inference enables robust, interpretable conditional causal discovery, extending Bayesian causal discovery to rare-event regimes and facilitating scientific exploration through pathway-level insights.

Abstract: Causal discovery aims to uncover the underlying causal relationships given data generated from a system. The goal, however, is not merely to predict causal edges given data, but also to be able to interpret and explain either observed or hypothesized phenomena, such as a particularly large causal effect. We consider this task of conditional causal discovery and cast it as a Bayesian inference problem, in which we target the posterior over causal graphs and parameters conditional on an event such as a causal-effect constraint. Unfortunately, this poses a computational challenge: existing approaches to Bayesian causal discovery struggle when the event has small posterior mass. To address this, we adapt rare-event estimation techniques to perform inference the joint graph-parameter space. Our method gradually drives a particle population toward the constrained region while maintaining samples that approximate the conditional posterior. Empirical evaluation on synthetic graphs validates the accuracy of our approach at small and large scales, and we show in a case study on the Sachs protein dataset how our method can be used to aid scientific exploration by providing pathway-level summaries.

</details>


### [113] [Training Under Challenge: Executable Certificates and Challenge-Closed Optimality for Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12655)
*Farhang Yeganegi,Arian Eamaz,Mojtaba Soltanalian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Training Under Challenge (TUC), an executable-certificate framework that constructs alternative candidates within the same certified class to diagnose empirical global-optimality gaps. It defines a resource-indexed challenge-power modulus, uses coverage checks (for squared loss with block-decrease operators), and demonstrates practical bounds and diagnostics on ResNet-18 distillation, plus predictive certificates and denoising studies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A flat training curve does not reveal whether a neural network has reached global optimum, is locally trapped, limited by representation, or mismatched to its trainer. The paper seeks a principled certificate-based framework to reveal and bound the empirical global-optimality gap.

Method: Predeclared, architecture-valid procedures generate complete alternatives in the same certified class and reevaluate the same objective. Any lower-valued candidate serves as a replayable witness to lower-bound the gap. A finite suite of checks is suite-relative; global-gap conclusions require a coverage mechanism. Introduces a resource-indexed challenge-power modulus. For squared loss, block-decrease operators enable coverage checks and yield uniform and realized-residual bounds. Proves the necessity of coverage: without it, a first-order ReLU trainer can converge to non-global optima while achieving many exact conditional head optima. Empirical demonstration on channel-gated ResNet-18 distillation with known optimum uses eight internal challenges to cover 240 output directions; realized-residual bounds lie within 1.74–3.02× of the true gap. Also presents paired predictive certificates distinguishing decoder under-use from representation insufficiency and shows quantized-denoising studies for diagnosis, repair, and recertification.

Result: A concrete certification framework (TUC) with a formal coverage mechanism and bounds on the empirical global-optimality gap. Demonstrated bounds are within a defined factor of the true gap on a concrete distillation task (ResNet-18) and supported by predictive certificates and quantized-denoising studies.

Conclusion: Training Under Challenge provides principled, executable certificates for diagnosing and certifying global-optimality gaps in neural training, enabling diagnosis of under-use versus representation insufficiency and offering mechanisms for repair and recertification.

Abstract: A flat training curve does not reveal whether a neural network has reached a global optimum, is locally trapped, is representation-limited, or is mismatched to its trainer. We introduce Training Under Challenge, an executable-certificate framework in which predeclared, architecture-valid procedures construct complete alternatives in the same certified class and reevaluate the same objective. Any lower-valued candidate is a replayable witness that lower-bounds the checkpoint's empirical global-optimality gap. Passing a finite suite is only suite-relative; global-gap conclusions require a separately justified coverage mechanism. We define a resource-indexed challenge-power modulus that characterizes the largest gap compatible with passage. For squared loss, current block-decrease operators make coverage checkable and yield uniform and realized-residual bounds. We prove the converse frontier: without coverage, a first-order ReLU trainer can reach infinitely many exact conditional head optima while converging to a non-global point. On a channel-gated ResNet-18 distillation problem with known optimum, eight internal challenges cover all 240 audited output directions, and realized-residual bounds lie within factors of 1.74--3.02 of the true gap. Paired predictive certificates separate decoder under-use from representation insufficiency, while quantized-denoising studies demonstrate diagnosis, repair, and current-state recertification.

</details>


### [114] [Demand Transfer Estimation at Scale via Restricted Logit Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12680)
*Lakshya Garg,Deep Narayan Mishra,Swapnil Yadav,Haoan Wang,Sujal Alugubelli,Karthik Kumaran,Anupriya Sharma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A scalable demand-forecasting framework using Demand Transfer (DT) coefficients to adjust independent item forecasts for very large assortments (1M+ items), modeling substitution effects when a target item is removed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the computational burden of forecasting demand for every possible assortment in large item universes by decoupling independent item forecasts from cross-item substitution adjustments.

Method: Estimate DT coefficients that quantify the percentage of a target item's demand redirected to other items if the target item is unavailable. Compute these coefficients on large item universes using transaction data and reasonable substitution assumptions, then apply DT adjustments to independent forecasts to improve overall demand accuracy.

Result: Under plausible substitution assumptions, the procedure accurately estimates DT coefficients and yields improved demand forecasting in large assortments across multiple locations/categories.

Conclusion: DT-based demand forecasting enables scalable, accurate forecasting for assortment optimization in extremely large item spaces (1M+ items); further work should address assumption sensitivity, sparsity, and integration with dynamic assortment decisions.

Abstract: Item demand forecasting is an integral component of store assortment optimization. Existing literature focuses on learning a suitable customer choice model and using this model to determine the value of an objective function (i.e. expected demand) with respect to an assortment proposal. However, for large item universe with many categories, this approach can prove inefficient, needing a separate demand forecast for every possible item assortment. An alternate approach exists whereby we combine the efficiency of forecasting item demand independently, while at the same time applying adjustments to the independent forecasts that account for the relations between item demand and the availability of other similar items on the shelf.
  Central to this approach is the estimation of Demand Transfer (DT) coefficients. These DT coefficients represent the percent of a particular target item's (item that the customer walked in the store to buy) demand that is redirected to each other item in the universe should the target item be removed from the shelf. We introduce an approach that allows us to compute these DT coefficients on large item universes (assortments having 1 million+ items). Experiments on data as well as historical transaction data for multiple locations within categories demonstrate that when certain reasonable assumptions about substitution behavior are satisfied, our procedure is able to accurately estimate underlying DT coefficients and lead to improvements in demand forecasting.

</details>


### [115] [Finding the Needle in a Haystack: Test-Time Analog Circuit Representation Adaptation for Bayesian Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12687)
*Fin Amin,Sounak Dutta,Paul D. Franzon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TTARO introduces online representation adaptation in Bayesian optimization for circuit topology search, aligning representations with the objective to improve sample efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed embeddings may be misaligned with the FoM; adapt representations online to reduce mismatch and improve optimization performance in costly circuit simulations.

Method: Start from pretrained embeddings; jointly learn a nonlinear feature transform and a Gaussian process surrogate using observed FoM labels; update both after each evaluation; compare to fixed-embedding BO and Deep Kernel Learning; apply to analog circuit topology search.

Result: TTARO achieves substantial reductions in regret AUC: ~15.2% vs standard BO and ~20.7% vs DKL across 40 encoder/kernel/acquisition settings, with up to 46.7% improvements in some cases.

Conclusion: Online, test-time adaptation of representations in BO boosts performance and can outperform fixed representations and DK learning in circuit topology optimization.

Abstract: Bayesian optimization (BO) is a sample-efficient framework for analog circuit topology search, where evaluating each candidate topology can require costly simulation. However, representation-based BO methods typically treat circuit embeddings as fixed after encoder training. This creates a mismatch between representation learning and optimization: embeddings learned to encode or reconstruct circuit structure are not necessarily organized according to the figure of merit (FoM) being optimized. This paper introduces Test-Time Analog Representation Adaptation for Bayesian Optimization (TTARO), an online deep-kernel BO framework that adapts circuit representations throughout the search process. Starting from pretrained circuit embeddings, TTARO jointly learns a nonlinear feature transformation and a Gaussian-process surrogate using the FoM labels of the circuits evaluated so far. Following each new evaluation, TTARO updates the representation and surrogate before selecting the next candidate. We compare TTARO with conventional Gaussian Process-based BO over fixed embeddings and with Deep Kernel Learning (DKL), which learns the representation only from the initial evaluated designs and keeps it fixed throughout the remainder of the search. By continually incorporating newly observed FoM labels into representation learning, TTARO aligns the search space with the optimization objective as BO progresses. In our experiments, TTARO reduces regret AUC by 15.2% on average relative to BO and by 20.7% relative to DKL across 40 encoder/kernel/acquisition settings, outperforming prior art in most settings with reductions as large as 46.7%.

</details>


### [116] [The Impact of Temporal Context Length and Encoding Strategies on Self-Supervised ECG Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12695)
*Ahmed Sameh,Ramzi Al-Sharawi,Yogatheesan Varatharajah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Longer temporal context (up to 5–10 minutes) improves transfer and patient-level retrieval in self-supervised ECG models, and continuous patch embeddings outperform fixed token quantization across horizons; advise ECG foundation models to favor extended context and continuous encoders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether input horizon and front-end representation affect rhythm inference and cross-session stability in self-supervised ECG models.

Method: Controlled study on Icentia11k single-lead data varying (i) input horizon: 16s, 1 min, 5 min, 10 min; (ii) front-end: continuous convolutional patch embeddings vs fixed vector-quantized tokens; fixed Transformer backbone and training protocol; evaluate on downstream abnormal rhythm detection and patient-level retrieval.

Result: Increasing horizon beyond 16s yields stronger transfer and higher retrieval accuracy, with 5–10 minute models performing best; continuous patch embeddings outperform discretized tokens across horizons, suggesting quantization discards clinically relevant waveform detail.

Conclusion: ECG foundation models should emphasize extended context and continuous encoders for clinical prediction and similarity-based tasks; code and pretrained models are publicly available.

Abstract: Self-supervised electrocardiogram (ECG) models are often trained on a few seconds of ECG signal and, increasingly, on discretized token sequences. It remains unclear whether these choices sacrifice information needed for rhythm inference and longitudinal consistency in real-world ambulatory recordings. We present a controlled study on the Icentia11k single-lead dataset that varies (i) the input horizon (16 seconds, 1 minute, 5 minutes, and 10 minutes) and (ii) the front-end representation (continuous convolutional patch embeddings vs. fixed vector-quantized tokens), while holding the Transformer backbone and training protocol constant. Representations are assessed by downstream abnormal rhythm detection and by patient-level retrieval that probes cross-session stability. Our results show that increasing temporal context beyond 16-second snapshots yields stronger transfer and higher retrieval accuracy, with the strongest performance achieved by the 5- and 10-minute models, indicating improved capture of slow-varying rhythm dynamics and individual-specific structure. Across all evaluated horizons, continuous patch embeddings outperform discretized tokens, suggesting that quantization can discard clinically relevant waveform detail. These findings motivate ECG foundation models that emphasize extended context and continuous encoders for clinical prediction and similarity-based applications. Our code and pretrained models are publicly available at https://github.com/muha-0/ecg-ssl-representation-learning.

</details>


### [117] [A Contract-Grade Verifier for LLM-Generated GPU Kernels, and a Native Blackwell Backward for the Gated-Linear-Recurrence Family](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12700)
*Rishi Shah,Rishav Shrestha*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Robust, tolerance-free verification of ML-generated GPU kernels reveals large correctness gaps missed by standard test; many kernels fail under 12-property verifier, suggesting reported progress is overstated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation relies on a single-latent test at fixed shapes and numeric tolerance, which can misclassify incorrect kernels as correct. A rigorous, tolerance-free verifier is needed to truthfully assess kernel correctness.

Method: Develop a contract-grade verifier with twelve adversarial gates (tolerance-free). Audit 2,638 machine-generated kernels; use external benchmarks, threshold-calibration sweeps, and stratified hand-audits; apply inward verification on their own tcgen05 GDN-based kernel, validated against a double-precision oracle; train five family members through it.

Result: 51-62% of kernels violate the tolerance-free contracts (39.5% beyond any tolerance; 62.1% with at least one violation). The field’s standard test accepts 1,487 kernels that the verifier rejects, versus only 14 the other way. High agreement (98.5%) with the reference benchmark's correctness code; multiple independent validations (7/7 positive controls, calibration sweeps, hand-audit) support the findings.

Conclusion: Reported progress in kernel generation is weaker than it appears; tolerance-free contracts would close most of the gap, underscoring the need for stricter correctness verification in ML-generated GPU kernels.

Abstract: Systems that generate GPU kernels with language models report high correctness rates. Those rates come from a single loose test: run the kernel on a few random inputs at one fixed shape and accept it if the output is close to a reference. A kernel can pass that test and still be silently wrong. It can return an ordinary number where the true answer is a NaN or an infinity, differ from run to run, break when the shape changes, or accumulate in fp16 where the reference keeps an fp32 total. We build the instrument that checks correctness properly: a contract-grade verifier of twelve adversarial gates, each a property a correct kernel must satisfy, several of them tolerance-free, so no choice of threshold can explain a failure away. Aimed outward, the verifier audits 2,638 machine-generated kernels that a public system's own harness had already accepted as correct. It finds 39.5% broken beyond any tolerance argument and 62.1% carrying at least one violation. The field's standard test accepts 1,487 kernels the verifier rejects, against only 14 the other way. We defend the finding four independent ways: a 7/7 positive control, a threshold-calibration sweep, 98.5% agreement with the reference benchmark's own correctness code, and a stratified hand-audit. Aimed inward, the verifier judges a kernel of our own: the first native Blackwell tcgen05 training backward for the gated-linear-recurrence (GDN) family, including the reverse-state stage the field still runs on a fallback. We establish its correctness independently, against a double-precision oracle, and train five family members through it. The correctness signal behind reported progress in kernel generation is far weaker than the numbers suggest, and a set of tolerance-free contracts would close most of the gap.

</details>


### [118] [Federated Compositional Muon Optimizer for Matrix-Wise Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12710)
*Wang Yan,Feihu Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Federated compositional optimization for matrix-wise problems using FedCoMuon and its VR variant, achieving improved convergence and empirical performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the mismatch between existing Muon-family optimizers and hierarchical, matrix-wise, and distributed optimization problems in federated settings; fill the gap with a specialized FedCoMuon approach.

Method: Introduce FedCoMuon leveraging compositional gradient tracking and orthogonalized momentum; propose FedCoMuon-VR with momentum-based variance reduction; analyze convergence under non-i.i.d. and non-convex conditions.

Result: Theoretically, FedCoMuon-VR attains a lower sample complexity of O(ε^{-3}) for finding an ε-stationary solution than existing FedMuon algorithms. Empirically, the methods are competitive with baselines and achieve state-of-the-art accuracy in several robust FL and task-distributed risk-sensitive meta-learning tasks.

Conclusion: FedCoMuon and FedCoMuon-VR provide effective solutions for distributed matrix-wise compositional optimization, delivering improved sample efficiency and strong empirical performance in hierarchical federated settings.

Abstract: Muon, a more recently developed optimizer, is useful for matrix-wise models in AI areas. Although many works have studied Muon and its variants, these methods are still not particularly well-suited for hierarchical structured problems. To fill this gap, we propose an effective federated compositional Muon (FedCoMuon) optimizer to solve distributed matrix-wise compositional optimization problems. Specifically, our FedCoMuon optimizer builds on compositional gradient tracking and orthogonalized momentum. Moreover, we propose a variance reduced variant of FedCoMuon (FedCoMuon-VR) based on a momentum-based variance reduced technique. In theory, we analyze the convergence properties of our algorithms under the non-i.i.d. and non-convex settings. In particular, we prove that our FedCoMuon-VR obtains a lower sample complexity of $O(ε^{-3})$ for finding an $ε$-stationary solution than the existing FedMuon algorithms. Extensive numerical experiments on robust federated learning and task-distributed risk-sensitive meta learning show that our proposed methods are competitive with existing compositional baselines and achieve the best reported accuracy in several settings.

</details>


### [119] [Perturbation-based Regional Interpretability through Subtraction Mapping (PRISM): naming-error dissociations in language models and post-stroke aphasia](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12717)
*Xiang Guan,Roger D. Newman-Norlund,Yong Yang,Saeed Ahmadi,Regan Willis,Nadra Salman,Kalil Warren,Srihari Nelakuditi,Chris Rorden,Leonardo Bonilha,Julius Fridriksson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PRISM is a perturbation-based subtraction mapping framework that transfers neuroimaging subtraction analysis to LLMs and parallels lesion-mapping in aphasia to test functional specialization in transformers; it finds phonemic bias in deep layers and frontal-perisylvian cluster, with semantic bias non-significant, and offers a falsifiable, spatially resolved test with Stage 3 ROI work for causal claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a lack of spatially resolved, falsifiable tools to test whether internal components of large language models are specialized for distinct cognitive operations. By importing subtraction analyses from human neuroimaging and applying them to perturbed transformers, the work aims to establish a testbed for functional specialization that is directly comparable across biological and artificial substrates.

Method: Develop PRISM by mapping seven Philadelphia Naming Test categories in LLM perturbations, performing pairwise error-proportion subtraction, and treating perturbation seeds as subjects in a group-analysis with threshold-free cluster enhancement along the layer axis. Use a structurally matched design on 213 chronic post-stroke aphasia patients with correlation-difference lesion-symptom mapping, replicating findings on held-out splits. Compare within-subject LLM error contrasts to between-subject cortical lesion-symptom contrasts, ensuring parity in subject, spatial, and thresholding dimensions.

Result: Both substrates reveal a robust phonemic-favoring dissociation, a deep-layer cluster, and a frontal-perisylvian cortical cluster, with replication across splits. Semantic-favoring effects are consistently signed but non-significant in both domains. The framework provides a falsifiable, spatially resolved test of functional specialization in transformers.

Conclusion: PRISM offers a falsifiable, spatially resolved method to test functional specialization in transformer models; a confirmatory ROI-level intervention (PRISM Stage 3) for causal mechanism claims remains for future work.

Abstract: Mechanistic interpretability of large language models lacks spatially resolved, falsifiable tools for testing whether internal components are specialized for distinct cognitive operations. We adapt subtraction analysis, the standard framework of human neuroimaging, from biological brains to perturbed transformers, and apply the same logic to both substrates in parallel. Building on the Brain-LLM Unified Model (BLUM), which showed that layer-perturbed LLaVA-1.6-Vicuna-13B error profiles match the lesion patterns of aphasic patients, we develop PRISM (Perturbation-based Regional Interpretability through Subtraction Mapping). PRISM maps the seven clinical Philadelphia Naming Test categories, subtracts error classes pairwise, and treats each perturbation seed as a subject in a group analysis with threshold-free cluster enhancement along the layer axis. We run a structurally matched analysis on 213 chronic post-stroke aphasia patients using correlation-difference lesion-symptom mapping, and replicate both sides on held-out splits. The designs match in subject dimension (seeds, patients), spatial dimension (layers, atlas-parcellated cortex) and thresholding, but the contrast operator differs: a within-subject error-proportion difference for the LLM, a between-subject correlation difference for the cortex. Both substrates recover a robust phonemic-favoring dissociation, a deep layer cluster and a frontal-perisylvian cortical cluster, both replicating; the semantic-favoring direction is a consistently signed but non-significant trend on both. PRISM thus gives a falsifiable, spatially resolved test of functional-specialization claims in transformer language models. A confirmatory ROI-level intervention (PRISM Stage 3) licensing the strongest causal-mechanism claim is left to subsequent work.

</details>


### [120] [Towards Socially Compliant Navigation in Deep Reinforcement Learning via Proxemics-Based Reward Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12917)
*Takieddine Soualhi,Jacques Saraydaryan,Laetitia Matignon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A proxemics-based reward for DRL social navigation uses a robot-centric, radially distributed Gaussian-mixture field (based on Hall's proxemics) to encode personal space and generate a dense local cost over the robot's field of view, integrated into DRL policies to improve social compliance without sacrificing navigation efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the gap between task-focused DRL navigation and social compliance by introducing an interpretable, dense social learning signal derived from human proxemics, enabling robots to navigate crowded environments more socially in real time.

Method: Model each human's personal space as a radial Gaussian-mixture field informed by Hall's proxemics. Compute a robot-centric local cost from the robot's field of view using this proxemics-based representation. Integrate this reward into established DRL navigation frameworks and evaluate across multiple crowd scenarios, baselines, and densities using navigation and social metrics.

Result: The proposed proxemics-based reward consistently improves social metrics in simulation while maintaining competitive navigation performance compared to other reward models.

Conclusion: A proxemics-informed reward provides a dense, interpretable social learning signal for DRL-based navigation that enhances social compliance without sacrificing efficiency, with potential for generalization across DRL methods and scenarios.

Abstract: Developing effective robot navigation methods in crowded environments is essential for real-world applications. Although recent deep reinforcement learning (DRL) methods have improved navigation performance in crowded environments, they often focus primarily on task-centric objectives and underrepresent social compliance objectives. In this paper, we introduce a novel proxemics-based reward formulation for DRL social navigation that provides a dense, interpretable social learning signal while maintaining navigation efficiency. Our approach models each human's personal space as a radial Gaussian-mixture field derived from Hall's proxemics theory and computes a robot-centric local cost over the robot's field of view. We integrate the proposed reward into established DRL navigation methods and evaluate it in simulation across multiple crowd scenarios, reward baselines, and crowd densities using both navigation metrics and social metrics. Results show that the proposed reward consistently improves social metrics in simulation while maintaining competitive navigation performance relative to the compared reward models.

</details>


### [121] [MAG: MAnifold Guided Semi-Supervised Multi-modal In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12724)
*Zirui Cheng,Xun Xu,Tiankai Chen,Fady Rezk,Bowen Zheng,Xiaodong Shi,Shijie Li,Kangkang Lu,Bharadwaj Veeravalli,Nancy F. Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MAG is a semi-supervised, graph-based framework for multi-modal in-context demonstration selection. It uses relevance propagation on a multi-modal graph to pick high-impact unlabeled samples for pseudo-labeling, then selects final demonstrations using multi-modal relevance. Textual features excel for propagation, while both textual and visual cues drive high-quality demonstration selection, yielding strong gains on eight benchmarks in label-scarce settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In few-shot ICL with MLLMs, performance hinges on demonstration quality; unlabeled multi-modal data is abundant but underutilized for ICL. MAG aims to exploit unlabeled data to improve demonstration selection without model updates.

Method: Construct a multi-modal graph and perform semi-supervised relevance score propagation to identify high-impact unlabeled samples for pseudo-labeling; adopt a two-stage process: (i) relevance score propagation to select compact pseudo-labeled set, (ii) use multi-modal relevance to choose final demonstrations; textual representations are more effective for propagation, while both textual and visual modalities are crucial for demonstration selection.

Result: MAG consistently beats strong baselines in label-scarce regimes across eight multi-modal benchmarks, achieving significant gains with a limited pseudo-labeling budget.

Conclusion: MAG provides an effective, cost-efficient way to leverage unlabeled data for ICL in MLLMs by combining semi-supervised propagation with multi-modal demonstration selection, balancing textual and visual cues to improve few-shot performance.

Abstract: Few-shot in-context learning (ICL) with multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) enables task adaptation without parameter updates, but its performance is highly sensitive to the quality and coverage of the selected demonstrations. While unlabeled multi-modal data is abundant, it remains elusive how to exploit them for ICL. We propose MAG (MAnifold-Guided semi-supervised in-context demonstra- tion selection), an efficient framework that leverages unlabeled data to improve multi-modal ICL. MAG formulates demonstration selection as a semi-supervised propagation problem on a multi-modal graph and adopts a two-stage strategy: (i) relevance score propagation identifies a compact set of high-impact unlabeled samples for pseudo-labeling, reducing MLLM inference cost; (ii) multi-modal relevance is used to select the final demonstrations. We show that textual represen- tations are more effective for relevance propagation, while both visual and textual modalities are crucial for high-quality demonstration selection. Experiments on eight multi-modal benchmarks demonstrate that MAG consistently outperforms strong baselines in label-scarce regimes, achieving significant gains with a limited pseudo-labeling budget.

</details>


### [122] [A Cloud-Edge System for Multimodal Clinical Screening in Resource-Constrained Rural Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12745)
*Hei Ting,Chan,Chenwei Wu,Xueshen Liu,Zesen Zhao,Boyuan Zheng,Luis Filipe Nakayama,Michael G. Morley,Liyue Shen,Jiasi Chen,Z. Morley Mao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Cloud-edge collaboration for multimodal medical AI in low-bandwidth settings: edge models produce structured outputs; cloud LLM integrates them; dynamic tool selection; evaluated on 20 cases with 3 network profiles; achieves high recall/precision, maintains bandwidth-invariant latency with lower token cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable high-performing multimodal medical AI in resource-constrained rural settings where bandwidth and compute are limited, addressing the gaps of cloud-only approaches.

Method: Lightweight, domain-specific models run on edge devices to convert raw medical data into compact structured outputs. A cloud LLM acts as an orchestrator, synthesizing outputs into clinical summaries and dynamically selecting diagnostic tools based on patient context. Evaluation on 20 multimodal clinical cases across cardiac, obstetric, trauma, and screening scenarios under three network profiles (500 kbps–5 Mbps). Metrics include diagnostic tool recall, precision, clinical accuracy, latency, and token cost.

Result: Diagnostic tool recall 98–99%; precision 92–96%. Matches or exceeds cloud-only baselines on clinical accuracy. Bandwidth-invariant latency of 25–35 seconds with 4–15x lower token cost across profiles.

Conclusion: Architectural design is key for efficient multimodal integration and improved factual grounding compared with cloud-only approaches under deployment constraints.

Abstract: Medical AI has demonstrated specialist-level diagnostic accuracy, yet these capabilities remain largely inaccessible in resource-constrained rural settings where bandwidth is scarce, compute is limited, and clinical decision-making requires integrating heterogeneous modalities. We introduce a cloud--edge collaborative architecture that addresses these constraints: lightweight, domain-specific models on the edge transform raw medical data into compact structured outputs, while a cloud LLM synthesizes these outputs into clinical summaries. An LLM-based orchestrator dynamically selects diagnostic tools based on patient context, promoting comprehensive modality coverage without processing irrelevant inputs. We evaluate on 20 multimodal clinical cases spanning cardiac, obstetric, trauma, and screening scenarios under three simulated network profiles (500,kbps--5,Mbps). The hybrid system achieves 98--99% diagnostic tool recall with 92--96% precision, matches or exceeds cloud-only baselines on clinical accuracy, and maintains bandwidth-invariant latency (25--35,s) at 4--15x lower token cost. These results highlight the role of architectural design in enabling efficient multimodal integration and improving factual grounding compared to cloud-only approaches under deployment constraints.

</details>
