### [123] [Decentralized Multi-Player Q-Learning in Episodic Markov Decision Processes with Information Asymmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12753)
*Larissa Xu,King Bi,William Chang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Decentralized multi-player RL in episodic tabular MDPs under three information asymmetries; introduces mQ-learning, mQ-learning-intervals, mEXC, and mEXC-Bellman; achieves regret bounds matching single-agent Q-learning up to log factors; exponential scaling of joint action space limits practicality to small M or per-player action sizes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Characterize the performance of decentralized reinforcement learning without inter-player communication under information asymmetries, and provide algorithms with provable regret guarantees that approach centralized performance.

Method: For problems A and B: propose mQ-learning and mQ-learning-intervals with regret ~ Õ(√(H^4 S A_joint T)). For problem C: propose two-phase explore-then-commit algorithms mEXC and mEXC-Bellman with regret ~ Õ(H (S A_joint)^{1/3} T^{2/3}). Compare to centralized joint-action benchmarks.

Result: Regret bounds derived: ṫODO(√(H^4 S A_joint T)) for A/B; ṫODO(H (S A_joint)^{1/3} T^{2/3}) for C. Decentralized performance matches single-agent Q-learning rate up to logs. Highlight exponential growth of A_joint with the number of players makes results most relevant for small M or small per-player actions.

Conclusion: Decentralized learning under information asymmetry can attain regret rates comparable to single-agent Q-learning, but practical relevance is limited by the joint action space’s exponential growth; the proposed algorithms adapt to different information asymmetries and learning phases.

Abstract: We study decentralized multi-player reinforcement learning in episodic tabular Markov decision processes (MDPs) under three forms of information asymmetry: (A) unobserved actions with common rewards, (B) observed actions with independent rewards, and (C) unobserved actions with independent rewards. Players cannot communicate during learning but may agree on a protocol a priori. For Problems A and B we propose \texttt{mQ-learning} and \texttt{mQ-learning-intervals}, achieving $\tilde{O}(\sqrt{H^4 S A_{\text{joint}}\, T})$ regret, where $H$ is the horizon, $S$ the state count, $T = KH$ the total steps, and $A_{\text{joint}} = \prod_{i=1}^M |\mathcal{A}_i|$ the joint action space across $M$ players. For Problem C we give \texttt{mEXC} and \texttt{mEXC-Bellman}, two-phase explore-then-commit algorithms with regret $\tilde{O}(H (S A_{\text{joint}})^{1/3} T^{2/3})$. Against the centralized joint-action benchmark, decentralized learning under information asymmetry matches the single-agent Q-learning rate of \cite{jin2018q} up to logarithmic factors. Because $A_{\text{joint}}$ grows exponentially in $M$, the bounds are most meaningful for small $M$ or small per-player action sets.

</details>


### [124] [Beyond Outcome Rewards: Step-Level Self-Distilled Policy Optimization for Deep Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12764)
*Haoze Wu,Chuqiao Kuang,Tianyi Zhuang,Xiaoguang Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces Evidence Anchors and Step-Level Self-Distilled Policy Optimization (SSPO) to resolve information asymmetry in deep search agents; improves credit assignment by step-level weighting on incorrect trajectories, yielding better performance with modest compute.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep search agents suffer from extremely sparse feedback on long trajectories; naive distillation from privileged teacher information can leak answer paths and harm learning. The work aims to align teacher signals with the student's exploration to improve credit assignment without collapsing diversity.

Method: Two contributions: (1) Evidence Anchors—concise, step-level evidence snippets sourced from the web as privileged information capturing key reasoning steps without revealing full answer paths; (2) Step-Level Self-Distilled Policy Optimization (SSPO)—transforms teacher-student disagreement into step-level advantage weights within GRPO, applied only to incorrect trajectories; decouples update direction (driven by outcome reward) from update magnitude (modulated by the teacher).

Result: Empirical results on Qwen3-8B show SSPO consistently outperforming GRPO across BrowseComp, GAIA, and FRAMES; it matches or surpasses GRPO trained with twice as many gradient steps while adding only about 5% overhead per step due to a single extra forward pass.

Conclusion: The approach mitigates the information-availability gap between privileged teacher signals and exploration-based student reasoning, enabling more effective credit assignment in long-horizon search with efficient training overhead; Evidence Anchors provide bounded privileged information and SSPO yields robust step-level policy updates.

Abstract: Deep search agents operate over trajectories spanning dozens of steps, yet standard reinforcement learning provides only a single outcome reward per trajectory, which is far too sparse for effective credit assignment. On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) addresses this by using the model's own logits as dense token-level teachers, but extending it to search agents introduces a fundamental tension: the teacher, having access to privileged information such as the correct answer, produces a distribution that differs systematically from the student's exploration-based reasoning, and naive distillation causes the student to inherit this information asymmetry rather than learn better search strategies. We resolve this tension through two contributions. First, we construct Evidence Anchors, which are concise, step-level evidence snippets extracted from the web, as privileged information that captures key reasoning steps without revealing the entire answer path. Second, we propose Step-Level Self-Distilled Policy Optimization (SSPO), which converts teacher-student disagreement into step-level advantage weights within GRPO, applied exclusively to incorrect trajectories. This design decouples what to update from how much to update: the outcome reward determines the direction of policy change, while the teacher modulates its magnitude at each step. Correct trajectories are left untouched, preserving their diversity. On Qwen3-8B, SSPO consistently outperforms GRPO across BrowseComp, GAIA, and FRAMES, surpassing or matching GRPO trained with twice as many gradient steps while adding only about 5 percent overhead per step from a single additional forward pass.

</details>


### [125] [CoMedBench: A Multi-Source Benchmark of Synthetic Medical Data Fidelity and Downstream Utility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12805)
*Akanta Das,Al Amin Farhad,Mrinmoy Sarkar Anto,David Rehkopf,Ayin Vala,Tanmoy Sarkar Pias*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A reproducible benchmark (CoMedBench) evaluates a family of synthetic-data generators for healthcare ML across 37 dataset-task pairs, assessing both statistical fidelity and downstream task utility; results show substantial retention of predictive signal, especially for tabular data (AUROC ~90-97%), while temporal ICU data are more generator-sensitive (CTGAN ~82% AUROC, 64% AUPRC; TVAE ~95% AUROC).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Access to clinical data for healthcare ML is restricted by privacy, IRB, and re-use constraints. Synthetic data offers a privacy-preserving alternative that may retain useful statistical and clinical structure. There is a need to systematically evaluate when synthetic data preserves task-critical signal across diverse datasets and tasks.

Method: Introduce CoMedBench, a reproducible benchmark with a common training/evaluation engine, evaluating a family of generators on static tabular and temporal ICU tasks across seven public data sources (MIMIC-III, MIMIC-IV, eICU, UCI, BRFSS, NHANES, pycox survival datasets). The benchmark uses 37 dataset-task pairs and measures both statistical fidelity and task utility by training/testing models on real vs synthetic data and comparing performance.

Result: Synthetic training data preserves substantial downstream signal: tabular tasks show a synthetic-to-real AUROC ratio of 90.6% on average, rising to 97.3% for CoMed-TVAE. Temporal ICU tasks are more generator-sensitive: CoMed-CTGAN attains 81.6% AUROC and 64.0% AUPRC; CoMed-TVAE attains ~95% AUROC.

Conclusion: CoMedBench provides a reproducible framework to evaluate synthetic data generators for healthcare ML. Overall, synthetic data retains much of the downstream signal, particularly for tabular data, while temporal data pose greater challenges and depend on the generator; CoMed-TVAE is among the strongest performers.

Abstract: Access to clinical data is essential for developing reliable healthcare machine learning systems, but direct use of electronic health records is constrained by privacy regulation, institutional review, data-use agreements, and the risk of re-identification. Synthetic data promises a practical alternative: it can preserve useful statistical and clinical structure while reducing exposure of sensitive patient records. Prior studies often evaluate a single generator, one dataset, or a narrow downstream task, making it difficult to know when synthetic data can support model development and when it fails to preserve task-critical signal. We introduce CoMedBench, a reproducible benchmark that evaluates a family of generators under a common clinical-validity framework and one shared training and evaluation engine, spanning static tabular and temporal downstream tasks on established critical-care datasets. In total the benchmark spans 37 dataset-task pairs across two modalities consists of 20 static tabular and 17 temporal ICU time-series-drawn from seven public data sources: three intensive-care databases (MIMIC-III, MIMIC-IV, and eICU) together with the UCI Machine Learning Repository, the CDC BRFSS diabetes cohort (2015), NHANES (1999-2014), and the pycox survival datasets (GBSG and METABRIC). The benchmark evaluates both statistical fidelity and task utility by comparing models trained and tested across real and synthetic data. In these settings, synthetic training data preserves most of the downstream signal: on tabular tasks the reference generator CoMed-CTGAN retains a mean AUROC utility (the synthetic-to-real performance ratio) of 90.6%, rising to 97.3% for the strongest generator, CoMed-TVAE. Temporal ICU tasks are harder and more generator-sensitive: CoMed-CTGAN retains 81.6% (AUROC) and only 64.0% under the imbalance-sensitive AUPRC, whereas CoMed-TVAE still retains ~95% (AUROC).

</details>


### [126] [HiRoute: Hierarchical Routed Prompt Tuning for Safety Alignment of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12821)
*Fangzhou Chen,Shiji Zhao,Mengyang Wang,Qihui Zhu,Ranjie Duan,Maoxun Yuan,Xingxing Wei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HiRoute introduces an input-adaptive hierarchical prompt-tuning framework that decouples category-agnostic safety from category-specific response guidance, using a frozen LLM-based router and a mix of prompt experts to tailor responses by risk level.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static, global safety prompts struggle to maintain a cross-category safety boundary while remaining helpful and non-refusable for benign inputs. There is a need for input-adaptive, risk-aware safety control that can generalize across diverse harmful intents without sacrificing general performance.

Method: Train a lightweight hierarchical router on frozen LLM representations to jointly detect harmful intent and assign multi-label risk scores. Freeze backbone and router; perform preference optimization with alternating gradient updates to learn a shared coarse prompt and multiple fine-grained prompt experts as continuous embeddings. At inference, benign inputs bypass safety; risky inputs use the shared prompt plus a router-weighted mixture of risk-specific prompt experts.

Result: Across three instruction-tuned models, HiRoute achieves high safety rates on multiple benchmarks while preserving helpfulness and general-task performance, reducing over-refusal compared to static prompts.

Conclusion: Separating safety control from response guidance via an input-adaptive, hierarchical prompt-tuning architecture enables effective, risk-aware generation without sacrificing utility.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) remain vulnerable to harmful requests and jailbreak attacks. Parameter-efficient safety alignment methods based on prompt tuning typically rely on a single global prompt or externally selected prompt modules. Such static designs struggle to maintain a cross-category safety boundary while generating constructive responses tailored to specific risks and avoiding over-refusal of benign inputs. To address these limitations, we propose HiRoute, an input-adaptive hierarchical prompt-tuning framework that separates category-agnostic safety control from category-specific response guidance. HiRoute first trains a lightweight hierarchical router on representations extracted from a frozen LLM to jointly detect harmful intent and predict multi-label risk scores. It then freezes both the backbone model and the router and uses preference optimization with alternating gradient updates to learn a shared coarse-grained prompt and a set of fine-grained prompt experts as continuous embeddings. At inference time, benign inputs bypass the safety branch, whereas risky inputs are processed using the shared prompt together with a router-weighted mixture of risk-specific prompt experts. Experiments across three instruction-tuned models show that HiRoute achieves high safety rates across multiple safety benchmarks while preserving safe-response helpfulness, reducing over-refusal, and maintaining competitive performance on general-purpose tasks.

</details>


### [127] [Fast A/B/n Testing: Exact Multi-Policy Comparison via Tree-Coupled Feedback Sharing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12831)
*Yuxiao Wen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Tree-Coupled A/B Testing (TCAB) enables exact feedback sharing across multiple history-dependent contextual-bandit policies, achieving near-linear reward queries instead of JT; with optimality properties and favorable variance bounds, demonstrated experimentally on model evaluation, language-model evaluation, and adaptive search.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce the reward query cost in evaluating many adaptive policies (J policies, horizon T) without sacrificing their individual finite-horizon laws.

Method: Construct a predictable tree of policy histories; apply maximal coupling on parent-child context-action laws; share one reward per matched tree edge; track mismatches D_e,t to bound total rewards N(T) = T + sum D_e,t; analyze edge-local optimality and MST myopic optimality; derive sublinear pseudo-regret and E[N(T)] = T + o(T); provide variance bounds.

Result: N(T) is T plus cumulative edge variation; conditional optimality among edge-local designs; sublinear regret and almost-sure oracle action; finite-sample variance bounds; empirical experiments show improved cost-precision tradeoffs.

Conclusion: TCAB achieves cost-efficient, exact feedback-sharing across multiple adaptive policies, with provable efficiency and strong empirical gains; applicable to reward-model evaluation, language-model evaluation, and adaptive search.

Abstract: Online platforms increasingly compare many adaptive decision policies---ranking systems, recommendation algorithms, pricing rules, and language-model agents---while each reward-bearing interaction can be costly or risky. A direct A/B/n design gives each of $J$ policies its own horizon-$T$ trajectory and therefore uses $JT$ outcomes. We introduce Tree-Coupled A/B Testing (\TCAB), an exact feedback-sharing design for arbitrary history-dependent contextual-bandit policies. At each round, a predictable tree connects the current policy histories; every parent--child context--action law is maximally coupled, and one reward is shared within each component of matched tree edges. Every policy retains exactly its standalone finite-horizon trajectory law, even though the policies are deliberately dependent. If $D_{e,t}$ records a mismatch on tree edge $e$ at round $t$, the number of reward queries satisfies the pathwise identity $N(T)=T+\sum_{t,e}D_{e,t}$ and hence equals $T$ plus cumulative tree-edge total variation in expectation. This cost is conditionally optimal among exact edge-local designs on the selected tree, and a current-round minimum-spanning tree is myopically optimal among tree designs. For fixed $J$, sublinear pseudo-regret of every policy and almost-sure uniqueness of the oracle action imply $\mathbb{E}[N(T)]=T+o(T)$, versus $JT$ for independent runs. We also obtain finite-sample variance bounds for pairwise policy contrasts. Experiments on reward-model evaluation, multiple-choice language-model evaluation, and adaptive search policies demonstrate substantial improvements in the cost--precision frontier.

</details>


### [128] [A Compositional Theory of Curvature in Probabilistic Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12869)
*Hrithik Suresh,Sahil Sidheekh,Shelar Parth Vijay,Yasir Z,Sriraam Natarajan,Narayanan Chatapuram Krishnan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Global sharpness regularization is mis-specified for probabilistic circuits (PCs) due to their compositional curvature. The Hessian trace decomposes per sum node into a circuit flow (usage) and a local sharpness term, explaining depth-bias and potential underfitting. An adaptive, node-wise regularizer is proposed that preserves EM updates and targets intrinsic local curvature, matching PCs’ structure and improving generalization while retaining sharpness-aware benefits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the mismatch between global sharpness regularization and the inherently compositional curvature of probabilistic circuits, aiming to improve generalization without sacrificing tractable learning or robustness.

Method: Derive an exact factorization of the Hessian trace for PCs: each sum node contributes as the product of circuit flow and a local sharpness term. Analyze how global regularization induces depth bias and underfitting. Propose an adaptive sharpness-aware regularizer that penalizes nodes using intrinsic local curvature, while preserving closed-form EM updates.

Result: The decomposition clarifies why global sharpness regularization biases toward depth and can hamper fit. The adaptive regularizer yields comparable or improved generalization to global regularization, while maintaining the benefits of sharpness-aware learning and compatibility with EM updates beyond global approaches.

Conclusion: PC-specific, local curvature-aware regularization aligns with the circuit’s compositional structure, avoids underfitting caused by global penalties, preserves EM tractability, and improves generalization and robustness in practice.

Abstract: Probabilistic Circuits (PCs) are generative models that support exact inference and, unlike deep neural networks, admit an exact and tractable measure of loss-surface curvature: the trace of the Hessian of the log-likelihood. Recent work regularizes this trace globally to bias learning toward flatter, better generalizing optima. We show that treating sharpness as a global regularizer can be misspecified for PCs, whose curvature is inherently compositional. We prove that each sum node's contribution to the Hessian trace factorizes exactly into its circuit flow, which measures how heavily the node is used, and a local sharpness term determined by its output distribution. This decomposition provides insights into why global sharpness regularization is depth biased and can lead to underfitting. Building on it, we introduce an adaptive sharpness aware regularizer that penalizes nodes based on intrinsic local curvature and preserves closed form EM updates. We also show that empirically, this targeted regularization recovers the generalization that global regularization sacrifices while retaining the robustness and benefits of sharpness aware learning.

</details>


### [129] [Sustaining Plasticity via Learnable Wavelet Activations in Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12874)
*Zeyang Zhang,Tieliang Gong,Junyan Lu,Weizhan Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A learnable wavelet activation with dynamic injection counteracts spectral bias and preserves plasticity in continual learning, backed by theory and strong empirical results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Plasticity loss and spectral bias hinder sequential task learning in continual learning. Fixed activations bias toward low-frequency variations, while unconstrained learnable variants cause forgetting. A hybrid, learnable mechanism is needed to balance plasticity and stability.

Method: Introduce a learnable wavelet activation that splits activation into low- and high-frequency components. Use dynamic wavelet injection to boost plasticity for new tasks, with a regularization strategy to preserve previous knowledge. Provide mathematical guarantees: hybrid wavelet necessity for efficient L2 approximation and a decoupled learning-rate mechanism to restore high-frequency plasticity. Derive a loss-driven injection trigger mechanism to guide injections.

Result: The framework yields theoretical guarantees on representational efficiency and plasticity preservation. Empirically, it maintains trainability and generalization across continual learning tasks and achieves state-of-the-art performance on diverse benchmarks.

Conclusion: A hybrid, learnable wavelet activation with loss-driven dynamic injection effectively mitigates spectral bias and plasticity loss, enabling robust continual learning with strong theoretical and empirical support.

Abstract: Plasticity loss has emerged as a critical challenge in continual learning that significantly hinders the acquisition of sequential tasks. While optimizing activation designs offers a potential solution, current fixed-form functions suffer from an inherent spectral bias towards low-frequency variations, whereas learnable variants permit unconstrained updates that induce catastrophic forgetting. To address these limitations, we propose a novel learnable wavelet activation that decomposes the activation function into low-frequency and high-frequency components to explicitly counter spectral bias. Furthermore, we employ dynamic wavelet injection to adaptively enhance plasticity for new tasks, alongside a regularization strategy to ensure the stability of previous learned knowledge. Theoretically, we provide rigorous mathematical guarantees for the proposed framework, proving the structural necessity of the hybrid wavelet architecture for efficient $L^2$ approximation and demonstrating that the decoupled learning rate mechanism successfully restores network plasticity for high-frequency information. Additionally, we provide a formal derivation of the loss-driven injection trigger mechanism to precisely guide the injection. Extensive empirical evaluations demonstrate that our approach maintains superior trainability and generalization throughout the learning process and achieves state-of-the-art performance across diverse continual learning benchmarks.

</details>


### [130] [Robust data-driven discovery of fractional differential equations via weak formulations and Pareto-based subset selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12879)
*Pongpisit Thanasutives,Yoshinobu Kawahara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A robust framework (Weak-Pareto) for discovering fractional PDEs from noisy data using adjoint-consistent weak formulations, Pareto-based structure selection, and a continuous-order search, yielding parsimonious, noise-robust models with improved efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fractional PDEs model nonlocal dynamics; noise in data and unknown derivative orders hinder discovery; need a method that mitigates noise amplification and determines operator terms and fractional orders automatically.

Method: Use adjoint-consistent weak formulation to transfer fractional operators to smooth test functions; for linear RHS terms, reduces to smoothing integrals; nonlinear terms partially robust; coefficients via ridge regression; search over fractional orders via branch-aware differential evolution; select model complexity by validation-error vs elbow; analyze variance under grid refinement; compare across fractional advection-diffusion, reaction-diffusion, and Burgers benchmarks; ablations show contributions of weak library and continuous-order Pareto search.

Result: Recovered parsimonious operators on fractional advection-diffusion, reaction-diffusion, and Burgers benchmarks even with noise; preserves correct support under multiplicative noise and additive Gaussian noise; weak library essential for robustness; continuous-order Pareto search avoids pitfalls of fixed dense dictionary; lower runtime compared to contemporary neural baselines in advection-diffusion benchmark.

Conclusion: Weak-Pareto delivers robust, efficient fractional PDE discovery from noisy data, leveraging weak formulations and Pareto-driven order selection to achieve parsimonious models with strong noise resilience.

Abstract: Fractional partial differential equations describe nonlocal dynamics, but discovering them from noisy data is difficult because fractional differentiation amplifies high-frequency measurement noise and the derivative orders are unknown. We propose Weak-Pareto, which combines an adjoint-consistent weak formulation of fractional terms with Pareto-based subset selection over discrete term types and continuous fractional orders. For linear right-hand-side terms, the adjoint transfers fractional operators from measured fields to smooth test functions, replacing noise-sensitive pointwise differentiation with smoothing integration; for nonlinear terms, the noise-suppression effect is partial yet useful. Coefficients are fitted by ridge regression within a branch-aware differential-evolution search over the orders. The support size is then selected at the validation-error-complexity elbow. We show that the variance of fixed linear right-hand-side weak features vanishes under grid refinement, whereas noise amplification in pointwise fractional features increases with derivative order. Across fractional advection-diffusion, reaction-diffusion, and Burgers benchmarks, Weak-Pareto recovers parsimonious structures from clean and noisy measurements. In controlled advection-diffusion and Burgers comparisons, it retains the correct support at every tested multiplicative-noise level, whereas the unregularised strong-form counterpart largely fails once noise is introduced; this advantage persists under additive Gaussian noise. Ablations show that the weak library drives noise robustness and that continuous-order Pareto search avoids the support-selection failure of a dense fixed dictionary. On the advection-diffusion benchmark, Weak-Pareto yields more consistent operator recovery and substantially lower measured runtime than a contemporary neural baseline.

</details>


### [131] [Adaptive $k$ Nearest Neighbors Classifier via Granular Ball Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12903)
*Xiaoyu Lian,Shuyin Xia,Hongxuan He,Lifeng Shen,Guoyin Wang,Xinbo Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Adaptive granular-ball KNN (GB-KNN) with a two-stage coarse-to-fine partitioning and Fisher criterion for multi-granularity representation; dynamic k selection via adaptive neighborhood; improved accuracy and efficiency; open-source.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Choosing the right k in KNN is crucial and often causes performance degradation; existing methods may be sensitive to data distribution and noise. The authors aim to reduce complexity and enhance robustness by representing data with granular balls and by adapting the neighborhood size during prediction.

Method: Training: partition the data into granular balls at multiple scales using a coarse-to-fine strategy; apply the Fisher criterion to guide splitting and stopping, forming a multi-granularity representation. Prediction: locate the nearest granular ball using a weighted distance; construct an adaptive neighborhood around the test sample within that ball; set the effective k to the number of samples in the neighborhood. The neighborhood induced by the nearest granular ball is intended to provide more stable local information and robustness to noise.

Result: Experiments show the proposed method outperforms existing KNN variants in both accuracy and efficiency across multiple datasets. The authors provide open-source code at the linked GitHub repository.

Conclusion: Granular-ball based adaptive KNN yields robust, scalable performance with automatic k selection and enhanced local stability, making it competitive for noisy or complex data and practical for diverse datasets.

Abstract: The $k$-Nearest Neighbor~(KNN) algorithm is widely used across various tasks. The selection of the $k$ value is a key issue because it significantly impacts performance. In this paper, an adaptive and efficient KNN approach via granular-ball computing is proposed. The method consists of two stages. \textcolor{black}{In the training stage, the dataset is first coarsely partitioned to reduce the complexity of data distributions within a granular ball, and then the Fisher criterion is introduced to control ball splitting and stopping, yielding a multi-granularity granular ball representation. In the prediction stage, the nearest granular ball is first located through a weighted distance mechanism, and an adaptive neighborhood is then constructed around the test sample. The effective $k$ value is dynamically determined by the actual number of samples contained in this neighborhood. The neighborhood induced by the nearest granular ball provides more stable local group information, thereby improving robustness against noise and local perturbations.} Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms existing KNN variants across multiple datasets in terms of both accuracy and efficiency. The code has been open-sourced for reproducibility: https://github.com/lianxiaoyu724/Adaptive-GBKNN.

</details>


### [132] [EGRL: Edge generation-guided relation-aware learning for RNA-protein interaction prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12906)
*Danyu Li,Ling Zhou,Rubing Huang,Xian Zhong,Bin Zou,Kui Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Edge Generation-guided Relation-aware Learning (EGRL) for RNA-Protein Interactions; uses implicit meta-paths, relation-aware attention, a graph generator for soft edges to aid cold-start, and a multi-feature predictor; achieves strong cold-start generalization (AUROC 0.867, AUPR 0.861) and competitive overall performance on four benchmarks; code coming soon.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: address data sparsity and cold-start problems in RPIP, where traditional GNNs rely on homogeneous graphs or handcrafted meta-paths, limiting generalization to unknown molecules.

Method: propose EGRL with components: (i) implicit meta-path learning to capture relational semantics; (ii) multi-relation-aware attention for adaptive fusion of interaction patterns; (iii) graph generator predicting potential soft edges to support cold-start nodes; (iv) multi-feature fusion predictor for final interaction scoring; jointly trained with primary task loss and auxiliary generator loss.

Result: evaluated on four benchmark datasets; achieves competitive overall performance; superior generalization in cold-start settings with AUROC 0.867 and AUPR 0.861 on unknown molecules, improvements of 8.6% AUROC and 5.0% AUPR over prior SOTA.

Conclusion: EGRL demonstrates enhanced cold-start generalization in RPIP while maintaining competitive performance; code will be released soon.

Abstract: RNA-Protein Interactions (RPIs) are critical for regulating cellular functions. While traditional wet-lab experiments for RPI detection are costly and time-consuming, Deep Learning (DL) methods provide an efficient computational alternative for RPI Prediction (RPIP). In particular, Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) are promising, as they naturally model RPI networks. However, existing GNN-based methods often rely on homogeneous graphs or predefined meta-paths, which limit their ability to handle data sparsity and to generalize to cold-start scenarios involving unknown molecules. To address these limitations, we propose Edge Generation-guided Relation-aware Learning (EGRL), a novel framework with several key components: implicit meta-path learning to capture relational semantics without handcrafted paths; a multi-relation-aware attention mechanism for adaptive fusion of interaction patterns; a graph generator that predicts potential ("soft") edges to support cold-start nodes; and a multi-feature fusion predictor for final interaction scoring. EGRL is jointly trained with a primary task loss and an auxiliary generator loss. Comprehensive evaluations on four benchmark datasets demonstrate that EGRL achieves competitive overall performance. More importantly, it exhibits superior generalization in cold-start settings, achieving an Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic curve (AUROC) of 0.867 and an Area Under the Precision-Recall curve (AUPR) of 0.861 on unknown molecules, corresponding to improvements of 8.6% in AUROC and 5.0% in AUPR over prior state-of-the-art methods. The code will be released soon.

</details>


### [133] [Revisiting Overestimation Bias Problem of Q-learning: Settling Large Discrete Action Space via Action Intersection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12912)
*Pu Li,Tao Tan,Hong Xie,Xiaoyu Shi,Mingsheng Shang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Action intersection between two Q-functions balances coupling and decoupling biases in Q-learning with large action spaces, enabling a tunable semi-decoupling strategy that improves performance in both tabular and deep RL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In large action spaces, randomness in Q-value estimation causes overestimation bias (coupling paradigm) or underestimation bias (decoupling paradigm). A simple mechanism is needed to control and mitigate these biases.

Method: Introduce an action intersection strategy where two Q-functions share a fraction of trajectory data. If a data sample is shared, update using the coupling paradigm; otherwise, update using the decoupling paradigm. The shared fraction controls bias range and granularity, and the intersection can be made arbitrarily fine.

Result: The method achieves a large bias range from underestimation to overestimation by varying data sharing. Deep RL experiments show drastic outperformance of SOTA baselines; tabular experiments provide insight into the method’s effectiveness.

Conclusion: Action intersection provides a simple, powerful semi-decoupling mechanism to relieve overestimation bias in large action spaces, with tunable bias and fine granularity, effective in both tabular and deep RL settings.

Abstract: This paper considers the overestimation bias problem of Q-learning in the setting of a large action space, for the purpose of relieving the bottleneck of existing methods. We find that the large action space increases the randomness in Q-value estimation. The randomness makes two paradigms that drive the major literature on the overestimation problem have their own bottlenecks: the coupling paradigm, i.e., the optimal action and its Q-value are estimated with the same Q-function, always has a positive bias. This is because randomness leads to some actions having abnormally high estimated values than their true values, and the coupling methods prefer these actions. The decoupling paradigm, i.e., the optimal action and its Q-value are estimated with two independent Q-functions, always has a negative bias. This is because randomness increases the estimation gap between the two independent Q-tables for the same action. This paper shows that action intersection can be a simple yet powerful strategy to relieve these bottlenecks. The action intersection strategy enables semi-decoupling via two designs: (1) it allows two Q-functions to share a certain fraction of trajectory data; (2) if a data sample is shared, each Q-function is updated using the coupling paradigm; otherwise, using the decoupling paradigm. Two properties make the action intersection strategy powerful: (1) attaining a large bias range, i.e., varying the data sharing fraction, the estimation bias varies from underestimating to overestimating; (2) fine granularity: the action intersection size can be made arbitrarily finer to enable finer control. We consider two experiment settings, i.e., tabular and deep RL, deep RL experiments show that our method outperforms several SOTA baselines drastically; tabular experiments reveal why our method can achieve superior performance.

</details>


### [134] [Momentum as Residual-Driven Multiplier Correction for Deep Learning Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12925)
*Zhixin Ren,Yau Lyu,Congrong Li,Liping Zhang,Shengbo Eben Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AIM provides an ADMM-inspired, momentum-based framework that separates update geometry (via residual penalties) from acceleration (via subproblem approximation), by interpreting momentum as a multiplier-like correction; RADAR extends AIM with relativistic adaptive geometry, decoupled residual correction, and second-order momentum filtering, and proves stochastic convergence with Lyapunov analysis, showing consistent improvements across vision, language, and RL tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To demystify momentum dynamics in modern optimizers by decoupling geometry and acceleration mechanisms, and to design algorithms with improved update directions and momentum estimates.

Method: Introduce AIM: an ADMM-inspired momentum mechanism using residual-penalty variable splitting; interpret momentum as a multiplier-type correction from the splitting residual; geometry set by residual penalties, acceleration by subproblem approximation. Build RADAR by incorporating relativistic adaptive geometry, decoupled residual correction, and second-order momentum filtering; establish stochastic convergence via variance-perturbed Lyapunov drift analysis.

Result: The framework yields a theoretically grounded interpretation of momentum and a new optimizer RADAR that achieves consistent improvements over strong adaptive baselines across supervised vision, language modeling, and reinforcement learning.

Conclusion: AIM clarifies how momentum’s geometry and acceleration components interact; RADAR leverages this separation to improve update direction and momentum estimation, supported by convergence theory and diverse empirical gains.

Abstract: Momentum-based optimizers are widely used in modern deep learning, yet the relations among momentum recursion, update geometry, and acceleration remain only partially understood. We develop an $\textbf{A}$DMM-$\textbf{I}$nspired $\textbf{M}$omentum (AIM) framework based on residual-penalty variable splitting, which interprets momentum as a multiplier-like correction driven by the splitting residual. AIM recovers the exponential moving average of gradients from an ADMM-style multiplier update and separates two mechanisms that are usually intertwined in practical optimizers: the residual penalty determines the update geometry, whereas the approximation of the objective-related subproblem determines the acceleration form. Building on AIM, we propose $\textbf{R}$elativistic $\textbf{A}$daptive gradient $\textbf{D}$escent with $\textbf{A}$ccelerated $\textbf{R}$esidual (RADAR), which combines relativistic adaptive geometry, decoupled residual correction, and second-order momentum filtering to improve the update direction and momentum estimation. We establish stochastic convergence through a variance-perturbed Lyapunov drift analysis. Experiments on supervised vision learning, language modeling, and reinforcement learning show that RADAR achieves consistent improvements over strong adaptive optimizer baselines.

</details>


### [135] [H-VAEP and H-xT: Valuing Offensive On-the-Ball Actions in Handball by Estimating Probabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12926)
*Julius Broermann,Oliver Müller,Michael Döring,Jochen Baumeister*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: First comprehensive adaptation of xT and VAEP to handball (Handball-xT and Handball-VAEP) using five seasons of tracking data; introduces court-native zoning, demonstrates robustness over grid-based approaches; optimizes VAEP feature space and context to reduce team-identity leakage; yields stable, discriminative player ratings focused on build-up play; code repository released for clubs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional handball evaluation relies on basic metrics and heuristics and fails to credit multi-player build-up; extends successful football analytics (xT, VAEP) to handball to provide principled, event-based valuations and improve player assessment.

Method: Use five seasons of tracking-derived event data from the Handball Bundesliga. Develop Handball-xT (H-xT) with a handball-native court zoning layout and show via simulations that it is more robust than standard rectangular grids. Optimize Handball-VAEP (H-VAEP) by tailoring its feature space and selecting context length to minimize team-identity leakage.

Result: H-xT is shown to be systematically more robust than rectangular grids. H-VAEP yields stable, discriminative, and intuitive player ratings that emphasize build-up play.

Conclusion: Release the complete code repository to enable professional clubs to deploy these models, enabling principled event-based evaluation in handball.

Abstract: Traditional player evaluation in professional handball relies on basic box-score metrics or heuristic indices, which fail to credit the multi-player build-up chain. While football (soccer) analytics has adopted Expected Threat (xT) and Valuing Actions by Estimating Probabilities (VAEP), these event-based action valuation frameworks have not yet been adapted to handball. In this paper, we present the first comprehensive adaptation and evaluation of xT and VAEP for handball, utilizing five seasons of tracking-derived event data from the Handball Bundesliga. We develop Handball-xT (H-xT) using a handball-native court zoning layout, demonstrating via simulations that it is systematically more robust than standard rectangular grids. We optimize Handball-VAEP (H-VAEP) by tailoring its feature space and selecting the context length to limit team-identity leakage. Our evaluation shows that H-VAEP yields exceptionally stable, discriminative, and intuitive player ratings that highlight build-up play. Finally, we release our complete code repository to help professional clubs deploy these models.

</details>


### [136] [Multi-perspective Imbalance-Conscious 6G Beamforming Optimization and Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12929)
*Chukwunonso Henry Nwokoye,Blessing Oluchi Iloka,Chikwue V. Umeugoji,Christopher Anene Egemba,Nnenna D. Duroha*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Systematic ML study on 6G-IoT beamforming optimization (6GBO) comparing supervised and unsupervised approaches across feature groups; network features strongest for prediction; environment and device type drive clustering; explainability highlights bandwidth/sensors/mobility; future work includes deep and reinforcement learning for throughput/latency and SNR-based rewards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address how diverse feature groups influence supervised prediction and unsupervised clustering in 6G-IoT beamforming, and to identify key drivers of performance and clustering structure, with attention to data imbalance and explainability.

Method: Systematic evaluation using supervised learning with imbalance-aware experiments across four feature groups (network, environmental, device, vision); unsupervised clustering (K-means, DBSCAN, hierarchical) assessed via Elbow, Silhouette, Davies-Bouldin; explainability analysis for global feature importance.

Result: Network features outperform other groups in predictive power (higher recall, F1-score, ROC-AUC) under imbalance; clustering mostly shaped by deployment environment and device type rather than mobility; bandwidth, IoT sensors, and mobility show higher global feature importance.

Conclusion: ML approaches are promising for 6GBO, with network features as primary predictors and environmental/device context shaping clustering; future work to integrate deep learning and reinforcement learning to predict throughput/latency or optimize SNR-based rewards.

Abstract: The study presents a systematic machine learning (ML) study of 6G-IoT beamforming optimization (6GBO) using supervised and unsupervised approaches. We compared the predictive power of network, environmental, device, and vision feature groups for 6GBO. Additionally, it addressed other unsupervised perspectives that can enhance 6GBO, including clustering network scenarios using methods such as K-means, DBSCAN, and hierarchical clustering. Several imbalance-aware experiments revealed that network features possess better prediction power than device, environmental, and vision feature groups, as evidenced by their recall, F1-score and ROC-AUC values. For unsupervised ML exploration (assessed using Elbow, Silhouette score, and Davies-Bouldin Index methods), the results indicate that the deployment environment and type of device primarily influence clustering, rather than mobility-based attributes. Furthermore, the explainability analysis showed that bandwidth, IoT sensors, and mobility possess higher global feature importance across the feature groups. In the future, we would apply deep and reinforcement learning techniques to predict throughput/latency or to optimize rewards determined by performance indicators like SNR enhancement

</details>


### [137] [Diagnosing JEPA World Models with Action-Conditioned Predictive Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12939)
*Guo An,Zijing Wu,Honghua Dong,Yuhao Yan,Zixuan Gui,Haochong Chen,Shanzhao Ruan,Xiang Wang,Yurong Ling,Qi Tian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Action-Conditioned Predictive Consistency (ACPC) as a diagnostic for joint-embedding predictive architectures (JEPAs) to assess robustness to visual perturbations. Introduces invariance radius (IR) and separation rate (SR) derived from ACPC, provides theoretical bounds linking perturbations to multi-step prediction error and planner cost, and validates the diagnostics across multiple visual control tasks and architectures (LeWM, PLDM).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visual perturbations can alter latent representations in JEPAs and degrade downstream predictions and planning. A rigorous criterion is needed to enforce equivalence of states under action-conditioned consequences, akin to bisimulation.

Method: Define ACPC as the divergence between a clean history and its perturbed view after rolling forward under the same action sequence. Prove that this divergence bounds perturbation-induced changes in multi-step prediction error and planner cost. From pairwise ACPC, derive two metrics: Invariance Radius (IR) for rollout spread and Separation Rate (SR) for whether different states remain distinguishable after rollout. Empirically evaluate on four visual control tasks; test cross-task transfer on LeWM; assess robustness under blur and resize; also report trends for PLDM with a different architecture.

Result: Pairwise ACPC successfully predicts perturbation-induced increases in prediction error and planner cost. On LeWM, IR-SR transfers across tasks; the diagnostic remains informative under blur and resize. PLDM shows similar diagnostic trends under an alternative architecture.

Conclusion: ACPC and its IR/SR instantiations provide practical, architecture-agnostic diagnostics for robustness of world models to visual perturbations, with transferability across tasks and resilience to common visual distortions.

Abstract: Joint-embedding predictive architectures (JEPAs) learn world models that predict in a compact latent space rather than in pixels, reducing the pressure to model nuisance appearance. Yet this provides no guarantee against visual perturbations: they can still alter the encoded representation and affect subsequent action-conditioned predictions. Bisimulation captures this requirement precisely: two observations should be treated as the same state only when their action-conditioned consequences agree. Guided by this criterion, we introduce Action-Conditioned Predictive Consistency (ACPC), a diagnostic that measures how far a clean history and a visually perturbed view of it diverge after being rolled forward under the same action sequence. We prove that this divergence bounds the perturbation-induced change in multi-step prediction error and planner cost. Building on pairwise ACPC, we define two complementary measures: the Invariance Radius (IR) summarizes clean-perturbed rollout spread, while the Separation Rate (SR) checks whether different states remain distinguishable after rollout. Experiments on four visual control tasks show that pairwise ACPC predicts perturbation-induced prediction and cost changes. On LeWM, the IR-SR screen transfers across tasks, and the joint diagnostic remains informative under blur and resize. PLDM exhibits similar diagnostic trends under a different architecture.

</details>


### [138] [CardioState-JEPA: Delay-Aware Cross-Modal Learning of a Shared Cardiac Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12944)
*Hamza Shafiq,Hung Manh Pham,Bin Zhu,Pan Zhou,Jun Hu,Aaqib Saeed*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CardioState-JEPA introduces a single shared representation for ECG, PPG, and PCG via a physiology-aware joint-embedding predictive architecture. It uses a common Transformer encoder and a learned delay aligner to handle temporal offsets, trained first on unimodal data then aligned with paired data. Evaluated on 25 downstream tasks, it substantially improves performance over self-supervised baselines and rivals models with supervised/clinical text signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cardiac foundation models are modality-specific and do not leverage cross-sensor shared physiology. A shared representation across ECG, PPG, and PCG could improve robustness and generalization for downstream cardiac tasks.

Method: A single Transformer encoder processes tokens from ECG, PPG, and PCG projected into a common latent space. Pretraining targets are masked latent cardiac states (predictive of shared physiology) rather than waveform appearance. A learned delay aligner compensates for cardiac-time misalignment between modalities. Training proceeds in two stages: (1) learn within-modality structure from abundant unimodal data; (2) use paired data to align modalities in latent cardiac time. Evaluation uses the frozen encoder across 25 downstream tasks spanning the three modalities.

Result: On 25 downstream tasks, the frozen encoder improves average AUROC by 8.2 points for PPG, 18.8 points for PCG murmur detection, and 15.5 points for ECG, over the best self-supervised baseline; matches or exceeds models trained with privileged clinical text or supervised labels on several ECG benchmarks.

Conclusion: Heterogeneous cardiac signals can mutually supervise a single foundation model of cardiac physiology, enabling cross-modality transfer and robust performance without requiring modality-specific supervision.

Abstract: Electrocardiography (ECG), photoplethysmography (PPG), and phonocardiography (PCG) provide complementary views of the same cardiac cycle, yet existing cardiac foundation models are trained for a single sensing modality, leaving the shared physiology across sensors unexploited. We introduce CardioState-JEPA, a cardiac foundation model to learn a single shared representation jointly across ECG, PPG, and PCG, built on a physiology-aware joint-embedding predictive architecture. The model maps heterogeneous waveforms into a common token space, processes them with a single shared Transformer encoder, and learns by predicting masked latent cardiac states, placing the pretraining target on shared physiology rather than sensor-specific waveform appearance. To handle the temporal offsets between electrical, mechanical, and hemodynamic events, cross-modal prediction uses a learned delay aligner that matches signals at the corresponding cardiac time. Because synchronized multi-sensor recordings are scarce, CardioState-JEPA first learns within-modality structure from abundant unimodal data and then uses paired data to align modalities in latent cardiac time. Evaluated as a frozen encoder across 25 downstream tasks spanning ECG, PPG, and PCG, our encoder improves average PPG classification by 8.2 AUROC points, PCG murmur detection by 18.8 AUROC points, and ECG classification by 15.5 AUROC points over the best self-supervised signal baseline and matches or exceeds cardiac models trained with privileged clinical text or supervised labels on several ECG benchmarks. These results establish that heterogeneous cardiac signals can mutually supervise a single foundation model of cardiac physiology.

</details>


### [139] [I-SDPO: Instance-Level Adaptive Self-Distillation Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12957)
*Yubo Zhang,Xinhong Ma,Zezhong Tan,Ziqiang Dong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: I-SDPO adaptively applies self-distillation only when GRPO’s group-relative rewards are uninformative (all-incorrect groups), and uses standard GRPO otherwise, with a routing rule that decays distillation as success probability rises. This yields state-of-the-art performance on SciKnowEval across four domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address a key failure mode of GRPO: when all proposals in a rollout group are incorrect, relative rewards provide little signal and a biased teacher can hinder reward-driven improvements. Introduce instance-level routing to selectively apply privileged self-distillation to preserve imitation signals without biasing updates when useful reward signals exist.

Method: Propose Instance-Level Adaptive Self-Distillation Policy Optimization (I-SDPO): for each input instance, decide once and share across its rollout group. If the group is all-incorrect, apply privileged self-distillation objective; if any success, retain standard GRPO. Analyze alignment of teacher vs. reward directions and show a nonzero distillation weight creates a floor. Routing reduces distillation weight as success probability grows, removing teacher influence automatically.

Result: Empirical results on SciKnowEval show I-SDPO achieves best results across four scientific domains, increasing average mean@16 accuracy from 56.67% (GRPO) to 70.31%, with a maximum domain gain of 18.24 points.

Conclusion: Adaptive, instance-level routing for self-distillation effectively balances imitation with reward optimization, mitigating bias when the teacher is misleading and leveraging GRPO when reward signals are informative; the approach yields substantial gains on SciKnowEval and provides theoretical insights into teacher-reward alignment.

Abstract: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) learns from reward differences within a rollout group, but receives no useful relative signal when every sampled response is incorrect. Privileged self-distillation can fill this gap with dense token supervision, yet applying it throughout training creates a different failure mode: the teacher is a biased, low-variance surrogate for the reward objective, so persistent imitation can oppose reward-improving updates after the policy becomes capable of producing successful trajectories. We introduce I-SDPO (Instance-Level Adaptive Self-Distillation Policy Optimization), which treats teacher reliance as capability-dependent. I-SDPO makes one routing decision per input instance and shares it across that instance's rollout group: all-incorrect groups use a privileged self-distillation objective, whereas any-success groups remain intact for GRPO. This design uses imitation only where group-relative rewards are uninformative. A local analysis characterizes when teacher and reward directions align and shows that a non-vanishing biased distillation weight induces an optimization bias floor. The routing rule automatically reduces the expected distillation rate as success probability rises, withdrawing teacher influence without a hand-designed schedule. On SciKnowEval, I-SDPO obtains the best result in all four scientific domains and improves average mean@16 accuracy from 56.67% with GRPO to 70.31%, with a maximum domain gain of 18.24 points.

</details>


### [140] [The Objective Is the Bottleneck: Latent World Models Encode What Their Planners Cannot Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12959)
*Joyjeet Singh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Long-horizon planning failures in latent world models arise from the planner’s objective, not predictor quality; changing the objective (without retraining) yields dramatic gains, showing the model learns reachability more than proximity and that latent distance is a mis-specified planning metric.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate why long-horizon planning struggles in latent world models—whether the predictor limits performance or if the planning objective misleads search—and whether the reported prediction error correlates with planning success.

Method: Reproduce LeWorldModel on TwoRoom; evaluate predictor horizon vs planning horizon; use cross-entropy-method planning to minimize squared latent distance; replace only the objective (no retraining) and test gains; assess information content with a ridge probe; compare different heads (frame-separation vs position probe); analyze four checkpoints and varying horizons.

Result: Predicted state 75 steps ahead yields small error (0.189) but the planner imagines up to only 25 steps; cross-entropy planning using squared latent distance yields metric that correlates with true distance at r=0.426 and saturates around ~80 units; planning cost can decrease by moving away from the goal; ridge probe recovers position with R^2 of 0.9922, indicating information is present throughout; replacing the objective (without retraining) raises success from 26% to 98% at offset 100, and 92% with much less computation, showing horizon-dependent performance is due to the objective; a frame-separation head predicts distance worse than a position probe but plans better, indicating learning reachability rather than proximity.

Conclusion: The failure is methodological: the planning objective, not predictor accuracy, limits long-horizon planning. Aligning planning cost with reachability/task success dramatically improves performance, revealing latent space encodes reachability; mis-specified objectives cause planning to ignore actual goals (e.g., crossing walls) despite seemingly accurate predictions.

Abstract: Latent world models are judged by how well they predict, so when planning fails at long horizons the natural reading is that the predictor degrades. On a reproduction of LeWorldModel on TwoRoom we show the binding constraint is the planner's objective instead.
  The predictor is not the limit: its imagined state seventy-five environment steps ahead is still only 0.189 as wrong as assuming the world froze, while the planner never imagines beyond twenty-five. The objective is. Cross-entropy-method planning minimises squared latent distance, which tracks true distance at r = 0.426, saturates by about eighty arena units and decreases beyond a hundred and twenty, so moving away from the goal can lower the cost. The information is present throughout: a ridge probe recovers position from the frozen embedding at R^2 0.9922.
  The pathology is the method's, not one reimplementation's. It is present in the authors' released weights, and across four checkpoints long-horizon success rank-orders exactly with metric quality and inversely with prediction accuracy.
  Replacing only the objective, with nothing retrained and no GPU, lifts goals reached at offset 100 from 26.0% to 98.0%, equals the 98.0% at offset 25, and reaches 92.0% under a third of the budget: planning stops depending on the horizon. The best cost is not the most accurate. A head learned from frame separation alone predicts spatial distance worse than a position probe (r = 0.819 against 0.9897) yet plans better, charging 24% more to cross the environment's dividing wall where squared latent distance charges 4% less. It has learned reachability, not proximity.

</details>


### [141] [Understanding Backdoor Vulnerabilities in Vertical Federated Learning: The Gap Between Research and Practice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12962)
*Ziqi Zhao,Jialin Lu,Junjie Shan,Junyuan Zhang,Shuya Yang,Ka-Ho Chow*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Backdoor vulnerabilities in Vertical Federated Learning are poorly understood under realistic constraints; current evaluations rely on unrealistic assumptions. The authors redefine threat models, propose practical backdoor workflows, and introduce BVBench, a benchmark for fair, practical evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VFL enables collaboration on complementary features with privacy demands, creating an asymmetric information structure. This setup is susceptible to backdoor attacks that can poison training and be activated at inference. Prior work reports near-perfect attack success and defenses but often relies on unrealistic assumptions, revealing a gap between research and practice.

Method: Conduct a practice-oriented analysis of VFL backdoors: redefine threat models under realistic constraints, design practical backdoor workflows, and develop BVBench—a backdoor-centric benchmark with state-of-the-art baselines for fair, comprehensive evaluation.

Result: BVBench exposes the fragility of current VFL backdoor understanding, showing that previous approaches rely on unrealistic prior knowledge and evaluation practices. It provides a structured platform to assess practical vulnerabilities and to guide the development of more meaningful defenses.

Conclusion: Real-world VFL backdoor risks are not well captured by existing studies. BVBench offers a foundation for rigorous, realistic evaluation and accelerates progress toward practical, robust defenses and vulnerability discovery.

Abstract: Vertical Federated Learning (VFL) enables organizations holding complementary features of shared entities to collaborate and train models. In this setting, the initiator can withhold information about the learning task, while other contributors participate without exposing their local datasets, creating an asymmetric information structure aligned with growing privacy demands. However, this asymmetry is a double-edged sword. Among various threats, backdoor attacks are particularly concerning because VFL not only enables malicious contributors to poison the model during training, but also allows them to activate the backdoor at inference time to manipulate predictions. Although prior work has reported near-perfect attack success rates and proposed effective defenses, we find that most findings fail to hold under realistic conditions, exposing a fundamental gap between research and practice. In this paper, we present a systematic, practice-oriented study of backdoor vulnerabilities in VFL, revealing this gap in both methodological design and evaluation practices. We show that existing approaches overlook key practical constraints and therefore rely on unrealistic prior knowledge. Furthermore, these limitations have remained hidden due to poorly designed evaluation practices in the literature. To bridge this gap, we redefine threat models under realistic constraints, propose practical backdoor workflows, and introduce BVBench, a backdoor-centric benchmark that enables fair, practical, and comprehensive evaluation, preloaded with state-of-the-art baselines. BVBench provides strong evidence of the fragility of the current understanding of VFL backdoor risks and establishes a foundation for steering research toward uncovering practical vulnerabilities and developing more meaningful defenses.

</details>


### [142] [Comment on "Modeling rapid language learning by distilling Bayesian priors into artificial neural networks"](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12974)
*Orr Well,Idan Tarshish,Nur Lan,Roni Katzir*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: M&G claim MAML distills Bayesian priors into ANNs; critique shows standard interpretation fails to embed a prior; permissive interpretation has challenges; MAML does not match true Bayesian learning and tends to overfit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clarify whether meta-learning with MAML implements Bayesian priors in neural learners and assess empirical validity.

Method: Theoretical critique of prior interpretation; analysis of meta-learning initialization vs objective; comparison to Yang & Piantadosi 2023; empirical assessment of generalization.

Result: Under a standard Bayesian view, no explicit prior is instantiated by MAML; permissive view encounters nontrivial challenges; M&G's meta-learning approach overfits and generalizes poorly relative to genuine Bayesian learners.

Conclusion: Do not treat MAML-initialized nets as Bayesian priors; explicit probabilistic modeling or more careful evaluation is needed; further work to delineate when meta-learning approximates Bayesian induction.

Abstract: McCoy & Griffiths (2025, henceforth M&G) suggest that a Bayesian prior can be distilled into Artificial Neural Networks (ANNs) through Model-Agnostic Meta-Learning (MAML, Finn et al., 2017). They support this empirically by showing that meta-trained networks demonstrate formal language learning abilities comparable to Yang & Piantadosi (2023)'s Bayesian learner, significantly outperforming standard ANNs. We point out that under the standard interpretation of a prior, M&G's procedure does not actually instill one; it merely initializes network weights favorably, leaving the objective function unchanged. We then consider a more permissive interpretation, where the system as a whole can be seen as implementing a Bayesian learner even without an explicit prior in the objective. We show that this interpretation faces nontrivial challenges. Finally, we assess how well MAML approximates the empirical results of Bayesian learning, showing that unlike genuine Bayesian learners, M&G's model overfits and generalizes poorly to unseen data.

</details>


### [143] [Learning the Mathematical Property for Designing Low Mutual Coherence Binary Sensing Matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12982)
*Rekha,Santosh Singh,S. K. Neogy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a data-free, learning-based construction of a binary sensing matrix for compressive sensing, guided by mutual coherence, with aims to achieve robust, low-storage recovery; claims novelty in not using datasets and in defining the loss function via a mathematical property.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome NP-hardness and computational cost of designing sensing matrices with RIP/NSP/spark, by learning a matrix without data and with a simple architecture.

Method: Neural network framework that outputs a binary sensing matrix; uses a loss function based on mutual coherence (and potentially other properties) rather than data-driven reconstruction errors; claims to rely on a shared underlying rule for matrix entries and not to require large training datasets.

Result: Abstract asserts that the framework yields a binary matrix with low mutual coherence, with claimed advantages in generality, robustness, and reduced storage; however, no empirical results are provided in the excerpt.

Conclusion: If validated, this approach could offer a universal, data-free method to generate sensing matrices with favorable coherence properties and reduced computational/storage costs; critical questions remain about how well RIP/NSP/Spark-like guarantees are achieved in practice, actual recovery performance, and scalability.

Abstract: In this research work, we are constructing the sensing matrix, which is essential for the success of the compressive sensing technique. We have chosen a learning-based technique for the construction of the sensing matrix. The novelty and uniqueness of the proposed technique is that it does not use any data set and also does not use a specific application. It uses the mathematical property/constraint for the construction of the sensing matrix for the perfect recovery of the signal. The perfect recovery of signals is an old and still very challenging problem in real-world applications. In late 2000, compressive sensing became a popular mathematical tool for the perfect recovery of sparse signals. The core of the compressive technique is the construction of the sensing matrix, which satisfies certain special properties such as restricted isometry property (RIP), null space property (NSP), and spark property (SP). All these properties are NP-hard problems and hence computationally challenging to solve. For all practical purposes, the construction of the sensing matrix needs to achieve low mutual coherence to achieve the perfect recovery of the signals. We have used a neural network for the construction of the sensing matrix, and this framework constructs a binary sensing matrix with low mutual coherence. The entries in the matrix are generated through a shared underlying rule. The proposed architecture is simple and does not use large-scale training data sets. Such uniqueness and novelty bring a drastic reduction in computational cost, and also, for the first time in literature, the use of a mathematical property for defining the loss function. In this proposed research work, the mutual coherence property has been used in the neural network framework. Such a neural network framework brings generality, robustness, and reduces storage requirements.

</details>


### [144] [Balanced Adaptive Prototype Selection for Scalable TabPFN Inference on Large-Scale Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12989)
*Mahboobe Jadid,Melika Rezaye Garkani,Ali Mousavi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BAPS selects a compact set of prototypes (512) to preserve key information for TabPFN, achieving ~1953x context compression while maintaining accuracy and calibration on million-row datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcoming limited inference context in pretrained tabular foundation models to enable scalable inference on very large datasets without retraining.

Method: Balanced Adaptive Prototype Selection (BAPS) jointly preserves representative structure, decision boundaries, local density, class balance, and feature-space diversity to construct a compact, informative context for inference.

Result: On million-row HIGGS and SUSY datasets, 512 prototypes retain strong predictive performance and reliable calibration, achieving approx. 1953-fold compression; experiments ran on CPU without GPUs.

Conclusion: Context construction via BAPS is a practical mechanism to extend pretrained tabular foundation models to million-scale datasets without retraining.

Abstract: Pretrained tabular foundation models have demonstrated strong predictive capability; however, their application to large-scale datasets remains constrained by the limited inference context. This paper introduces Balanced Adaptive Prototype Selection (BAPS), a framework for constructing compact, information-preserving contexts for scalable TabPFN inference. Without modifying or retraining the pretrained model, BAPS jointly preserves representative structure, informative decision boundaries, local density, class balance, and feature-space diversity. Experiments on the million-row HIGGS and SUSY datasets show that 512 prototypes retain strong predictive performance and reliable calibration, corresponding to an approximately 1,953-fold context compression. All experiments were conducted on an Intel Core i7 CPU with 16 GB RAM and no GPU acceleration. These findings establish effective context construction as a practical mechanism for extending pretrained tabular foundation models to million-scale datasets.

</details>


### [145] [Incremental Evaluation and Training in Relational Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13023)
*Jakub Peleška,Gustav Šír*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes an incremental, multi-episode evaluation/training framework for Relational Deep Learning (RDL) on temporal graphs, showing that temporal drift is common and that incremental fine-tuning with transfer learning improves near-future accuracy over from-scratch training using a new temporal metric.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RDL evaluation uses static, single-episode snapshots, failing to reflect performance as real-world databases evolve. A temporal, evolving setting is necessary to assess robustness, adaptability, and transferability of RDL models.

Method: Introduce an incremental, multi-episode evaluation and training paradigm for RDL on temporal heterogeneous graphs; develop multiple incremental training regimes; propose a new temporal evaluation metric emphasizing near-future accuracy; conduct experiments on large-scale datasets to study data evolution, training dynamics, and the feasibility/effectiveness of transfer learning.

Result: Temporal concept drift is present in the majority of predictive tasks; incrementally fine-tuned models consistently outperform standard from-scratch baselines; transfer learning is feasible and effective in the RDL setting; the new temporal metric yields improved near-future performance.

Conclusion: Temporal-aware evaluation and incremental training with transfer learning improve RDL robustness to data drift, suggesting practical deployment benefits and the need for benchmarks that capture performance over time.

Abstract: Relational Deep Learning (RDL) models multi-tabular databases as temporal heterogeneous graphs to enable end-to-end representation learning. However, prevailing RDL evaluation practices rely on static, single-episode dataset snapshots, overlooking the continuous, time-evolving nature of real-world databases. Consequently, current RDL benchmarks fail to capture how model performance changes as new data accumulates over time. To address this limitation, we introduce an incremental, multi-episode evaluation and training paradigm to assess and improve the temporal robustness and adaptability of state-of-the-art RDL models. Using established large-scale datasets, we examine data evolution and model training dynamics, demonstrating that temporal concept drifts occur in the majority of predictive tasks. We present multiple incremental training regimes for fine-tuning the models and demonstrate that transfer learning is both feasible and highly effective in the RDL setting. Alongside a new temporal evaluation metric that prioritizes near-future accuracy, we show that our incrementally fine-tuned models consistently outperform the standard, expensive, from-scratch trained baselines.

</details>


### [146] [On the global feature importance for interpretable and trustworthy heat demand forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13039)
*Milan Zdravković*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes an ante-hoc XAI framework to assess global feature importance for ML-based heat demand forecasting in district heating. It compares intrinsic interpretability of Gradient Boosting with post-hoc PD, ALE, and SHAP, avoiding feature permutation biases, and discusses complementarities and domain-specific interpretations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enhance interpretability and trustworthiness of ML models in district heating control to meet communal standards, improve customer satisfaction, and mitigate liability risks.

Method: Four approaches: intrinsic interpretability of Gradient Boosting and post-hoc PD, ALE, and SHAP; none rely on feature permutation or perturbations that could bias data; assess complementarities and provide interpretations in district heating processes.

Result: Discussion of results with domain-specific interpretations and an assessment of complementarities among methods where applicable.

Conclusion: Ante-hoc interpretability framework improves understanding and trust in ML-based district heating control, reduces biases from perturbation-based methods, and supports compliance, accountability, and stakeholder confidence.

Abstract: The paper introduces the ante-hoc Explainable AI methodology to assess the global feature importance of the Machine Learning models used for heat demand forecasting in intelligent control of District Heating Systems, with motivation to facilitate their interpretability and trustworthiness, hence addressing the challenges related to adherence to communal standards, customer satisfaction and liability risks. Methodology includes use of four different approaches, namely intrinsic interpretability of Gradient Boosting method and selected post-hoc methods, namely Partial Dependence, Accumulated Local Effects and SHAP. None of the selected methods assume feature permutation or perturbations which can introduce bias due to introduction of random unrealistic values of data instances. Discussion of results is provided, including the assessment of complementarities where applicable, with specific interpretations in context of the district heating processes.

</details>


### [147] [Latent On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13040)
*Guibin Zhang,Jiayang Lyu,Ran Sun,Xinlei Yu,Haoyu Zhao,Qibing Ren,Shuicheng Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LOPD proposes a fully learnable privileged context for on-policy self-distillation by assembling latent tokens from retrieved experiences to condition a self-teacher, with the student receiving dense token-level supervision. It achieves strong performance and sample efficiency against OPSD baselines and RLVR, and ablations show learnable context is essential.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the reliance on hand-crafted privileged artifacts in on-policy self-distillation (OPSD) methods, which limits end-to-end learnability and scalability for continual self-improvement.

Method: Retrieve and compose relevant experiences into continuous latent tokens to condition a self-teacher; the student generates trajectories from task/history and receives dense token-level supervision at every visited prefix; introduce a privileged-margin objective to stabilize learning of latent context.

Result: Empirically strong performance across agentic tool use and code generation; outperforms RLVR and OPSD variants (OPSD, SDPO, Skill-SD); higher learning efficiency with less than 30% of the rollout budget of GRPO and Skill-SD. Ablation studies indicate that learnable privileged context is necessary for these gains.

Conclusion: LOPD demonstrates a scalable, self-directed pathway for agent evolution by making privileged context learnable end-to-end, improving both performance and sample efficiency over prior OPSD approaches.

Abstract: Enabling agents to learn from experience and internalize it into their policy has become a central problem in self-evolving AI.
