On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) offers an effective pathway by using a privileged self-teacher to provide dense supervision on the student's own trajectories; however, existing methods still rely heavily on designer-specified privileged artifacts (e.g., answers, feedback, skills, or trajectories), limiting the end-to-end learnability and scalability required for continual self-improvement. In this work, we introduce Latent On-Policy Self-Distillation (LOPD), which, rather than proposing another hand-crafted OPSD variant with a newly prescribed form of privileged context, makes the teacher's privileged context itself learnable end-to-end from experience. Technically, LOPD retrieves relevant experiences and composes them into continuous latent tokens that condition a self-teacher, while the student generates trajectories from the task and interaction history and receives dense token-level supervision at every visited prefix. We further introduce a privileged-margin objective to stabilize and regulate the learning of latent context. Empirically, LOPD demonstrates (I) strong performance, outperforming RLVR and representative OPSD methods including OPSD, SDPO, and Skill-SD across both agentic tool use and code generation; and (II) high learning efficiency, surpassing GRPO and Skill-SD with less than 30% of their rollout budget. Ablation studies further provide direct evidence that making privileged context learnable is necessary for realizing these gains. Together, these results position LOPD as a step toward a more scalable and self-directed paradigm for agent evolution.

</details>


### [148] [A Multispectral Framework for the Detection of Calcium Carbide-Induced Ripening and Shelf-Life Estimation in Climacteric Fruits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13073)
*Gurbhit Chaurakoti,Harshit Kumar,Hani Kumar,Anurag Singh,Ram Asrey*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Non-invasive multispectral approach using 18 VIS-NIR bands to classify calcium carbide ripening vs natural/ethephon ripening and to estimate ripening progression and shelf life for mango and banana; PCA reduces dimensionality to 5–7 components, with XGBoost models achieving mango accuracy 95% (carbide recall 0.67) and banana accuracy 81% (carbide recall 0.74).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address health risks from industrial-grade calcium carbide used for fruit ripening by delivering a rapid, non-destructive method to detect carbide-ripened fruits and quantify ripening status and remaining shelf life.

Method: Spectral data collected with an AS7265x spectral triad sensor over 18 wavelengths in the 410–940 nm range for mango and banana. Feature engineering combines inter-method spectral variance, wavelength intensity ratios, and environmental parameters (temperature and humidity). PCA reduces dimensionality, retaining >90% variance in the first 5–7 components. Three independent XGBoost-based models are trained for ripening-method classification, with simultaneous estimation of ripening progression (percentage) and remaining shelf life (days).

Result: Mango: classification accuracy 95% with carbide-class recall 0.67. Banana: accuracy 81% with carbide-class recall 0.74. The framework leverages spectral changes linked to chlorophyll degradation and carotenoid development under CaC2 treatment, supporting non-invasive discrimination and progress estimation.

Conclusion: The non-invasive multispectral framework demonstrates feasibility for separating carbide-ripened from naturally/ethephon-ripened fruit and for estimating ripening status and shelf life. Strengths include non-destructive sensing and data-driven modeling; limitations likely include limited sample size, potential overfitting, and generalizability across different fruit types, growing conditions, and lighting environments. Future work should expand fruit coverage, validate across sites, address class imbalance, and explore robustness to ambient light and sensor calibration.

Abstract: Significant health risks are associated with the illegal, yet commonly practiced use of industrial-grade Calcium Carbide (CaC2) for ripening climacteric fruits like mango and banana, which leaves behind trace residues of arsenic and phosphorus. To address this, the proposed study explores a novel, non-invasive multispectral framework for distinguishing safely ripened fruits (naturally ripened and ethephon-induced) from calcium carbide-ripened samples, while also estimating their ripening progression (in percentage) and remaining shelf life (in days). The spectral profiles of mango (Mangifera indica) and banana (Musa acuminata) at 18 discrete wavelengths in the visible-near infrared (NIR) range (410 nm - 940 nm) are studied using the AS7265x spectral triad sensor. CaC2-treated samples exhibit sharper spectral intensity drops in the visible region, consistent with accelerated chlorophyll degradation and carotenoid development. To characterize these physiological changes, the feature engineering strategy integrates inter-method spectral variance, intensity ratios at distinct wavelengths, and environmental parameters including temperature and humidity. Dimensionality reduction using Principal Component Analysis (PCA) retains >90% of spectral variance within the first 5-7 components. The resulting feature set is used to train three independent eXtreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost)-based learning algorithms for ripening method classification, along with quantitative estimation of remaining shelf life and ripening progression. A classification accuracy of 95% along with carbide class recall of 0.67 is observed for mango samples, while the model achieves an accuracy of 81% and carbide class recall of 0.74 for banana. This instrumentation and data-driven approach demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed non-invasive framework.

</details>


### [149] [Learning Discrete Decisions for MIPs with Constraint-Aware Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13079)
*Vincenzo Di Vito,Mehdi Taghizadeh,Deepjyoti Deka,Kaarthik Sundar,Ferdinando Fioretto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A diffusion-based, graph-generative method for approximately solving mixed-integer optimization problems by learning the discrete decisions with an integrated feasibility projection in the reverse diffusion process; after discrete decisions, the continuous subproblem is solved efficiently, yielding large speedups and improved feasibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mixed-integer optimization (MIO/MINLP) is computationally intractable due to combinatorial constraints and nonlinearities. A scalable, problem-agnostic approach that reliably yields feasible, high-quality solutions is highly desirable.

Method: Constrained Graph Diffusion (CGD): a graph-based generative diffusion model that learns the discrete component of MIOs and embeds a training-free feasibility projection operator into the reverse diffusion process to steer samples toward feasibility. Once discrete decisions are produced, the continuous subproblem is solved with existing solvers. CGD is designed to be problem-agnostic via suitable projection operators.

Result: Empirical evaluation on optimal transmission switching for ACOPF and discrete portfolio optimization shows CGD achieves substantially better feasibility and solution quality than learning-based baselines, with speedups up to 425× over state-of-the-art MINLP solvers.

Conclusion: CGD provides a general, modular framework for constrained MIOs, enabling broad applicability by choosing appropriate projection operators. Demonstrates strong performance on selected benchmarks, suggesting potential for wider adoption; future work may focus on projection design, scalability, and theoretical guarantees.

Abstract: This paper proposes a novel learning-based approach to approximately solve instances of mixed-integer optimization problems. These problems are computationally challenging, as they require jointly determining discrete and continuous decisions while satisfying complex combinatorial constraints. The proposed method relies on a graph-based generative diffusion model that learns the discrete component of mixed-integer optimization problems while integrating a training-free feasibility projection operator directly into the reverse diffusion process to steer intermediate samples toward the feasible set throughout generation. Once the discrete decisions are generated, the remaining optimization reduces to a continuous problem that can be solved efficiently (relative to the original problem) using existing numerical methods. The resulting framework named Constrained Graph Diffusion (CGD), is problem-agnostic and can accommodate a broad class of mixed-integer optimization problems through suitable projection operators. We evaluate CGD on optimal transmission switching for ACOPF and discrete portfolio optimization, demonstrating substantial improvements in feasibility and solution quality over learning-based baselines while achieving speedups of up to $425\times$ over state-of-the-art numerical solvers for MINLPs.

</details>


### [150] [Sampling Luck Masquerades as Allocation Gain: Auditing Test-Time Budget Allocation for Neural Combinatorial Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13087)
*Jinhyung Bae*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Non-uniform budget allocation for NCO solvers reveals phantom in-distribution gains but real benefits under distribution shift; a correction and preregistered protocol show how to measure true gains and avoid bias, with data/code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether allocating a fixed evaluation budget non-uniformly across samples yields genuine improvements in neural combinatorial optimization solvers, given potential evaluative biases when per-instance budgets are constant.

Method: Three pretrained solvers (POMO, AM, SymNCO) evaluated on uniform TSP-100. Used an oracle allocation based on stored samples to estimate in-sample gains and tested out-of-sample. Conducted a preregistered confirmatory experiment under distribution shift with held-out statistics to guide allocation, included a negative control, and tested a 20-sample exploratory probe. Measured best-of-k gains with and without acquisition costs; corrected for phantom gains and provided a reporting checklist.

Result: In-distribution: oracle-based gains 2.2–2.6% but out-of-sample gains indistinguishable from zero (≈0.0–0.5%). This suggests standard in-sample gains are phantom. Under distribution shift, allocation guided by held-out statistics yields substantial gains: AM ~11.5%, SymNCO ~12.0% (with 95% CIs), negative control (POMO) shows no benefit; a 20-sample probe yields 3.4–4.6% gains; the gains exceed a frozen distribution-label baseline by ~4.2 points. A correction procedure preserves real gains while removing phantom ones.

Conclusion: Phantom gains plague in-sample evaluation of non-uniform budget allocation, but genuine improvements exist under distribution shift when guided by held-out statistics. The authors provide a correction method, a reporting checklist, and openly release data/code and preregistration to facilitate reproducible measurement.

Abstract: Neural combinatorial optimization (NCO) solvers report the best of many sampled solutions per instance, and the sample count is, by convention, identical for every instance. Whether a non-uniform allocation of a fixed total budget would buy anything has not been measured. We measure it, and we audit the measurement itself.
  First, on in-distribution workloads the allocation headroom is not detectable. Across three pretrained solvers (POMO, AM, SymNCO) on uniform TSP-100, an oracle allocation computed and evaluated on the same stored samples reports a 2.2-2.6% gain with intervals excluding zero; measured out of sample the same gain is indistinguishable from zero (0.457, 0.015, -0.512 percent). Following the customary in-sample procedure, all three solvers would have supported a published 2%-level gain that does not exist. We calibrate this bias against an instance-wise null in which the true gain is zero by construction; over the ranges we test it does not shrink with more samples or more instances.
  Second, the same correction that removes the phantom gains preserves a real one. Under distribution shift (a workload mixing uniform and clustered instances), a pre-registered confirmatory experiment finds that allocation guided by held-out sample statistics improves best-of-k by 11.5% (AM, primary endpoint; 95% CI [7.4, 19.7]) and 12.0% (SymNCO, replication) at equal evaluation budget, with the signal-acquisition cost not charged; a pre-registered negative control (POMO, an order of magnitude more robust to shift) shows -0.3% [-0.7, 0.24]. The gain exceeds a frozen distribution-label baseline by 4.2 points [1.9, 7.7]. An exploratory policy charging a 20-sample probe against the same budget retains 3.4% (AM) and 4.6% (SymNCO).
  We give a correction procedure and a reporting checklist, and release all data, code, and the pre-registration record.

</details>


### [151] [FlowLOB: Efficient and Controllable Limit Order Book Generation with Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13096)
*Zhuohan Wang,Andreea Bacalum,Ollie Olby,Carmine Ventre,Namid Stillman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FlowLOB is a conditional flow-matching generator for LOB trajectories trained on HKEX data across multiple symbols and sampling frequencies, offering superior sampling efficiency and realism than diffusion-based and baseline models, with zero-shot transfer to unseen instruments and controllable counterfactuality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for LOB simulators that (i) produce realistic market microstructure dynamics, (ii) are computationally efficient, (iii) support controllable scenario generation, and (iv) generalize to instruments not seen during training. Existing agent-based and deep generative methods satisfy these properties only partially.

Method: The authors implement FlowLOB, a conditional flow-matching model, trained on multiple HKEX symbols at 0.1s, 1s, and 10s frequencies in a tick-relative representation. They train FlowLOB and a diffusion-based comparator using identical data, architecture, and budget, and sample via the same fixed-step ODE solvers to enable a fair comparison. They evaluate realism across distributional metrics, efficiency via solver steps, and counterfactual controllability with a tail-focused distributional test. They perform ablations on architecture and learning rate and test zero-shot transfer to held-out symbols.

Result: FlowLOB achieves best quality at only 10 ODE solver steps, outperforming baselines (two learned, two agent-based) on most distributional metrics at the finer sampling frequencies (0.1s and 1s). It requires markedly fewer function evaluations than diffusion to reach comparable fidelity. It passes a distributional tail controllability test in most settings, indicating controllable scenario shifts move generated statistics toward the real tail regimes. Zero-shot transfer to a held-out symbol succeeds, and ablation studies illuminate effects of architecture choices and learning rate on performance.

Conclusion: FlowLOB offers a more efficient, realistic, and controllable LOB simulation framework with strong zero-shot generalization to unseen instruments. The flow-matching approach provides advantages in sampling efficiency over diffusion while maintaining fidelity, making it a promising direction for scalable LOB generation and scenario analysis.

Abstract: Limit order book (LOB) simulators are most useful to practitioners when they combine realistic market dynamics, computationally efficient sampling, controllable scenario generation, and the ability to generalize beyond the instruments seen during training---properties that existing agent-based and deep generative simulators provide only partially. We present \textbf{FlowLOB}, a conditional \textbf{flow}-matching generator of \textbf{LOB} trajectories, trained on multiple Hong Kong Exchange (HKEX) symbols at three sampling frequencies ($0.1$s, $1$s, $10$s) in tick-relative representation that transfers to unseen instruments. Because flow and diffusion models admit a common formulation, we train both with identical data, architecture, and budget, and sample both through the same fixed-step ODE solvers, yielding a controlled comparison of sampling efficiency and fidelity. Flow matching attains its best quality with only $10$ ODE-solver steps, whereas diffusion needs many more function evaluations to approach the same fidelity. At this efficient operating point, FlowLOB improves realism over baselines, two learned and two agent-based models, in most distributional metrics at the two finer sampling frequencies. We evaluate counterfactual controllability with a distributional test that asks whether changing a scenario condition moves the generated statistic toward the corresponding real tail regime; FlowLOB satisfies this criterion in most tested settings. Both realism and control effects transfer zero-shot on a held-out symbol. We additionally conduct ablation studies on the network architecture and the learning rate.

</details>


### [152] [Branch and Bound for Relational Verification of Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13118)
*Kota Fukuda,Zhenya Zhang,Guanqin Zhang,Jianjun Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces SaBRe, a branch-and-bound verifier for relational neural-network properties that splits relational neurons guided by a dual-based selection strategy, achieving improved solved instances and efficiency across 817 problems on ACAS Xu, MNIST-F/C, CIFAR, GTSRB.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Relational specifications (e.g., global robustness) require tracking relationships across multiple inferences, and current abstractions are sound but incomplete, leading to false alarms; there is a need for effective refinement to prune spurious counterexamples and improve verification efficiency in safety-critical CPS.

Method: BaB framework that splits relational neurons (not single neurons); relational neuron selection via dual formulation of the verification problem to identify the most impactful neuron to refine; evaluation on diverse datasets (ACAS Xu, MNIST-F, MNIST-C, CIFAR, GTSRB) with 817 problems.

Result: SaBRe outperforms baselines in both the number of solved instances and verification efficiency, demonstrating the effectiveness of relational-neuron-aware splitting and dual-based selection.

Conclusion: Relational-neuron BaB with dual-guided refinement is a promising approach for scalable verification of relational neural-network properties in CPS; the method reduces incompleteness-driven false alarms and improves practicality.

Abstract: Verification of neural networks against relational specifications, such as global robustness, is crucial for safety-critical applications of cyber-physical systems (CPS), given their increasing adoption of AI components. Compared to simple trace properties (e.g., local robustness), verifying relational specifications requires reasoning about the relationship between multiple network inferences, which brings significant technical challenges. Existing research has explored abstraction techniques based on sound and convex over-approximation of neural network outputs; however, since these approaches are inherently incomplete and may raise false alarms, they further underscore the need of effective abstraction refinement.
  In this paper, we propose a branch-and-bound (BaB) framework to mitigate the issue, which iteratively splits the problem until all sub-problems are verified. Specifically, our BaB framework features splitting of relational neurons rather than individual neurons as prior works do, and as the core of our technique, we devise a relational neuron selection strategy based on the dual formulation of the verification problem, which allows us to efficiently select the (most likely) optimal relational neuron that maximizes the refinement brought by problem splitting. We evaluate SaBRe on 817 verification problems across ACAS Xu, MNIST-F, MNIST-C, CIFAR and GTSRB. The results show that SaBRe outperforms different baseline approaches, in terms of the number of solved instances and verification efficiency, which demonstrates the effectiveness of our proposed techniques.

</details>


### [153] [ProME: Prototype-Margin Environments with Repair-Aware Selection for Group-Robust Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13190)
*Qianqian Wang,Yunshan Li,Dawei Huang,Wenwu Gong,Lili Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ProME (Prototype-Margin Environments) provides a practical, alignment-consistent solution for group-robust learning without training-group labels by constructing environments from prototype margins and ranking predictors via worst-group validation accuracy, achieving superior worst-group performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Maintaining accuracy on rare subpopulations when training-group labels are unavailable. Existing methods infer environments separately from the deployed predictor, causing misalignment. This work defines endogenous environments with repair-aware selection (ERAS) and seeks alignment with the deployed predictor.

Method: ProME splits prototype margins at their median to form approximately balanced environments along the training trajectory. It then trains a group-balanced linear head on group-annotated validation data to rank candidate predictors by validation worst-group accuracy. The approach provides a theoretical bound on the worst risk across the inferred environments for a fixed predictor and partition, and shows this bound transfers to oracle groups under an explicit alignment condition.

Result: Empirical evaluation shows that prototype margins enrich shortcut-conflicting examples, predictor repair reshapes candidate evaluation, and ProME achieves the highest average worst-group accuracy among methods with the same group-label access.

Conclusion: ProME effectively aligns environment inference with the deployed predictor, improving worst-group performance under limited group-label availability. Theoretical guarantees support transfer to true groups under the alignment condition, and experiments validate practical effectiveness.

Abstract: Group-robust learning is crucial for maintaining accuracy on rare subpopulations when training-group labels are unavailable. However, existing methods often infer environments from a separate reference model and select representations before fitting the classifier used at deployment, leaving both decisions misaligned with the deployed predictor. In this work, we formulate group robustness without training-group labels as the endogenous environments with repair-aware selection (ERAS) problem, and propose ProME (Prototype-Margin Environments) to align both decisions with the deployed predictor. ProME splits prototype margins at their median to construct approximately balanced environments along the training trajectory, and fits a group-balanced linear head on group-annotated validation data to rank the resulting predictors by validation worst-group accuracy. We theoretically bound the worst risk across the inferred environments for a fixed predictor and partition, showing that this bound transfers to the oracle groups under an explicit alignment condition. Extensive experiments show that prototype margins enrich shortcut-conflicting examples, classifier repair reshapes candidate evaluation, and ProME achieves the highest average worst-group accuracy among the compared methods with the same group-label access.

</details>


### [154] [Beyond Simulated Benchmarks: Evaluating Motion Representations for Fall Detection Under Real-World Data Scarcity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13197)
*Timilehin B. Aderinola,Ilaria D'Ascanio,Luca Palmerini,Lorenzo Chiari,Jochen Klenk,Clemens Becker,Brian Caulfield,Georgiana Ifrim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Under real-world data scarcity for wearable fall detection, this study systematically compares motion representations (interval-based, kernel-based, symbolic, and foundation-model-based) using accelerometer data. Kernel/foundation models excel on simulated data but falter with scarce or shifted data; interval-based performs best in real-world data; a lightweight symbolic representation augmented with physically grounded impact descriptors offers the most robustness under domain shift and scarcity, albeit with lower precision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world falls are rare, making labeled data scarce and challenging ML model training. Simulated datasets may not generalize well to real-world conditions. The work investigates which representations offer robustness under scarcity and domain shift.

Method: They extract accelerometer signals and compare four representation approaches: interval-based, kernel-based, symbolic, and foundation-model representations. They also propose a lightweight symbolic approach converting short motion segments into symbolic sentences with physically grounded impact descriptors. Experiments on FallAllD (simulated) and FARSEEING (real-world) with cross-validation, data-scarcity scenarios, and cross-dataset transfer tests.

Result: Kernel and foundation models perform well on simulated data but degrade under data scarcity and domain shift. Interval-based representation achieves the strongest absolute real-world performance. The augmented symbolic representation with impact descriptors shows the smallest degradation under domain shift and maintains detection sensitivity under extreme scarcity, though with lower precision.

Conclusion: Model robustness for fall detection under real-world data scarcity hinges on representation choice. Relying solely on simulated benchmarks is insufficient; integrating physically-grounded symbolic representations can improve resilience to data scarcity and domain shifts, guiding deployable systems.

Abstract: Falls are a major health concern for older adults, and wearable sensors have been widely explored for detecting falls and enabling timely intervention. However, real-world falls are extremely rare: collecting 100 of them requires an estimated 100,000 days of monitoring, resulting in severely limited labelled data for training machine learning models. Consequently, many approaches rely on simulated datasets, often reporting high laboratory performance but limited real-world generalisation. We present a systematic evaluation of motion representations for wearable fall detection under real-world data scarcity. Using accelerometer signals, we compare interval-based, kernel-based, symbolic, and foundation model representations. As an interpretable baseline, we additionally investigate a lightweight symbolic representation that converts short motion segments into symbolic sentences augmented with physically-grounded impact descriptors. Experiments use FallAllD, a simulated falls dataset, and FARSEEING, a clinically verified real-world falls dataset. Through cross-validation, controlled data scarcity, and cross-dataset transfer, we examine how representation choices affect robustness under realistic deployment. Our results reveal that highly parameterised kernel and foundation models excel on simulated data but degrade severely under both data scarcity and domain shift. Although the interval-based representation achieves the strongest absolute real-world performance, augmenting a symbolic representation with physically-grounded impact descriptors yields the smallest degradation under domain shift and retains detection sensitivity under extreme scarcity, albeit at lower precision. These findings highlight the importance of evaluating beyond simulated benchmarks and show that representation choice is critical for deployable fall detection given the scarcity of real-world data.

</details>


### [155] [TANGCO: Learning Topology-Aware Capacity Allocation for Overload-driven Cascading Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13212)
*Orkun Irsoy,Leman Akoglu,Osman Yagan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A topology-aware neural-graph-guided capacity optimization (TANGCO) allocates a fixed capacity budget to resist cascades under local load redistribution. It uses a graph neural network policy trained via policy gradient with a cascade simulator and an anchor heuristic. It achieves substantial robustness gains across synthetic and real networks, shows transferability, and offers near-linear training scalability; pre-training reduces deployment cost; analysis yields a closed-form heuristic and regimes where topology-awareness is essential.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Networked systems can experience cascades when node loads exceed capacity, with local load redistribution spreading failures. The optimization problem is hard: no known optimal allocation, and the objective is non-differentiable and piecewise constant, hindering gradient-based methods. A scalable, topology-aware method is needed to allocate a fixed capacity budget to maximize cascade resistance.

Method: Introduce TANGCO, a graph neural network (GNN) policy that allocates node capacities under a fixed total budget. The policy is trained with policy-gradient learning using a cascade simulator, guided by a heuristic anchor. Evaluations span five synthetic graph families and five real networks (power, road, air, Internet topologies). Variants include: (i) TANGCO with GNN, (ii) TANGCOpre, pre-trained on synthetic graphs, (iii) Free-vector variants without GNN (to assess the value of topology).

Result: The learned policy outperforms the best of four hand-designed heuristics in all 450 synthetic instances and in 40 of 45 real-network conditions, with robustness gains ranging from 1.6% to 246%. Policies transfer to unseen graphs within a family and partially across related topologies. TANGCOpre matches per-network training on unseen real networks. Training scales near-linearly with graph size. Free-vector variants stay close to heuristics, indicating the value of the graph representation. A closed-form heuristic is derived from the analysis, and regimes are identified where a topology-aware learned policy remains necessary.

Conclusion: Graph-based, topology-aware learned policies provide gains beyond naive search and generalize across graphs and related topologies. Pre-training reduces deployment cost to that of a hand-designed heuristic. The study also yields practical insights: when local risk suffices, a simpler heuristic can perform well, while certain regimes still require a topology-aware policy.

Abstract: Networked systems, from power grids to traffic networks and cloud clusters, carry loads across nodes with limited capacity. A node whose load exceeds its capacity fails and sheds its load onto its neighbors, which can trigger a system-wide cascade. We study how to allocate a fixed capacity budget across nodes to resist these cascades under local load redistribution. The problem is difficult because no optimal allocation is known, and the fail-or-survive objective is non-differentiable and piecewise constant, so exact and gradient-based optimization methods do not directly apply. We introduce TANGCO (Topology-Aware Neural Graph-Guided Capacity Optimization), which uses a graph neural network policy trained through the cascade simulator with policy-gradient learning and a heuristic anchor. We evaluate TANGCO on five synthetic graph families and five real networks spanning power, road, air, and Internet topologies. The learned policy improves on the best of four hand-designed heuristics in all 450 synthetic instances and in 40 of 45 real-network conditions, with robustness gains ranging from 1.6% to 246%. The learned policies transfer to unseen graphs within a family and partially across related topologies, and TANGCO$^{pre}$, pre-trained on synthetic graphs, matches per-network training on unseen real networks. Training scales near-linearly with graph size, and TANGCO$^{pre}$ allocates on a new network with no per-target training, matching the deployment cost of a hand-designed heuristic. Free-vector variants without the GNN, stay close to the heuristics, so the graph representation carries the gain beyond numerical search. Finally, analysis of the learned allocations identifies when local risk is sufficient, leads to an improved closed-form heuristic, and reveals the regimes where a topology-aware learned policy remains necessary.

</details>


### [156] [History-informed Lagrangian Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13215)
*Tianshuo Zhang,Xianglei Xing,Wenzhe Zhai,Jia Gao,He Cao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HiLNN extends Lagrangian Neural Networks with a history-driven latent context to infer hidden velocities and adapt dynamic parameters, enabling accurate long-horizon predictions from position-only data while preserving energy-consistent dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LNNs require complete state inputs and struggle to adapt to changing parameters; long-horizon, position-only forecasting with physical plausibility is challenging.

Method: A recurrent encoder extracts a latent context from history; this context reconstitutes unobserved initial velocity and adaptively modulates mass matrix, potential energy, and damping within a structured Lagrangian; differentiable RK4 rollout; end-to-end training with multi-step trajectory supervision and energy-consistency regularization.

Result: HiLNN outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on conservative, dissipative, and heterogeneous variable-parameter systems, achieving superior long-term accuracy and tighter energy-profile alignment.

Conclusion: History-informed latent context enables accurate, energy-consistent long-horizon forecasting from position-only observations by inferring hidden velocities and adapting system parameters; code is publicly available.

Abstract: Forecasting the long-horizon evolution of mechanical systems from position-only observations is a pivotal yet difficult task, as hidden velocities and trajectory-specific physical properties must be inferred simultaneously. Although physics-guided neural networks like Lagrangian Neural Networks (LNNs) guarantee physical plausibility, they generally require complete state inputs and lack adaptability to changing system parameters. To break these limitations, we introduce History-informed Lagrangian Neural Networks (HiLNN). Grounded in the insight that temporal position sequences implicitly encode underlying dynamics, HiLNN employs a recurrent encoder to extract a latent context from history. This context not only reconstructs the unobserved initial velocity but also adaptively modulates the mass matrix, potential energy, and damping coefficients of a structured Lagrangian system. By leveraging a differentiable RK4 rollout scheme, the entire pipeline is optimized end-to-end under multi-step trajectory supervision and energy-consistency regularization. Empirical evaluations across conservative, dissipative, and heterogeneous variable-parameter systems show that HiLNN delivers superior long-term prediction accuracy and maintains precise energy profiles compared to state-of-the-art baselines. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/yingtian22/History-informed-LNN.

</details>


### [157] [Knowledge-guided Pattern Discovery via Coupled Tensor Factorizations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13234)
*Gaute Johannessen,Geert Roelof van der Ploeg,Evrim Acar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Knowledge-guided, coupled tensor factorization integrates real multiway data with simulated data from a computational model to improve pattern discovery and enable model-data discrepancy analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multiway biological datasets (e.g., subject x metabolite x time) are noisy and hard to interpret. Incorporating mechanistic or computational model priors via coupling can guide tensor factorization and improve interpretability and discovery.

Method: Jointly factorizes real data tensor and simulated data tensor using coupled tensor factorization with linear coupling constraints, enabling information transfer from simulations to data and vice versa; applied to metabolomics datasets.

Result: Guided analysis enhances pattern discovery on real data compared to unguided approaches and helps identify discrepancies between data and the computational model, informing model refinement.

Conclusion: A knowledge-guided tensor analysis framework effectively leverages simulations to improve extraction of meaningful patterns from multiway data while also serving as a tool for validating and refining computational models.

Abstract: In order to understand complex systems such as the human metabolome or human brain, different sensing technologies are used, generating complex data. These datasets are often multiway, i.e., with more than two axes of variation such as a subjects by metabolites by time array. While tensor factorizations have successfully revealed interpretable patterns from such complex data, they have so far been mainly data-driven. On the other hand, there is more to data -- there are computational models (of these systems), which are rich sources of prior information. In this paper, we introduce a knowledge-guided approach that brings together data and computational models by jointly analyzing real data and simulated data (generated using a computational model) using coupled tensor factorizations with linear coupling. Our experiments on real metabolomics measurements demonstrate that guiding the analysis of such noisy data with simulated data improves the pattern discovery performance while also revealing potential discrepancies between data and computational models.

</details>


### [158] [Novel Knowledge-Guided Generative Methods for Synthetic Transcriptomic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13256)
*Francesca Pia Panaccione,Sofia Mongardi,Marco Masseroli,Pietro Pinoli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A comparative benchmark of three GAN variants for transcriptomic data, highlighting MK-TGAN, a multi-kernel GNN-based model that uses gene-graph priors to improve realism and utility of synthetic data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data-intensive biomedical research is hindered by imbalances, biases, and access constraints (privacy, ethics). Synthetic data with incorporated biological priors can expand usable data while preserving downstream utility.

Method: Benchmark three GAN variants for transcriptomic data; develop MK-TGAN that integrates prior gene graphs via a multi-kernel Graph Neural Network; evaluate realism and downstream task performance, comparing against baseline methods.

Result: MK-TGAN achieves superior realism and biological plausibility; integrating prior knowledge improves performance across variants; MK-TGAN consistently yields high-quality synthetic samples.

Conclusion: Graph-informed GANs with GNN-based priors effectively synthesize transcriptomic data, with MK-TGAN representing a notable advance in realism and utility by leveraging biological knowledge graphs.

Abstract: As biomedical research increasingly relies on data-intensive tools, the quality and utility of datasets are critical. Challenges such as imbalances, biases, and ethical or legal constraints often limit access to high-quality data. Synthetic data generation can help overcome these limitations. Here, we present a comparative analysis of generative models for transcriptomic data, investigating strategies to incorporate prior biological knowledge via gene graphs. This ensures that synthetic data capture real-world gene patterns, maintaining their usefulness for downstream tasks. In particular, we introduce and benchmark three variants of the Generative Adversarial Network. Among the alternatives, MK-TGAN - an innovative multi-kernel, Graph Neural Network-based model - stands out for its performance in terms of both the realism and utility of the generated data. Unlike other methods, MK-TGAN leverages prior knowledge graphs by exploiting graph neural networks. Our results show that prior knowledge integration strategies improve performance, and that MK-TGAN consistently produces synthetic samples with superior realism and biological plausibility.

</details>


### [159] [Virtual Temperature Sensors in Power Transformers Using Neural Ordinary Differential Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13260)
*Berk Hadzhamolla,Alexander Johannes Stasik,Signe Riemer-Sørensen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A physics-informed Neural ODE approach for transformer thermal forecasting that fuses simplified heat-transfer physics with continuous-time dynamics, enabling robust, interpretable predictions across heterogeneous transformers; validated on 15 Norwegian units.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome computational burden and geometric constraints of FEM/CFD and the data scarcity/physical inconsistency issues of pure ML by delivering a continuous-time, physics-aware model that generalizes across different transformer designs and conditions.

Method: Develop a Neural Ordinary Differential Equation (ODE) framework that embeds simplified heat-transfer equations into the ODE dynamics, trained on real-world time-series data from transformers with diverse cooling mechanisms, and evaluated on fifteen units in Norway.

Result: Demonstrates standardized, physics-aware, and robust forecasting capability across heterogeneous transformer units, suggesting good generalization and interpretability compared to purely data-driven or purely physics-based models.

Conclusion: A practical, scalable forecasting framework that leverages continuous-time neural dynamics and embedded physics to improve transformer thermal prediction across varied geometries and operating conditions.

Abstract: Accurate modeling and forecasting of power transformer thermal behavior are critical for reliability, asset lifetime, and optimized power system operation. Numerical approaches such as finite element methods (FEM) and computational fluid dynamics (CFD) offer high fidelity but are computationally expensive, require complex mesh generation, and are often impractical for real-time or large-scale applications, particularly when transformer geometries are unknown. Lumped-parameter thermal models are more practical but depend on transformer-specific thermal constants and may fail to capture dynamic responses under varying operating and environmental conditions. Purely data-driven machine learning methods, including artificial neural networks, convolutional neural networks, and long short-term memory (LSTM) networks, have shown success in forecasting transformer temperatures but typically require large volumes of high-quality training data and may produce physically inconsistent or uninterpretable results. This paper develops a physics-aware Neural Ordinary Differential Equation (Neural ODE) framework for forecasting transformer thermal behavior from real-world time-series data. Neural ODEs model system dynamics in continuous time, providing smooth trajectory prediction and a natural representation of continuously evolving thermal dynamics. A key contribution is the integration of simplified heat-transfer equations directly into the Neural ODE formulation. The model is evaluated across datasets from fifteen transformers in different regions of Norway with varying designs and cooling mechanisms. The results demonstrate that the developed Neural ODE framework provides a standardized, physics-aware, and robust forecasting approach for heterogeneous transformer units.

</details>


### [160] [Into the ORBIT for Time Series: Training Regimes for Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13262)
*Hongjie Xia,Yiding Liu,Yifan Hu,Peiyuan Liu,Zewei Dong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ORBIT is a training paradigm for time series foundation models that makes data distribution explicit via Bootstrap Multi-Level Sampling and Omni-Range Incremental Training, enabling controlled exposure across domains and horizons. It uses a Rank-Guided Cross-Depth Alignment objective and demonstrates strong zero-shot forecasting on diverse data with Falcon-2.0.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pre-training distributions for TSFMs are often poorly controlled (domain imbalance, context requirements, prediction horizons, missingness), limiting generalization across heterogeneous temporal data. ORBIT aims to make data exposure explicit and tunable.

Method: Bootstrap Multi-Level Sampling controls dataset exposure, records, targets, context windows, and horizons; Omni-Range Incremental Training varies context lengths and horizons within a single training stage. Implementation on Falcon-2.0 (univariate encoder-only Transformer) with missingness-aware triple-channel patch tokenization and parallel patch prediction. Introduces Rank-Guided Cross-Depth Alignment using late-layer representations as stop-gradient teachers for shallower layers, incurring no extra inference cost.

Result: Empirical evaluations on GIFT-Eval and fev-bench show strong zero-shot forecasting performance across diverse domains and frequencies, indicating robust generalization under explicit distributional control.

Conclusion: ORBIT provides a principled, controllable pre-training regime for TSFMs that improves cross-domain generalization, with a cost-efficient training objective (Rank-Guided Cross-Depth Alignment) that leverages teacher signals without additional inference.

Abstract: Time series foundation models (TSFMs) have advanced primarily through architectural innovation, while training regimes for large-scale heterogeneous corpora remain under-explored. As a result, pre-training distributions are often poorly controlled with respect to domain imbalance, context requirements, prediction horizons, and missingness. We introduce ORBIT (Omni-Range Bootstrap Incremental Training), a training paradigm that makes this distribution explicit and controllable. ORBIT combines Bootstrap Multi-Level Sampling, which controls dataset exposure and samples records, target variables, context windows, and prediction horizons, with Omni-Range Incremental Training, which varies context lengths and prediction horizons throughout a single training stage. Under ORBIT, we train Falcon-2.0, a simple univariate encoder-only Transformer with missingness-aware triple-channel patch tokenization and parallel patch prediction. We further introduce Rank-Guided Cross-Depth Alignment, a training objective that uses late-layer representations as stop-gradient teachers for shallow layers without additional inference cost. Evaluations on GIFT-Eval and fev-bench demonstrate strong zero-shot forecasting performance across diverse domains and frequencies.

</details>


### [161] [EEG Decoding Using CNN and LSTM Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13285)
*Athanasios Karagounis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hybrid CNN-bi-LSTM architecture for motor-imagery EEG decoding; improves two- and three-class MI-EEG classification with promising cross-subject generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MI-EEG signals are noisy with weak, complex relationships to intended movement; deep learning can learn from raw EEG but MI-EEG applications require robust feature learning and temporal modeling to improve decoding and cross-subject performance.

Method: A CNN learns high-level spatial and temporal representations from raw MI-EEG; a bidirectional LSTM models temporal dependencies among extracted features; evaluated on a public dataset and a privately collected dataset; tests include 2-class and 3-class MI classification and subject-independent decoding.

Result: The CNN+bi-LSTM architecture yields robust performance for both 2- and 3-class MI-EEG classification and shows promising subject-independent decoding across the evaluated methods.

Conclusion: Combining CNN and bi-LSTM effectively captures discriminative spatial-temporal patterns in MI-EEG, enhancing decoding accuracy and cross-subject generalization; supports potential for more reliable MI-BCI systems.

Abstract: Motor imagery (MI) brain--computer interfaces (BCIs) have emerged as a promising approach for establishing flexible communication pathways between the human brain and external devices , particularly for individuals affected by stroke or neurodegenerative disorders. Reliable decoding of motor-imagery electroencephalography (MI-EEG) remains challenging because EEG recordings contain substantial noise and exhibit complex, weakly informative relationships with the underlying brain activity. Although deep learning provides an effective means of learning representations directly from EEG signals, its application to MI-EEG feature learning remains comparatively limited. This study introduces a hybrid deep-learning architecture that integrates a convolutional neural network (CNN) with a bidirectional long short-term memory (bi-LSTM) network. The CNN is used to learn high-level spatial and temporal representations directly from raw MI-EEG recordings, whereas the bi-LSTM models temporal dependencies and relationships among the extracted features. The proposed approach is evaluated using both a publicly available dataset and a privately acquired dataset obtained with an EEG acquisition system. The experimental results indicate that the CNN\&bi-LSTM architecture provides robust performance for both two- and three-class motor-imagery classification and demonstrates promising subject-independent decoding capability across the evaluated methods.

</details>


### [162] [Large-scale Testing Global Optimization Methods with Black-box Adversarial Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13296)
*Wojciech Zarzecki,Jarosław Arabas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BBAA tasks offer a high-dimensional global optimization benchmark to reduce bias from traditional small-function suites; the paper demonstrates evolutionary algorithms and metaheuristics on these BBAA problems, signaling alignment with modern ML needs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional global optimization benchmarks are small and biased toward legacy analytical functions; there is a need for benchmarks that reflect realistic, high-dimensional ML contexts, such as black-box adversarial attacks.

Method: Empirically evaluate several evolutionary algorithms and other metaheuristics on example BBAA problems, i.e., black-box adversarial attack tasks treated as optimization problems.

Result: BBAA problems can be effectively solved by various evolutionary methods; these results suggest BBAA tasks are viable, scalable benchmarks for global optimization in many dimensions.

Conclusion: Adopting BBAA tasks can drive convergence of global optimization methods toward ML-relevant challenges and diversify benchmark suites.

Abstract: Existing global optimization benchmark suites are of a moderate size and are based on a small number of analytical functions that date back even to the 1970s. This causes a risk of biasing the development of global optimization methods. We argue that the tasks related to the black-box adversarial attack (BBAA) can serve as valuable global optimization benchmark in many-dimensional space. We demonstrate the efficiency of several types of evolutionary algorithms and other metaheuristics in solving example BBAA problems. Thus, we take a step towards convergence of global optimization methods to the challenges and needs that arise in the modern machine learning field.

</details>


### [163] [The Time Value of Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13297)
*Matthew Siper,Ahmed Khalifa,Julian Togelius*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A long-horizon, lineage-aware policy gradient framework (LVPG) for automated trading policy discovery improves finite-budget search and policy quality by explicitly valuing lineage potential rather than only immediate returns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Immediate-return optimization neglects the delayed utility of beneficial mutations (lineages). The paper formalizes the time value of evolution within a finite-horizon MDP to capture long-horizon credit assignment in evolutionary search.

Method: Introduce LVPG, a long-horizon actor-critic with a shared generative backbone and two heads: (1) a bootstrapped critic that estimates finite-horizon lineage value from multi-step mutation trees, and (2) an actor that modulates mutation intensity based on remaining search budget. The approach uses path-based credit assignment and is evaluated in 90 paired runs under matched operators, lineage supervision, folds, seeds, and budgets.

Result: Path-based credit assignment substantially accelerates finite-budget search and increases validation best-so-far AUC by 0.394 Sharpe units. LVPG yields fewer temporary regressions and recovers from them more often. Finite-horizon lineage value leads to more selective non-monotonic search and stronger policies within identical resource constraints.

Conclusion: Accounting for finite-horizon lineage value improves search efficiency and policy quality in evolutionary search for trading policies, outperforming immediate-return optimization under the same resource constraints.

Abstract: In evolutionary search, a weak child can be a valuable ancestor that makes high-fitness regions reachable. Immediate-return control is blind to this delayed utility, penalizing mutations through their immediate offspring even when they open productive future lineages. We formalize this hidden dynamic as the time value of evolution within a finite-horizon Markov decision process. To exploit it, we introduce Lineage-Value Policy Gradients (LVPG), a long-horizon actor-critic framework for automated trading policy discovery. Our architecture decouples search control into specialized policy heads over a shared generative backbone: a bootstrapped critic head estimates the value of finite-horizon lineage potential from multi-step mutation trees, while an actor head dynamically modulates mutation intensity over the remaining search budget. We isolate the impact of long-horizon credit assignment against immediate-return optimization across 90 paired runs under matched operators, lineage supervision, folds, seeds, and budgets. Path-based credit assignment substantially accelerates finite-budget search, increasing validation best-so-far AUC by 0.394 Sharpe units. LVPG also produces fewer temporary regressions than immediate-return optimization and recovers from them more often. Finite-horizon lineage value yields more selective non-monotonic search and stronger policies within identical resource constraints.

</details>


### [164] [A Probe Direction Is a Property of Its Prompt](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13329)
*Valentin Noël*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A single-prompt evaluation of model activations is unreliable: prompt choice, not model, largely drives reported scores and their relation to model size. A defensible comparison requires treating prompts as design factors and using multiple prompts; single-prompt designs cannot support cross-model comparisons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess the reliability and interpretability of prompting-based probes that claim to reveal model-sensing behavior, and to resolve conflicting findings by isolating the source of variance.

Method: Systematically vary only the 'evaluation-announcing' prompt while holding task text fixed; analyze how the score direction with respect to model size changes with prompt choice; perform variance decomposition to separate model vs. prompt contributions; check whether scoring is tied to surface-form or to actual evaluation signals; examine reproducibility of published results under different prompts.

Result: The readings are highly prompt-dependent; the same design reproduces different model-size trends or signs depending on prompt choice. Model explains only a small portion of reported variance; most variance comes from how prompts elicit responses. Two published studies with opposite conclusions can both be reproduced by swapping prompts. Increasing the number of evaluation items cannot fix the measurement if prompts themselves are the dominant source of variance.

Conclusion: Single-prompt designs are inadequate for cross-model comparison. A defensible protocol requires multiple prompts treated as facets of the measurement design, with an explicit accounting of variance components across models and prompts; the minimum number of prompts should be specified to ensure reliable comparisons.

Abstract: A model that behaves differently when it senses it is being tested would undermine the evaluations we rely on, so recent work has sought to read that sense directly from a model's activations. The standard instrument contrasts activations on prompts that announce an evaluation against prompts that do not, and reports how well the resulting direction separates held-out cases. That number is then compared across models and correlated with scale. We observe that the instrument has a free parameter its readings do not disclose: "a prompt that announces an evaluation" is not a prompt but a choice among many, and nothing in the method fixes which. Holding the task text fixed and varying only that choice, we find that the reported score, and even the direction in which it trends with model size, follows the prompt rather than the model; two published studies that disagree about the sign of that trend are both reproducible from a single design, by choice of prompt alone. Treating the prompt as a facet of a measurement design rather than an implementation detail, we find the model under study accounts for a small share of the variance in the number reported about it, and most of the rest lies in how each model responds to each prompt: collecting more evaluation items cannot repair the measurement, while varying prompts can. A further check finds that the split these probes are scored on is largely separable from surface form alone, so a direction carrying no information about evaluation at all still reproduces a substantial fraction of each published score. We conclude that a single-prompt design cannot support comparison between models, and we give the number of prompts a defensible comparison requires.

</details>


### [165] [Training AI Scientists to Replicate Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13331)
*Damon Falck,Samer Sabri,Anja Surina,Thom Foster,Anya Sims,Sam Devlin,Dylan Rogers,Tantum Collins,Kaloyan Aleksiev,Louis Kirsch,Edward Hughes*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Replica provides a scalable AI-driven framework for paper replication with an auto-generated rubric judge; Faraday, a 27B AI Scientist, outperforms baselines on held-out replication tasks and shows a principled approach, suggesting potential for long-horizon scientific innovation without complex harnesses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Replication is essential for scientific reliability but traditional replication is slow and underspecified. An AI-assisted, scalable task space could accelerate replication and reveal hidden details, enabling more robust science.

Method: Introduce Replica, a scalable task space for paper replication. Use an auto-generated rubric-based judge with low noise that correlates with human replication quality. Post-train Faraday (27B) as an AI Scientist leveraging coding agents as tools. Compare performance to Claude Opus 4.8 and GPT-5.5 on held-out replication tasks. Conduct qualitative analysis of Faraday's rollouts to assess scientific principled behavior.

Result: Faraday surpasses the baselines on held-out replication tasks. The rubric judge aligns with human assessments and provides a low-noise reward signal. Qualitative analysis indicates Faraday adopts a more scientifically-principled approach than competitors.

Conclusion: This work lays groundwork for AI agents capable of long-horizon scientific innovation without requiring complex harnesses, advancing AI-assisted replication and potentially broader scientific exploration.

Abstract: The replicability of papers is a cornerstone of scientific knowledge, ensuring the reliability of existing results and providing a base for further experiments. The act of replication typically illuminates details that were previously underspecified, and thus requires similar hypothesis-driven exploration to open-ended research. In this work, we develop Replica, a scalable task space for paper replication. To provide reward signal, we introduce an auto-generated rubric-based judge that has low noise and agrees with human assessment of replication quality. We post-train Faraday, a 27B-parameter "AI Scientist" agent that leverages coding agents as tools, surpassing the performance of Claude Opus 4.8 and GPT-5.5 on held-out replication tasks. Qualitative analysis of individual rollouts reveals that Faraday adopts a more scientifically-principled approach. We believe that our results provide a stepping stone towards AI agents capable of long-horizon scientific innovation without requiring complex harnesses.

</details>


### [166] [Neural Quadratic Forms: A Unified Minimal Model for Sudden Learning and Scaling Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13335)
*Liu Ziyin,Yizhou Xu,Tomaso Poggio,Isaac Chuang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A universal, symmetry-driven quadratic leading-order training dynamics emerges across architectures, reducing to a Lotka–Volterra–type flow on the order parameter M=WW^T; plateaus and power-law temporal behavior arise from sequential activation of modes as initial weights shrink.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explain why diverse networks trained with gradient descent exhibit plateaus and abrupt drops, and unify their dynamics under a single universal framework dictated by unit-interchangeability symmetry.

Method: Identify interchangeability of units to enforce a universal quadratic expansion Tr[WW^T A(x)]. Compute a architecture-specific structure matrix A(x) for perceptrons, attention, mixtures of experts, and convolutions. Reduce dynamics to the order parameter M=WW^T. When data matrices share an eigenbasis, derive a Lotka–Volterra system whose modes activate sequentially. Analyze how smaller initial weights separate switch-on times and yield plateaus as a singular limit; derive power-law regimes when many modes remain unresolved.

Result: A universal leading-order description: a single structure matrix A(x) captures architectural detail; dynamics of W reduce to LV-like equations on M with sequential mode activation. Exhaustively explains plateau formation, abrupt drops, and power-law training times. Numerical evidence across training methods and architectures supports the theory.

Conclusion: Symmetry fixes the relevant collective variables, yielding a universal, architecture-agnostic dynamics for trained networks. The initial weight scale sets switch-on times, and a low-dimensional order-parameter flow accounts for observed plateaus and power laws, unifying perceptrons, attention mechanisms, mixtures of experts, and convolutions under one framework.

Abstract: Neural networks trained by gradient descent on a smooth cost function can nevertheless learn in steps: the cost holds on long plateaus and then drops abruptly. Meanwhile, training losses instead follow smooth power laws. Variants of both behaviors occur in architectures with very different microscopic structures, which is the signature of a few relevant collective variables. We show that a symmetry fixes what those variables are: a network layer is a sum over interchangeable units, so relabeling the units leaves it unchanged; given smoothness and the condition that a unit's gradient vanish at the origin, symmetry then enforces a universal leading form for the expansion about the near-zero weights present at the start of training, the quadratic $\Tr[WW^{\top}A(x)]$, in which every architectural detail is confined to a single ``structure matrix" $A(x)$ that we compute for each architecture. Perceptrons, attention layers, mixtures of experts, and convolutions become one model at different $A$. Its training dynamics then close on the ``order parameter" $M=WW^{\top}$ and, whenever the data matrices share an eigenbasis, reduce to a Lotka--Volterra equation whose modes switch on one after another. The smaller the initial weights, the further apart the switch-on times, and the plateaus appear as a singular limit of a smooth flow; when many modes are unresolved the same events merge into a power law in training time whose exponent the theory predicts. We confirm both numerically across training methods and architectures.

</details>


### [167] [Where You Measure Decides What You Measure: Position Selection in Ablation-Based SAE Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13337)
*Valentin Noël*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Latent causality in sparse autoencoders is confounded by the token/location chosen for evaluation; a position-aware, ablation-based protocol is proposed to enable fair cross-study comparisons, with evidence that measurement position drives most variance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reliably name what latents represent in language models, one must separate the semantics of a latent from the token/position used to measure its effect; current practice ties measurement to the dictionary, biasing comparisons.

Method: 1) Compare two Google-supplied sparse autoencoders by aligning latents via decoder similarity; 2) train six autoencoders from a common initialization with different fitting choices to keep latent semantics constant; 3) quantify variance due to measurement position; 4) evaluate across a 16-fold corpus-size range; 5) propose a one-line correction and an ablation-based protocol; 6) audit five published papers for compliance.

Result: Measurement variance due to token position drops from 7.6% and 11.9% to near zero when each dictionary is evaluated at the same token; larger corpus sizes do not resolve the issue; the issue persists across dictionaries and scales; a simple one-line code change plus the proposed protocol can restore comparability; audits show current papers often lack position-aware reporting.

Conclusion: Causal numbers must report the exact token/position used for measurement; without this, the reported causality conflates the latent with the token; adopting the proposed protocol enables fair cross-paper comparisons, revealing a pervasive unlabeled methodological bias in current evaluations.

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders are meant to name the things a language model computes, and the usual way to check that a latent matters is to switch it off and see what changes. But a latent fires at many tokens, and the effect has to be measured at one of them. The convention is to measure where the latent fires hardest. That choice is almost never reported, and it is not made by the experimenter: it is made by the dictionary under evaluation. Change the dictionary and the measurement moves to a different token. We show this is not a detail. Take two sparse autoencoders released by Google for the same model and match their latents by decoder similarity: even among the pairs the two dictionaries encode almost identically, they pick different tokens for a large share of them. Two dictionaries compared under the usual protocol are therefore very often compared at different places. To separate the convention from the dictionaries we train six autoencoders from one initialisation, differing only in fitting choices, so that a latent means the same thing in each. Most of the variance such a comparison reads as "these dictionaries disagree about this latent" turns out to be the position instead: it falls from 7.6% and 11.9% of variance to near zero once every dictionary is measured at the same token. More evaluation data does not rescue it. Across a sixteenfold range of corpus sizes the dictionaries agree less about where to measure, not more, so the problem grows with scale. The correction is one line of evaluation code. We give the protocol an ablation-based causal number must report to be comparable across papers, and an audit of five published papers against it. In short: a causal number reported without its position describes the token it was taken at as much as the latent it was taken from.

</details>


### [168] [Simulation-to-real transfer learning for infrared spectroscopic chemical sensing and analysis from molecules to complex samples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13341)
*Yusen Tan,Yixuan Chen,Zheng Fang,Pan Liu,Yifan Li,Qinyu Guo,Zhedong Lin,Yuqiang Li,Xiangxiang Zeng,Tong Wang,Jun Xia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: UltraIR is a 100M+-parameter IR spectroscopy foundation model trained on ~60M simulated spectra to enable simulation-to-real transfer. It supports diverse tasks and performs zero-shot transfer across instruments and labs, achieving data-efficient, adaptable chemical sensing beyond task-specific models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chemical sensing via IR spectroscopy is hindered by labor-intensive interpretation, reliance on references, and poor cross-dataset transfer; conventional ML is often task-specific and data-hungry. A generalizable foundation model could enable scalable, cross-domain spectral analysis from molecules to complex samples.

Method: Pretrain on ~60 million simulated IR spectra using spectral reconstruction, molecular fingerprint similarity alignment, and functional-group prediction. Then adapt to downstream objectives with task-specific labels, enabling transfer learning to various tasks (e.g., functional-group prediction, structure elucidation, property prediction, mixture quantification, microbial and sample-origin classification, microplastics, soil properties).

Result: UltraIR outperforms conventional ML and task-specific DL baselines across multiple tasks, including functional-group prediction, structure elucidation, property prediction, mixture quantification, bacterial classification, medicinal-herb traceability, microplastics classification, and soil properties. It shows strong performance with limited labeled experimental spectra and supports zero-shot inference across different FTIR spectrometers and laboratories.

Conclusion: A data-efficient, adaptable IR spectroscopy foundation model with simulation-to-real transfer enables broad, cross-task analysis of complex real-world samples, reducing reliance on large labeled spectra and enabling cross-instrument generalization.

Abstract: Infrared (IR) spectroscopy is widely used for chemical sensing, but extracting reliable chemical information from spectra remains challenging. Conventional interpretation is labor-intensive, relies on prior knowledge and reference spectra, and is difficult to scale, whereas most machine-learning methods are tailored to individual tasks or datasets, require large labeled training sets, and transfer poorly across analytical objectives and experimental datasets. Here we introduce UltraIR, a foundation model for IR spectroscopy with more than 100 million parameters that enables simulation-to-real transfer learning for chemical sensing and analysis from molecules to complex samples. UltraIR is pretrained on approximately 60 million simulated IR spectra using spectral reconstruction, molecular fingerprint similarity alignment, and functional-group prediction, then adapted to downstream objectives with task-specific labels or targets. Across functional-group prediction, molecular structure elucidation, physicochemical property prediction, mixture-component identification and quantification, bacterial classification, medicinal-herb geographic origin traceability and constituent quantification, microplastics classification, and soil property prediction, UltraIR outperforms conventional machine-learning and task-specific deep-learning baselines. It performs strongly with limited labeled experimental spectra and in zero-shot inference for the same analytical task across Fourier-transform infrared spectrometers and laboratories, providing a route to adaptable, data-efficient chemical sensing from complex real-world samples.

</details>


### [169] [When Local Variance Optimality Is Not Enough: RoPE-Aligned Q/K Rotations for Dynamic 4-Bit Quantisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13365)
*Shuhan Wang,Yilin Luo,Nan Xu,Chi Wang Cheung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Explores whether a RoPE-aligned, pairwise rotation can outperform full-head mixing for rotation-based post-training quantisation; finds no accuracy gain in tested settings, and that surrogate-optimality does not guarantee lower quantisation error when the surrogate and mixing support misalign with the quantiser's scale-setting statistic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess if respecting RoPE's 2D frequency decomposition with a structured, head-shared rotation can reduce outlier-induced error in PTQ compared to standard full-head Hadamard mixing.

Method: - Prove that, for distinct RoPE frequencies, only per-pair rotations commute with RoPE; state the converse result. - Derive, under head-shared parameterisation, the rotation angle that minimises the larger channel variance under a pooled covariance, position-averaged surrogate; verify analytic minimum is attained in implementation. - Empirically evaluate a head-shared pairwise configuration vs full-head Hadamard on dynamic W4A4KV4; interpolate between two-channel and full-head mixing; assess K-based angle estimation impact.

Result: The head-shared pairwise configuration does not improve accuracy in the tested setting; replacing the full-head Hadamard with this configuration increases perplexity at both short and long contexts. Composing the pairwise rotation with Hadamard satisfies a ±0.05-PPL criterion under the default estimator. Estimating the shared angle from K improves pairwise-only performance but does not close the gap to full-head mixing. The analytic objective targets a position-averaged second moment of a pooled calibration covariance, while the dynamic quantiser uses a tokenwise group-range. The pairwise transform supports only two-channel mixing. Interpolating from two-channel to full-head mixing shows degradation and K-range, relative quantisation error, and perplexity degradation narrow as mixing support grows.

Conclusion: Optimisation under a structured surrogate (the pairwise RoPE-aligned transform) need not reduce quantisation error if the surrogate’s mixing support and the quantiser’s scale-setting statistic are misaligned; surrogate-optimality does not imply practical PTQ gains for RoPE-based quantisation in these settings.

Abstract: Rotation-based post-training quantisation commonly applies an orthogonal transform across an entire attention head to reduce outlier-induced error. RoPE instead partitions each head into two-dimensional frequency pairs, raising the question of whether a transform respecting this decomposition can improve on full-head mixing. Prior work has established the per-pair rotations that commute with RoPE. We state the converse result that, for distinct frequencies, no other single-head orthogonal map commutes with RoPE. For the head-shared parameterisation used in our experiments, we then derive the rotation angle that minimises the larger channel variance under a pooled-covariance, position-averaged surrogate and verify that the implementation attains its analytic minimum. The evaluated head-shared pairwise configuration does not improve accuracy in the tested dynamic W4A4KV4 setting. Across four checkpoints, replacing the full-head Hadamard with this configuration increases perplexity at both short and long context lengths. Composing the pairwise rotation with the Hadamard satisfies the selected $\pm0.05$-PPL interval criterion under the default estimator. Estimating the shared angle from K alone improves pairwise-only on every checkpoint but does not close its gap to full-head mixing. The analytic objective controls a position-averaged second moment of a pooled calibration covariance, whereas the dynamic quantiser sets its step from a tokenwise group range. The pairwise transform also has only two-channel mixing support. Along a controlled interpolation from two-channel to full-head mixing, K range, relative quantisation error, and perplexity degradation decrease as support increases. These results show that optimality for a structured surrogate need not reduce quantisation error when the surrogate and mixing support are misaligned with the quantiser's scale-setting statistic.

</details>


### [170] [Reduced Matrix Multiplication: Input-Adaptive Matrix-Product Reduction for LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13426)
*Zixuan Lan,Yanhong Li,Jiawei Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RMM reduces Transformer inference cost by selectively retaining informative contraction slices during matrix multiplications, without changing weights, enabling input-adaptive accuracy-efficiency trade-offs and practical speedups across language models and vision-language tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inference-time cost of large Transformer matrix multiplies; need a training-free, input-adaptive method that preserves performance while reducing computation; reveal where reductions are most effective (attention vs MLP) and whether benefits scale with model size.

Method: Retention-ratio controlled selection of informative slices along contraction dimensions to approximate matrix products, no weight updates; applied to self-attention and MLP blocks; uniform scheme across model scales (1B–70B); extended to vision-language; hardware benchmarks with custom kernels.

Result: Under moderate reduction, RMM is robust across discriminative, autoregressive, and long-context tasks; reduction tolerance depends on model family, task, component, and retention ratio but typically improves with scale; attention-side computations are more reducible than MLP; practical wall-clock speedups at longer sequences on NVIDIA A100.

Conclusion: RMM offers a scalable, training-free path for input-adaptive inference-time optimization in Transformers with cross-modal applicability; supports a smooth accuracy-efficiency trade-off and motivates further exploration of selective contraction in attention versus MLP components.

Abstract: Transformer-based language models achieve strong performance but incur substantial inference cost due to repeated high-dimensional matrix multiplications. We propose Reduced Matrix Multiplication (RMM), a training-free, input-adaptive inference method that reduces Transformer matrix products by selecting informative slices along their contraction dimensions, without modifying model weights. Under a simple retention-ratio control, RMM provides a smooth and predictable accuracy-efficiency trade-off. Across language models ranging from 1B to 70B parameters, we find that reduction tolerance depends on the model family, task, component, and retention ratio, although it often improves with model scale. Under moderate reduction, RMM remains robust across the evaluated discriminative, autoregressive generation, and long-context settings. We further show that the same principle extends to multimodal vision-language inference. Mechanistic ablations reveal a structural asymmetry within Transformers: attention-side computations are substantially more reducible than MLP components.
