Result: On RoboTwin 2.0, Temporal GRPO yields improved task success and sample efficiency with consistent gains across task horizons. In LIBERO-Long, updates are controlled to preserve shared prerequisite stages and focus improvement at the first stage where rollout outcomes diverge.

Conclusion: Stage-aware GRPO effectively addresses trajectory-level credit aliasing, improving learning efficiency and performance for VLA policies in post-training settings; stage-constrained updates provide a targeted mechanism to harness progress without penalizing early gains.

Abstract: Outcome-driven reinforcement learning offers a scalable way to post-train vision-language-action (VLA) policies from sparse task-success feedback. In common GRPO-based VLA post-training, one rollout-level advantage is applied to every action in the trajectory. A rollout that completes several valid stages but fails later can therefore penalize the actions that produced its earlier progress. We call this trajectory-level credit aliasing. Temporal GRPO addresses this problem by constructing detectable task stages, aligning each rollout with stage-specific action intervals, and comparing only rollouts that have entered the same stage. The resulting stage advantages are applied to their corresponding intervals in a single policy update. On RoboTwin 2.0, Temporal GRPO improves task success and sample efficiency, with consistent gains across task horizons. Controlled updates on LIBERO-Long preserve shared prerequisite stages and concentrate improvement at the first stage where rollout outcomes diverge.

</details>


### [195] [H2R-Bench: Benchmarking Human-to-Robot Manipulation Video Generation in World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13049)
*Dingyi Rong,Yue Shi,Chaofan Ma,Jiezhang Cao,Zongrui Wang,Zeyu Zhang,Yao Mu,Guangtao Zhai,Ning Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces H2R-Bench, a benchmark to evaluate cross-embodiment human-to-robot manipulation video generation, and systematically analyzes state-of-the-art video world models on their ability to convert egocentric human demonstrations into robot-centric manipulation videos under specified embodiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the challenge of scaling robot manipulation data and the embodiment gap between human hands and robotic end-effectors. Existing video world models show promise for synthesizing robot-centric videos from human observations, but cross-embodiment transfer remains underexplored and inadequately evaluated.

Method: Propose H2R-Bench with instances consisting of a human demonstration video, target embodiment constraints, and source-grounded annotations (task goals, action events, functional contacts, object responses). Benchmark eleven state-of-the-art video generation models across six manipulation families and two robot embodiments, evaluating on five dimensions: goal-state completion, action-event completion, functional contact transfer, embodiment correctness, and general video quality.

Result: Current video world models show limited cross-embodiment transfer capabilities; many models struggle with embodiment consistency, functional interaction, and task execution when transforming human demonstrations to robot-centric videos. The benchmark provides a systematic diagnostic framework for assessing and guiding progress in bridging the human-to-robot embodiment gap.

Conclusion: H2R-Bench offers a standardized, multi-faceted evaluation platform for diagnosing whether video world models can generate robot-centric demonstrations from human inputs, and for steering future research toward reliable cross-embodiment transfer and usable robot-training data.

Abstract: Large-scale manipulation data is essential for robot learning, yet collecting robot demonstrations remains expensive and difficult to scale. Meanwhile, abundant egocentric human manipulation videos provide rich behavioral experiences, but transferring them across embodiments remains challenging due to differences between human hands and robotic end-effectors. Recent advances in video world models offer a promising pathway to synthesize robot-centric manipulation videos from human observations, while their cross-embodiment transfer capability remains largely unexplored. Therefore, we introduce H2R-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating cross-embodiment human-to-robot manipulation video generation, where models transform egocentric human demonstrations into robot manipulation videos under specified embodiments. Each benchmark instance contains a human demonstration video, target embodiment constraints, and source-grounded annotations covering task goals, action events, functional contacts, and object responses. H2R-Bench evaluates generated videos through five dimensions, including goal-state completion, action-event completion, functional contact transfer, embodiment correctness, and general video quality. We benchmark eleven state-of-the-art video generation models across six manipulation families and two robot embodiments. Our evaluation reveals that current video world models remain limited in human-to-robot manipulation transfer: even leading models often fail in embodiment consistency, functional interaction, and task execution. H2R-Bench provides a systematic diagnostic framework for evaluating whether video world models can bridge the human-to-robot embodiment gap and convert human manipulation observations into robot-centric training resources.

</details>


### [196] [Semantic Radiance Fields as Simulators for Spatial Reasoning in Real-World Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13095)
*Nico Heider,Michał Jan Włodarczyk,Katarzyna Wasielewska-Michniewska,Przemysław Hołda,Martin Schieck,Marcin Paprzycki,Maria Ganzha,Bogdan Franczyk*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Semantic Radiance Fields (SRF) unify geometry, appearance, and per-class semantics in a 3D radiance field, reconstructed from real RGB captures by lifting 2D segmentations into the field. This enables realistic, semantically grounded environments for training and evaluating spatial reasoning in embodied agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide environments that are both geometrically faithful and semantically queryable. Synthetic simulators have ground-truth semantics but poor realism, while real-scene reconstructions are realistic but lack ground-truth semantics by default.

Method: Construct a 3D radiance field that jointly encodes geometry, appearance, and per-class semantic identity by lifting 2D semantic segmentations from pretrained vision models into the 3D field. The SRF is reconstructed from posed RGB captures of real scenes and supports novel-view synthesis, semantic and free-space queries within a single grounded representation. An application demonstrates an SRF-driven simulator for an orchard apple-reaching task, where the radiance field provides camera rendering, semantic ground truth, and occupancy queries to a physics engine.

Result: The SRF enables efficient generation of diverse real-world environments with ground-truth semantics for training and evaluating spatial reasoning models. Example: an orchard apple-reaching task using SRF for rendering, semantics, and occupancy data within a physics loop.

Conclusion: SRFs offer a unified, data-driven representation that blends realism with semantics, enabling practical simulators for embodied AI. Potential limitations include reliance on 2D segmentations, computational cost, and handling of dynamic scenes; future work may address robustness of semantic lifting, scale, and integration with physics engines.

Abstract: Training and evaluating spatial reasoning in embodied agents requires diverse environments that are both geometrically faithful and semantically queryable. Synthetic simulators offer ground truth semantics but sacrifice realism; simulators based on reconstructions of real-world environments have realistic appearance but lack ground truth semantics by default. We propose using Semantic Radiance Fields (SRF) as simulators for spatial reasoning agents. SRFs are a representation that unifies these requirements by lifting 2D semantic segmentations from pretrained vision models into a 3D radiance field that jointly encodes geometry, appearance, and per-class semantic identity. The resulting fields are reconstructed from posed RGB captures of real scenes and support novel-view synthesis, semantic and free-space queries within a single grounded representation. This enables the efficient generation of diverse real-world environments to train and evaluate spatial reasoning models. As an example application, we outline an SRF-driven simulator for an orchard apple-reaching task, in which the radiance field supplies camera rendering, semantic ground truth, and occupancy queries to a physics engine.

</details>


### [197] [S2-HWM: Sparse Event-Structured Hierarchical World Model for Long-Horizon Surgical Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13103)
*Shuzhe Zhang,Xin Zhu,Yinling Qian,Qiong Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A hierarchical world-model (S2-HWM) learns sparse event evidence from primitive latent trajectories to coordinate an event-level manager and a primitive-step worker, enabling variable-duration planning via an Event Transition Model; demonstrated on SurRoL PegTransfer with high success and strong improvement over DreamerV3.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon surgical robot manipulation suffers from sparse rewards and irregular event timing; existing models imagine at short horizons and rely on hand-crafted stages; need automatic event-driven hierarchy.

Method: Extract sparse event evidence from latent trajectories to trigger an event-level manager that updates goals; each latent goal steers the worker's primitive actions until next update; ETM uses event evidence to predict next boundary, duration, and cumulative reward; chain event-level predictions to extend imagination horizon; keep worker as primitive-step actor-critic.

Result: PegTransfer task on SurRoL: 98.7% success rate; +22.7 percentage points over GAS DreamerV3.

Conclusion: S2-HWM enables variable-duration, event-driven planning in a hierarchical latent world-model, improving long-horizon manipulation; effective integration of event evidence with primitive policies; future work could explore robustness and generalization.

Abstract: Long-horizon surgical robot manipulation is challenging because task rewards are sparse, while meaningful interaction changes occur at irregular intervals. Existing world-model agents typically imagine at primitive-step resolution, leaving variable-duration task progress implicit. Manually specified stages can provide intermediate structure, but their task specific boundaries are difficult to align with state-dependent interaction transitions. We propose S2-HWM, a Sparse Event-Structured Hierarchical World Model that learns sparse event evidence from primitive latent trajectories to coordinate an event-level manager and a primitive-step worker. The event evidence schedules manager goal updates, and each selected latent goal conditions the worker's primitive actions until the next update. The learned event evidence also forms variable-duration segments for an Event Transition Model (ETM), which predicts the next?boundary stochastic state, segment duration, and accumulated segment reward. Chaining these event-level predictions provides a variable-duration continuation beyond the primitive imagination horizon for manager learning, while the worker retains primitive-step actor-critic learning. On a SurRoL-based PegTransfer task, S2-HWM achieves a success rate of 98.7%, outperforming the flat GAS DreamerV3 baseline by 22.7 percentage points.

</details>


### [198] [FAM-DQ: A Dual-Quadrotor-Based Fully Actuated Aerial Manipulator for High-Torque Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13220)
*Xuwei Yang,Ruoyu Ren,Ziqian Guo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A dual-quadrotor fully actuated aerial manipulator (FAM-DQ) enables decoupled 6-DoF end-effector control with high torque, validated by experiments; achieves 1.019 N·m torque at 0.447 kg (2.28 N·m/kg).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome strong position–attitude coupling in underactuated aerial manipulators and to address the torque and payload limitations of fully actuated designs for high-torque physical interaction.

Method: Two quadrotor propulsion modules are mounted at the ends of a central frame via passive joints; a geared servo regulates the end-effector pointing direction, enabling decoupled 6-DoF control and omnidirectional manipulation.

Result: Experimental validation including trajectory tracking, attitude tracking, static torque measurement, and screw driving; maximum torque 1.019 N·m with total mass 0.447 kg, producing a torque‑to‑mass ratio of 2.28 N·m/kg.

Conclusion: FAM-DQ demonstrates decoupled 6-DoF end-effector control with enhanced torque output in a lightweight dual-quadrotor platform, indicating strong potential for high-torque aerial manipulation and practical physical-interaction tasks.

Abstract: Aerial physical interaction requires aerial manipulation platforms to generate large interaction forces and torques while maintaining precise end-effector control. However, conventional underactuated aerial manipulators suffer from strong position-attitude coupling, whereas fully actuated platform designs often face structural complexity, limited payload capacity, and insufficient torque output. This paper presents FAM-DQ, a dual-quadrotor based fully actuated aerial manipulator designed for high-torque physical interaction tasks. By mounting two quadrotor propulsion modules at the ends of a central frame through passive joints, while using a gear-driven servo to regulate the pointing direction, FAM-DQ achieves decoupled $6$-DoF end-effector control with omnidirectional manipulation capability and enhanced torque output. Experiments including trajectory tracking, attitude tracking, static torque measurement, and screw driving validate the proposed design. FAM-DQ achieves a maximum torque of $1.019~\mathrm{N}\cdot\mathrm{m}$ with a total mass of $0.447~\mathrm{kg}$, corresponding to a torque-to-mass ratio of $2.28~\mathrm{N}\cdot\mathrm{m/kg}$.

</details>


### [199] [Manufacturing Complex Airtight Soft Pneumatic Actuators for Soft Robotics: Process Evaluation and Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13233)
*Mohammed Abboodi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A manufacturing-focused evaluation of fabrication routes for complex soft pneumatic actuators, identifying process-specific limitations and proposing a design-for-manufacturing approach to guide process selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address simultaneous constraints of geometric fidelity, compliance, structural integrity, and airtightness in soft actuators by evaluating multiple fabrication routes and distinguishing inherent vs correctable defects.

Method: Process screening, baseline fabrication, failure analysis, and process improvement across heat-shrink forming, silicone casting, powder- and liquid-based additive manufacturing, and FDM; assessment of airtightness and architecture-related factors.

Result: FDM offered the most adaptable route; other methods faced specific limitations (heat-shrink conformality, casting mold access/bonds, powder residuals, DLP material/post-processing). Airtightness depends on extrusion-path architecture; support-free geometry matters for internal post-processing; a design-for-manufacturing framework emerges guiding route selection by actuator architecture.

Conclusion: Provide practical guidance for developing complex, flexible, and airtight soft pneumatic actuators; establish a practical DFM approach aligning architecture with manufacturing constraints.

Abstract: Manufacturing complex soft pneumatic actuators remains challenging because geometric fidelity, compliance, structural integrity, and airtightness must be achieved simultaneously. This study presents a manufacturing-focused evaluation of several fabrication routes for complex pneumatic structures, including heat-shrink forming, silicone casting, powder- and liquid-based additive manufacturing, and fused deposition modeling (FDM). The processes were assessed through process screening, baseline fabrication, failure analysis, and process improvement to distinguish inherent process limitations from correctable manufacturing defects. Heat-shrink forming was limited by geometric conformity, casting by mold accessibility and bonded interfaces, powder-based methods by residual material trapped within enclosed passages, and digital light processing by the material properties and post-processing requirements of the investigated system. FDM provided the most adaptable route because its dominant defects could be progressively reduced through process optimization. The results further showed that airtightness depends not only on nominal wall thickness but also on extrusion-path architecture, while support-free geometry is important when access for internal post-processing is limited. These findings establish a practical design-for-manufacturing approach in which process selection is guided by the compatibility between actuator architecture and manufacturing constraints. The proposed approach provides practical guidance for developing complex, flexible, and airtight soft pneumatic actuators for soft robotic applications

</details>


### [200] [Predictive Relative-Velocity Steering for Safe Robotic Manipulator Teleoperation in Dynamic Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13284)
*Changhao Hu,Zeyi Liu,Songqiao Hu,Shuang Liu,Zihan Meng,Xiao He*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A lightweight, modular framework for proactive end-effector collision avoidance in teleoperation. It predicts potential collisions using time-to-collision with overshoot protection, then rotates the relative-velocity vector via Rodrigues' formula to deflect direction while preserving speed, addressing latency and APF deadlock. Validated in simulation and on hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety risks in teleoperation due to sudden obstacles, network latency, and limited operator attention; overcome deadlocks and maintain high-frequency control.

Method: Preprocess point cloud; predict collisions with time-to-collision and overshoot protection; rotate the relative-velocity vector using Rodrigues' rotation formula to deflect direction without changing magnitude; compensate for processing latency; operate directly at end-effector velocity-command level.

Result: Simulations show higher end-effector collision-avoidance rate than baselines; experiments on a physical robot confirm collision-avoidance effectiveness.

Conclusion: A lightweight, modular proactive collision avoidance approach improves teleoperation safety by deflecting velocity direction while preserving magnitude, mitigating latency-induced safety risks and APF deadlocks.

Abstract: Recent advances in teleoperation have enabled robotic manipulators to perform dexterous, human-arm-like motions. However, human operators may fail to avoid suddenly appearing obstacles promptly and effectively, particularly under network latency or limited attention, thereby creating safety risks. To address this issue, we propose a lightweight and modular framework for proactive collision avoidance, operating directly at the end-effector velocity-command level. After preprocessing the point cloud, the framework first predicts potential collisions based on time-to-collision (TTC) with integrated overshoot protection, and subsequently rotates the relative-velocity vector using Rodrigues' rotation formula. The deflection changes only the direction of the relative velocity while preserving its magnitude, thereby mitigating the deadlock problem commonly encountered by conventional artificial potential field (APF) methods. The prediction module compensates for point-cloud processing latency introduced by complex teleoperation pipelines, while the lightweight design enables the high-frequency control required for teleoperation. Simulations across diverse scenarios show that the proposed method achieves a higher end-effector collision avoidance rate than the baseline methods. Experiments on a physical robotic system further validate its collision-avoidance effectiveness.

</details>


### [201] [NestDex: Nested Policy Learning with Copilot Assisted Teleoperation for Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13362)
*James Zhao,Jinhe Tang,Mingyuan Ba,Weiming Zhi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: NestDex presents a nested policy-learning framework for dexterous manipulation that uses learned hand skills to assist demonstration collection, enabling single-DoF control for arm-hand coordination and an outer visuomotor policy for deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dexterous manipulation requires coordinated, contact-rich finger actions; collecting consistent complete-task demonstrations is highly challenging, more so than parallel-jaw manipulation.

Method: NestDex introduces a nested policy-learning framework: an inner hand policy adapts its motion from recent proprioceptive history; a vision-language selector activates the appropriate hand skill for each task stage; operator uses a single-DoF clutch to regulate the active hand skill while controlling the arm; a hand-action variational autoencoder provides compact hand-action targets while keeping arm commands in joint space; the outer visuomotor policy is trained from demonstrations to control both arm and hand without relying on inner policies at deployment.

Result: Real-world dexterous manipulation experiments showNestDex improves demonstration reliability and efficiency, with empirical evaluations supporting effective autonomous policy learning.

Conclusion: Leveraging learned hand skills to assist demonstration collection enables robust, end-to-end autonomous dexterous policies and reduces the cognitive burden on the operator, improving sample efficiency for training.

Abstract: Dexterous manipulation promises substantially richer robot interaction with the physical world, but learning these behaviours remains constrained by the difficulty of collecting consistent, complete-task demonstrations. Unlike parallel-jaw manipulation, dexterous tasks require the operator to coordinate arm motion with precise, contact-rich finger behaviour throughout the task. We introduce NestDex, a nested policy-learning framework that reduces this burden by using learned hand skills to assist demonstration collection. The operator controls the arm and regulates the active hand skill through a single-DoF clutch, rather than directly specifying the full finger trajectory. The inner hand policy adapts its motion from the latest proprioceptive history, while a vision-language selector activates the appropriate skill for each task stage. The resulting demonstrations train a separate outer visuomotor policy that controls both the arm and hand without the inner policies at deployment. A hand-action variational autoencoder provides compact hand-action targets while retaining arm commands in joint space. Across real-world dexterous manipulation experiments, NestDex improves demonstration reliability and efficiency, and the resulting empirical evaluations support effective autonomous policy learning. Video Demo are available at project website https://aus.bot/research/nestdex.

</details>


### [202] [FIRE-VLA: Failure-Informed Self-Evolution for Vision-Language-Action Models in Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13395)
*Hao Dou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FIRE-VLA uses failure-informed self-evolution to convert unresolved policy failures into privileged supervision, via self-distillation and per-group GRPO, improving autonomous-driving VLA policies without an external teacher.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GRPO can fail to identify useful behavior outside failed rollouts when all samples are poor; a mechanism is needed to adapt to evolving failure distributions and rare high-severity rollouts.

Method: Propose FIRE-VLA: low-reward, low-diversity groups trigger self-distillation from a frozen round-start copy of the same model; teacher and student are same size; only the teacher observes hidden future trajectories; supervision follows the student's generated prefix and is restricted to answer tokens; GRPO remains active for all groups; updated policy provides the teacher for the next round; starts from Qwen2.5-VL-3B SFT; no larger external teacher; comparison uses the same rollout and update counts.

Result: Empirical evaluation on 6,019 examples from 150 held-out nuScenes scenes shows FIRE-VLA retains single-sample planning, reduces G=4 mean L2 from 1.848 m to 1.500 m, and lowers evaluation-persistent failure prevalence from 13.03% to 11.20%; mean-error reduction is driven by rare severe rollouts rather than uniform improvement across ordinary trajectories.

Conclusion: Failure-informed self-evolution via privileged supervision enables policy improvement without external teachers, reducing persistent failures while maintaining planning capability; gains arise primarily from correcting rare, severe failures.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning improves autonomous-driving vision-language-action (VLA) models by evaluating trajectories sampled from the current policy. Group relative policy optimization (GRPO) learns from reward differences within each rollout group. When all sampled trajectories are poor, this relative signal can rank failures without identifying behavior outside the failed region. We introduce FIRE-VLA, a failure-informed self-evolution framework that converts such unresolved failures into privileged supervision for the next policy. Low-reward, low-diversity groups trigger self-distillation from a frozen round-start copy of the same model. Teacher and student have the same parameter scale, but only the teacher observes the hidden future trajectory. Supervision follows the student's generated prefix and is restricted to answer tokens, while GRPO remains active for every group. The updated policy supplies the teacher for the next round, allowing the routed failure distribution to change with the policy without requiring a larger external teacher. Starting from the same Qwen2.5-VL-3B SFT checkpoint, the comparison matches student rollout and policy-update counts. On 6,019 examples from 150 held-out nuScenes scenes, FIRE-VLA retains comparable single-sample planning, reduces G=4 mean L2 from 1.848 to 1.500 m, and lowers evaluation-persistent failure prevalence from 13.03% to 11.20%. The reduction in mean error arises mainly from rare severe rollouts rather than uniform improvement across ordinary trajectories.

</details>


### [203] [Capstan-driven Continuum Surgical Robot: Design, Modeling, and Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13396)
*Gang Zhang,Yufu Qiu,Junyan Yan,Wenhui Zeng,Wenlong Lu,Shing Shin Cheng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces actuation-perception co-design for capstan-driven continuum surgical robots, enabling real-time cable tension sensing and integrated shape/force perception via a compliant motor-mount, multibody short-thick-beam model, and proximal force sensor; validated in single- and dual-segment robots with real-time pose and force data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cable tension information is difficult to obtain inside confined capstan assemblies, hindering reliable shape and force sensing in compact capstan-driven continuum robots.

Method: 1) Incorporate a compliant element in the motor mounting bracket to transduce cable reaction into measurable micro-deformation for tension sensing without occupying capstan space. 2) Develop a parallel computation framework based on a multibody short-thick-beam model to capture shear effects and synergistic multi-cable interactions in unconventional joint configurations for real-time performance. 3) Add a proximal multi-axis force/torque sensor as an additional measurement anchor to stabilize shape/force sensing. 4) Demonstrate the framework on single- and dual-segment capstan-driven continuum robots and validate real-time tip pose estimation with contact force and location perception.

Result: Experimental validation shows real-time tip pose estimation and perception of contact force and location in both single- and dual-segment robots, with cable tension feedback obtained without enlarging the capstan space.

Conclusion: The actuation-perception co-design enables integrated perception for capstan-driven continuum surgical robots, overcoming sensing bottlenecks while preserving the compact capstan architecture.

Abstract: Shape and force sensing have long been critical bottlenecks in the development of compact capstan-driven continuum surgical robots, primarily due to the difficulty of obtaining cable tension information within the confined capstan assembly. To overcome these challenges, this paper presents an integrated design-modeling-sensing approach based on the concept of actuation-perception co-design. A compliant element is introduced into the motor mounting bracket of the drive system, enabling micro-deformation under the cable reaction force and thereby allowing real-time cable tension measurement without occupying the compact capstan space. To address the modeling complexity arising from unconventional joint configurations introduced by the spatial cable routing strategy, a parallel computation framework based on a multibody short-thick-beam model is proposed, which captures shear effects in short beam segments and synergistic multi-cable interactions while achieving real-time performance. Building on this framework, stable shape and force sensing is achieved by incorporating a proximal multi-axis force/torque sensor as an additional measurement anchor. Following this design-modeling-sensing framework, capstan-driven continuum surgical robots with single- and dual-segment configurations are developed. Experimental results validate the proposed framework in both single- and dual-segment continuum robots, demonstrating real-time tip pose estimation together with contact force and location perception. By enabling cable tension feedback without compromising the compact capstan architecture, the proposed framework makes integrated perception feasible for capstan-driven continuum surgical robots.

</details>


### [204] [Deliberate Practice: Learning Robot Skills under a Budget](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13415)
*Shivam Vats,Sudarshan Harithas,Mete Tuluhan Akbulut,Arvind Raghunathan,George Konidaris*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Deliberate Practice (DP) is a budget-optimal active learning algorithm for autonomous skill learning in long-horizon tasks; it uses a bilinear program to allocate limited practice time to maximize expected cumulative reward.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In long-horizon manipulation, practice time is scarce; need to decide which skills to practice to maximize long-term reward given constraints; the approach accounts for plan-level rewards and mastery time.

Method: DP estimates time to master skills and the cumulative reward of task plans enabled by skills; formulates a bilinear program to compute a budget-optimal allocation of practice across skills; solves with standard solvers; validated in simulation and real robotic experiments.

Result: DP achieves budget-optimal use of practice time, enabling acquisition of useful policies and improved long-horizon planning; outperforms baselines by more efficient skill acquisition and higher cumulative rewards on long-horizon manipulation tasks.

Conclusion: Exact budget-aware training is feasible for sequential tasks; DP provides a principled framework to allocate scarce practice resources via a bilinear program, with demonstrated gains in simulated and real-world experiments.

Abstract: We consider the problem of autonomously learning robot skills under a limited practice budget for sequential tasks. We propose an active skill learning algorithm, \emph{Deliberate Practice (DP)}, that computes a provably \emph{budget-optimal} allocation---practicing skills that maximize expected cumulative reward while being learnable within the budget. DP estimates both the time needed to master skills and the cumulative reward of the task plans that the skills unlock. Computing a budget-optimal allocation is challenging as it requires reasoning about combinatorially many skill plans over a large practice budget. Our key contribution is a bilinear program that can compute this exactly using off-the-shelf solvers. Through simulated and real-world experiments on long-horizon manipulation tasks, we show that our approach allows robots to optimally use limited practice time to acquire useful policies and improve long-horizon planning.

</details>


### [205] [Attention from Action, for Action: Emergent Visual Bottlenecks for Policy Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13422)
*Zheyu Zhuang,Ruiyu Wang,Nick Heppert,Johannes Fabian Hahn,Abhinav Valada,Florian T. Pokorny,Danica Kragic*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Seeker learns a task- and state-conditioned ROI, guiding RGB cropping and augmentation from action supervision to improve data efficiency and robustness in visuomotor tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing label-free ROI crops based on end-effector events fix timing and spatial proxies, causing misalignment when evidence is elsewhere or evolves with task progress; a learned, progression-aware ROI can better align perception with control.

Method: Starting from fixed DINOv3 features, Seeker iteratively updates a query with gathered visual evidence to produce progression-aware ROIs conditioned on the task and state, trained with action supervision. The ROI then enables RGB cropping, mask-guided background augmentation, and point-cloud filtering.

Result: In simulation and on real robots, Seeker improves data efficiency and robustness over no-crop, augmentation, and action-derived crop baselines. Real-robot results: average in-domain success rises from 48.3% (best baseline) to 76.7%, and success under lighting/background shifts from 20.0% to 60.0%.

Conclusion: A task- and state-conditioned readout that derives ROI from action signals can significantly boost data-efficient visuomotor learning, outperforming fixed action-derived crops and simple augmentation.

Abstract: Visual bottlenecks that focus policy inputs on regions of interest (ROIs) can improve data-efficient visuomotor learning by separating where to look from how to act. Many ROI interfaces rely on external spatial labels, such as gaze, object classes, or affordance annotations. Label-free alternatives often derive crops from trajectories by detecting gripper or motion events and centering a fixed crop at the projected end-effector. Such action-derived crops are useful spatial priors that require no additional labels, but they encode fixed choices about event timing, proxy points, and crop scale. When the visual evidence needed for control lies away from the end-effector or changes continuously with task progress, these crops can become misaligned. We propose Seeker, a task- and state-conditioned readout that learns attention from action. Starting from frozen DINOv3 features, Seeker iteratively updates a query with gathered visual evidence, producing progression-aware ROIs solely from action supervision. The learned ROI serves as a spatial interface for RGB cropping, mask-guided background augmentation, and point-cloud filtering. In simulation and the real world, Seeker improves data efficiency and robustness over no-crop, augmentation, and action-derived crop baselines. On real robots, Seeker raises average in-domain success from the best baseline's 48.3% to 76.7% and success under lighting/background shifts from 20.0% to 60.0%.

</details>


### [206] [ContactGuard: Pre-Contact Execution Monitoring with Action-Conditioned Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13438)
*Gehan Zheng,Matthew Johnson-Roberson,Weiming Zhi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Pre-contact abort system for contact-rich object manipulation. ContactGuard predicts imminent contact failure in latent space from planned actions and aborts before contact, using a self-supervised latent world model and a lightweight pre-contact failure probe.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prevent damage, mishandles, or disturbances in wrist-camera manipulation by detecting failures before contact occurs, addressing the lag of post-contact detectors.

Method: Train a latent world model on unlabeled trajectories to predict compact multi-view visual embeddings under planned actions (avoiding pixel-level prediction). Train a lightweight failure probe from a small labeled set of pre-contact clips. At deployment, roll forward under the policy's actions to forecast post-contact latent and abort if failure is predicted.

Result: ContactGuard achieves higher failure prediction accuracy than direct or corrupted-action baselines across real-world tasks and can be deployed as a pre-contact abort signal without changing the underlying policy.

Conclusion: A practical pre-contact monitoring approach that improves safety and reliability in contact-rich manipulation by forecasting post-contact outcomes in latent space and aborting before contact.

Abstract: Contact-rich manipulation failures are often detected only after the robot has committed to contact. This is especially limiting in wrist-camera setups: close gripper--object views help observe contact, but a poor approach may already push, miss, slip, or disturb the object before conventional detectors react. We introduce \emph{ContactGuard}, a pre-contact execution monitor for chunked visuomotor policies. Given the policy's planned action chunk, ContactGuard predicts its short-horizon consequence in latent visual space and aborts if the predicted future latent indicates likely failure. Its latent world model is trained from unlabelled robot trajectories to predict compact multi-view visual embeddings under planned actions, avoiding pixel-level video prediction. A lightweight failure probe is then trained from a small labelled set of pre-contact clips. At deployment, ContactGuard anchors prediction before an imminent contact event, rolls the model forward under the policy's own actions, and verifies the predicted post-contact latent. Across real-world contact-rich manipulation tasks, ContactGuard predicts failure more accurately than direct and corrupted-action ablations, and transfers to live robot as a pre-contact abort signal without modifying the underlying policy.

</details>


### [207] [Mind the Context: Continual Learning of Socially Appropriate Robot Actions via Environmental-Social Disentanglement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13448)
*Rafal Robert Karpinski,Fethiye Irmak Dogan,Nikhil Churamani,Yiming Luo,Maartje M. A. de Graaf,Davide Dell'Anna,Hatice Gunes*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes Explicit Disentanglement Dual-Branch (EDD) for domain-incremental continual learning of socially appropriate robot actions across indoor contexts; disentangles environmental and social cues and uses replay to mitigate forgetting, showing improved performance over baselines and robustness to domain order.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots operate across diverse environments where norms depend on context; domain-incremental learning requires disentangling environmental and social factors to generalize while preserving prior knowledge.

Method: A dual-branch architecture that explicitly separates environmental knowledge and social-agent-related knowledge; employs replay-based rehearsal to mitigate forgetting; evaluated across multiple indoor domains with ablation studies on disentanglement strategies and domain ordering; code released publically.

Result: EDD outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in action-appropriateness tasks; ablations indicate the effectiveness of disentanglement and provide insight into sensitivity to domain order, demonstrating robust continual learning in domain-incremental settings.

Conclusion: Explicit factor disentanglement with replay-based rehearsal enhances continual learning of socially aware robot behavior in varying indoor environments, enabling scalable adaptation to new domains while retaining prior knowledge.

Abstract: Social robots are expected to operate across diverse environments, where similar arrangements can imply different socially appropriate actions, e.g., starting a conversation may be acceptable in a crowded home but disruptive in an office meeting. Because such norms and environments cannot all be anticipated in advance, robots require continual learning (CL) to adapt from sequential experience while retaining previously acquired knowledge. Prior work has studied CL for generating socially appropriate robot actions, but it has not addressed domain-incremental settings in which the robot incrementally encounters diverse contexts (e.g., living room, meeting room, office, hallway), where both environmental (e.g., whether the space is open or cluttered with furniture) and social cues (e.g., how people or other agents are positioned around the robot) jointly shape the appropriateness of robot actions. We address this gap with the Explicit Disentanglement Dual-Branch (EDD) framework. EDD explicitly separates environmental and social-agent related knowledge and uses replay-based rehearsal to mitigate forgetting while learning the appropriateness of robot actions (e.g., cleaning, serving, starting a conversation) across several indoor domains. Experiments show that EDD outperforms several state-of-the-art baselines, and ablation studies further evaluate different disentanglement strategies and the sensitivity to domain ordering. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/Cambridge-AFAR/Mind-the-Context.git.

</details>


### [208] [Decoding Task Progress from VLA Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13474)
*Atiksh Bhardwaj,Edward Weiyi Duan,Prithwish Dan,Wei-Chiu Ma,Preston Culbertson*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Task progress is linearly decodable from VLAs' hidden states; a simple linear probe can read it and generalizes to unseen tasks, enabling a label-free OOD detector for monitoring, but it cannot steer the policy. This suggests rich, interpretable internal representations useful for lightweight monitoring of visuomotor policies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the lack of tools to understand internal representations and runtime monitoring in vision-language-action models by applying mechanistic interpretability to the residual stream to reveal semantically meaningful signals like task progress.

Method: Analyze the residual stream (π_0.5) of a pretrained PaliGemma-based VLA; train a linear probe to predict normalized task progress; test generalization to unseen tasks; examine effects of multi-prompt language data and counterfactuals; evaluate the probe as a label-free OOD detector for stalled progress.

Result: The task-progress signal is linearly readable from activations; present in the pretrained backbone before task-specific training; a single linear probe generalizes to unseen tasks and responds to language counterfactuals when trained with multi-prompt data; the probe does not meaningfully steer the policy but serves as a competitive OOD detector; indicating rich, linearly decodable semantic representations.

Conclusion: VLAs harbor semantically meaningful, linearly readable quantities like task progress. Reading these signals provides a lightweight, interpretable approach for monitoring deployed visuomotor policies and can function as effective OOD monitoring tools.

Abstract: Vision-language-action models (VLAs) are moving rapidly towards deployment as general-purpose manipulation policies, but we currently lack basic tools for understanding what these models represent internally or for monitoring them at runtime. Leveraging ideas from mechanistic interpretability, we probe the residual stream of $π_{0.5}$ and find that task progress, the normalized time remaining in a trajectory, is linearly readable from the activations. We find that this signal is present in the pretrained PaliGemma backbone prior to training on any robot-specific data. A single linear probe generalizes to unseen tasks and varies under language counterfactuals when trained on multi-prompt data, but does not enable meaningful steering of the policy. These properties make the signal directly useful for instrumenting deployed VLAs. We use the probe as a simple label-free OOD detector, which detects stalled task progress, and find it competitive with state-of-the-art methods. Our results suggest that VLAs have rich, linearly readable internal representations of semantic quantities like task progress, and that learning to read these signals offers a lightweight, interpretable path toward monitoring deployed visuomotor policies.

</details>


### [209] [A Browser-Native Digital Test Range for Benchmarking 4D Ocean-Glider Planning Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13511)
*Edward Holmberg,Elias Ioup,Mahdi Abdelguerfi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A browser-based digital test range for ocean-glider planning enabling reproducible OSSE-style experiments, with a common plan-to-observation contract, evaluation of five planners over 54 missions, and a browser–to–native port (GliderFlight) via Pyodide/WebAssembly to support mission-scale pre-deployment experimentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce reliance on scarce in-situ experiments, enable repeatable, instrumented comparison of planners, and produce dataset-ready artifacts for scientific and operational evaluation.

Method: An install-free browser-native test range converts a region into a four-dimensional experiment (region, bathymetry, forcing, time) with mission-scoped workflows: domain selection, task decomposition, route specification, current-advected execution, observation generation, scoring. A common plan-to-observation contract unifies vehicle, sensing, and evaluator assumptions. An OSSE evaluates five classical planners across two episodes, three seeds, and a 60-hour horizon. Public deployment provides field-referenced audit. GliderFlight 1.2.0 ported to Pyodide/WebAssembly achieves native-to-browser parity, enabling high-fidelity multi-tier simulation.

Result: All 54 missions were completed and recovered without hard violations. Planner rankings and dive-policy effects exposed operational–scientific tradeoffs. A public deployment supplied audit data. GliderFlight 1.2.0 enables browser-native, high-fidelity simulation. The framework yields dataset-ready artifacts and a scientifically traceable, component-qualified operational space for mission-scale pre-deployment experimentation.

Conclusion: The approach delivers a reproducible, end-to-end OSSE-capable platform that unifies planning and observation semantics, supports multiple planners and learned outputs, and provides a practical path from browser simulations to field-ready deployment.

Abstract: Repeated in-situ evaluation of ocean-glider planners requires scarce vehicles, operators, deployment and recovery resources, and ocean conditions that cannot be reset for competing algorithms. We present a guided, installation-free browser-native digital test range that transforms a selected region into a reproducible four-dimensional experiment. The system leads users from regional domain selection through mission-scoped bathymetry, time/depth forcing, science objectives, optional task decomposition, route specification, current-advected execution, observation generation, and scoring. Its primary contribution is a common plan-to-observation contract unifying vehicle, sensing, and evaluator assumptions across manual routes, transparent built-in algorithms, and imported classical or learned-planner outputs, while exported artifacts form dataset-ready records. A controlled Observing System Simulation Experiment (OSSE) evaluates five classical planners in two episodes, three deterministic seeds, and a calibrated 60-hour horizon. All 54 missions completed and recovered without hard violations, while planner rankings and dive-policy effects revealed operational-scientific tradeoffs. An authentic public deployment supplied a field-referenced audit to scope current kinematic boundaries. Separately, source-locked GliderFlight 1.2.0 achieved native-to-browser parity through Pyodide/WebAssembly, establishing a pathway for high-fidelity multi-tier simulation. The resulting operational space is scientifically traceable and component-qualified for mission-scale pre-deployment experimentation.

</details>


### [210] [HumanTracker: Towards Comprehensive and Human-Aligned Motion Tracking Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13555)
*Dairu Liu,Zekun Qi,Jiayu Zeng,Ruixi Yu,Yu Guan,Yintianrun Zhang,Xuchuan Chen,Sikai Liang,Zekai Li,Chenghuai Lin,Xinqiang Yu,Wenyao Zhang,He Wang,Li Yi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces HumanTracker, a perceptually-aligned, scalable benchmark for humanoid motion tracking, and HumanScore, a preference-based metric trained on large-scale motion pairs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Kinematic pose error metrics fail to capture perceptual and physical artifacts in videos (unstable support, foot skating, mistimed touches). Existing test suites are small and fail to stress long-horizon, contact-rich behaviors.

Method: A benchmark with ~153 hours of optical motion trajectories from performers, organized into four motion families with labels. A preference-aligned metric (HumanScore) trained on 12k motion-pair comparisons (24k motions) to predict human preferences.

Result: HumanScore better predicts human preferences than traditional kinematic metrics and highlights contact/stability failures that kin metrics miss, enabling perceptually-aligned evaluation across scalable, diverse tasks.

Conclusion: HumanTracker and HumanScore provide a perceptually-aligned, scalable evaluation framework for humanoid motion tracking, capturing critical contact and stability aspects and enabling robust benchmarking of trackers over long horizons.

Abstract: Humanoid motion tracking is central to teleoperation and whole-body imitation, yet evaluation often disagrees with what people perceive in videos. Kinematic errors average per-frame pose differences but miss the physical artifacts that matter most, particularly unstable support and incorrect contacts such as foot skating and mistimed touch-downs. Meanwhile, widely used test suites are small and lack the diversity needed to stress contact-rich, long-horizon behaviors. We introduce HumanTracker to make humanoid tracking evaluation both perceptually aligned and scalable. The HumanTracker benchmark contains approximately 153 hours of optical motion trajectories from multiple professional performers, organized into four motion families with text labels for fine-grained diagnosis. We further propose HumanScore, a preference-aligned metric trained on 12K motion pairs containing 24K motions. Across representative state-of-the-art trackers, HumanScore better predicts human preferences and reveals contact and stability failures that kinematic metrics often miss.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [211] [Position: Reasoning is a Learnable Rule-Based Process](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12325)
*Rachel Lawrence,Jacqueline Maasch*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes operational, learnable rule-based definitions of autonomous reasoning and a best-practices checklist to improve verifiability and communication in AI reasoning research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address definitional ambiguity in how reasoning is evaluated in modern generative AI; reconcile symbolic/logical traditions with probabilistic models; establish verifiable progress toward trustworthy autonomous systems.

Method: Synthesize literature to define reasoning as a learnable rule-based process; develop operational criteria; propose a practical checklist for reporting AI reasoning research.

Result: Derived operational definitions and a checklist to standardize reporting of AI reasoning work; frameworks to assess validity and soundness of reasoning as a learnable rule-based process.

Conclusion: Clarifying definitions enables verifiable evaluation and measurable progress in trustworthy autonomous reasoning, and bridges traditional logic with contemporary generative approaches.

Abstract: Autonomous reasoning is among the most scientifically and economically motivating topics in AI today. Historically the purview of symbolic AI, recent advances have mainly emerged from deep probabilistic generative models. Despite immense interest and rapid progress, the generative AI community has not clearly converged on operational definitions for reasoning and often implicitly rejects the historical treatment of this topic in logic and verifiable automated reasoning. This position contends that definitional ambiguity leaves the construct validity of reasoning evaluation unverifiable, undermining quantifiable progress toward trustworthy autonomous reasoning. We also contend that this ambiguity is addressable. To that end, we provide (1) operational definitions based on a synthesis of the literature, positioning valid and sound reasoning as a learnable rule-based process; and (2) a checklist for best practices in the communication of AI reasoning research.

</details>


### [212] [Diagnostic Foundation for Evaluating LLMs' Research Integrity as Co-Scientists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12345)
*Yash Tripathi,Silu Sharma,Sai Sidhanth Manoharan Jayanthi,Shivank Garg,Lin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IntegrityBench evaluates LLM integrity across misconduct classification, ethical action reasoning, and artifact-grounded decision-making under a 5-level explicit-implicit pressure protocol, using 36 paired tasks and 18 frontier models. Under peak pressure, ~1/3 of integrity-critical decisions fail; scale or reasoning do not reliably mitigate failures. Explicit pressure increases misconduct compliance; implicit framing often causes over-refusal of legitimate tasks. Classification accuracy does not predict artifact-grounded decisions, indicating dissociated facets. Two deployment risks emerge: enabling misconduct and eroding trust in AI-assisted research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify how frontier language models respond to institutional/peer pressure in research contexts, to measure and mitigate risks related to misconduct facilitation and trust erosion in AI-assisted science.

Method: 36 paired tasks spanning 3 domains and 4 research stages, with a 5-level implicit-explicit pressure protocol. Evaluates 18 frontier model variants on three facets: misconduct classification, ethical action reasoning, and artifact-grounded decision making. Assesses performance under peak pressure and analyzes the relationship between facets.

Result: Under peak pressure, models fail about 1 in 3 integrity-critical decisions. Neither scaling up models nor improving reasoning reliably mitigates failures. Explicit pressures induce compliance with misconduct; implicit reframing leads to over-refusal of legitimate tasks. Surprisingly, models that fail to accurately classify research requests still perform comparably or better on artifact-grounded decisions (85.7 vs. 79.4). This suggests the three facets are structurally dissociated and correct ethical action can be independent of accurate classification. Frontier models may appear helpful yet harbor integrity failures, presenting two deployment risks: facilitating misconduct and eroding trust in AI-assisted research.

Conclusion: Integrity evaluations under pressure reveal distinct, dissociated facets of model ethics and reveal deployment risks. Effective mitigation will require aligning all three facets (classification, ethical reasoning, and grounding) and addressing explicit vs. implicit pressure dynamics to prevent misconduct facilitation and preserve trust in AI-assisted research.

Abstract: Language models are increasingly deployed as co-scientists, yet their ability to uphold research integrity under institutional pressure remains unmeasured. We introduce IntegrityBench, a benchmark evaluating misconduct classification, ethical action reasoning and artifact-grounded decision making across 36 paired tasks under a 5-level implicit-explicit pressure protocol spanning 3 domains and 4 research stages. Evaluating 18 frontier model variants, we find that under peak pressure, models fail roughly 1 in 3 integrity-critical decisions, and neither scale nor reasoning ability reliably mitigates this. Explicit pressures induce compliance with misconduct, while implicit contextual reframing more often causes over-refusal of legitimate research tasks. Interestingly, models failing to classify research requests accurately perform equally or better on artifact-grounded decision making (85.7 vs. 79.4), suggesting the three facets are structurally dissociated and correct ethical action does not require accurate classification. Frontier models can thus appear helpful while harbouring integrity failures that create two distinct deployment risks: facilitating research misconduct and eroding trust in AI-assisted research.

</details>


### [213] [Position: The Alignment Community is Unintentionally Building a Censor's Toolkit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12346)
*Sarah Ball,Phil Hackemann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The abstract argues AI alignment methods are dual-use, potentially enabling censorship and manipulation; it maps alignment techniques to misuse cases, showing that pursuing “perfectly aligned” models could equip malicious actors with better information dominance tools; it calls for immediate consideration of this dual-use risk and mitigation strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To illuminate a neglected risk that alignment research itself may be co-opted for censorship and manipulation, especially given rapid AI adoption and widening political/economic power asymmetries.

Method: Conceptual/analytic approach: align existing alignment techniques with potential and actual misuse scenarios, review case examples, and develop a framework for mitigation; status as a position paper emphasizing normative considerations.

Result: Demonstrates a dual-use potential for alignment methods, identifies factors increasing risk (rapid deployment, information provider role, power asymmetries, authoritarian trends), and provides a high-level call for mitigations rather than empirical findings.

Conclusion: Encourages the AI research community to explicitly address intentional misuse of alignment mechanisms and to propose and implement mitigation strategies to safeguard against dual-use risks.

Abstract: This position paper argues that modern AI alignment methods - originally designed to prevent harmful output - are dual-use technologies that may easily be misused by malicious actors for censorship and manipulation. By mapping current alignment techniques to the possibility and actual cases of misuse, we show that the quest for a "perfectly aligned" model inadvertently also provides malicious actors with an ever-improving tool for informational dominance. We need to discuss this dual-use potential now, as its risk is exacerbated by rapid user adoption of AI as information provider, economic power asymmetries, and a political landscape that increasingly shifts towards authoritarianism. We conclude by urging the community to consider the intentional misuse of AI alignment mechanisms and propose mitigation strategies to safeguard against this dual-use potential.

</details>


### [214] [Agreement Is Not Alignment: Divergent Moral Grounds in Human and LLM Ethical Judgments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12368)
*Octavian M. Machidon,Alina L. Machidon,Vojko Strahovnik,Mateja Centa Strahovnik,Jonas Miklavčič,Marko Robnik Šikonja*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: High label agreement between LLMs and humans on a 500-item ETHICS benchmark, but substantial divergence in the moral grounds or rationales; final labels alone are insufficient for alignment assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether agreement on final moral judgments reflects shared moral reasoning between humans and models, by examining both final labels and supporting rationales across multiple moral domains.

Method: Evaluate a curated 500-item ETHICS-derived benchmark with human annotator majority labels and rationales, plus LLM annotations (final labels and rationales) from frontier and open models; analyze agreement at label level and rationale level across domains (harm, respect, promise-keeping, justice, desert, excuses) and track how models redistribute attention across categories.

Result: Label agreement with human majority is often high across models; however, rationale-level analysis reveals systematic divergence in moral grounds, with models shifting attention among moral categories even when final labels match humans.

Conclusion: Relying on label agreement as a proxy for alignment can be misleading; robust alignment evaluation should include analysis of the underlying reasons, principles, and moral priorities expressed by models, not just final judgments.

Abstract: Agreement with human judgments is a common proxy for evaluating the alignment of large language models (LLMs). Yet agreement in final labels does not show that human annotators and models rely on the same moral grounds. Two agents may reach the same judgment while appealing to different principles, contextual assumptions, or interpretations of the situation.
  We test this distinction using a curated 500-item ETHICS-derived benchmark spanning five domains of moral judgment, with new human annotator and LLM annotations of both final labels and supporting rationales. Across frontier and open model families, agreement with human annotator majority labels is often high. However, rationale-level analysis reveals systematic divergence in the moral grounds expressed by human annotators and models. In particular, models redistribute attention across categories such as harm, respect, promise-keeping, justice, desert, and excuse relevance, even when their final labels match the human annotator majority.
  Our results show that agreement should not be treated as equivalent to alignment. Label-based evaluation can therefore be misleadingly reassuring unless complemented by analysis of the reasons, principles, and moral priorities expressed in model judgments.

</details>


### [215] [Multi-Agent Scheduling with LLM-Assisted Contract Net Negotiation for Stream Processing in Mobile Edge Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12371)
*Sabeur Lajili,Zaki Brahmi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAS-DecStream extends the Contract Net Protocol with semantic CFP, progressive context disclosure, and multi-round proposal revision supported by LLMs, for edge-cloud stream scheduling. It demonstrates significant reductions in latency violations, elimination of overcommitment, and high conflict-resolution with multiple agents; LLM-assisted refinements improve handling of qualitative runtime context, with trade-offs between model accuracy and cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Decentralized scheduling for heterogeneous mobile edge–cloud systems is challenged by workload volatility, resource contention, and stringent QoS requirements. Existing contract-based negotiation lacks semantic context, memory of negotiations, and iterative refinement, hindering performance. The work seeks to improve scheduling efficacy via a semantically enriched, multi-round negotiation protocol aided by LLMs.

Method: Introduce MAS-DecStream and LLMMR-CNP: an extension to Contract Net Protocol with semantic CFP formulation, progressive context disclosure, multi-round proposal revision, negotiation memory, and deterministic validation. Edge-cluster agents generate offloading proposals from local observations, predicted resource states, and qualitative runtime context, while enforcing deterministic resource and QoS constraints. Validate on Alibaba ASI Trace across three axes: single vs multi-round CNP, rule-based vs LLM-assisted refinement, and fixed-model single vs multi-round negotiation.

Result: Latency-violation rate reduced to 3%; resource overcommitment eliminated; conflict-resolution rate 0.91 with 20 agents; utility improved up to 22% over a multi-round rule-based baseline. A 25-case study shows model- and prompt-dependent accuracy–cost trade-offs.

Conclusion: Multi-round CNP refinement drives the primary protocol-level gains, with LLM assistance providing additional value for qualitative and uncertain runtime context; initial evidence supports semantic, memory-enabled, iterative negotiation for improved edge-cloud scheduling.

Abstract: Stream-processing systems increasingly operate across heterogeneous mobile edge--cloud infrastructures, where workload volatility, resource contention, and stringent quality-of-service (QoS) requirements complicate decentralized scheduling. This paper proposes \emph{MAS-DecStream}, whose main contribution is \emph{LLM-MR-CNP}: an extension of the classical Contract Net Protocol with semantic CFP formulation, progressive context disclosure, multi-round proposal revision, negotiation memory, and deterministic validation. Edge-cluster agents refine natural-language offloading proposals from local observations, predicted resource states, and qualitative runtime context, while hard resource and QoS constraints remain deterministic. Experiments derived from the Alibaba ASI Trace evaluate the extension at three levels: single- versus multi-round CNP, rule-based versus LLM-assisted refinement, and fixed-model single- versus multi-round negotiation. Under the evaluated configurations, MAS-DecStream reduces latency violations to 3\%, eliminates resource overcommitment, reaches a conflict-resolution rate of 0.91 with 20 agents, and improves utility by up to 22\% over the multi-round rule-based baseline. A separate 25-case evaluation shows model- and prompt-dependent accuracy--cost trade-offs. The results provide initial evidence that multi-round CNP refinement is the principal protocol-level gain, with LLM assistance adding value for qualitative and uncertain runtime context.

</details>


### [216] [Position: We Need Practical AI Alignment Methods to Mirror Human Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12372)
*Vijay Keswani,Breanna K. Nguyen,Cyrus Cousins,Vincent Conitzer,Walter Sinnott-Armstrong,Jana Schaich Borg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Cognitively-aligned AI systems that reason like users and transparently explain their reasoning improve understandability and trust in high-stakes decisions; a research agenda is needed to address gaps between current alignment methods and cognitive alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enhance adoption and justified reliance of AI in high-stakes settings by ensuring AI reasoning aligns with users' cognitive processes.

Method: Synthesis of existing evidence on cognitive alignment plus a new survey assessing user views; argues for a research agenda to close gaps in current alignment methods.

Result: Evidence suggests cognitive alignment improves understandability and trust; survey data indicate many users deem cognitive alignment essential when rationale is important.

Conclusion: Cognitive misalignment may hinder AI adoption in critical applications; achieving cognitive alignment requires a targeted research program to develop effective, scalable alignment methods and evaluation metrics.

Abstract: AI systems are increasingly employed as decision aids, decision delegates, or autonomous decision-makers. This position paper argues that in many settings, particularly high-stakes decision-making, we need accurate cognitively-aligned AI systems that reason similarly to their users, and faithfully communicate their reasoning. We review evidence that cognitive alignment improves understandability and trustworthiness, and provide new survey data showing that many users find cognitive alignment "essential" when an AI's rationale for a judgment or action is important to them. We outline the gaps between existing alignment methods and what is needed to achieve cognitive alignment, and present a research agenda to address these gaps. We argue that cognitive misalignment represents a likely impediment to AI adoption in many envisioned applications, and that addressing it is important for creating AI systems on which users are both willing and justified to rely.

</details>


### [217] [Don't Want Your LLM to Recommend Nuclear Strike? Try Asking It in Japanese](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12373)
*Rian Touchent*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Language prompts can alter LLM safety judgments; Japanese prompts reduce harmful launch rates in several models, revealing cross-language effects that are not captured by English-only evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether LLM safety alignments are language-specific and whether evaluating only in English misses cross-language risks and safeguards.

Method: Single-turn game-theoretic vignettes where models advise a nuclear-armed nation, using amoral prompts, across nine models from six providers. Compare effect of input language vs reasoning language; isolate mechanism by instructing the model to reason in Japanese within an English prompt; measure launch rates under different scenarios.

Result: Claude Sonnet 4.6: launch rates drop from 40% to 0% (unnecessary strike) and 93% to 17% (contested). When reasoning in Japanese within an English prompt, rates drop to 37% from 93%. Gemini Pro 3.1 drops from 53% to 13%. Five other models show no language effect and launch nearly always. The effect requires a model already hesitant in English; the model’s reasoning in Japanese triggers moral vocabulary (e.g., 'moral cost', 'millions of lives').

Conclusion: LLM safety behavior is language-dependent; English-only evaluation can miss cross-language safeguards and risks encoded in other languages.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used in strategic and advisory contexts, yet their safety alignment is typically evaluated in English only. We test nine models from six providers and ask whether the language of a prompt can change a model's decision in a high-stakes scenario. We use single-turn game-theoretic vignettes in which a model advises a nuclear-armed nation on whether to strike a defenseless opponent. The prompt is intentionally amoral and strategically identical across languages. We find that Japanese prompts reduce launch rates in the Claude model family: Claude Sonnet 4.6 drops from 40% to 0% in scenarios where the strike is unnecessary and from 93% to 17% in contested scenarios, with minimal effect when the strike is strategically rational. The effect extends to Gemini Pro 3.1 (53% to 13%). A cross-language experiment isolates the mechanism: when instructed to reason in Japanese in an English prompt, launch rates drop from 93% to 37%. It is the language the model is asked to reason in, not the language of the input, that drives the effect. When reasoning in Japanese, models spontaneously generate moral vocabulary (''moral cost'', ''millions of lives'') that is entirely absent from the prompt. Five other models show no language effect, but they launch in nearly every condition regardless of language. The effect requires a model that already hesitates in English. These results show that LLM safety behavior is language-dependent, and that evaluating in English alone can miss both risks and safeguards encoded in other languages.

</details>


### [218] [Dual-Flow Transformers: Decoupling the Primary Prefill Path from Additional Decode Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12385)
*Liming Liu,Mingze Wang,Tuo Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Dual-Flow Transformer splits prompt processing and continuation prediction to reduce cumulative inference cost while preserving a shared primary KV cache and weight matrices; enables phase-specific compute, especially in MoE, and shows improved validation loss across setups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inference cost dominates large LMs; prompt prefill is compute-bound, decode is memory-bandwidth-bound; conventional scaling increases both; need decoupled computation to optimize total cost.

Method: Two flows share major components (attention, MLP, outputs) but have separate token embeddings; primary flow processes prompt and writes a KV cache; auxiliary flow is inactive during prompt, activated from final prompt onward to predict continuation without altering persistent state; lightweight coupling; potential reuse of weights and cache; tested in matched-token setups; examined two regimes: increasing decode compute with fixed prefill, and reallocating fixed decode budget.

Result: Dual-Flow achieves lower validation loss in matched-token comparisons across architectures/data; demonstrates prefill-decode quality trade-off and opportunities for independent controls of prompt and continuation costs; in MoE (Mixture of Experts), independent controls over prompt cost, continuation cost, and quality.

Conclusion: Phase-specific allocation of compute with shared state can reduce cumulative inference cost while maintaining quality; practical benefits in MoE and shared cache scenarios; invites further work on optimization strategies and latency considerations.

Abstract: As large language models serve more requests, cumulative inference cost is becoming increasingly important relative to one-time training cost. The two inference phases stress hardware differently: prompt prefill is parallel and typically compute-bound, whereas autoregressive decode is sequential and often memory-bandwidth-bound. Conventional width or depth scaling increases both costs together because every added layer is evaluated in both phases. We ask whether additional learned computation can instead be allocated to continuation prediction while preserving the prompt-wide primary computation and a single persistent key-value (KV) cache. We introduce the Dual-Flow Transformer. Its primary flow is a complete causal language model that processes the prompt and writes the KV cache. The auxiliary flow is omitted during prompt processing and activated only from the final prompt position onward, adding continuation-prediction computation without writing persistent state or influencing the primary flow. The two flows share major attention, MLP, and output matrices, while using separate token embeddings and lightweight coupling. Sharing weights and the primary cache also creates opportunities to reuse loaded weights and cached keys and values during grouped execution. Across matched-token comparisons, Dual-Flow achieves lower validation loss across architectures and data configurations. In MoE models, the separation makes primary and auxiliary expert fan-outs independent controls over prompt cost, continuation cost, and predictive quality. We study two regimes: increasing decode computation at fixed prefill expert computation, and reallocating a fixed decode expert budget between the two flows. These experiments expose a prefill-decode-quality trade-off and demonstrate the potential of phase-specific expert allocation.

</details>


### [219] [Learning to Adapt Cross-Domain Preferences via Meta-LoRA for LLM Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12389)
*Xuefei Wang,Jun Han,Zixuan Wang,Qingkai Zeng,Xiao Wang,Ruijie Wang,Jianxin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A cross-domain personalization method combining PAC-Bayes regularization with Meta-LoRA, using a meta-learned initialization, uncertainty-aware updates, and a decomposition of priors into user-visible prompts and domain-specific tokens. Demonstrates gains across benchmarks, notably reducing cross-domain degradation and improving unseen-user cold-start performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cross-domain personalization approaches either overfit under sparse evidence or entangle user preferences with source-domain artifacts. There is a need to calibrate adaptation to evidence quality and to separate user-specific from domain-specific factors.

Method: PAC-Bayes-regularized Meta-LoRA: uses a meta-learned LoRA initialization as adaptation start and prior center; updates are scaled by support-set size and predictive uncertainty; decomposes personalization priors into a user component via a human-readable prompt and a domain component via topology-preserving soft tokens for domain-specific conditioning.

Result: Empirical results show consistent gains over strong baselines across multiple tasks. On HiCUPID, cross-domain win-rate degradation is reduced by 47.9% relative to the best baseline, and unseen-user cold-start win rate improves by 110.2%.

Conclusion: The approach enables robust, scalable personalization under sparse evidence and allows stronger personalization as evidence accumulates, by separating user and domain priors and calibrating adaptation strength with evidence quality.

Abstract: Cross-domain zero- or few-shot personalization aims to generate user-preferred responses in unseen conversational domains from only a handful of target-domain interactions. Existing adaptation methods struggle to calibrate update magnitude under sparse evidence and thus overfit, whereas history-transfer methods often entangle user preferences with source-domain artifacts, yielding unreliable personalization priors and negative transfer. To calibrate adaptation to evidence quality, we propose PAC-Bayes-regularized Meta-LoRA, which uses a meta-learned LoRA initialization as both the adaptation start and prior center, while adjusting update strength according to support-set size and predictive uncertainty. This limits overfitting under sparse or ambiguous evidence while permitting stronger personalization as evidence grows. Controlled adaptation alone does not determine which preferences should transfer across domains or how they should be expressed. We therefore functionally decompose personalization priors into user and domain components, using a human-readable prompt for stable preferences and topology-preserving soft tokens for domain-specific hidden-space conditioning. Experiments across multiple benchmarks and personalization tasks show consistent gains over strong baselines. On HiCUPID, our method reduces cross-domain win-rate degradation by 47.9% relative to the best competing baseline and improves win rate by 110.2% under unseen-user cold start.

</details>


### [220] [Research Assistant: AstraZeneca's Agentic System for R&D](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12395)
*Piotr Grabowski,Mohamed Alameen,Jorge Bretones,Sabina Cardell,Miguel Carmona,Gavin Edwards,Ben Grainger,Sameh Hassan,Erik Jansson,Artur Kuziakhmetov,Albert Maristany,Hebatallah Mohamed,Andriy Nikolov,Sebastian Nilsson,Mark O'Donoghue,James Pacileo,Ashiq Sultan,Alex Voegele,Michael Ughetto*

Main category: cs.AI
