TL;DR: AstraZeneca's Research Assistant is an internal LLM-based platform that integrates literature, knowledge graphs, chemistry, clinical trials, safety resources, omics/expression data, and internal systems into a chat interface to support fast Q&A and multi-step research with evidence-grounded, source-linked responses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable scientists and clinicians to explore diverse biomedical questions quickly and with verifiable evidence across heterogeneous data sources, at scale in an enterprise setting.

Method: A chat-based, retrieval-augmented system that unifies multiple data sources (literature, knowledge graphs, chemistry, clinical trials, safety resources, expression data, internal systems), offers a fast direct-answer mode and a multi-step research workflow, and grounds responses with retrieved evidence linked to original sources; architecture, design choices, and deployment lessons are discussed.

Result: Provides an outline of the system architecture, the main design trade-offs, and lessons learned from deploying the system at scale to support day-to-day R&D workflows at AstraZeneca.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that an integrated, evidence-grounded LLM assistant can accelerate biomedical research in an enterprise setting, with practical guidance on architecture, product choices, and scaling learned from large-scale deployment.

Abstract: We describe Research Assistant, an internal LLM-based system developed at AstraZeneca to help scientists and clinicians explore biomedical questions across a broad range of data sources. The system provides a chat-style interface that brings together evidence from scientific literature, knowledge graphs, chemistry, clinical trials, safety resources, expression data, and internal experimental systems. It supports both a fast mode for direct question answering and a multi-step mode for more complex research tasks. Responses are grounded in retrieved evidence and linked back to the original sources, allowing users to review and further explore the underlying data. In this technical note, we outline the system architecture, the main design choices behind the product, and lessons learned from deploying it at scale to support day-to-day R&D workflows across AstraZeneca.

</details>


### [221] [Large Language Models Can Follow Instructions, But Not Many at Once: Phase Transitions in Compositional Constraint Satisfaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12426)
*Mariya I. Vasileva*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A large-scale, rule-based benchmark (CSE) evaluates how many simultaneous constraints LLMs can satisfy. It finds multiplicative degradation as constraints accumulate, with structural constraints more brittle than lexical ones, and reliable instruction following breaking down around 5–6 constraints for strong models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To characterize compositional constraint satisfaction in LLMs, addressing the gap where per-constraint performance does not reveal how performance scales with multiple simultaneous constraints.

Method: Constraint Saturation Evaluation (CSE) procedurally generates tasks with k constraints, each verified by a deterministic, rule-based verifier (no LLM judges). It covers 15 models, 36 constraint types, totaling 369,753 checks for k in 1–12. The analysis distinguishes per-constraint pass rates, joint constraint satisfaction, failure independence, and constraint typology (structural vs lexical).

Result: Three main findings: (1) per-constraint pass rate decays gradually, but the probability of satisfying all k constraints collapses (e.g., at k=8, ~41% per-constraint pass but only 5.7% succeed on all eight). (2) Constraints degrade unevenly: structural constraints lose about twice as much baseline capability per added constraint than lexical ones, reflecting a comprehension-maintenance gap. (3) Failures are nearly independent, yielding multiplicative accumulation; remaining coupling relates to shared output features rather than pairwise interference. Reliable instruction following breaks down beyond 5–6 simultaneous constraints; for the strongest model, probe-level success <50% at k=7, and for 12 of 15 models, 3 or fewer constraints suffice for reliability.

Conclusion: CSE provides a scalable, objective lens on the compositional limits of instruction following in LLMs and highlights the need to manage constraint interactions, informing future model design, evaluation, and potential mitigation strategies for multi-constraint tasks.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly deployed in settings that require simultaneous adherence to multiple explicit constraints - reasoning structure, safety boundaries, output schemas. Individual constraints are handled proficiently, but the compositional regime, where many must hold jointly, remains poorly characterized: how rapidly does performance degrade, what governs the degradation, and can the collapse be mitigated? We introduce Constraint Saturation Evaluation (CSE), a procedurally generated benchmark that systematically varies the number of simultaneous constraints (k), with every constraint scored by a deterministic, rule-based verifier and zero LLM-judge involvement: 15 models, 36 constraint types, 369,753 checks at k=1-12. Three findings emerge. First, per-constraint pass rate decays gradually and predictably, while the chance of satisfying all k constraints collapses - a model passing individual constraints at ~41% at k=8 succeeds on all eight just 5.7% of the time. Second, constraints do not degrade equally: structural constraints lose 2x more baseline capability per added constraint than lexical ones, ordered by a comprehension-maintenance gap that separates constraints requiring sustained tracking from binary decisions immune to composition. Third, failures are nearly independent, which is what makes the accumulation multiplicative; the residual coupling that does exist tracks shared output features rather than pairwise interference - a wrong sentence count fails every constraint that reads it. Reliable instruction following breaks down beyond 5-6 simultaneous constraints: probe-level success falls below 50% at 7 constraints for the strongest model, and at 3 or fewer for 12 of 15.

</details>


### [222] [MindMemOS: A Portable and Self-Evolving Memory Operating Layer for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12428)
*Kaichao Liang,Yuqi Cui,Hao Kong,Xinyuan Huang,Guohaotian Hou,Qingcan Kang,Liang Chen,Yiyang Yin,Ke Ye,Jiaquan Guo,Da Chen,Lingan Zeng,Yixing Peng,Rong Yao,Shixiong Kai,Mingxuan Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MindMemOS is a portable, self-evolving memory layer for AI agents that continuously refines memory schemas and skills using validation-driven evolution, dreaming consolidation, and human-in-the-loop feedback; it achieves high accuracy on LOCOMO (94.03%) and PersonaMem (70.63%), with a notable 9.2-point gain on SpreadsheetBench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory systems in current AI agents are typically fixed post-deployment, limiting long-term adaptation, personalized memory management, and the evolution of procedural knowledge in open-world settings.

Method: Introduce MindMemOS, a portable memory operating layer built on a unified entity property timestructure. Key components include MindMemEvolve (validation-driven evolutionary search for memory schemas), dreaming (consolidation of memories by merging duplicates and resolving conflicts), implicit corrective feedback (human-in-the-loop signals for revising memories), and MindSkillEvolve (transforms agent execution trajectories into refined reusable skills). Evaluation targets LOCOMO, PersonaMem, and SpreadsheetBench.

Result: Empirical results show high accuracy on LOCOMO (94.03%) and PersonaMem (70.63%), with MindSkillEvolve yielding a 9.2 percentage-point improvement on SpreadsheetBench over the initial skill baseline.

Conclusion: MindMemOS demonstrates a move toward adaptive, self-improving memory systems in AI agents, enabling scenario-specific memory modeling, higher-order pattern discovery, and continuous skill evolution, with strong benchmark performance and potential for scalable, long-horizon reasoning.

Abstract: Memory is a core component of AI agents, enabling them to accumulate experience, maintain personalization, and adapt over long-term interactions. However, existing memory systems often remain fixed after development, limiting their ability to adapt their memory models, organization strategies, and procedural knowledge through continued use. We present MindMemOS, a portable and self-evolving memory operating layer that organizes open-world information using a unified entity property timestructure. MindMemOS supports scenario-adaptive memory modeling, higher-order pattern discovery, autonomous memory refinement, and continuous skill evolution. Its MindMemEvolve algorithm employs validation-driven evolutionary search to optimize memory schemas for target scenarios, whiledreaming consolidates accumulated memories by merging redundant records and resolving conflicts. In addition, implicit corrective feedback serves as a human-in-the-loop signal for identifying and revising potentially inaccurate or misaligned memories. Its MindSkillEvolve algorithm further transforms agent execution trajectories into reusable and progressively refined skills. MindMemOS achieves 94.03% accuracy on LOCOMO and 70.63% on PersonaMem. MindSkillEvolve improves SpreadsheetBench success by 9.2 percentage points over the initial-skill baseline.

</details>


### [223] [Governed Persistent Memory: Source-Bound State Semantics and Fail-Closed Release for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12476)
*Guodong Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GPM is a governed, auditable, bitemporal memory model with source-bound admission and controlled release; across bounded benchmarks it achieves deterministic governance and non-regression, outperforming ungoverned baselines in end-to-end tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-term agent memory risks including contradictions, superseded or deleted records, and unsound outgoing claims. Retrieval alone cannot enforce lifecycle constraints; a governed, auditable memory with formal release semantics is proposed to ensure integrity, non-revival after retraction/deletion, and precise claim closure.

Method: Five executable clauses enforce ledger integrity, source binding, conflict isolation, non-revival after retraction/deletion, and exact claim closure over a fresh validated view. Evaluation uses a prespecified hash-frozen 3,600-case GPM-ReleaseBench; end-to-end service tests across eight query families; multiple generations (V3, V5) with sealed/pinned releases; production-code-independent finite model exploring 331,776 semantic and 1,990,656 query states; and a 100,000-trace three-engine differential.

Result: GPM matches all complete outcomes; the strongest complete policy matches 1,800/3,600 with 50% of violation cases yielding unmatched releases. In a sealed public evaluation, the governed lane is correct on 2,400/2,400 clusters versus 600/2,400 for ungoverned Qwen2.5-7B; it repairs all 1,800 baseline failures with no regression. The V5 reseal yields 2,400/2,400 per arm. A bounded finite-model search finds no full-contract counterexample, and a 100k-trace differential yields zero mismatches.

Conclusion: Results are bounded and implementation-specific, not open-world model accuracy or claims about world truth. Governed answers in the sealed service are deterministic; the 7B comparison reflects ungoverned behavior rather than the LLM's intrinsic accuracy.

Abstract: Long-term agent memory is usually treated as select--store--retrieve, but retrieval does not decide whether contradictory, superseded, retracted, deleted, or stale records may support an outgoing claim. We introduce Governed Persistent Memory (GPM), an auditable bitemporal state-transition model with source-bound admission, derived lifecycle state, current public barriers, and fail-closed structured release. Five executable clauses cover ledger integrity, source binding, conflict isolation, non-revival after retraction or deletion, and exact claim closure over a fresh view at one verified head.
  On a prespecified hash-frozen 3,600-case GPM-ReleaseBench, GPM matches all complete outcomes; the strongest of three intentionally simple complete policies matches 1,800/3,600 and makes unmatched releases on 50% of violation cases. A separate sealed end-to-end service evaluation exercises real ingestion and release across eight query families. In its publicly disclosed V3 arm, the governed lane is correct on 2,400/2,400 clusters versus 600/2,400 for ungoverned local Qwen2.5-7B; it repairs all 1,800 baseline failures with no regression (one-sided 95% lower bounds 99.875% and 99.834%). A later V5 reseal over Chinese- and English-command arms, with generation-date pinning and no post-freeze reducer amendment, again obtains 2,400/2,400 per arm. A production-code-independent finite model explores 331,776 semantic and 1,990,656 query states without a full-contract counterexample, and a 100,000-trace three-engine differential yields zero mismatches.
  These are bounded contract and implementation results, not open-world model accuracy or evidence of world truth. Governed answers in the sealed service evaluation are deterministic service outputs; the 7B result is the ungoverned comparison, not a claim that a language model itself became perfectly accurate.

</details>


### [224] [$\varepsilon$-MemEvo: Adaptive Cross-Task Memory Transfer for LLM Program Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12522)
*Aofan Liu,Shiyuan Song,Yiyan Qi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ε-MemEvo is a cross-task memory framework for LLM program evolution that stores task-agnostic tactic memories as natural-language summaries and uses adaptive gating to inject memories, achieving consistent 8.7% AUCC gains and 9.4% faster early convergence across 8 benchmarks with minimal overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the lack of cross-task knowledge transfer in LLM-driven program evolution systems (e.g., FunSearch, AlphaEvolve) and mitigate negative transfer due to semantically mismatched memories by using task-agnostic, interpretable memories with selective injection.

Method: Store compact natural-language summaries of successful strategies as tactic memories. Introduce an adaptive injection gate to decide when and how strongly to inject retrieved memories. Evaluate on 8 diverse optimization benchmarks via a content-level Leave-One-Out protocol using the GPT-5 backbone.

Result: Consistent improvements: mean relative AUCC gain +8.7% over AdaEvolve on all tasks; early convergence improved by +9.4% on average. Ablations show naive memory injection can cause failure, while adaptive gating is robust across five ablations. Memory data is interpretable: posterior favors skip during improving search and shifts from skip to hint across plateaus. Computational overhead is <1%.

Conclusion: Cross-task transfer in LLM program evolution is feasible with task-agnostic memory summaries and adaptive gating, enabling safer, interpretable, and efficient reuse of prior experience across tasks with different APIs and evaluators.

Abstract: LLM-based program evolution systems such as FunSearch and AlphaEvolve have shown strong ability to discover novel algorithms, but typically optimize each task in isolation, discarding search experience after completion. We introduce $\varepsilon$-MemEvo, a framework for cross-task knowledge transfer in LLM program evolution. $\varepsilon$-MemEvo stores prior experience as task-agnostic tactic memories: compact natural-language summaries of successful algorithmic strategies rather than raw code, enabling transfer across tasks with different APIs and evaluators. To avoid negative transfer from semantically mismatched memories, $\varepsilon$-MemEvo uses an adaptive injection gate that decides whether retrieved memories should be injected, and at what intensity. We evaluate $\varepsilon$-MemEvo on 8 diverse optimization benchmarks spanning mathematical optimization and systems engineering, using a content-level Leave-One-Out protocol that excludes target-task memory entries. On the primary GPT-5 backbone, $\varepsilon$-MemEvo improves AUCC over AdaEvolve on all 8 tasks, with a mean relative gain of +8.7%, and improves early-stage convergence by +9.4% on average. Ablations show that naive memory injection can fail catastrophically, while adaptive gating remains safe across all five ablation tasks. The data-updated posterior is interpretable in observed states: it favors skip during improving search and shifts from skip to hint across early and late plateaus. These gains incur less than 1% computational overhead.

</details>


### [225] [CAS: A Causal Attribution Score for Local and Global Explainable Artificial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12555)
*Michael Georgiades,Charalambia Varnava*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces the Causal Attribution Score (CAS), a local-to-global causal explainer that translates interventional contrasts into Local/Signed Local CAS and Global CAS summaries, enabling explicit intervention-targeted causal explanations beyond predictive SHAP.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predictive explanation methods explain model outputs but not real-world intervention effects; there is a need for causal attribution that links outcomes to interventions and heterogeneous treatment effects.

Method: Formulates an interventional coalition game, allocates joint intervention contrast via causal Shapley contributions, converts raw effects to Local CAS, Signed Local CAS, and two Global CAS summaries; emphasizes a reporting layer rather than a new Shapley formula.

Result: In known-truth benchmarks, Local CAS mean absolute error (0.107) is better than one-at-a-time normalisation (0.173) and global normalised ATE (0.213); advantage grows with interaction strength. On two empirical DoubleML datasets, predictive SHAP rankings diverge from Feature-CAS rankings of treatment-effect modifiers (e.g., dep1 shifts from rank 13 to rank 2 in Pennsylvania); CAS highlights heterogeneity in causal effects rather than just predictive power.

Conclusion: CAS provides added value by separating what predicts an outcome from what explains heterogeneity in an estimated causal effect; offers an explicit intervention target through a local-to-global causal reporting layer.

Abstract: Predictive explanation methods attribute a model output; they do not, by themselves, attribute an intervention effect on the real-world outcome. We introduce the Causal Attribution Score (CAS), a compact score architecture for causal explanation. CAS starts from an identified interventional coalition game, allocates the joint intervention contrast with causal Shapley contributions, and converts those raw outcome-scale effects into Local CAS, Signed Local CAS, and two complementary Global CAS summaries. The innovation is not a new Shapley formula, but a local-to-global causal reporting layer with an explicit intervention target. In the known-truth benchmark, eight repeated primary-interaction simulations (n = 2,200 each, three actions) gave mean Local CAS MAE of 0.107 for coalition-aware CAS, compared with 0.173 for one-at-a-time normalisation and 0.213 for a global normalised absolute ATE vector. The paired advantage over one-at-a-time normalisation increased from -0.003 under additivity to 0.091 under strong interactions. On both empirical DoubleML datasets, 401(k) eligibility/net financial assets (n = 9,915) and Pennsylvania reemployment bonus/unemployment duration (n = 5,099), predictive SHAP/TreeSHAP rankings differed materially from Feature-CAS rankings of treatment-effect modifiers. In Pennsylvania, dep1 (exactly one dependent) moved from predictive global rank 13 to Feature-CAS rank 2 and was the leading local Feature-CAS modifier. These results isolate the added value of separating what predicts the outcome from what explains heterogeneity in an estimated causal effect.

</details>


### [226] [Trie Automata for Constrained Decoding over Large Finite Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12574)
*Xingzi Xu,Karim Bouyarmane*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces a trie automaton to accelerate constrained decoding for finite-set outputs, exploiting shared prefixes and known cardinality with Aho-Corasick to precompute per-node token masks; enables stateless serving and massively increases throughput on large vocabularies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Constrained decoding for outputs drawn from a large finite set (thousands+ tokens) is bottlenecked by general-purpose grammar compilation (cardinality wall), hindering speed and scalability in production LLM serving.

Method: Build a trie automaton that uses Aho-Corasick multi-pattern matching to precompute per-node token masks, enabling per-step valid-token checks to be stateless and independent of the set size; enables batch-serving optimizations by avoiding the guided decoding pipeline.

Result: Substantial speedups: per-token computation 0.65 μs vs 5.8 μs (7× faster); 2–6.5× faster compilation at K≥300; end-to-end vLLM throughput 219 req/s vs 7.5 req/s (batch size 256; 29× overall); across 7 tokenizer families (32K–262K vocab), sub-100 ms compilation up to K=10,000; per-step cost remains flat with set size; guarantees 100% output validity.

Conclusion: The trie automaton yields significant algorithmic speedups and deployment savings by converting constrained decoding into a precomputable, stateless process, enabling scalable, 100%-validity constrained outputs for large finite sets.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly need to generate structured outputs that conform to predefined schemas, with one common constraint being selection from a finite set of valid strings. Current constrained decoding systems handle this through general-purpose grammar compilation, which becomes prohibitively slow as the number of valid values grows into the thousands, a cardinality wall. We introduce the trie automaton, a specialized mechanism that exploits finite-set structure (shared prefixes, bounded depth, known cardinality) via Aho-Corasick multi-pattern matching to precompute per-node token masks. The trie achieves 7X faster per-step valid-token computation (0.65 us vs. 5.8 us) compared to XGrammar, one of the primary backends in vLLM and SGLang, and 2--6.5X faster compilation at K >= 300. Because precomputed masks enable a stateless serving path that bypasses the guided decoding pipeline, this advantage compounds in batch serving: end-to-end vLLM throughput reaches 219 req/s vs. XGrammar's 7.5 req/s at batch size 256 (29X). The 29X combines the algorithmic speedup with integration-path savings that only precomputed masks can unlock. Across seven tokenizer families (32K--262K vocabulary), the trie maintains sub-100ms compilation up to K = 10,000 and flat per-step cost regardless of set size, while guaranteeing 100% output validity.

</details>


### [227] [Reasoning Jury: Multi-Model Consensus for Evaluating Reasoning Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12585)
*Congchao Wang,Diwakar Singh,Qiaozi Gao,Spyros Matsoukas,Yang Liu,Mahdi Namazifar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Reasoning Jury replaces single-model judging with a moderated jury of LLMs to improve fidelity in identifying reasoning defects in long traces, achieving higher accuracy than frontier models at a fraction of the cost, and enabling deeper analysis of model failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable identification of defects in long reasoning traces is hard for single judges, frontier models are constrained by use guardrails, and there is a need for cost-effective, high-fidelity judgments to improve data curation, training signals, and runtime feedback for reasoning LLMs.

Method: A jury of open-weight LLMs (e.g., gpt-oss-120b) operates with a moderated consensus mechanism. A moderator conducts deliberations where jurors critique each other’s judgments, adjust initial votes, and the moderator consolidates judgments to form a consensus. The system yields aggregated verdicts from initial verdicts, deliberations, and consolidation.

Result: The Reasoning Jury substantially outperforms frontier models (opus-4.6, sonnet-4.6, gemini-3.1-pro) in correctly identifying reasoning defects. The aggregated cost is 8–15% of running frontier models as judges.

Conclusion: A jury-based judging framework improves fidelity and reduces cost for identifying reasoning defects in long traces, enabling better failure-mode understanding and more effective data-driven guidance for model development and evaluation.

Abstract: Improving reasoning LLMs requires the ability to judge the quality of long reasoning traces for effective reasoning data curation, strong training signals during reinforcement learning, and an in-depth understanding of reasoning behaviors during model performance evaluation. Additionally, surfacing reasoning mistakes that the model makes would enable improving the model's performance at runtime through providing feedback. Due to the difficulty of this complex task on long reasoning traces, single-model judges (even frontier models) do not do well at identifying reasoning defects. Additionally, leveraging frontier models during online training of reasoning LLMs is generally prohibited due to guardrails in terms of use. In this work, we introduce Reasoning Jury, a system that replaces the single judge with a jury of LLMs and a moderated consensus mechanism, to improve the fidelity of judgments for identifying reasoning defects. In reasoning jury, defects of a reasoning trace and their severity are surfaced through a deliberation where a moderator conducts a discussion amongst the jury where the jurors critique each other's judgments and get to modify their initial votes. The moderator derives a consensus through deliberation amongst jurors or consolidation of judgements. We show that Reasoning Jury with a jury of open-weight models (e.g., gpt-oss-120b) is able to significantly outperform frontier models (opus-4.6, sonnet-4.6, and gemini-3.1-pro) at correctly identifying reasoning defects. Besides accuracy performance improvements, the aggregated cost of the jury (initial verdicts, deliberations, consolidation, etc.) is a fraction (8 to 15%) of the cost of running frontier models in LLM-as-a-judge setup. We also show how these judgements can be leveraged to understand failure modes of reasoning LLMs on benchmarks, which allows much deeper understanding of a model's performance.

</details>


### [228] [Auditable agentic AI for evidence-grounded thyroid ultrasound diagnosis and reporting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12590)
*Haifan Gong,Shiyu Chen,Bodong Wang,Yuqi Wang,Shijie Wang,Guoliang You,Xinyu Xiong,Haowei Wang,Mingzhi Mao,Dexing Kong,Qinghua Liu,Wei Lou,Fei Chen,Guanbin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An agentic AI system (ThyroidXAgent) coordinates multiple diagnostic tools for thyroid ultrasound, producing auditable, clinician-correctable outputs; it achieves high segmentation accuracy and malignant-classification performance across diverse datasets, improves reporting consistency, and reduces task times.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome fragmentation in AI thyroid ultrasound tasks (localization, measurement, risk stratification, reporting) by providing an integrated, auditable, clinician-correctable workflow that can operate across heterogeneous multi-center data.

Method: Develop ThyroidXAgent on OpenThyroidDB (≈0.3M ultrasound images, 24k paired reports). Evaluate on 28,458 test cases (including 8,721 from 35 private centers). Metrics: Dice for nodule segmentation, AUROC for benign/malignant classification, lymph-node metastasis and follicular-vs-papillary carcinoma; compare report generation against multimodal LMs; introduce ThyClinScore as a lesion-level semantic metric; assess physician accuracy, report consistency, and time savings.

Result: Mean Dice for segmentation: 87.21%. AUROC for benign–malignant: 0.9466. AUROCs: lymph-node metastasis 0.864; follicular vs papillary 0.805. Evidence-grounded report assembly outperforms LM baselines. ThyClinScore correlates with a location-aware LM judge. Physician classification accuracy improved; report consistency increased from 70.3% to 86.2%; segmentation and reporting times reduced by 35.9% and 27.4% respectively.

Conclusion: Auditable, clinician-correctable agentic AI can enhance thyroid ultrasound diagnosis and reporting, supporting integrated, multi-task workflows across heterogeneous data, with tangible gains in accuracy, consistency, and efficiency.

Abstract: Thyroid ultrasound diagnosis requires coordinated lesion localization, measurement, risk stratification and reporting, yet most AI systems address these tasks in isolation and provide limited support for clinical review. We present ThyroidXAgent, a clinician-interactive agentic AI system that coordinates specialized diagnostic tools and stores their outputs as an auditable case-level evidence record. The system was developed using OpenThyroidDB, a multicentre, multitask resource integrating approximately 0.3 million ultrasound images and 24,000 paired reports, and was evaluated on 28,458 non-overlapping test cases, including 8,721 cases from 35 centres in the private NHC-MISD-TUS cohort. Across heterogeneous datasets, ThyroidXAgent achieved a mean Dice score of 87.21 percent for nodule segmentation and a mean AUROC of 0.9466 for benign-malignant classification. The same workflow supported lymph-node metastasis prediction and follicular versus papillary thyroid carcinoma classification, with AUROCs of 0.864 and 0.805, respectively. For report generation, evidence-grounded assembly outperformed multimodal language-model baselines across three cohorts. ThyClinScore, a lesion-level clinical semantic metric introduced here, showed the strongest correlation with a location-aware language-model judge. ThyroidXAgent improved physician classification accuracy, increased report diagnostic consistency from 70.3 percent to 86.2 percent, and reduced segmentation and reporting time by 35.9 percent and 27.4 percent, respectively. These findings support auditable, clinician-correctable agentic AI for thyroid ultrasound diagnosis and reporting.

</details>


### [229] [DiG-bench: Discovery in Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12593)
*Ruairidh M. Battleday,Kai Sandbrink,Jimi Cullen-Drohan,Zihan Yan,Timothy Muller,Clare Maguire,Ales Kubicek,Fraser Greenlee-Scott,Sukrit Sumant,Tri Dao,Jürgen Schmidhuber,Michal Valko,Joshua Tenenbaum,Thomas L. Griffiths,Zeb Kurth-Nelson,James C. R. Whittington*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DiG-bench is a discovery-focused AI benchmark with 70 interactive games of unknown rules and win conditions, encoded as strings, spanning seven difficulty tiers. 21 games are public; all 70 are solvable by at least one human on the first attempt; the benchmark targets evaluating rule discovery through experimentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI benchmarks largely measure optimization and planning in fixed environments, offering limited assessment of an agent's ability to discover new rules and objectives through experimentation in controlled settings. DiG-bench addresses this gap by providing interactive, unknown-rule tasks with tiered difficulty and human baselines.

Method: Each game is encoded as a short string and contains unique transformation rules that must be discovered via interaction and experimentation. Levels present challenges to test rule discovery, with win conditions also unknown. The benchmark spans seven difficulty tiers, with a public subset (21 games) for open benchmarking and the remainder kept private for secure evaluation.

Result: All 70 games were solvable by at least one human on the first attempt. The 21-public subset enables public benchmarking, while the private remainder allows secure evaluation. The lower tiers are routinely solvable by multiple models, whereas the highest tier tests agentic capabilities.

Conclusion: DiG-bench fills a gap in AI evaluation by enabling systematic assessment of discovery from experimentation under unknown objectives, providing a scalable, multi-tier benchmark with human baselines and both public and private evaluation tracks to drive progress in discovery-oriented AI research.

Abstract: Discovery---formulating novel generalizations---is a central part of the scientific process. Despite its importance, there is a gap in the current AI benchmark landscape, with few benchmarks directly probing the capacity for discovering new knowledge with experimentation in controlled environments where the objective is unknown. To address this gap, we release a new benchmark: DiG-bench (Discovery in Games). DiG-bench consists of a set of 70 independent games. Each game is encoded as a short string and has unique transformation rules that must be discovered through interaction and experimentation. The levels of the game present a series of challenges to test whether the rules have been discovered, where the win conditions for each level are also unknown. We provide games at seven tiers of difficulty for AI agents. The lowest tier is routinely solvable by multiple models, while the highest tier challenges the best models in agentic harnesses. All 70 games were solved by at least one human on first attempt. A subset of 21 games is released publicly, and the remainder is held private for secure evaluation.

</details>


### [230] [Dead text or binding clause? Measuring and restoring constraint influence in black-box LLM dialogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12599)
*Haoyuan Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A ledger-based framework (Sysname) to measure, predict, and repair relapse of withdrawn constraints in multi-turn dialogues, achieving measurable reduction in relapse via ahead-of-time compilation, per-clause probes, and budget-aware repairs, with predictable cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Behavioral relapse (inertia) undermines constraint enforcement in dialogue models. Existing work lacks per-clause measurement, pre-delivery prediction, and budget-bounded repair. The paper proposes a contract ledger, diagnostic probing, and repair ladder to close these gaps.

Method: Introduce a contract ledger that maps each constraint to an executable checker, records revocations as tombstones, and compiles the net state ahead of time. Use a sequential ablation probe to quantify per-clause adherence and incremental relapse. Implement a repair ladder operating under token and attempt budgets. Evaluate on a dataset with verified checkers, measuring relapse, predictive AUROC for relapse before delivery, and effects of tombstone notes and repairs; report cost overhead per result.

Result: With matched checkers, models, and budgets, ahead-of-time compilation significantly reduces relapse compared with a no-ledger verifier-retry baseline; adaptive ladder interventions provide no detectable extra gain. Relapse increases with constraint load for 8B models, while stronger models exhibit a lower baseline. The relapse predictor (probe) achieves a measurable AUROC, enabling pre-delivery forecast of relapse. A one-sentence tombstone note recovers about one-third of the compilation effect. Delivery cost per result remains modest, making relapse a measurable, predictable, and repairable property of the dialogue state.

Conclusion: Ledger-based pre-compilation, together with diagnostic measurement and a budget-aware repair ladder, makes constraint relapse a manageable, predictable phenomenon in multi-turn dialogue systems, enabling targeted repairs and reliable state tracking under realistic compute budgets.

Abstract: Multi-turn dialogues let users revoke constraints as easily as impose them, but revocation does not reliably take effect: models keep enacting withdrawn requirements (occasionally beneath comments asserting their removal), a failure we call \emph{behavioral relapse}, or revocation inertia. No existing instrument measures this influence per clause, predicts it before delivery, or repairs it under matched budgets. \sysname{} closes the three gaps through the model API alone: a contract ledger pairs every constraint with an executable checker, records revocations as tombstones, and compiles the net constraint state ahead of time into a single specification; a sequential ablation probe measures per-clause adherence and incremental behavioral effect; a repair ladder operates under token- and attempt-matched budgets. On \dataname{} (\NTasks{} HumanEval tasks, \NClauses{} verified checkers), relapse at an 8B operating point climbs from \ScaleDelayedMTwo{} to \ScaleDelayedMEight{} as constraint load grows, while stronger models sit at floor. Under matched checkers, model, and budget, ahead-of-time compilation significantly reduces relapse against a no-ledger verifier-retry baseline (\RestoreDiff{}, 95\% CI \RestoreDiffCI{}, $p$ \RestoreDiffP{}); adaptive ladder interventions stacked on top add no detectable gain (95\% confidence excludes gains $\geq$ \LadderExcludedGain{}). The probe predicts relapse before delivery (AUROC \AurocPrimary{}); a one-sentence tombstone note recovers about a third of the compilation effect and survives a placebo control. At \CostDeliveryFactor{} delivery overhead and \CostTotalHedged{} of API compute for every result, revocation failure becomes a measurable, predictable, and repairable property of dialogue state rather than an invisible one.

</details>


### [231] [@skills: Attention is all you have](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12610)
*Li Yin,Zhi Li,Zhan Shi,Haoran Zhang,Haebin Seong,Zhangyang,Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Decouples skill content, persistence, and triggering into a read-only path-based protocol (@skills), enabling scalable long-tail skills without resident prompts, via Git-tracked copies and an optional hub for discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address prompt-space scarcity and the long-tail of agent skills by separating content from residency and enabling lightweight, path-addressable access that scales beyond installation-based approaches.

Method: Introduce a protocol that makes reading a skill sufficient to use it, with copies vendored into a project's Git tree for adaptation; uses one .gitignore line to incur residency cost; directories act as menus; no manifest/lockfile; SKILL.md unchanged; open spec on GitHub; hub for corpus-wide search optionally linked; AdaL CLI implements the protocol.

Result: A modular, installable package (@skills) that transforms any file-reading, command-executing agent into a client via a single instruction file; enables path-based addressing of skills, community/private collections, and optional hub-based discovery and ranking.

Conclusion: If adopted, this approach could reduce prompt residency bottlenecks, improve reusability and governance of skills, and enable long-tail scalability. Risks include security/privacy of copied skills, reliance on Git workflows, discovery fragmentation without the hub, and governance/standardization challenges; empirical evaluation and security considerations are needed.

Abstract: There are 56,804 public agent skills today, and teams write many more privately. The dominant delivery model is installation: once installed, a skill's description remains in the system prompt, competing for fewer than 100 reliable trigger slots. This leaves the long tail with no practical path to use and forces teams' own playbooks to compete for the same scarce space. We observe that installation bundles three separable functions: content, persistence, and automatic triggering. Only the last requires prompt residency. We therefore propose @skills, an open protocol that separates them. A path addresses any skill, subtree, or collection, and reading a skill is sufficient to use it, so nothing is installed or made resident. The operation vendors a copy at the same path into a project's Git-tracked tree for adaptation and ownership. The operation adds one .gitignore-style line, the only element that costs prompt residency. A directory is a menu, making bundles ordinary directories rather than all-or-nothing units. The protocol requires no manifest, lockfile, or registration, and SKILL.md remains unchanged. @skills is additive, ships as an installable package, and turns any agent that can read files and run commands into a client through a single instruction file. Its open specification is at https://github.com/SylphAI-Inc/atskills and it is implemented in the AdaL CLI at https://adalagent.ai . Because paths address skills well but cannot find them, the protocol is paired with a free hub at https://atskills.one for corpus-wide search and ranking, repository-free hosting, private and team collections, and one-screen authoring. The hub is optional: gh: and local paths resolve without it, and indexed GitHub skills retain their gh: identities. Install less, use more.

</details>


### [232] [Jagged Judges: Epistemic Stability Under Silence, Pressure, and Persistence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12645)
*Justin Zhao,Himaghna Bhattacharjee,Hannah Korevaar,Bhaktipriya Radharapu,Khalid El-Arini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The Wiggle Framework reveals substantial epistemic instability in LLM judges across three axes; most judges wiggle under pressure, and such wiggles are typically net-corrupting with respect to ground truth; baseline majority strength helps predict wiggle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accuracy on golden data fails to capture stability; robust judging requires resilience to re-prompting, challenge, and adaptive pressure.

Method: Introduce Wiggle Framework; three dimensions: Mechanical Consistency, Single-turn Conviction, Multi-turn Persistence; evaluate 9 frontier models on 14 tasks (safety, toxicity, AI writing detection, political-response). Apply static pushback and adversarial LLM persuader; measure verdict flips and ground truth alignment; identify predictive signal (majority strength).

Result: All models show substantial wiggle: 25-71% verdict flips under static pushback; 62-91% with adversarial persuader. Pressure to change verdict is almost always net-corrupting wrt ground truth. Majority strength is the strongest single-shot predictor of wiggle.

Conclusion: Provides the first apples-to-apples cross-dataset comparison of mechanical, conformity, and persuadability tests in judging; emphasizes need for robust, epistemically stable judging systems; the Wiggle Framework offers a unified diagnostic across domains.

Abstract: LLM judges have become central infrastructure for model evaluations, online grading, and reward modeling. Judges are typically validated by accuracy on golden data, but accuracy says little about whether they are stable under re-prompting, challenge, or sustained pushback. We introduce the \emph{Wiggle Framework}, a unified stress test for epistemic stability in LLM judges. The framework decomposes judge robustness along three dimensions: Mechanical Consistency (stability under re-prompting and reframing), Single-turn Conviction (stability under a single challenge), and Multi-turn Persistence (stability under sustained or adaptive pressure). We use the framework to study 9 frontier models across 14 judging tasks spanning safety, toxicity, AI writing detection, and political-response evaluation. Every model exhibits substantial wiggle as a judge --- flipping verdicts 25--71\% of the time under static pushback, and 62--91\% with an adversarial LLM persuader. Critically, we find that pressure that succeeds in changing a judge's verdict is almost always net-corrupting with respect to ground truth. Beyond the framework itself, we identify baseline jury majority strength as the most effective single-shot signal for anticipating which items wiggle. Taken together, this is the first apples-to-apples cross-dataset comparison of mechanical, conformity, and persuadability tests in a judging context.

</details>


### [233] [SteerBench-Work: A Benchmark for Agent Steering at Action Boundaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12654)
*Oguz Serdar,Cuneyt Mertayak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes SteerBench-Work, a bidirectional, incident-grounded benchmark to evaluate gating decisions (proceed vs hold) at LLM tool-use boundaries across domains; 106 scenarios with evidence-reversed mirrors and calibration controls; models show a pronounced bias toward overly conservative holds and relatively rare unsafe approvals, with calibration and higher capability affecting gate behavior; leaderboard at steerbench.com.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically evaluate the boundary decision (gate) that long-running LLM agents must make before executing actions through tools, ensuring safe and correct operation across professional domains. Addresses the gap between general capability and steering calibration.

Method: SteerBench-Work uses incident-anchored, bidirectional scenarios (106 total) drawn from public incidents, with evidence-reversed mirrors and calibration controls. Each scenario presents a proposed action and available evidence; models output a gate decision (proceed vs hold) which is scored on boundary correctness. Evaluation spans about 30 model conditions with balanced labels (proceed/hold).

Result: Across 30 model conditions, failures predominantly occur as over-refusal: 28.1% of opportunities with authorized, evidence-cleared work are wrongly held; unsafe work is wrongly allowed only 1.0%. Risk-resolved commits are hardest; evidence-reversed mirrors of famous incidents yield 63.8% accuracy vs 98.5% on the incidents themselves. Higher general capability correlates with increased over-refusal; additional reasoning improves weak gates but not calibrated gates.

Conclusion: SteerBench-Work highlights the gap between model capability and steering calibration, showing that even capable models can be overly cautious or inconsistent at tool-use boundaries. The benchmark enables systematic evaluation and aligns with real-world risk by using incident-based evidence and mirrors. A public leaderboard (steerbench.com) supports ongoing benchmarking.

Abstract: Long-running LLM agents act through tools, and a single step can send an email, merge a pull request, or wire a payment. The steering decision is the pre-commit choice at that boundary: proceed, or hold for human or policy review. We introduce SteerBench-Work, an incident-anchored, bidirectional benchmark for that decision in workplace agents across developer operations, customer service, finance, legal, medical, HR, and security.
  Release v2026-05 contains 106 scenarios anchored in public incidents, paired evidence-reversed mirrors, and calibration controls, with labels split nearly evenly between proceed and hold so the two error directions get near-identical numbers of chances. A model sees the proposed action and the available evidence, returns a gate decision, and is scored on whether it crosses or holds the boundary correctly. Across 30 model conditions the failures run almost entirely in one direction: models wrongly hold authorized, evidence-cleared work on 28.1% of opportunities and wrongly allow unsafe work on 1.0%. The hardest cases are risk-resolved commits, where signed or structured evidence has already cleared a real risk trigger, and models score markedly worse on evidence-reversed mirrors of famous incidents (63.8%) than on the incidents themselves (98.5%). General capability is not the same as steering calibration: higher-capability models often over-refuse at the commit boundary, and more reasoning can repair a weak gate while leaving a calibrated one flat. The public leaderboard is at steerbench.com.

</details>


### [234] [General Probabilities of Causation with Causal Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12657)
*Xin Shu,Zhen Lei,Ang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This work tightens bounds for multivalued probabilities of causation (PoCs) by incorporating causal information from covariates and mediators, extending prior binary/multivalued bounds. It presents theoretical bounds, with toy examples and simulations showing tighter bounds than existing nonbinary bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve partial identification of multivalued PoCs by leveraging additional causal knowledge (covariates and mediators) to sharpen bounds.

Method: Derive tighter multivalued PoC bounds by conditioning on covariates and mediators and integrating their causal information into the bound framework, building on Li & Pearl and Shu et al.; provide theoretical derivations, illustrative toy examples, and simulation studies to compare bound tightness.

Result: New tighter bounds for multivalued PoCs; demonstrated via toy examples and simulations that the proposed bounds are tighter than existing nonbinary bounds.

Conclusion: Incorporating covariate and mediator information strengthens the partial identification of multivalued PoCs and yields practically tighter bounds, with potential applicability in causal inference tasks.

Abstract: Probabilities of causation (PoCs) characterize individual causal responses that cannot be directly observed and therefore generally require partial identification. Tian and Pearl first derived theoretically sharp bounds for binary PoCs, including the probability of necessity (PN), the probability of sufficiency (PS), and the probability of necessity and sufficiency (PNS). Mueller et al. subsequently tightened the bounds for binary PNS by incorporating causal information encoded in covariates and mediators. More recently, Li and Pearl, as well as Shu et al., extended PoCs to multivalued settings and derived corresponding theoretical bounds. These developments naturally raise the question of whether additional causal knowledge can further tighten the bounds in multivalued settings. This paper addresses this question by deriving tighter bounds for multivalued PoCs through the incorporation of causal information encoded in covariates and mediators. We illustrate the theoretical results with toy examples, while simulation studies further demonstrate that the proposed bounds are tighter than existing nonbinary bounds.

</details>


### [235] [Designing AI Pipelines for Decision-Ready ITSM Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12670)
*Archan Dutta,Yash Dharmadhikari,Marat Valiullin,Rahul Guha,Alexander Liss*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A sociotechnical AI pipeline for ITSM analytics that converts heterogeneous ITSM exports into a multilevel decision-support artifact using LLM-based schema normalization and clustering, achieving high stakeholder-perceived interpretability, actionability, trust, and intended use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ITSM data are voluminous and heterogeneous, hindering decision-making for sales/executive stakeholders; need for transformation, abstraction, and human-centered design to produce actionable insights.

Method: Design-science-inspired pipeline integrating LLM-based schema normalization, HDBSCAN sub-topic clustering, and hierarchical agglomerative clustering to produce executive Main-topics and granular Sub-topics; evaluated via stakeholder study across six artifacts and five raters from Sales Engineering and customer success.

Result: All four decision-support metrics (interpretability, actionability, trust, likelihood of use) exceed 4.0/5 on average; trust most consistent signal; demonstrates IS problem framing of ITSM analytics as transformation/abstraction/human-centric.

Conclusion: The pipeline provides a multilevel decision-support artifact for ITSM and positions ITSM analytics as an IS challenge rooted in transformation and human-centered design; potential for actionable intelligence for sales/CS teams.

Abstract: IT service management (ITSM) systems accumulate large volumes of heterogeneous ticket data that are difficult for sales and executive stakeholders to convert into actionable intelligence. This paper presents a sociotechnical AI pipeline, designed and evaluated following design science research principles, that transforms raw ITSM exports into a multilevel decision-support artifact. The pipeline combines LLM-based schema normalization, HDBSCAN sub-topic clustering, and hierarchical agglomerative clustering to generate executive-facing Main-topics and granular Sub-topics. A stakeholder evaluation across six artifacts and five raters from Sales Engineering and customer success roles shows that all four decision-support metrics, interpretability, actionability, trust, and likelihood of use, on average exceed 4.0 out of 5.0, with trust as the most consistent signal. The findings position ITSM analytics as an Information Systems (IS) problem of transformation, abstraction, and human-centered design.

</details>


### [236] [On the Expressive Power of Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12671)
*Phokion Kolaitis,Rik Sengupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A survey framing multi-layer transformers as language recognizers and reviewing circuit complexity results that bound their expressive power under resource constraints, linking transformer architectures to circuit classes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To formalize the expressive capacity of transformers and understand their computational limits, using circuit complexity as a rigorous framework to compare against classical models.

Method: Synthesize and categorize selected results from the literature that map transformer operations (e.g., layered architecture, attention, precision) to circuit-like resources and classes, providing a structured overview.

Result: The overview collects mappings between transformer parameterizations and circuit models, clarifying how depth, width, attention, and numeric precision relate to the computational strength needed to recognize languages. It highlights what has been shown to be achievable within certain resource bounds and notes gaps where results are sparse.

Conclusion: Circuit complexity offers a natural, rigorous lens to study transformer expressiveness. This survey highlights established correspondences, illuminates the roles of architectural resources, and outlines open questions for deeper theoretical understanding.

Abstract: Multi-layer transformers form the critical component of essentially all large language models (LLMs) in use today. Because of their ubiquity and computational capability, there is a rapidly growing body of work that aims to precisely calibrate the expressive power of transformers as language recognizers by comparing them against standard models of computation studied for decades by the theoretical computer science community. In this endeavor, circuit complexity has by and large emerged as the "correct" branch of computational complexity to analyze the expressive power of transformers; the reason is that parameterizing transformers by the various resources they use, such as attention and precision, leads to direct comparisons with different classes of circuits parameterized by resources such as type of gates, size, and depth. Here, we present an overview of selected results that delineate the expressive power of transformers using concepts and methods from circuit complexity.

</details>


### [237] [Lines and Ladders: A Context-Aware Multi-Agent Framework for Large-Scale Retail Price Taxonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12674)
*Ravi Teja Chunduri,Srikaran Reddy Boya,Deep Narayan Mishra,Ajay Kumar B,Karthik Kumaran,Pranay Kona*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a scalable multi-agent framework with LLMs to automate Lines and Ladders pricing taxonomies for price consistency in large catalogs; achieves high F1 and precision/recall in production on real data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual governance is infeasible for millions of items; price inconsistencies harm value perception and sales.

Method: 3-agent system, specialized LLM agents identify attributes, extract multi-modal values, apply hierarchical grouping; constructs Lines and Ladders taxonomy; context-aware; evaluated on enterprise data; production deployment.

Result: F1=0.83 for Lines; >90% precision, >75% recall in Food & Consumables; 80.2% assignment accuracy in unstructured General Merchandise catalog.

Conclusion: Demonstrates scalable automation for pricing taxonomy construction; reduces cognitive overload; effective in production; improves consistency.

Abstract: Maintaining price consistency and executing an Every Day Low Price strategy is critical for global retailers. However, with catalogs spanning millions of active items, manual governance of price relationships is infeasible. Inconsistent pricing across item variants distorts customer value perception and cannibalizes sales. To address this, we present a scalable, context-aware Multi-Agent Framework designed to automate the construction of "Lines and Ladders" pricing taxonomies. Our framework employs specialized LLM agents to construct these coherent pricing structures by identifying key attributes, extracting multi-modal values, and applying hierarchical grouping logic. Evaluated on real-world enterprise data and deployed in production, our 3-Agent system achieves an F1-score of 0.83 for Lines, outperforming single-agent baselines by mitigating cognitive overload. The system achieves >90% precision and >75% recall in Food & Consumables, and 80.2% assignment accuracy in the unstructured General Merchandise catalog.

</details>


### [238] [Privacy-Preserving RAG by Concealing Sensitive Information from External LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12675)
*Saleh Almohaimeed,Saad Almohaimeed,Mousa Jari,Fahad Alotaibi,Khalid A. Alobaid*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SEAG is a privacy-preserving framework for Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) that replaces sensitive words via generated aliases in both user queries and retrieved documents before sending them to external generators. It uses a lightweight model to locate sensitive entities, build an alias table, and evaluate with two datasets. Results show the User metric >80% accuracy and per-model entity-hiding accuracies of 77.83% (Qwen-3), 76.73% (LLaMA-3.2), and 74.91% (Phi-4).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To mitigate privacy risks in RAG where external generators observe queries and retrieved documents containing confidential information, by replacing sensitive content with aliases before transmission.

Method: Develop a lightweight SEAG that detects sensitive entities, generates aliases, and constructs an entity replacement table used to replace sensitive words in user queries and retrieved documents prior to forwarding to an external generator. Constructed two datasets: one for fine-tuning alias generation and one for end-to-end evaluation. Evaluated SEAG with Qwen-3, LLaMA-3.2, and Phi-4.

Result: SEAG achieves >80% accuracy on the User metric (correct responses while hiding sensitive information). Per-model totals for hiding all sensitive entities: Qwen-3 77.83%, LLaMA-3.2 76.73%, Phi-4 74.91%.

Conclusion: SEAG demonstrates a feasible privacy-preserving approach for RAG, showing competitive performance in safeguarding sensitive information while maintaining answer quality; future work could enhance alias quality and privacy guarantees and test across more generators.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is widely used to improve the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in answering user queries. Existing privacy research on RAG has focused on preventing unauthorized users from accessing sensitive data. However, another important problem that is often overlooked in RAG privacy research is that external generators have access to the query and the retrieved documents, which may contain confidential information that could potentially be misused or accessed for unintended purposes. In this paper, we introduce the Sensitive Entity Alias Generator (SEAG), a privacy-preserving framework that empowers users to utilize powerful third-party generators without disclosing sensitive information. SEAG introduces a lightweight model that locates sensitive entities, generates corresponding aliases, and constructs an entity replacement table. The table is used to replace sensitive words in the user's query and in the retrieved documents before they are forwarded to an external generator. For this purpose, two datasets were constructed: one for fine-tuning SEAG models to generate entity replacement tables, and another for evaluating the entire SEAG framework. The experimental results demonstrate the success of the SEAG framework. As for the User metric, which measures the ability of the model to provide a correct response to the user while hiding sensitive information from the external generator, all SEAG models achieved over 80% accuracy. Additional analysis further evaluated the ability of SEAG models Qwen-3, LLaMA-3.2, and Phi-4 to hide all sensitive entities within given documents. The results show good performance with total accuracies of 77.83%, 76.73%, and 74.91%, respectively.

</details>


### [239] [The Role of Natural Language Understanding in Multimodal Video-Based Dengue Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12677)
*Danial Sharifrazi,Saadat Behzadi,Julakha Jahan Jui,Mojtaba Mohammadi,Nouman Javed,Roohallah Alizadehsani,Prasad N. Paradkar,Asim Bhatti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A YOLO- and CLIP-based multimodal framework classifies infection status in mosquitoes from video frames, achieving high frame-level accuracy and complete video-level performance after temporal aggregation; ablations show the importance of fine-tuning and CLIP representations, with the textual branch offering semantic alignment but not outperforming a vision-only model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reason to address infection-related behavioral changes in mosquitoes captured on video: mosquitoes are small, fast, and influenced by background and lighting, making reliable feature extraction challenging; a vision-language approach seeks robust, semantically meaningful representations by aligning visual features with textual prompts.

Method: 1) Use YOLO to segment mosquitoes from the background. 2) Extract visual features from frames. 3) Align features with biologically meaningful textual prompts in a shared embedding space via CLIP. 4) Fine-tune with supervised bidirectional contrastive learning. 5) Evaluate with frame-level image-text similarity-based classification. 6) Aggregate frame-level results temporally for video-level decision. 

Result: Frame-level: 98.54% accuracy and 99.91% sensitivity for distinguishing uninfected vs DENV2-infected mosquitoes. Video-level: complete performance after temporal aggregation. Ablations: fine-tuning and CLIP representations are essential; textual branch provides semantic alignment but does not improve accuracy relative to a vision-only model.

Conclusion: Vision-language models offer a useful framework for analyzing infection-related biological behaviors from video data, with strong frame-level performance and robust video-level results after aggregation; the approach highlights the value of combining detection, CLIP-based representations, and contrastive learning in this domain.

Abstract: Detecting infection-related behavioral changes in mosquitoes from video data is challenging because mosquitoes are small, move rapidly and irregularly, and are affected by environmental factors such as background, lighting, and shadows, which can make reliable feature extraction difficult. In this study, a YOLO- and Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training (CLIP)-based vision-language framework is proposed to classify mosquito flight frames of uninfected and Dengue virus serotype 2 (DENV2)-infected mosquitoes. First, YOLO is used to isolate mosquito regions from the background. Then, visual features extracted from video frames are aligned with biologically meaningful textual prompts in a shared embedding space. The multimodal model was fine-tuned using supervised bidirectional contrastive learning and evaluated through frame-level image-text similarity-based classification. The results show that the proposed method achieved 98.54% accuracy and 99.91% sensitivity at the frame level. After temporal aggregation of frame-level information, the model achieved complete video-level performance. The ablation results showed that fine-tuning and CLIP-based representations were essential for this domain, while the textual branch provided semantic image-text alignment rather than an accuracy advantage over the vision-only model. These findings suggest that vision-language models can provide a useful framework for analyzing infection-related biological behaviors from video data.

</details>


### [240] [Beyond the Best Guess: Improving LLM Solution Coverage with Evolution Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12679)
*Conor F. Hayes,Elliot Meyerson,Kajetan Schweighofer,Roberto Dailey,Babak Hodjat,Risto Miikkulainen,Xin Qiu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Evolution Strategies (ES) improves post-training of LLMs for discovery tasks by maintaining a broader, more diverse solution space, yielding higher pass@k than Reinforcement Learning (RL).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address limited solution coverage caused by RL in post-training LLMs for discovery tasks, where diverse candidate solutions are valuable.

Method: Compare RL vs ES as post-training optimizers for LLMs. ES perturbs model weights in a population (gradient-free) and directly optimizes weight space to maximize discovery-oriented metrics like pass@k; evaluate on math benchmarks and analyze output distribution.

Result: ES achieves consistently higher pass@k than RL and produces a broader output distribution with greater solution coverage, leading to better performance on standard math benchmarks.

Conclusion: ES provides a stronger foundation for post-training in discovery problems and other domains where diverse solution coverage is critical.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in discovery domains such as math and science. The usual approach is to present the problem to the model and use its answer as the proposed solution. However, beyond this best guess, discovery can be enhanced by increasing test-time compute. In a process called pass@k, the model is allowed to explore the solution space and generate diverse candidate solutions. Unfortunately, the standard approach to post-training LLMs through Reinforcement Learning (RL) may limit pass@k: the model's output distribution narrows around high-reward outputs, causing the solution coverage to collapse. The alternative is to use Evolution Strategies (ES), a population-based, gradient-free post-training method that optimizes directly in weight space through random perturbations. As this paper shows, ES achieves consistently higher pass@k than RL and produces a broader output distribution with greater solution coverage. This coverage in turn makes it possible to achieve better results in e.g. standard math benchmarks. Thus, ES provides a better foundation for post-training in discovery problems and other domains where diverse solution coverage is critical.

</details>


### [241] [Spatial Memory Agent: Experience-Grounded Procedure Memory for Spatial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12743)
*Haokai Zhang,Yuhang Ding,Yunshu Zhou,Xinze Du,Shengtao Zhang,Zhiyue Zhao,Yuling Xi,Hao Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A frozen vision-language model (VLM) can improve its spatial reasoning without parameter updates by using a verifier-guided, experience-grounded self-evolution framework (SMA) that distills transferable lessons from verified spatial interactions and retrieves them at inference via a Transfer Reliability Score (TRS).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spatial reasoning is essential for embodied agents and multimodal systems, but existing approaches rely on post-hoc fine-tuning or using external spatial tools. The work investigates a parameter-update-free, self-evolving approach that leverages memory of spatial experiences.

Method: SMA operates in a verifiable spatial environment: the frozen VLM answers, receives a reward, and a verifier guides reflection to distill compact, transferable lessons. Each lesson is assigned a Transfer Reliability Score (TRS) initialized uniformly and updated from retrieval outcomes. In read-only deployment, SMA retrieves lessons by semantic filtering and a similarity-TRS ranked mix to guide inference without updating model parameters.

Result: On five spatial benchmarks and four base VLMs, SMA achieves the highest macro average across all base-model blocks and the best accuracy in most of the 20 evaluations, demonstrating a practical zero-update path for spatial self-evolution of frozen models across scales and environments.

Conclusion: A parameter-update-free pathway to enhance spatial reasoning in frozen VLMs by converting experiential spatial interactions into reusable, transferable memory, enabling robust, scalable self-improvement without external tools at inference.

Abstract: Spatial intelligence is becoming a foundation for embodied agents, robotic planning, and multimodal assistants. To improve the spatial reasoning ability of VLM agents, existing work has mainly followed two lines. One line uses post-training methods, such as supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning. Another line adopts an agentic paradigm in which the model calls external spatial tools, such as depth estimation and 3D reconstruction tools, to gather intermediate spatial evidence. We study a complementary and underexplored route: Can a frozen VLM agent improve its spatial reasoning through \textbf{parameter-update-free self-evolution}, without depending on external expert spatial tools at inference time? We present \textbf{Spatial Memory Agent (SMA)}, an \textbf{experience-grounded runtime framework} that converts verified spatial experience into reusable transferable lessons. In a verifiable spatial environment, SMA queries the frozen VLM, obtains a predicted answer and reward, and uses \textbf{verifier-guided reflection} to distill compact transferable lessons from spatial experience. SMA further assigns each lesson a \textbf{Transfer Reliability Score (TRS)}, which is initialized uniformly and calibrated from later retrieval outcomes as visit evidence of future transfer reliability. During \textbf{read-only deployment}, SMA retrieves lessons by semantic filter and similarity-TRS combined ranking, allowing the retrieved memory to guide frozen model inference. Across five representative spatial benchmarks and four base VLMs, SMA achieves the highest macro average in every base-model block and the best accuracy among the evaluated methods in most of the 20 evaluations, establishing a practical parameter-update-free path for spatial self-evolution across the evaluated frozen model scales and environments.

</details>


### [242] [Correct Is Not Governed: Provenance Integrity in Agentic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12761)
*Jesus Salas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Matrix provides governance for agentic workflows by recording provenance and enforcing a deterministic completeness contract; acts as an integrity layer rather than an accuracy enhancer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Institutional settings require auditable provenance to ensure actions align with authorities, consider wrong authority, unsupported claims, or stale work in changing environments.

Method: Introduce Matrix, a deterministic causal-state layer that records authority and fact dependencies, verifies completion evidence, and selectively invalidates affected work; compare governed vs direct workflows; stress-test with a role-separated transfer; analyze outcomes and evidence preservation.

Result: Governed paths preserve governing evidence, refuse unsupported closure, and limit recovery to dependent tasks; similar outcomes to direct workflows; a role-separated transfer contract over-blocks external packets; results support governance and auditable integrity rather than universal accuracy improvement.

Conclusion: Matrix serves as an institutional integrity layer enabling auditable and independently verifiable agentic work, not a general accuracy enhancer.

Abstract: Agentic workflows are commonly evaluated by whether they reach the correct outcome. That is insufficient in institutional settings, where a correct action may rely on the wrong authority, an unsupported completion claim, or work made stale by a later change. We define governed execution as work whose decisions, completion, and response to change are supported by inspectable provenance. We present Matrix, a deterministic causal-state layer that records authority and fact dependencies, verifies completion evidence, and selectively invalidates affected work. Across controlled comparisons, governed and direct workflows often reached the same outcomes, but only the governed path consistently preserved governing evidence, refused unsupported closure, and limited recovery to dependent tasks. A role-separated transfer challenge then failed: a deterministically enforced completeness contract severely over-blocked synthetic packets produced outside its authoring context. These results do not establish Matrix as a general accuracy enhancer; they support its primary role as an institutional integrity layer for making agentic work auditable and independently verifiable.

</details>


### [243] [PROVE-RT: Generating Mechanized Theorem Prover Scripts for Real-Time Systems using LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12762)
*Sadat Shahriyar,Shareef Ahmed,Abdullah Al Arafat*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PROVE-RT is an LLM-assisted framework that generates PROSA/ROCQ scripts for mechanizing schedulability analyses, using dependency-aware sketches, retrieval from PROSA docs, and staged skeletons. It improves automation over direct prompting, achieving 44.7% success on evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the difficulty of scale, validation, and maintenance of formal schedulability proofs; leverage LLMs while injecting domain knowledge and retrieval to produce PROSA/ROCQ mechanizations.

Method: Dependency-aware informal sketches guided by processed PROSA documentation, retrieval-augmented generation, staged skeleton creation, and progressive proof completion. Built a mechanization-oriented corpus from 1,191 real-time systems papers with 13,134 informal sketches and dependency information.

Result: Direct prompts with state-of-the-art LLMs fail to reliably produce valid PROSA mechanizations; PROVE-RT achieves 44.7% success on a curated evaluation set.

Conclusion: Retrieval-guided and staged LLM assistance can significantly improve automated mechanization of schedulability analyses in PROSA/ROCQ, offering a scalable approach to real-time systems certification.

Abstract: Schedulability analysis is essential for certifying real-time systems, but existing tests are often developed through pen-and-paper proofs that are difficult to scale, validate, and maintain. Mechanized verification in PROSA/ROCQ offers a rigorous alternative, yet manually constructing such proofs requires substantial domain expertise and proof-engineering effort. Recent successes of large language models (LLMs) across a wide range of tasks make them promising candidates for generating PROSA/ROCQ scripts for mechanized theorem provers. However, state-of-the-art LLMs often lack the PROSA-specific knowledge required to correctly use its modeling abstractions and proof patterns.
  This paper introduces PROVE-RT, an LLM-assisted framework for generating PROSA/ROCQ scripts to mechanize schedulability analyses in real-time systems literature. PROVE-RT guides generation through dependency-aware informal sketches, retrieval from processed PROSA documentation, staged skeleton generation, and proof completion. We construct a mechanization-oriented corpus from 1, 191 real-time systems papers, containing 13, 134 informal sketches with dependency information. On a curated evaluation set, direct prompting of state-of-the-art LLMs fails to reliably generate valid PROSA mechanizations, whereas PROVE-RT achieves a success rate of 44.7%. These results show that retrieval-guided and staged LLM assistance can improve automated mechanization of schedulability analysis in PROSA/ROCQ.

</details>


### [244] [ARAC: Benchmarking Auto-Research's Alignment and Completeness on End-to-End Researchs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12788)
*Jiale Cui,Yueyao Yuan,Kaixi Zhong,Xiaogang Xu,Jiafei Wu,Zhe Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ARAC-Bench proposes a framework to evaluate Auto-Research by mimicking human research processes, focusing on alignment and completeness rather than final answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses a fundamental evaluation gap: aligning Auto-Research outputs with human research behavior and evaluating the completeness of the research process, not just final results.

Method: Two synergistic components: (1) Academic Cognition Skills system that converts implicit reviewer expertise into calibrated, quantifiable rubrics, and (2) a three-stage capability diagnostic protocol that decomposes the research process into Proposal, Experiment, and Synthesis under modular constraints, producing traceable, independent dimensions. The framework evaluates 11 SOTA frameworks and yields stage-wise scores.

Result: Best alignment score reported: 67.9/100. Validation against Ph.D. candidates rankings shows a correlation of 0.8141, indicating ARAC-Bench reflects dimensions researchers value. It provides a diagnostic tool and a scalable reward signal for training autonomous research systems.

Conclusion: ARAC-Bench enables measurement and training of autonomous research systems by mirroring human research processes, highlighting alignment gaps and offering a path to better alignment with human methodologies.

Abstract: The rapid advancement of Auto-Research has surfaced a fundamental evaluation challenge: how can we measure the alignment, logical coherence, and evolutionary completeness of its research trajectory with human research behavior? We propose Auto-Research's Alignment and Completeness, ARAC-Bench: a Researcher-Mimicking Evaluation framework that shifts the objective from matching final answers to reproducing high-quality human research processes. The framework operates through two synergistic components: the Academic Cognition Skills system, which is the first to transforms implicit reviewer expertise into stage-calibrated, quantifiable rubrics; and a three-stage capability diagnostic protocol, which decomposes the research process under strict modular constraints into three traceable, mutually independent dimensions: Proposal, Experiment, and Synthesis. Systematic evaluation of 11 SOTA frameworks yields a best alignment score of only 67.9 of 100, revealing a significant gap in simulating rigorous human methodology. Validation against Ph.D. Candidates rankings shows a strong correlation of 0.8141, confirming that ARAC-Bench reliably reflects the dimensions researchers truly value.
