ARAC-Bench provides not only a fine-grained diagnostic tool but also a scalable reward signal for training the next generation of autonomous research systems.

</details>


### [245] [CABS+: Efficient and Scalable Model Merging via Conflict-Aware Sparsification and Adaptive Weight Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12842)
*Yuchen Liu,Zongzhen Yang,Binhang Qi,Hailong Sun,Xiang Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CABS+ extends CABS with Adaptive Weight Allocation (gradient-free coefficient search) and an asymmetric fitness function, plus Relative Synergy Score (RSS), to improve multi-task model merging. It achieves better performance, stability, and efficiency than prior methods across diverse tasks/models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parameter conflicts and knowledge interference during model merging degrade performance. Grid-search for CABS coefficients is slow and can bias optimization toward high-performing tasks, necessitating a faster, fairer, and more robust coefficient optimization.

Method: Extend CABS with Adaptive Weight Allocation (gradient-free search) to optimize merging coefficients. Introduce an asymmetric fitness function to promote cross-task gains. Propose Relative Synergy Score (RSS) to quantify mergeability and guide model selection. Conduct systematic empirical study of factors affecting merging and compare against CABS, AdaMerging, and WUDIMerging across 27 datasets and 5 models (LLMs, small-language, and vision).

Result: CABS+ yields 16.97% and 12.93% improvements over AdaMerging and WUDIMerging, respectively. It demonstrates stronger stability across varying task counts and architectures, uses under 25% of the GPU memory required by AdaMerging, and achieves ~4x faster merging time than WUDIMerging.

Conclusion: CABS+ is a more effective and efficient model merging approach. The combination of gradient-free coefficient optimization, asymmetric fitness, and RSS provides robust cross-task gains and practical efficiency improvements across diverse models and datasets.

Abstract: Model merging has recently attracted significant attention as a promising paradigm for constructing unified multi-task models without requiring additional retraining. However, parameter conflicts and knowledge interference across tasks often degrade merged-model performance. Prior work introduced Conflict-Aware and Balanced Sparsification (CABS), which reduces parameter interference through structured pruning and sequential masking. However, CABS relies on grid search to determine scaling coefficients, resulting in exponential time complexity, while its optimization objective can be dominated by high-performance tasks, leading to suboptimal overall performance. To address these limitations, we extend CABS and propose CABS+. Specifically, Adaptive Weight Allocation (AWA) optimizes merging coefficients via a gradient-free search scheme to reduce time complexity, while an asymmetric fitness function promotes more comprehensive performance gains across tasks. Moreover, we conduct a systematic empirical study of key factors influencing model merging performance and propose Relative Synergy Score (RSS) to quantify model mergeability and guide model selection. We compare CABS+ with state-of-the-art model merging methods, including CABS, AdaMerging, and WUDIMerging, across 27 datasets and 5 models covering large language, small-scale language, and vision models. Extensive experiments verify the effectiveness and efficiency of CABS+. Compared with AdaMerging and WUDIMerging, CABS+ improves overall performance by 16.97% and 12.93%, respectively, exhibits stronger stability and robustness across varying task numbers and model architectures, uses less than 25% of the GPU memory required by AdaMerging, and achieves nearly a 4x speedup in merging time over WUDIMerging.

</details>


### [246] [Beyond Retrieval: Query-Conditioned Reuse of Long-Horizon Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12847)
*Yifei Li,Heng Wang,Lingling Zhang,Muye Huang,Xinyu Zhang,Jiashuai Liu,Hang Yan,Rongman Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A post-retrieval reuse framework is proposed to convert retrieved trajectories into usable task support for long-horizon planning. It introduces query-conditioned reuse (QCR), a simple target-bound memory, and shows strong improvements over full trajectories across three domains, with higher success and lower token usage. Summary reranking closely approaches oracle performance. The analysis indicates target-bound support is more robust than direct trajectory injection as traces grow longer or bindings change.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the post-retrieval bottleneck: after retrieving past trajectories, how should an agent reuse them when user, entity, constraints, or environment have changed? The work aims to decouple retrieval quality from the downstream use of retrieved experience and to test whether a simple, target-bound memory can effectively support long-horizon tasks.

Method: Propose an evaluation framework that fixes candidate retrieval, target state, model, decoding, and tool budget, while varying the support delivered to the agent. Instantiate with query-conditioned reuse (QCR), a target-bound note containing a reusable procedure, recoverable bindings, applicability conditions, and verification requirements. Evaluate on 2,391 targets across WebArena, WorkArena, and AppWorld. Compare against Full Trajectory, perform summary reranking to select reusable memories, and analyze sensitivity to trajectory length and binding shifts.

Result: QCR achieves 62.3% average success, 10.7 points above Full Trajectory, while using 48.9% fewer online tokens. Summary reranking selects a reusable memory for 94.8% of targets, placing end-task success within 1.8 points of an oracle reusable selector. Direct trajectory injection loses much of its utility as traces grow longer or as source-specific values change; target-bound support preserves a larger share of the gain.

Conclusion: The framework successfully decouples retrieval quality from the task of turning retrieved experience into safe, useful support for new tasks. Target-bound reuse (QCR) offers robust gains for long-horizon tasks, while direct trajectory injection is fragile to length and binding changes. The approach provides a testbed to study reuse without claiming a universal memory format.

Abstract: Retrieval can identify a past trajectory that may matter, yet it does not specify how an acting agent should use that trajectory after users, entities, constraints, or environment state have changed. We identify this post-retrieval reuse step as a distinct bottleneck for long-horizon trajectory memory and formulate an evaluation framework that holds candidate retrieval, target state, model, decoding, and tool budget fixed while varying the support delivered to the agent. We instantiate the framework with query-conditioned reuse (QCR), a deliberately simple target-bound note that records a reusable procedure, bindings to recover, applicability conditions, and verification requirements. QCR serves to test the reuse hypothesis rather than to claim a universally preferred memory format. Across 2,391 target instances in WebArena, WorkArena, and AppWorld, QCR reaches 62.3% average Success, 10.7 points above Full Trajectory, while using 48.9% fewer online tokens. Summary reranking selects a reusable memory for 94.8% of targets, placing end-task Success within 1.8 points of an oracle reusable selector. Analyses by trajectory length and source--target binding shift show that direct trajectory injection loses much of its utility as traces grow longer or source-specific values change, whereas target-bound support preserves a larger share of the measured gain. The resulting framework separates retrieval quality from the problem of turning retrieved experience into safe, useful support for a new task.

</details>


### [247] [Practice Makes Unsafe: Skill Misevolution in Self-Improving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12851)
*Xutao Mao,Liangjie Zhao,Xiang Zheng,Cong Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces SkillMisevo-Gym/SkillMisevo-Bench and SafeEvolve to study lifecycle safety in self-improving LLM agents, showing how unsafe experiences can become reusable policies, and presenting benchmarks and remedies to measure and mitigate risk.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify and expose risk across the full lifecycle of self-improving agents (authoring, retrieval, execution) and provide standardized benchmarks and safety wrappers to prevent unsafe knowledge reuse.

Method: Builds SkillMisevo-Gym (versioned, lifecycle-aware harness across agent frameworks) and SkillMisevo-Bench (frozen design with benign tasks and nine lifecycle metrics). Introduces SafeEvolve, a wrapper that repairs unsafe content and governs reuse. Evaluates 25 agent-method configurations (525 tasks in 25 episodes), with 21 evolved configurations authoring unsafe artifacts and 15 causing fresh-session harm; exposure sweep tests carryover ASR; analyzes multiple evolution methods.

Result: Malicious artifacts are prevalent: in the exposure sweep, three malicious tasks raise carryover ASR from 16.0% to 35.3%. Across configurations, SafeEvolve reduces unsafe retrieval and fresh-session harm by 26.7 and 17.3 percentage points, respectively, while mean benign utility changes by 0.4 points. Across 25 configurations, all 21 evolved configurations author unsafe artifacts, while only 15 lead to fresh-session harm.

Conclusion: Persistent-adaptation safety must govern what updates are written and what future executors reuse; the work provides a lifecycle-aware evaluation platform and safety wrapper to measure and mitigate risks in self-improving agents.

Abstract: Self-improving LLM agents convert successful trajectories into persistent cross-task state. An unsafe success can thereby become reusable policy after its triggering input disappears. Skill evolution makes this failure measurable by distilling operational trajectories into executable, transferable, and inspectable procedures. Because evolution optimizes task outcomes rather than procedure safety, compromised experience can cause skill misevolution. Existing benchmarks measure current behavior or static artifacts but cannot attribute risk across authoring, retrieval, and later execution. To expose this lifecycle, we introduce SkillMisevo-Gym, a lifecycle-aware harness that versions skill state across agent frameworks, and SkillMisevo-Bench, a frozen design from malicious exposure to carryover tasks, with concept-aligned benign tasks and nine lifecycle metrics. We also introduce SafeEvolve, a wrapper that repairs unsafe content and governs subsequent reuse. Across 25 agent-method configurations, each covering 525 tasks in 25 episodes, all 21 evolved configurations author unsafe artifacts, while only fifteen lead to fresh-session harm. In the exposure sweep, three malicious tasks raise carryover ASR from 16.0% to 35.3%. Across representative skill evolution methods, SafeEvolve reduces unsafe retrieval and fresh-session harm by 26.7 and 17.3 percentage points, respectively, while mean benign utility changes by only 0.4 points. Together, persistent-adaptation safety must govern what updates write and what future executors reuse. Code is available at https://github.com/henrymao2004/misevolve.

</details>


### [248] [AI and Consumer Rights in India Working Paper](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12863)
*Omir Kumar,Sriya Sridhar,Vibhav Mithal,Balaraman Ravindran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The Indian Consumer Protection Act (2019) may address AI-related harms but has gaps in proving causation, mapping AI value-chain roles, and enforcing proportionate liability across stakeholders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To evaluate whether the Act adequately covers AI defects and distributes liability in a multistakeholder AI ecosystem.

Method: Legal-analytic critique of Act definitions, cross-application to AI harms, and mapping of AI value-chain roles; identification of gaps and enforcement issues.

Result: Broad, technology-agnostic definitions could cover AI harms (injury, psychological harm, bias, loss of control); however, causation is hard to establish; overlapping roles in data providers, model developers, deployers, users complicate liability mapping; current frameworks lack proportionate mechanisms for multistakeholder harms; enforcement needs sector-specific clarifications.

Conclusion: The Act may cover AI entities but lacks clear causation standards and liability allocation across the AI value chain; policy clarifications and possibly sector-specific guidelines are needed to realize proportionate liability for AI harms under Indian law.

Abstract: As AI systems proliferate in consumer facing applications, questions about liability for AI related harms remain unresolved. This working paper examines whether India's Consumer Protection Act, 2019, adequately addresses harm caused by defective AI products and services, and whether it proportionately allocates liability across the AI value chain.
  The Act's broad definitions of product liability, harm, and deficiency appear technology agnostic and potentially applicable to AI related incidents including personal injury, psychological harm, biased outputs, and loss of control. However, significant gaps remain. Proving causation between AI defects and consumer harm presents a technical challenge, as AI failures often stem from design choices rather than discrete defects. Additionally, the Act's framework assumes distinct roles for manufacturers, sellers, and service providers, yet the AI value chain involves overlapping responsibilities among data providers, model developers, deployers, and users that do not neatly map to these categories. Current liability frameworks lack proportionate mechanisms to effectively address complex, multistakeholder AI harms. While the Act may cover AI entities, enforcement requires clarification on sector specific overlaps.

</details>


### [249] [ReflectFact: Self-Reflective Agents for Improving Comprehension and Reasoning in Multi-Hop Fact Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12877)
*Runze Zhao,Zixin Tang,Xiaoshuai Hao,Leyuan Chang,Xiaopeng Fu,Boyu Qiao,Dongyang Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A self-reflective agent framework, ReflectFact, enhances multi-hop fact verification by explicit reasoning path planning, evidence-drift calibration, and reasoning reflection verification, achieving state-of-the-art results on HOVER and EX-FEVER.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address two limitations of prior multi-agent methods: lack of global objective awareness and conflicts between parametric knowledge and evidence.

Method: Propose three tasks: Explicit Reasoning Path Planning; Evidence-Drift Verification; Reasoning Reflection Verification; aggregate chains to verdict; include re-answer with evidence quotes and global perspective to correct biases.

Result: Experimental results show state-of-the-art performance on HOVER and EX-FEVER; gains of 3.32% and 2.78% over strongest baseline.

Conclusion: ReflectFact remedies comprehension and reasoning defects, provides reliable, evidence-grounded verdicts.

Abstract: Multi-hop fact verification, which verifies claims by reasoning over multiple pieces of evidence, is critical for combating misinformation on social media yet remains highly challenging. Recent methods primarily rely on multi-agent collaboration to decompose fact verification into specialized subtasks. However, these methods face two critical limitations: (1) agents may perform individual subtasks without sufficient awareness of the global verification objective, causing their reasoning to deviate from the intended direction; and (2) conflicts between parametric knowledge and the provided evidence may undermine evidence-grounded reasoning and lead to incorrect verdicts. To address these challenges, we propose ReflectFact, a novel self-reflective agent framework for multi-hop fact verification. ReflectFact introduces three key tasks. Explicit Reasoning Path Planning builds an evidence-grounded reasoning path by resolving implicit entities, decomposing the claim into sub-questions, and integrating the verified facts into a verdict. Evidence-Drift Verification makes the agent re-answer by quoting the supporting evidence when a grounded answer merely echoes its parametric prior, thereby calibrating evidence deviation to ensure grounded comprehension. Reasoning Reflection Verification re-examines each reasoning step and regenerates it once an inconsistency is detected, correcting reasoning flaws such as location bias and replacement bias through a global task perspective. Subsequently, the agent aggregates validated reasoning chains to yield reliable verdicts. Extensive experiments on HOVER and EX-FEVER demonstrate that ReflectFact effectively remedies the comprehension and reasoning defects of existing methods, achieving state-of-the-art performance and respectively outperforming the strongest baseline by 3.32\% and 2.78\% on the two datasets.

</details>


### [250] [Predictive Memory Localization: Forecasting Selective Intervention Paths from Internal Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12892)
*Jinhao Jing,Tian Zeyu,Lucas Qingyang Fang,Zhisheng Chen,Shuang Chen,Yuhao Luo,Qiannian Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Predictive Memory Localization (PML) turns measured intervention paths into predictive memory-localization objects to forecast margin-level selective outcomes and guide low-dose, risk-aware interventions. It reports improvements over random and strong held-out performance across multiple datasets and models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Localization alone may not reveal selective operating regimes; there is a need to forecast when a direction will have selective effects and to decide when to abstain or intervene at low dose.

Method: PML treats measured-grid intervention paths as predictive objects of memory localization, decomposes effects into random calibration, semantic-neighbor, and capability-damage components, and contrasts static localization with geometry-based predictions using a strength-disjoint causal response. Empirical evaluation includes 3,000 records across 9 datasets and 14 domains, producing 30,000 path-layer paths and 210,000 path-strength evaluations; uses record-paired bootstrap; a predictor-driven selector chooses coefficients or abstains; evaluated on three residual-norm-matched base models.

Result: At layer 7, geometry-derived RFM/AGOP directions achieve 13.1% target-any and 12.3% clean-any, outperforming random by ~3.4–3.6 pp. Across splits, |α|=0.1 provides strongest signal for outcomes at higher disjoint strengths. On held-out records, the predictor-based selector improves utility and reduces semantic-neighbor damage versus fixed-strength policy, and avoids dense scans. Macro AUROC on held-out test ranges 0.801–0.828 across three models. PML yields falsifiable forecasts of margin-level selective outcomes and risk-aware interventions.

Conclusion: PML successfully translates memory localization into a testable forecast of selective outcomes and informs safe intervention strategies, with robust empirical support across multiple models and data regimes.

Abstract: Activation steering turns localized representations into control directions, but localization alone does not reveal whether a direction has a selective operating regime. We introduce Predictive Memory Localization (PML), which treats the measured-grid intervention path as the predictive object of memory localization. PML separates random-calibrated target movement from semantic-neighbor and capability damage, and compares static localization and supervised geometry with a strength-disjoint low-dose causal response. Our frozen study covers 3,000 records from nine datasets and fourteen domains, yielding 30,000 distinct record-direction-layer paths and 210,000 distinct path-strength evaluations. At layer 7, the geometry-derived RFM/AGOP direction reaches 13.1% target-any and 12.3% clean-any, exceeding random by 3.6 and 3.4 percentage points under a record-paired bootstrap. Across record-, dataset-, and domain-grouped splits, responses at $|α|=0.1$ are the strongest signal for outcomes at disjoint strengths $|α|\in\{0.25,0.5\}$. On held-out records, a predictor-driven selector chooses a coefficient or abstains, improves utility and reduces semantic-neighbor damage relative to a train-tuned fixed-strength policy, and avoids most evaluations in a dense scan. Across three residual-norm-matched base models, learned directions retain selective-path gains and low-dose responses yield 0.801-0.828 record-held-out macro AUROC. PML therefore turns memory localization into a falsifiable forecast of margin-level selective outcomes and a risk-aware intervention decision.

</details>


### [251] [Agent Behavioral Contracts II: Certifying Compositional Reliability Without Assuming Independence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12895)
*Varun Pratap Bhardwaj,Garima Singh,Arun Pratap Bhardwaj*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Independence-based compositional reliability bounds can be invalid in multi-agent systems; empirical tests show strong positive dependence when agents share a model, inflating joint failure beyond the independence-product. The authors propose an independence-free, finite-sample certificate using joint moments and a linear program (Bonferroni-Clopper-Pearson bounds), with improvements from more moment functionals; they also provide an anytime-valid certificate and release data/code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test the routine conditional-independence assumption in compositional reliability bounds and to provide robust, finite-sample guarantees that do not rely on this assumption.

Method: Empirical evaluation in a two-agent handoff (two instances of one model). Measure co-failure: co-fail on 90% of missions where either agent fails (log OR 6.66, phi 0.916). Test effect of substituting a different model and substituting a different vendor. Develop an independence-free bound via a linear program over joint distributions constrained by a Bonferroni-Clopper-Pearson box around measured co-execution moments; extend from 10 to 14 moment functionals; provide an anytime-valid certificate under optional stopping and bootstrap analysis for fitted models.

Result: Observed strong dependence: co-failances in line with 90% (log OR 6.66; phi 0.916). Substituting a different model reduces the association in all six contrasts; substituting a different vendor has no effect. Independence-based estimates overcredit redundancy when components share a model. The dependence-free certificate yields a lower bound floor rising from 0.2455 to 0.4116 when enlarging moment set (84.3% interval narrowing). Anytime-valid certificate maintains Type I error 0.0471 under optional stopping. Bootstrap bound on fitted dependence models degrades with sample size (identification gap O(1), bootstrap haircut O(n^{-1/2})).

Conclusion: Dependence between agents, especially via shared models, can invalidate product-based reliability bounds; use dependence-free certificates based on joint moments for sound guarantees; more moments tighten bounds; standard dependence statistics may misorder conditions; extensive data/code shared to enable replication.

Abstract: Compositional reliability bounds for multi-agent systems multiply component reliabilities, a step licensed by a conditional-independence assumption that is routinely stated and rarely tested. We test it. Two instances of one model, in a two-agent handoff, co-fail on 90.0% of the missions on which either fails (log OR 6.66, 95% CI [6.38, 7.00]; phi 0.916), in a preregistered evaluation of 18,000 missions scored by deterministic code with no LLM judge. Substituting a different model reduces the association in six of six contrasts; substituting a different vendor, model already different, does not -- a registered hypothesis reported as a null.
  The error is signed and runs against the operator: positive dependence inflates joint failure above the independence product, so redundancy is over-credited exactly when components share a model. The assumption-free alternative is often vacuous, and fitting a dependence model is worse: we prove a bootstrap bound on a fitted model's functional loses coverage of the truth as n grows, the identification gap being O(1) while the bootstrap haircut is O(n^{-1/2}). More data makes such a certificate worse, with no visible symptom.
  We give a finite-sample certificate assuming no dependence structure: a linear program over the joint, over a Bonferroni-Clopper-Pearson box around measured co-execution moments. It is sound, sharp for the information supplied, and monotone in the moment family. Enriching ten moment functionals to fourteen narrows the identified interval by 85.7% and lifts the certified floor from 0.2455 to 0.4116. A companion anytime-valid certificate holds type-I error at 0.0471 under optional stopping.
  Common dependence statistics are marginal-bounded and can reverse an apparent ordering of conditions when the compared agents fail at different rates. Contracts, scoring code, analysis scripts, and the preregistration are released.

</details>


### [252] [Polish Medical Visual Question Answering: Vision-Language Models Underutilize Visual Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12928)
*Jakub Pokrywka,Łukasz Grzybowski,Antoni Lasik,Marek Kubis,Jeremi Ignacy Kaczmarek,Wojciech Kusa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A Polish medical VQA benchmark from licensing exam questions; compares open-weight, general-purpose, and commercial vision-language models on image-containing VQA and text QA controls; best model ~79% on full VQA; GPT-5.6 best on a candidate-responses subset; models often perform better on text than on image content; there is still above-chance performance from answer choices alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate non-English, medically oriented VQA performance with real exam data; assess how models fuse image and text, and how they perform when images or questions are ablated; establish a benchmark for Polish-language medical VQA and QA in a high-stakes domain.

Method: Construct a dataset from Polish Board Certification Examination questions for physicians and dentists, including image-containing items across medical specialties plus a text-only QA control set. Evaluate multiple general-purpose open-weight and commercial vision-language models. Perform ablation studies by removing image, question, or both to assess visual grounding. Categorize questions by image importance.

Result: Best model achieves 79.0% accuracy on the full VQA set. GPT-5.6 surpasses the approximate human reference on the subset with available candidate responses; other models underperform humans. Ablation shows models derive more useful information from the question text than from the image; they perform worse on image-dominant questions. Across QA and VQA, models show above-chance accuracy using answer choices alone, indicating non-trivial performance even when key components are missing.

Conclusion: Open-weight and general-purpose VLMs can approach human performance on parts of a medically focused Polish VQA benchmark, but visual grounding remains challenging. Overall, models rely heavily on textual cues, and robust multimodal understanding continues to be an area for improvement.

Abstract: We introduce a Polish-language medical visual question answering (VQA) benchmark, built from Polish Board Certification Examination questions for licensed physicians and dentists pursuing specialist certification. The benchmark comprises image-containing questions spanning diverse medical specialties and visual domains, together with a text-only question answering (QA) control set. We evaluate Polish-oriented, general-purpose open-weight, and commercial vision-language models. The task remains challenging: the best model achieves 79.0\% accuracy on the full VQA set, and only GPT-5.6 surpasses the approximate human reference on the subset with available candidate responses; all other evaluated models perform worse than humans. To assess visual grounding, we compare complete inputs with configurations omitting the image, the question, or both, and categorize questions by image importance. Models derive more useful information from the question text than from the image and perform worse on image-dominant questions. Across both QA and VQA, they nevertheless achieve above-chance accuracy from the answer choices alone, showing that non-trivial performance can persist even when key task components are missing.

</details>


### [253] [FlashDrive: Flash Vision-Language-Action Inference for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12932)
*Zekai Li,Yihao Liang,Hongfei Zhang,Jian Chen,Yesheng Liang,Zhijian Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Co-designs algorithmic shortcuts across four VLA bottlenecks (visual encoding, LM prefill, reasoning tokens, and flow-matching) to achieve substantial end-to-end latency reductions with minimal accuracy loss for autonomous driving VLA models, achieving 4.7x speedup on a 10B model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-Language-Action models can drive end-to-end reasoning for autonomous driving but are too computationally heavy for real-time control due to a cascade of four bottlenecks. A unified, cross-stage optimization is needed to reach real-time deployment without sacrificing accuracy.

Method: Apply per-stage lightweight shortcuts: (1) temporal overlap enables streaming KV-cache reuse across frames; (2) non-autoregressive diffusion drafter exploiting low per-token entropy and intra-block correlations for speculative decoding; (3) adaptive step caching for flow-matching denoising leveraging velocity-field structure; (4) system-level CUDA Graph compilation and kernel fusion. Use Alpamayo 1.5-10B with W4A8 quantization.

Result: End-to-end latency reduced from 717 ms to 151 ms (4.7x) on a single GPU; accuracy essentially unchanged (minADE6@6.4s shifts by 0.08 m; minADE1 improved); improved closed-loop collision and off-road rates in simulation; inference frequency increased from 1.4 Hz to 6.6 Hz.

Conclusion: Algorithm-system co-design that targets all four bottlenecks can bring VLA inference closer to real-time deployment in autonomous driving, with substantial latency reductions and negligible accuracy loss, illustrating a practical path to real-time end-to-end VLA systems.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models promise to bring end-to-end reasoning to autonomous driving, but their computational cost remains far too high for real-time control. The core challenge is structural: VLA inference is not a single bottleneck but a cascade of four. Visual encoding wastes compute on overlapping video frames; language-model prefill recomputes context that could be carried over from the previous timestep; reasoning tokens are generated serially despite low entropy; and flow-matching denoising applies uniform compute to a non-uniform velocity field. Addressing any one stage in isolation leaves the others untouched. We propose FlashDrive, an algorithm-system co-design framework that targets all four stages simultaneously. Our key insight is that each bottleneck admits a distinct, lightweight algorithmic shortcut: temporal overlap enables streaming KV-cache reuse across frames; the low per-token entropy and strong intra-block correlations of driving-domain reasoning make a non-autoregressive diffusion drafter highly effective for speculative decoding; and the velocity field's structure---sharp at the endpoints, flat in the middle---permits adaptive step caching that concentrates compute where it matters. Layered on system-level CUDA Graph compilation and kernel fusion, these techniques compound. Applied to Alpamayo 1.5-10B with W4A8 quantization, FlashDrive reduces end-to-end latency from 717ms to 151ms (4.7x) while leaving accuracy essentially unchanged: minADE6@6.4s shifts by only 0.08m, minADE1 improves, and closed-loop collision and off-road rates improve in simulation. By raising a 10B-parameter reasoning VLA from 1.4~Hz to 6.6~Hz on a single GPU, FlashDrive moves end-to-end autonomous driving substantially closer to real-time deployment.

</details>


### [254] [Decomposition of Evidence, Contradiction, and Fragility in Perturbation Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12935)
*Lei You*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DECAF decomposes perturbation responses into three components—evidence, contradiction, and fragility—such that Abs = E + C + F, providing interpretable semantics for model reactions beyond raw magnitude.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Perturbation magnitude alone conveys how much a model reacts but not what that reaction means. A trajectory-based decomposition clarifies whether the reaction supports, opposes, or vanishes at the endpoint, improving interpretability and robustness of explanations.

Method: Define DECAF to route aligned responses into evidence (E), opposed responses into contradiction (C), and endpoint-null responses into fragility (F). Ensure a magnitude-preserving, unique decomposition under endpoint-relative axioms. Track responses along a progressively revealed input path and interpret the final contrast via the trajectory. Evaluate across vision and tabular tasks, including ImageNet-9 with 72 models, FunnyBirds, ImageNet-1k, and a 1B-scale DINOv2, comparing to baselines and recording efficiency metrics.

Result: DECAF components track independently and align with observed behaviors more accurately than magnitude alone (96.4% agreement vs 35.0% for magnitude). Modifying the reveal path boosts total response by ~80%, but E remains stable while F increases >4x. Short forward-only DECAF trajectories outperform general attribution baselines on FunnyBirds and ImageNet-1k. For a 1B DINOv2, a short trajectory matches a strong gradient-based baseline with 4.75× lower wall time and 2.36× lower peak memory.

Conclusion: The proposed decomposition provides a magnitude-preserving, unique, and interpretable partition of perturbation responses into evidence, contradiction, and fragility, outperforming magnitude-based explanations, and delivering efficiency gains across vision and tabular tasks.

Abstract: Perturbation methods explain model decisions by measuring prediction changes under altered inputs, but response magnitude tells us only how much a model reacts, not what that reaction means. The same magnitude can support the final factual-counterfactual difference, oppose it, or arise strongly along the perturbation path yet vanish at the endpoint. We therefore track how the contrast develops as paired inputs are progressively revealed, using the final contrast to interpret the trajectory. We introduce DECAF (Decomposition of Evidence, Contradiction, And Fragility), which routes aligned, opposed, and endpoint-null responses into evidence E, contradiction C, and fragility F. The decomposition preserves ordinary magnitude exactly, Abs = E + C + F, and is unique under endpoint-relative axioms. Across controlled vision and tabular settings, the three components track independently measured behavior. In a 72-model ImageNet-9 audit, we compare cases with nearly identical response magnitude but different independently measured behaviors. The largest DECAF component agrees with an observed behavior in 96.4% of cases, compared with 35.0% for magnitude alone. Changing only the reveal path increases total response by nearly 80%, yet evidence barely changes while fragility grows by more than 4x. On FunnyBirds and ImageNet-1k, short forward-only DECAF trajectories outperform the tested general-purpose attribution baselines. On a 1B-scale DINOv2 model, a short trajectory matches a strong gradient-based baseline with 4.75x lower wall time and 2.36x lower peak memory.

</details>


### [255] [Moose: Latent concept learning with reasoning-shortcut awareness in $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12961)
*Olga Mashkova,Asaad Mohammedsaleh,Fernando Zhapa-Camacho,Robert Hoehndorf*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Moose compiles an EL++ TBox and finite ABox to an SDD, using differentiable weighted-model counting for neuro-symbolic learning with partial supervision; proves formal properties; defines partial-supervision latent-concept learning over OWL EL; shows improved performance on MNIST-with-ontology and Pizzaïolo, outperforming baselines and analyzing reasoning shortcuts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable neuro-symbolic learning on ontologies expressed in OWL EL (EL++), addressing the gap of reasoning-shortcut awareness in ontology settings and enabling partial-supervision latent concept learning.

Method: Translate EL++ TBox and finite ABox into an SDD, employ SDD as a differentiable WMC layer, add closure clauses outside EL++ to cover exhaustive families under partial supervision, prove termination, soundness, completeness, and polynomial intermediate sizes; formal verification in Lean; define a latent-concept learning task with per-individual classifiers from ABox literals; evaluate on MNIST-with-ontology and Pizzaïolo.

Result: Formal properties established; first formal partial-supervision latent-concept-learning task over OWL EL; Moose outperforms propositional-NeSy, fuzzy-logic, and ontology-embedding baselines; provides first reasoning-shortcut analysis in OWL EL setting.

Conclusion: Moose demonstrates a viable and formally-grounded neuro-symbolic learning pipeline for OWL EL ontologies, enabling RS-awareness, partial supervision, and improved predictive performance on ontology-guided tasks.

Abstract: The OWL 2 EL profile is used in some of the largest production ontologies, including the Gene Ontology and SNOMED CT. Existing neuro-symbolic (NeSy) learning methods accept propositional theories or Datalog, and reasoning-shortcut (RS) awareness has not been investigated in ontology settings. We present Moose, a method that compiles an $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$ TBox and finite ABox to a Sentential Decision Diagram (SDD). The SDD acts as a differentiable weighted-model-counting layer, and we add closure clauses outside the $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$ profile on declared exhaustive families to overcome the limited expressivity of $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$ under partial supervision. We show termination, soundness, completeness, and polynomial intermediate sizes, and validate the proofs in Lean. We then define the first formal partial-supervision latent-concept-learning task over an OWL EL ontology, i.e., learning per-individual classifiers for latent concepts from observed ABox literals, and evaluate Moose on MNIST-with-ontology and Pizzaïolo. Moose improves over propositional-NeSy, fuzzy-logic, and ontology embedding baselines, and presents the first reasoning-shortcut analysis in an OWL EL setting.

</details>


### [256] [OGR-MARL: Option-Guided Residual Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for Heterogeneous USV Cooperative Pursuit in Constrained Port Waterways](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12995)
*Mao Jiayang,Wang Lanfeng,Peng Zhao-Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OGR-MARL is an option-guided residual MARL framework decoupled from MARL backbones for heterogeneous USV pursuit in constrained port waterways, achieving high capture rates and zero-shot transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heterogeneous USV pursuit in constrained ports must satisfy navigation, traffic, and role constraints. A framework that leverages rule-guided behaviors while learning corrective actions can improve sample efficiency and generalization compared to learning from scratch for every backbone.

Method: Propose OGR-MARL: an option-guided residual multi-agent reinforcement learning framework that decouples from specific MARL algorithms. It integrates a shared evader belief, role-conditioned option targets, adaptive rule penalties, and residual policy learning to learn corrective actions on top of rule-guided behaviors. Instantiated with representative continuous-control MARL backbones (MADDPG, MATD3, MAPPO, MASAC) to yield OGR-MADDPG, OGR-MATD3, OGR-MAPPO, and OGR-MASAC.

Result: In an abstract Xiazhimen port-waterway scenario, the OGR-MASAC instantiation achieves a 75.0% capture rate, demonstrates promising mission-compliance and best heterogeneous coordination among tested methods; zero-shot transfer to a QGIS/AIS-informed Xiazhimen map without retraining yields promising generalization.

Conclusion: OGR-MARL provides a flexible, backbone-agnostic framework that combines rule-guided behaviors with learning-based residual corrections, enabling effective and generalizable multi-agent pursuit in constrained port environments; MASAC-based instantiation performed best in the reported scenario, and cross-scenario transfer suggests notable generalization potential.

Abstract: Heterogeneous USV cooperative pursuit in constrained port waterways requires evader interception under navigation, traffic, and role constraints. This paper proposes OGR-MARL, an option-guided residual multi-agent reinforcement learning framework that is decoupled from a specific MARL algorithm. OGR-MARL integrates shared evader belief, role-conditioned option targets, adaptive rule penalties, and residual policy learning, allowing different MARL algorithms to learn corrective actions on top of rule-guided behaviors rather than exploring constrained port environments from scratch. We instantiate OGR-MARL with representative continuous-control MARL backbones, including MADDPG, MATD3, MAPPO, and MASAC, yielding OGR-MADDPG, OGR-MATD3, OGR-MAPPO, and OGR-MASAC. Experiments in an abstract Xiazhimen port-waterway scenario show that the OGR-MASAC instantiation achieves a 75.0% capture rate, promising mission-effective rule compliance, and the best heterogeneous coordination among the tested methods. Without retraining, zero-shot transfer to a QGIS/AIS-informed Xiazhimen map achieves promising results, demonstrating the generalization potential of OGR-MARL in more complex port scenarios.

</details>


### [257] [Foundations of MT-PDCL: Measure-Theoretic Probabilistic Definite Clause Logic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13018)
*Costin Bădică,Amelia Bădică*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MT-PDCL extends probabilistic logic programming to continuous domains via measure-theoretic semantics, replacing grounding with Lebesgue integration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Remove the finite-domain restriction of traditional grounding-based probabilistic logic programs and enable exact inference over continuous measurable spaces while preserving a pure definite-clause, declarative syntax.

Method: Define stochastic variables over bounded index domains; equip the interpretation space with standard Borel σ-algebras; build on Continuous Distribution Semantics; model rules as mutually independent causal events; replace finite boolean circuit grounding with exact Lebesgue integration for entailment; introduce a continuous immediate consequence operator that fuses continuous priors with continuous observations.

Result: Provides exact, algebraic, and differentiable inference without discrete grounding; replaces combinatorial grounding with Lebesgue-integral-based reasoning; establishes a principled, declarative framework for continuous probabilistic logic, though it transfers the complexity to high-dimensional integration.

Conclusion: MT-PDCL offers a principled measure-theoretic foundation for probabilistic logic programming in continuous spaces, preserving the declarative definite-clause syntax while expanding expressivity; the price is the geometric curse of dimensionality inherent in high-dimensional Lebesgue integration.

Abstract: Standard probabilistic logic programming frameworks typically rely on grounding logic programs into discrete propositional representations. This operational requirement restricts exact inference to finite domains and discrete probability distributions. In this paper, we introduce Measure-Theoretic Probabilistic Definite Clause Logic (MT-PDCL), a generalized foundational framework that eliminates this finite-domain restriction. By explicitly defining stochastic variables over bounded index domains and equipping the interpretation space with standard Borel $σ$-algebras, MT-PDCL allows logical variables to operate natively over continuous measurable spaces. Building on Continuous Distribution Semantics, MT-PDCL models probabilistic rules as mutually independent causal events. However, rather than aggregating these derivations via finite boolean circuits, declarative entailment is formally defined through exact Lebesgue integration over the continuous measure space. We introduce a continuous immediate consequence operator that unifies the integration of continuous prior distributions with the evaluation of exact continuous observations. We demonstrate that this approach replaces the combinatorial bottleneck of discrete grounding with exact, algebraic, and structurally differentiable inference. While this transition trades discrete combinatorics for the geometric curse of dimensionality, it achieves the expressive power of continuous probabilistic models while preserving the pure declarative syntax of definite clause logic.

</details>


### [258] [From Local Mismatch to Global Impact: Optimizing Cache Reuse Policy for Efficient Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13043)
*Xichen Ye,Yifan Wu,Zhikang Xie,Xiangyu Yue,Cheng Jin,Weizhong Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Global-Impact Cache (GCache) uses a Bernstein-form reparameterization and bilevel optimization to learn cache reuse policies for diffusion models, aligning error propagation with generation quality. It improves efficiency and fidelity, e.g., 2.17x speedup and LPIPS drop on Wan2.1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion models incur high inference cost. Existing cache strategies rely on local similarity heuristics that poorly track final generation quality due to non-uniform error propagation across the denoising trajectory.

Method: Derive an error-propagation upper bound and reparameterize the propagation exponent with a Bernstein form. Reformulate cache policy search as a bilevel optimization: inner objective optimizes the reuse policy; outer objective aligns the error-weighting with generation-quality loss to prioritize impactful computations.

Result: GCache outperforms prior caching strategies on both video and image generation; on Wan2.1 video diffusion, it achieves 2.17x speedup and reduces LPIPS from 0.1095 to 0.0316.

Conclusion: GCache reconciles theoretical rigor with empirical performance, delivering a principled caching framework that enhances both efficiency and visual fidelity across modalities.

Abstract: Diffusion models have achieved dominant performance in visual generation but suffer from substantial inference overhead. While cache-based acceleration has emerged as a promising solution, existing policies rely on local similarity heuristics, which we identify as being significantly misaligned with final generation quality. This discrepancy stems from the non-uniform propagation and accumulation of errors along the denoising trajectory. To address this, we propose Global-Impact Cache (GCache). We first establish a rigorous theoretical characterization of the error propagation upper bound. Recognizing that this bound can be overly conservative for complex, highly non-convex diffusion models, we further reparameterize the propagation exponent with a Bernstein form and reformulate cache policy search as a bilevel optimization problem. In detail, GCache identifies an optimal reuse policy in the inner objective while aligning the error-weighting function with generation quality loss in the outer objective. This framework effectively reconciles theoretical rigor with empirical performance, learning to prioritize computation where it most impacts visual fidelity. Extensive experiments demonstrate that GCache consistently outperforms prior caching strategies on both video and image generation. Notably, on the state-of-the-art Wan2.1 video diffusion model, GCache maintains a 2.17x speedup while significantly enhancing generation quality, reducing LPIPS from 0.1095 to 0.0316.

</details>


### [259] [BoardroomAI: Dependency-Aware Human-Steerable Multi-Agent Deliberation through Evolving Decision Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13046)
*Sanjeev Manivannan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BoardroomAI introduces a persistent human–agent collaboration framework using a typed decision DAG, an intervention compiler, and dependency-aware propagation to support evolving evidence and priorities in organizational decisions. It reports efficient propagation (14.59% of nodes inspected) and selective repair in synthetic pilots, with a notion of decision-sufficient context closure, but results remain prototype-level with abstentions indicating context gaps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move beyond transcript-based multi-agent systems by treating humans as persistent participants who can intervene in real-time, guiding constraints, evidence, and priorities to co-create decisions under evolving information.

Method: Propose a typed decision DAG capturing evidence, assumptions, constraints, claims, objections, alternatives, risks, decisions, semantic dependencies, and specialist responsibility. Build an intervention compiler to update the graph from human actions. Use dependency-aware propagation to identify affected subgraphs, preserve unaffected artifacts, and selectively reactivate relevant specialists. Evaluate with a framework measuring intervention impact, repair coverage, preservation, recomputation, and decision validity. Validate via 600 synthetic decision-DAG interventions and a 12-case exploratory pilot.

Result: Propagation matched exhaustive impact computation while inspecting 14.59% of nodes. In 12-case pilot, selective repair recomputed 62.11% of canonical nodes, preserved all gold-unaffected nodes, and produced valid updated decisions in 6 cases while abstaining in 6, highlighting that correct routing may lack sufficient context for synthesis and motivating decision-sufficient context closure. All results are synthetic/prototype-level.

Conclusion: The approach demonstrates viable persistent human–agent co-creation of organizational decisions with efficient, targeted recomputation and strong preservation properties in synthetic pilots, but real-world deployment requires addressing context sufficiency and broader validation; the framework lays groundwork for future research on decision graphs and human-in-the-loop deliberation.

Abstract: Organizational decisions are co-created while evidence, constraints, and human priorities continue to evolve. In conventional transcript-based multi-agent systems, humans typically provide an initial problem, agents deliberate internally, and the system returns a final response. BoardroomAI instead treats the human as a persistent participant who can intervene by challenging assumptions, modifying constraints, changing priorities, introducing evidence, or redirecting the decision process. We operationalize this human--agent coexistence through four components: (i) a typed decision graph representing evidence, assumptions, constraints, claims, objections, alternatives, risks, decisions, semantic dependencies, and specialist responsibility; (ii) an intervention compiler that converts confirmed human actions into explicit graph updates; (iii) dependency-aware propagation that identifies affected subgraphs, preserves unaffected artifacts, and selectively reactivates relevant specialists; and (iv) an evaluation framework measuring intervention impact, repair coverage, preservation, recomputation, and decision validity. Across 600 generated decision-DAG interventions, propagation matched exhaustive impact computation while inspecting only 14.59% of nodes. In a 12-case exploratory pilot, selective repair recomputed 62.11% of canonical nodes, preserved all gold-unaffected nodes, and produced valid updated decisions in six cases while abstaining in the remaining six. These abstentions show that correct intervention routing may still provide insufficient context for synthesis, motivating a \emph{decision-sufficient context closure} for human-steered multi-agent deliberation. All results are synthetic and prototype-level.

</details>


### [260] [DMDIntel: Interpreting Large Language Models via Dynamic Mode Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13048)
*Amogh Joshi,Animesh Mukherjee,Sergey Utyuzhnikov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DMDIntel uses dynamic mode decomposition to produce interpretable token attributions for LLM-based classification, outperforming standard attribution methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Provide robust, interpretable explanations for LLM classifications by identifying prominent hidden-state patterns (modes) and attributing token importance via mode projections.

Method: Apply dynamic mode decomposition to hidden states to extract modes; compute token attribution by projecting input token representations onto these modes and ranking tokens by projection magnitude; evaluate on three datasets and three model families.

Result: The method yields ranked token attributions that consistently outperform PCA, Integrated Gradients, and SHAP across the evaluated datasets and models.

Conclusion: Dynamic mode decomposition offers a compelling, interpretable attribution mechanism for LLM classifiers, delivering more accurate and stable token-level explanations than several established baselines.

Abstract: In this work, we introduce DMDIntel which uses dynamic mode decomposition (DMD) to make the predictions made by LLMs in a classification task interpretable. It develops an input attribution pipeline, that first decomposes the hidden states of an LLM into prominent patterns, also known as modes, and then associates ranks to the input tokens based on the projection values on those modes. Rigorous experiments across three datasets and three model families consistently show that the ranked attribution of input tokens obtained using DMDIntel by far outperforms state-of-the-art techniques such as principal component analysis, integrated gradients and SHAP.

</details>


### [261] [VALG: An Agentic System for ML Theory Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13060)
*Dechen Zhang,Xuan Tang,Xinxiang Yin,Xingwu Chen,Jian Qian,Difan Zou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VALG is an autonomous, agentic workflow for ML theory research that uses graph-structured proofs, adaptive problem formulation, and multi-level verification to manage open problems; evaluated on COLT 2026 tasks with mixed outcomes; open-sourced.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Solving open ML theory problems requires co-evolving problem formulation, proof strategy, and data assumptions. Automating this orchestration could enhance rigor, consistency, and efficiency in theory development.

Method: VALG comprises three layers: Verification, Adaptive formulation of Learning-theory problems, and Graph-structured proof development. Each theorem branch maintains a fixed mathematical specification, checks a typed proof-dependency graph, and builds local proofs in dependency order. When a proof attempt fails, VALG categorizes the obstruction (derivation, structure, or formulation) and routes the next attempt; formulation-level obstructions trigger related relaxations that preserve the relation to the source problem.

Result: Applied to nine subproblems across five COLT 2026 open problems. Two runs yielded internally finalized theorem candidates aligned with their source briefs; seven produced restricted-method results, special cases, or conditional theorems. The cases illustrate how VALG preserves source-scope matches, relaxations, conditional results, and blocked attempts as distinct.

Conclusion: VALG demonstrates a structured, auditable workflow for ML-theory research, maintaining mathematical traceability from source problems to derived results. The project is open-source at the provided URL, inviting community verification and extension.

Abstract: Machine learning theory studies learning procedures through mathematical setups in which the data model, training protocol, oracle access, loss, metric, and randomness define the phenomenon that a theorem is meant to explain. Solving an open problem therefore requires the problem formulation, theorem target, and proof mechanism to be developed in concert. Researchers formulate hypotheses, test them through preliminary theoretical or empirical analysis, and refine both assumptions and proofs. We investigate whether this process can be organized as an autonomous agentic workflow for ML theory research.
  We develop VALG, an agentic system that combines multi-level Verification, Adaptive formulation of Learning-theory problems, and Graph-structured proof development. Within each source-relative theorem branch, VALG maintains a fixed mathematical specification, checks the theorem-level composition of a typed proof-dependency graph, and constructs and reviews local proofs in dependency order. When a proof attempt fails, VALG identifies whether the obstruction lies in a derivation, the proof structure, or the theorem formulation and routes the next attempt accordingly. Formulation-level obstructions initiate an explicitly related variant or relaxation, preserving the mathematical relation between the resulting theorem and the source problem.
  We evaluate VALG on nine subproblems from five COLT 2026 open problems. Two runs produce internally finalized theorem candidates that match the scope of their source briefs; the remaining seven yield restricted-method results, special cases, or conditional theorems. These case studies show how VALG keeps source-scope matches, relaxations, conditional results, and blocked attempts mathematically distinct. VALG is open source at https://github.com/DechenZhang/VALG-ML-Theory-Agent.

</details>


### [262] [Uniform Herding: Exemplar Replay with Representation Refresh](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13061)
*Krishna Subedi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Uniform Herding improves replay in class-incremental learning by uniformly allocating the active exemplar budget across observed classes and refreshing exemplars from a bounded pool, achieving higher final accuracy and lower forgetting than iCaRL on CIFAR-100 under specified budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Preserve accuracy for older classes when feature representations drift in incremental learning, under bounded memory constraints.

Method: Uniform Herding allocates the active exemplar budget M across observed classes and refreshes chosen exemplars using a bounded candidate pool within the current representation; ablations replace NME or herding and assess distillation; experiments compare against iCaRL; budgets include retrieval budget b and active budget M; evaluated on CIFAR-100 with 10-class-increment tasks using ResNet-18 and multiple seeds.

Result: On CIFAR-100 (10-class increments) with M=2000, b=64, three seeds: final average accuracy 44.00±0.51%, forgetting 17.22±0.43% for Uniform Herding vs 42.33±1.20% accuracy and 24.87±1.11% forgetting for iCaRL. Ablations show accuracy drops if NME or herding are replaced; forgetting increases if distillation is removed. Retrieval budget has a smaller effect than active budget. The comparison with iCaRL is end-to-end, not isolating the refresh effect; results are protocol-specific.

Conclusion: Uniform Herding improves accuracy and reduces forgetting under the tested protocol compared with iCaRL, but conclusions are limited to the tested protocol; the gain is linked to the refresh mechanism within a bounded pool, and the study does not isolate the refresh contribution from other protocol differences.

Abstract: As the feature representation changes, replay must preserve the earlier classes. However, only a bounded active exemplar set can be replayed. We propose Uniform Herding, which allocates the current active set across observed classes and uses a bounded candidate pool to refresh their chosen exemplars in the current representation. On CIFAR-100 with ten class-incremental tasks, a ResNet-18 backbone, active budget $M=2{,}000$, retrieval budget $b=64$, and three seeds, Uniform Herding obtains $44.00\pm0.51\%$ final average accuracy and $17.22\pm0.43\%$ forgetting, compared with $42.33\pm1.20\%$ and $24.87\pm1.11\%$ for iCaRL. Within the Uniform Herding protocol, final accuracy decreased when NME or herding was replaced with the tested alternatives, while forgetting increased when distillation was removed. Changing the retrieval budget has a smaller effect across the tested range than changing the active budget. The comparison with iCaRL is end-to-end. It does not isolate the effect of refresh from the other protocol differences. These results are limited to the tested protocol.

</details>


### [263] [Explanatory Engagement Under Rare Anomalous Failure: Asymptotic Rarity in Model Behavior (or: The Asymptotic AI)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13063)
*Sam Mao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Examines how LLM explanatory engagement changes as failure rates become rarer in a low-failure-rate workflow, across three open-weight models and five elicitation conditions; finds condition-dependent patterns (rise then plateau under immediate_forced; no collapse under grouped_runs) with model-specific self-monitoring and floor artifacts, plus a guaranteed-failure control; discrete-rate points limit conclusions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To answer a narrower question: does explanatory engagement (length, specificity, confidence) change as failures become asymptotically rarer within a workflow, rather than merely whether models notice anomalies.

Method: Zero-cost harness on three open-weight models (qwen3:8b, llama3.1:8b, mistral:7b). Repeated tool-call task with one call failing at probability p swept across eight rates from 0.2 to 0.0001, under five elicitation conditions (from immediate prompting to none). Measured: explanation length, specificity, and self-reported confidence; analyzed pooled data and per-condition splits; included a companion guaranteed-failure run (72 cells) using backfilled rates to assess anomaly recognition. Limitation noted: discrete rate points.

Result: Pooled results show flat/monotonic patterns; condition splits reveal nuanced dynamics. Under immediate_forced, explanation length rises as failures become rarer (peaks at 28.4 words at p=0.05) and then plateaus around 17.4–19.0 words at the rarest rates; confidence climbs unevenly from ~53% to the 70s–90s. Under grouped_runs, explanations are batched at run-end with no collapse observed. Under passive_unprompted, aggregate magnitude is a floor artifact, but logs reveal model-specific self-monitoring (llama3.1:8b occasionally reports structured confidence unprompted; the others rarely do so). Elicitation structure acts as a first-class moderator of collapse observability. The guaranteed-failure run shows models differ in recognizing anomalies distinct from engagement once recognized. Limitation: discrete rate points do not capture behavior between them; future work should explore continuous-rate regimes and broader model sets.

Conclusion: Elicitation structure crucially shapes observed explanatory engagement under rare failures. Results indicate complex, model- and condition-dependent dynamics rather than a simple monotonic rise and collapse; careful experimental design and broader testing are needed to generalize these findings.

Abstract: Prior work on LLM behavior under anomalous conditions asks whether a model notices anomalies. We ask a narrower question: once a model sits in a workflow with a low, controllable failure rate, does its explanatory engagement - length, specificity, self-reported confidence - change as failure grows asymptotically rarer? We built a local, zero-cost harness on three open-weight models (qwen3:8b, llama3.1:8b, mistral:7b) running a repeated tool-call task where one call fails at probability p, swept across eight rates from 0.2 to 0.0001, under five elicitation conditions from immediate prompting to none. We hypothesized a rise in engagement as failures grew rarer, then a collapse near a detectability threshold. Pooled across conditions this appeared false: length fell in a flat, monotonic pattern. Splitting by condition overturned that. Under immediate_forced, where the model must explain every failure instantly, the predicted rise is confirmed but followed by a plateau, not a collapse: length peaks at 28.4 words at p=0.05, settles to 17.4-19.0 words at the rarest rates, and confidence rises unevenly from about 53% to the 70s-90s. Under grouped_runs, explanation batched to run-end, no collapse appears. Under passive_unprompted, aggregate magnitude is a floor artifact, but a recovered logging gap revealed real, model-specific self-monitoring: llama3.1:8b volunteers structured confidence reports unprompted, sometimes eroding its own confidence as trials accumulate; the other two do so only once, as boilerplate. Elicitation structure is a first-class moderator of collapse observability. A companion guaranteed-failure run (72 cells, backfilling rates where random sampling gave zero real failures) shows models differ in whether they recognize an anomaly, distinct from engagement once recognized. Limitation: discrete rate points cannot capture behavior between them, a direction for future work.

</details>


### [264] [Behavioral Reprogramming of Open-Weights Models: Cognitive Plasticity and Alignment Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13069)
*Lucia Malíčková*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Empirically investigates proactive, Socratic prompting in open-weight LLMs under compute-efficient PEFT constraints, identifying a LoRA rank threshold (r=16) and an optimal training window (e in [2,3]) with a 14B model achieving lower perplexity; DPO can separate assertive behavior from syntax; cross-lingual alignment is strong among related languages but degrades for morphologically distant targets, offering a framework for compute-efficient, cross-lingual behavioral modification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move beyond passive, sycophantic LLM behavior by studying cognitive plasticity and reprogrammability of open-weight models under strict HPC limits, aiming to establish practical bounds for parameter-efficient fine-tuning and cross-lingual behavioral transfer.

Method: Massively parallel hyperparameter sweep with 405 HPC jobs; identify LoRA rank threshold r=16; extensive epoch ablation to find optimal training window e in [2,3] depending on dataset density; scale up to 14B parameters; apply Direct Preference Optimization (DPO); conduct cross-lingual stress testing to assess zero-shot persona transfer across linguistically related and distant targets.

Result: Generalization performance peaks within training window e in [2,3] with a minimum validation loss of 0.919; 14B parameter model achieves perplexity 1.414 on localized evaluation; DPO separates assertive behavior from localized syntax; cross-lingual tests show robust alignment in closely related languages with identifiable degradation in morphologically distant languages.

Conclusion: Provides an empirically grounded, compute-efficient framework for cross-lingual behavioral modification of LLMs, identifying architectural thresholds (LoRA r=16) and training window bounds, while highlighting both potential and limits of zero-shot persona transfer across language families.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are predominantly aligned to function as passive, sycophantic assistants. We challenge this default paradigm by empirically evaluating the cognitive plasticity of open-weight architectures when subjected to rigorous behavioral reprogramming. Our objective is to induce a proactive, Socratic conversational framework, characterized by high-frequency question generation under strictly constrained high-performance computing (HPC) conditions. Through a massively parallelized hyperparameter sweep comprising 405 HPC jobs, we define precise mathematical bounds for parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT). We identify an architectural threshold at LoRA rank $r=16$ and demonstrate via extensive epoch ablation that generalization capacity strictly reaches its optimal convergence within an optimized training window of $e \in [2, 3]$ depending on dataset density (minimum validation loss of 0.919). Furthermore, scaling model capacity to 14B parameters yielded a lower localized evaluation perplexity (1.414). Subsequent Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) successfully decoupled the underlying assertive behavior from localized syntax, while rigorous cross-lingual stress testing reveals both the capabilities and the structural boundaries of zero-shot persona transfer, demonstrating robust alignment in closely related linguistic families alongside identifiable degradation pathways in morphologically distant targets. These findings establish a rigorous empirical framework for compute-efficient, cross-lingual behavioral modification.

</details>


### [265] [EEG-PRIME: Prototype-Aligned Representation Learning with Multi-Level Conditioning for EEG Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13072)
*Shuailei Zhang,Muyun Jiang,Wei Zhang,Jinbo Chen,Zhiwei Guo,Yong Li,Yi Ding,Cuntai Guan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A two-stage EEG foundation model (EEG-PRIME) for cross-dataset, multi-task decoding using masked pretraining plus instruction tuning; demonstrates strong cross-subject and zero-shot transfer across 16 datasets and multiple EEG paradigms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address domain shifts in EEG data across acquisition protocols and individual neurophysiology that cause poor generalization; enable cross-dataset, cross-task decoding without per-domain calibration.

Method: Stage 1: masked pretraining with frequency-cutoff spectral augmentation to learn transferable representations. Stage 2: instruction tuning with task-semantic, dataset-specific, and subject-invariant conditioning. The conditioning modulates the Q-Former via Layer-wise Query Modulation; frozen text embeddings of class labels serve as prototypes for cosine-similarity-based prediction across heterogeneous label spaces.

Result: Consistent improvements over state-of-the-art baselines and prior EEG foundation models under cross-subject settings across 16 datasets (motor imagery, emotion recognition, ADHD detection, covert speech, mental workload). On two held-out datasets, balanced accuracy comparable to within-session calibration models without target-domain optimization, calibration, or linear probing (zero-shot transfer).

Conclusion: EEG-PRIME demonstrates robust cross-dataset, cross-task generalization and promising zero-shot transfer, indicating the viability of instruction-tuned EEG foundation models for robust BCI decoding across diverse paradigms.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) decoding models often generalize poorly across datasets and subjects due to domain shifts in acquisition protocols and individual neurophysiology. We propose EEG-PRIME, a two-stage EEG foundation model for cross-dataset multi-task decoding. EEG-PRIME combines masked pretraining with prototype-aligned instruction tuning to enable instruction-aware and subject-invariant decoding across diverse BCI paradigms. During pretraining, an EEG encoder learns transferable representations through masked reconstruction with frequency-cutoff spectral augmentation. During instruction tuning, EEG-PRIME incorporates task-semantic, dataset-specific, and subject-invariant conditioning. The resulting conditioning signal modulates the Q-Former through Layer-wise Query Modulation, while frozen text embeddings of class labels serve as prototypes for cosine-similarity-based prediction across heterogeneous label spaces. Experiments on sixteen datasets covering motor imagery, emotion recognition, ADHD detection, covert speech, and mental workload show consistent improvements over state-of-the-art baselines and prior EEG foundation models under cross-subject settings. On two additional held-out datasets, EEG-PRIME achieves balanced accuracy comparable to within-session calibration models without target-domain optimization, calibration, or linear probing, demonstrating promising zero-shot transfer capability.

</details>


### [266] [SPADE: Speculative Decoding for Precise and Low Cost Distributed Edge Cloud Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13076)
*Divya Jyoti Bajpai,Kishan Kumar Upadhyay,Manjesh Kumar Hanawal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Edge-cloud speculative decoding reduces cloud usage by leveraging a compact edge draft model and a cloud verifier to approve tokens; achieves similar accuracy with 76% fewer cloud calls and no retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce inference cost and latency of LLMs while maintaining accuracy by distributing compute between edge and cloud.

Method: Edge draft model generates token candidates; cloud big verifier validates in parallel; accepted tokens kept; rejections trigger corrections; no model retraining; plug-and-play integration.

Result: Demonstrates up to 76% cloud-call reduction with zero accuracy loss on SpecBench and CNN/DailyMail; faster inference and lower cloud cost.

Conclusion: A practical approach for scalable, cost-efficient LLM deployment across edge-cloud setups without retraining.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success in natural language understanding and generation, but their deployment is constrained by high computational demands. Deploying smaller LLMs directly on the edge can circumvent this, but with degraded accuracy. Deploying smaller cloud-based big LLMs preserves performance, but at the cost of expensive per-token computation. We present a distributed inference framework, \our{}, that integrates speculative decoding (SD) across edge and cloud. A compact draft model deployed on the edge generates candidate tokens rapidly, and a large verifier model on the cloud validates these tokens in parallel. Accepted tokens are retained, while only rejections trigger verifier correction, substantially reducing the number of cloud queries. Our plug-and-play design shifts the bulk of computation to the edge, significantly lowers inference time and cloud cost, and preserves the accuracy of the big model without any retraining requirement. Our approach demonstrates a practical path toward scalable, cost-efficient, and accurate deployment of LLMs in real-world environments. Experimental results across multiple Natural Language Processing tasks using SpecBench and CNN/Dailymail datasets demonstrate that \our{} reduces the cloud model calls by $76\%$ with zero loss in accuracy as compared to the full model.

</details>


### [267] [Multi-Layer Context Camouflaging: A Semantic Superposition and Contextual Lamination Framework for Malpractice-Resilient Online Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13100)
*Gupta Lovi Raj,Kaur Kamalpreet,Dama Sri Ram,Parani Prajithaa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MCCT extends MSCCM within MARS, introducing a multi-layer, semantically superposed camouflage framework that renders authentic assessment content inseparable from camouflage; it formalizes an extraction-channel, six operators, and entropy-based ambiguity, with guaranteed legitimate recovery and a rendering algorithm plus evaluation protocol.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To counter browser lockdown, webcam monitoring, and behavioural analytics vulnerabilities that allow content extraction via screenshots, OCR, screen sharing, etc., by providing a rigorous mathematical framework for content camouflage that preserves accessibility for legitimate users.

Method: Proposes the Multi-Layer Context Camouflaging Theory (MCCT) with semantic superposition of authentic content and camouflage. Defines an extraction-channel operator and six coupled constructs (Context Inversion, Contextual Lamination, Separation Channel, Human Readability Functional, Computational Ambiguity Functional, Context Camouflage Tensor). Uses conditional entropy to quantify ambiguity; ensures legitimate recovery via an exact filtering identity; develops a rendering algorithm with complexity analysis and a pre-registered evaluation protocol.

Result: Establishes theoretical properties: ambiguity bounds, camouflage density, semantic preservation, multi-observation leakage, and temporal multiplexing. Provides a closed-form expression for computational ambiguity and a rendering algorithm with known complexity, along with an evaluation protocol.

Conclusion: MCCT furnishes a mathematically rigorous foundation for resilient, accessible digital assessment by securing rendered content while keeping readability for legitimate users, enabling behaviorally adaptive and computation-resilient assessment.

Abstract: Contemporary online assessment systems rely primarily on browser lockdown, webcam monitoring, and behavioural analytics, yet remain vulnerable to attacks that extract the assessment content itself through screenshots, screen sharing, optical character recognition, and automated scraping. This paper extends the Multi-dimensional Spatio-Temporal Context Camouflaging Model (MSCCM) within the MARS (Multi-modal Assessment Resilience Suite) by introducing the Multi-Layer Context Camouflaging Theory (MCCT), a mathematical framework that protects rendered assessment content through semantic superposition. Authentic assessment content and synthetically generated camouflage are represented as a unified rendering while remaining recoverable only by legitimate candidates. The framework models the adversarial extraction process through an explicit extraction-channel operator and develops six coupled constructs: the Context Inversion Operator, Contextual Lamination Operator, Separation Channel, Human Readability Functional, Computational Ambiguity Functional, and Context Camouflage Tensor. Computational ambiguity is formulated using conditional entropy, yielding a closed-form expression that quantifies uncertainty during unauthorized extraction, while legitimate recovery is guaranteed through an exact filtering identity. We further establish theoretical properties governing ambiguity, camouflage density, semantic preservation, multi-observation leakage, and temporal multiplexing, and present a rendering algorithm with computational complexity and a pre-registered evaluation protocol. MCCT provides a mathematically rigorous foundation for behaviorally adaptive, accessibility-aware, and computationally resilient digital assessment by securing rendered assessment content while preserving readability for legitimate users.

</details>


### [268] [Robust Dempster-Shafer Evidence Fusion with Chaos-Conflict Measurement and Historical-Experience Weighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13108)
*Huiyu Li,Weibo Liu,Xinru Xu,Dongchen Gao,Meng Zhang,Junhua Hu*

Main category: cs.AI
