TL;DR: A unified DS-based evidence fusion framework addressing cross-evidence conflict and intra-evidence non-specificity, using a chaos-conflict measure with five properties, and a historical experience-driven weighting (spectral clustering + regret theory) to form a context-aware hybrid rule and belief-interval decision strategy; verified on 16 datasets with improved F1 and AUC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome two gaps in existing DS literature: (1) decoupled assessment of cross-evidence conflict and intra-evidence uncertainty, and (2) fusion methods relying only on instantaneous comparisons without leveraging long-term reliability in varied decision contexts.

Method: Introduce a chaos-conflict measurement with five proven properties; develop a historical experience driven weighting scheme that partitions decision space via spectral clustering and derives context-specific reliability profiles from past fusion outcomes using regret theory; integrate these into a hybrid combination rule that adaptively balances uncertainty preservation with weighted consensus based on global conflict; apply a belief-interval decision strategy to maintain epistemic uncertainty.

Result: Empirical validation on 16 real-world benchmarks showing average F1 of 85.78 and mean AUC of 93.30, outperforming eight DST-based baselines and three gradient boosting methods; ablation confirms each proposed component's contribution.

Conclusion: The framework provides an effective, adaptive approach for multi-source evidence fusion in decision-making tasks, combining improved conflict/uncertainty assessment, context-aware reliability, and robust decision strategies.

Abstract: Multi-source evidence fusion under Dempster-Shafer theory faces two persistent challenges: existing conflict measures assess inter-evidence inconsistency and intra-evidence uncertainty independently, yielding incomplete evaluations, and current fusion methods evaluate evidence sources exclusively through instantaneous comparisns without exploiting their long-term reliability across diverse decision contexts. This paper proposes a unified evidence reasoning framework that addresses both limitations. Specifically, a chaos-conflict measurement is introduced to jointly quantify cross-evidence conflict and intra-evidence non-specificity, with five formally proven properties ensuring consistent assessment. A historical experience driven weighting scheme partitions the decision space via spectral clustering and applies regret theory to compute context-specific reliability profiles from past fusion outcomes. These mechanisms feed into a hybrid combination rule that adaptively balances uncertainty preservation against weighted consensus, controlled by the global conflict level, followed by a belief-interval decision strategy that enables robust classification without discarding epistemic uncertainty. Experiments on 16 real-world benchmark datasets demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves an average F1 score of 85.78 and a mean AUC of 93.30, outperforming eight DST-based baselines and three gradient boosting methods. Ablation analysis confirms the contribution of each component we proposed. The framework offers an effective approach for adaptive evidence fusion in multi-source decision making.

</details>


### [269] [SkillEvo: Self-Renewing Evolution Gradients from Multi-Turn Interaction Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13120)
*Qianxi Yan,Chunrong Chen,Jiuzhou Zhao,Min Zhang,Yongzhou Xu,Xiaochuan Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillEvo delivers a multi-turn feedback loop plus an independent governance layer to sustain and direct skill evolution, outperforming prior QA-driven approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current skill evolution relies on single-turn QA or end-to-end scoring, which fails to expose and repair defects that emerge across multiple interactions. A trustworthy gradient and governance are needed to sustain improvement.

Method: Two components: (1) feedback generator that uses multi-turn user simulation to expose defects layer by layer via follow-up questions, turning feedback into revisions in each round; (2) governance layer that actively repairs degradation and structural bloat, preventing gradient drift, and constraining direction.

Result: Across six cloud-service categories, 9 production Skills, and 98 skill-reference files, SkillEvo outperforms self-reflection-based evolution by 23.0 points and single-turn QA-driven evolution by 15.4 points.

Conclusion: Trustworthy, continuous feedback plus an active governance mechanism enables sustained skill evolution beyond single-turn evaluation, with empirical gains across diverse skills.

Abstract: Agent Skills are today either hand-authored or produced in a single LLM generation pass, and consequently possess no closed loop through which they might improve from the interaction failures they actually cause. Recent work does close this loop, but derives its feedback from single-turn question-answering evaluation. The consequence is a sharp asymmetry: once the first round has patched the gaps that a single exchange can reveal, the evolution gradient decays, the defects that surface only across multiple turns remain invisible, and evolution stalls. Governance in these systems is likewise driven by an end-to-end verification score, a scalar gate that can reject a degraded candidate but can neither localize nor repair its structural cause. We argue that the binding constraint on sustained skill evolution is neither editing capability nor the number of iterations, but whether the evaluation feedback keeps supplying trustworthy evolution gradients. We introduce SkillEvo, in which trustworthy feedback generates the gradient and controllable governance constrains its direction. The first component recasts multi-turn user simulation from an evaluation endpoint into a feedback generator: follow-up questions expose defects layer by layer, so that every round of revision both consumes feedback and produces new feedback. The second replaces the passive rejection of a scalar gate with an independent governance layer that actively repairs factual degradation and structural bloat, preventing the gradient from drifting as degradation accumulates. Across six categories of cloud services, 9 production Skills, and 98 skill-reference files, SkillEvo surpasses self-reflection-based evolution by 23.0 points and single- turn-QA-driven evolution by 15.4 points.

</details>


### [270] [Numeracy in Large Language Models: Fundamental Limitations and Paths to Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13129)
*Aoxin Ni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NGF decomposes numeracy into Representational Grounding (mapping numerals to values) and Procedural Grounding (performing arithmetic by definitions) to diagnose, benchmark, and mitigate numerical shortcomings in LLMs; evaluates frontier models on Number Cookbook, NumericBench, and GSM-Symbolic; discusses tokenization, embeddings, and pretraining; proposes digit-aware tokenization and Abacus Embeddings; offers deployment guidance and future research directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs excel at general math reasoning but falter on elementary numerical tasks. A dedicated framework is needed to separate numeracy from higher-level math and to guide diagnostics, evaluation, and mitigation.

Method: Propose the Numerical Grounding Framework (NGF) that splits numeracy into Representational Grounding and Procedural Grounding; organize diagnostic benchmarks and failure modes; review tokenization, embedding geometry, and pretraining data; apply NGF to a coordinated evaluation of frontier models across three benchmarks; discuss architectural interventions and practical deployment strategies.

Result: A diagnostic and organizing framework that clarifies numerical failure modes, links architectural and data-related causes to performance, and surfaces practical mitigations; cross-model evaluation highlights where existing pretrained systems struggle and where targeted interventions may help; notes limited accessibility of some interventions for users of pretrained models and emphasizes fine-tuning, scaffolds, and tools as practical remedies.

Conclusion: Advancing numeracy in foundation models requires a combination of architectural tweaks (e.g., digit-aware tokenization, Abacus Embeddings), targeted training/fine-tuning, reasoning scaffolds, and external tools; the NGF provides a structured path for future research and deployment improvements.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) achieve strong results on mathematical reasoning benchmarks yet remain unreliable on elementary numerical tasks, including magnitude comparison, large-integer arithmetic, fractions, and scientific notation. This survey examines basic numerical understanding as a capability distinct from high-level mathematical reasoning. We propose the Numerical Grounding Framework (NGF), which decomposes numeracy into Representational Grounding (RG), mapping numeral forms to value, magnitude, and equivalent representations, and Procedural Grounding (PG), executing arithmetic operations in accordance with their mathematical definitions. Using NGF, we organize recent diagnostic benchmarks, failure modes, structural explanations, and mitigation strategies. We review evidence concerning tokenization, positional encoding, embedding geometry, and pretraining-data distribution. We also apply NGF in a coordinated evaluation of three frontier model families across Number Cookbook, NumericBench, and GSM-Symbolic, comparing atomic, contextual, and reasoning-assisted numeracy. Architectural interventions such as digit-aware tokenization and Abacus Embeddings can improve models trained from scratch but are generally unavailable to users of pretrained systems, for whom supervised fine-tuning, reasoning scaffolds, and external tools are more practical. We conclude with deployment recommendations and research directions for more reliable numerical behavior in foundation models.

</details>


### [271] [Rethinking Normalization Placement for LLMs: Post-Norm under Curriculum Depth Growing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13156)
*Sheng Ren,Yadong Wang,Naiqiang Tan,Jiangang Kong,Jun Fang,Rui Liu,Jun Wang,Kai Chen,Lipeng Liang,Xiang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Post-norm and curriculum growth interact in distillation: pre-norm and post-norm are nearly identical under joint training, but post-norm markedly outperforms pre-norm when blocks are appended, implying normalization placement should be treated as coupled with depth curriculum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether the standard Transformer pre-norm placement persists when depth is introduced via a curriculum growth strategy, and whether normalization placement and curriculum interact in knowledge distillation with a mid-size teacher-student setup.

Method: Distillation with a Qwen3-8B teacher and a nine-layer student, comparing pre-norm vs post-norm; evaluating joint training versus curriculum depth growth; controlled ablations including a post-joint control matched by active-layer tokens, single-block and freeze controls; diagnostic analyses of boundary scales and residual mappings.

Result: Under joint training, pre-norm and post-norm are indistinguishable (validation CE difference = 0.0004). Under curriculum growth, post-norm improves over pre-norm by 0.0328 CE, an order of magnitude larger. A post-joint control remains worse than post-grow (ruling out compute as sole factor). The ranking crosses over during curriculum: post-norm leads after blocks are appended. Localized effects from single-block/freeze controls point to block-appending rather than shallow-block quality. Boundary diagnostics show post-norm with stable residual scales and pre-norm with drift in structural-token scales; the final pre-grow block is nearly identity-mapped. These patterns support boundary-scale conditioning after appending new blocks.

Conclusion: Normalization placement and training curriculum are coupled design choices in this distillation setting. The observed phase-wise crossover and boundary-scale conditioning suggest that post-norm is advantageous specifically when conditioning on newly appended blocks, motivating joint consideration of normalization placement and curriculum strategy in depth-augmented Transformers.

Abstract: Pre-norm is the standard normalization placement in modern Transformers because it facilitates joint optimization of full-depth models. We ask whether this preference persists when depth is introduced through a curriculum. In curriculum depth growth, each appended block receives the boundary representation produced by a trained prefix, making normalization placement relevant to forward conditioning. We therefore test whether placement and training curriculum interact. In a controlled distillation study with a Qwen3-8B teacher and a nine-layer student, pre-norm and post-norm are indistinguishable under joint training, differing by $0.0004$ validation CE, while post-norm improves over pre-norm by $0.0328$ under curriculum growth, an order of magnitude larger. A post-joint control matched by student active-layer tokens remains worse than post-grow, which rules out compute as the sole explanation. The ranking crosses over during the curriculum: post-norm takes the lead once blocks are appended. Single-block and freeze controls localize the ranking change to block appending rather than shallow-block quality or retraining. Boundary diagnostics associate post-norm with stable residual scales and pre-norm with structural-token scale drift; on a fixed batch, the final pre-grow block is also nearly identity-mapped. Together with the phase-wise crossover, these observations are consistent with boundary-scale conditioning after new blocks are appended. The results motivate treating normalization placement and training curriculum as coupled design choices in this distillation setting.

</details>


### [272] [SkillShapley: Boundary-Adaptive Shapley Valuation for Skill Step Attribution in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13173)
*Chang Liu,Yuqi Zhang,Yiman Zhong,Boyi Liu,Hengjun Wang,Shuyue Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillShapley presents a Shapley-value-based, two-phase framework to attribute the contribution of individual steps in agent skills, enabling efficient identification of high- and low-value steps through adaptive coalition sampling and discretized reward cliffs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantifying step-level contributions within agent skills is crucial for improving skill design, yet existing methods rely on manual crafting or execution traces and fail to quantify each step's impact on task performance.

Method: Two-phase approach: (1) identify informative coalitional regions by exploiting discretized benchmark rewards that create sharp performance cliffs; (2) adaptively sample coalitions to obtain reusable marginal evidence, leveraging the largely additive nature of step interactions.

Result: Experiments on SkillsBench demonstrate that SkillShapley can effectively and efficiently identify high- or low-value skill steps, providing actionable insights for agent skill creation.

Conclusion: SkillShapley offers a practical, Shapley-based attribution framework for agent skills, enabling targeted improvement of individual steps and more efficient skill design by focusing on informative coalitions and additive interactions.

Abstract: Agent skills are crucial external instructions that enable language agents to execute long procedural tasks such as coding or document processing. Existing agent skills are primarily created through human manual crafting or agent execution traces, with limited understanding of how each step contributes to overall skill performance on specific tasks; i.e., there remains an open problem in quantifying the contribution of individual steps within an agent skill. To address this issue, we first model skill-step attribution as a Shapley value-based contribution estimation problem, and then propose SkillShapley, a step-level attribution framework for agent skills. Notably, SkillShapley operates in two phases, motivated by key empirical insights, i.e., discretized benchmark rewards that create sharp performance cliffs, and step interactions that are largely additive rather than synergistic. Specifically, it first identifies informative coalitional regions, and then adaptively samples new coalitions that can yield reusable marginal evidence. Experiments on skills from the widely adopted SkillsBench demonstrate that our SkillShapley can effectively and efficiently identify high- or low-value skill steps, providing several key takeaways for agent skill creation.

</details>


### [273] [Teach the Magnitude, Not the Direction: Verifier-Bounded Credit Assignment for Multi-Turn Multi-step LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13179)
*Zechuan Wang,Siyuan Lu,Hongxuan Zhang,Linjian Mo,Chenyi Zhuang,Leilei Gan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CrEST is a hierarchical credit assignment framework that blends reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards and dense token-level supervision. It uses turn-segmented verified advantages to fix inter-turn dilution and an entropy-gated self-teacher to refine intra-turn token contributions, achieving superior performance across model scales and long trajectories while preserving verifier-bounded guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RLVR provides a verifier-bounded performance ceiling but suffers from trajectory-level credit assignment that mixes heterogeneous per-turn outcomes; on-policy distillation offers dense token-level supervision but can be teacher-bounded and prone to gradient concentration collapse. There is a need to retain the verifier bound while enabling dense, token-level credit assignment.

Method: CrEST introduces two-level credit assignment: (1) turn-segmented verified advantages to mitigate inter-turn dilution, and (2) entropy-gated self-teacher modulation to fine-tune intra-turn token contributions. A privileged self-teacher supplies dense supervision without altering the verifier-bounded ceiling; the self-teacher modulates update magnitudes via entropy-based gating rather than dictating update directions.

Result: Empirical evaluation on BFCL V3 and WildToolBench shows CrEST consistently outperforms both reinforcement learning and distillation baselines across two model scales, with the largest gains on long trajectories and strict session-level metrics.

Conclusion: A teacher’s role in policy optimization can be reframed from selecting update directions to modulating update magnitudes, enabling dense credit assignment while preserving the verifier-bounded ceiling.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) offers a verifier-bounded performance ceiling for training multi-turn tool-use agents, yet its trajectory-level credit assignment conflates heterogeneous per-turn outcomes into a single reward signal. On-policy distillation provides dense per-token supervision but is either teacher-bounded or prone to gradient concentration collapse. We introduce $\textbf{CrEST}$, a hierarchical credit assignment framework that retains RL's verifier-bounded ceiling while incorporating dense token-level signals from a privileged self-teacher. $\textbf{CrEST}$ resolves credit at two levels: turn-segmented verified advantages address inter-turn dilution, while entropy-gated self-teacher modulation refines intra-turn token contributions. Experiments on BFCL V3 and WildToolBench show that $\textbf{CrEST}$ consistently outperforms both RL and distillation baselines across two model scales, with the largest gains on long-trajectory and strict session-level metrics. Our work demonstrates that the teacher's role in policy optimization can be reduced from determining update directions to modulating update magnitudes, unlocking dense credit assignment without sacrificing the verifier-bounded ceiling.

</details>


### [274] [TsuGO: Probing Search Efficiency in LLM Reasoning via Go Life-and-Death Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13221)
*Shunwen Bai,Ziping Ma,Chaoyang Zhang,Yarong Wang,Jiale Liu,Zhen Qin,Qingpei Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TsuGO introduces a process-level reasoning benchmark for LLMs, focusing on search efficiency in Go life-and-death problems by constraining solution spaces and parsing CoT as a search tree to separate search organization from domain knowledge; results show current LLMs underperform, with longer CoT not guaranteeing better search, highlighting a missing dimension in evaluating LLM reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation shifts from final-answer accuracy to process-level reasoning, but existing methods miss how models plan reasoning paths and allocate resources. There is a need to isolate search organization from domain knowledge to properly assess reasoning strategies.

Method: Use closed, adversarially structured Go tsumego problems with verifiable solution spaces. Force candidate generation, response checking, branch comparison, and backtracking as integral reasoning steps. Parse chain-of-thought into a structured search tree, and report metrics such as Search Efficiency and Token Efficiency along with diagnostics and visualizations.

Result: Stronger models reach correct candidates earlier and sustain productive branches, but many models resemble unguided search and do not show neural-guided search patterns like KataGo. Longer CoT or higher Token Efficiency does not guarantee better search performance; overall, search organization remains a gap in current LLM reasoning evaluation.

Conclusion: TsuGO provides a framework to quantify search organization and resource allocation in LLM reasoning, disentangling search strategy from domain knowledge and highlighting the need to incorporate reasoning-process metrics into evaluation.

Abstract: The evaluation of LLM reasoning is moving from final-answer accuracy to process-level assessment, yet existing methods still fail to capture how models plan reasoning paths and allocate reasoning resources--that is, how they organize search. Prior process-level methods focus on the coherence and redundancy of chain-of-thought (CoT), and most benchmark tasks have a single objective solvable by static capabilities such as derivation and tool use, leaving search organization unmeasured. We introduce TsuGO, a process-level reasoning benchmark for evaluating Search Efficiency in LLM reasoning through Go life-and-death problems. These problems provide closed and verifiable solution spaces with an inherent adversarial structure, making candidate generation, response checking, branch comparison, and backtracking necessary parts of reasoning rather than incidental trace patterns. By constraining the solution space, TsuGO disentangles domain knowledge from search organization, parses CoT into a structured search tree, and reports Search Efficiency together with Token Efficiency and other diagnostic metrics and visualizations. Experiments show that current LLMs remain far from stable tsumego solving: stronger models succeed by finding the correct candidate earlier and sustaining effort on productive branches, but most models still behave much closer to unguided search algorithms than to neural-guided KataGo. Longer CoT or higher Token Efficiency does not necessarily imply better search. Our results identify search organization and reasoning-resource allocation as missing dimensions in LLM reasoning evaluation.

</details>


### [275] [Capability Sheaves for Compositional Agent-Harness Repair: Controlled Quotients and a Real-Repository Stress Test](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13228)
*Saveliy Batruin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a capability-sheaf model to diagnose and route shared-state disagreements in agent systems, using exact CSPs and linearized relative cohomology. Experimental results show invariance to stale representations but no clear real-world cohomological advantage; identifiability corrections improve some metrics, yet gains are not consistently robust across repositories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address inconsistent shared-state among locally successful components in autonomous agents by formalizing a global consistency mechanism via sheaf theory, CSPs, and cohomology; aim to enable reliable verification and patch-level search.

Method: Model components as a capability sheaf with stalks for typed behavior, restriction maps for shared fields, and global sections as accepted runs. Use an exact finite CSP for acceptance and a linearized relative cohomology class for diagnostics and search. Experimentally inject hidden mediators, quotient coboundaries to study invariance, and evaluate on PatchFuseBench-derived discovery splits with pool-level constructions, candidate-indexed repairs, and leave-one-repository-out abstention gates.

Result: Exact CSP matched the quotient, indicating invariance to stale representatives rather than superior reasoning. On PatchFuseBench discovery, pool-level construction is constant; candidate-indexed repair is nontrivial on most candidates and resolves a subset of issues, but gains are not statistically consistent across repositories. Leave-one-repo-out gate ties strong anchor, exceeding its matched gate by one issue; overall the discovery gate fails and confirmatory split remains sealed. The study supports the invariance mechanism and identifiability correction but not a clear real-world cohomological advantage.

Conclusion: The framework validates controlled invariance and an identifiability correction mechanism, but does not demonstrate a robust cohomological advantage in real-world tasks; further work is needed to translate the theoretical gains into consistent performance improvements.

Abstract: Agent harnesses combine retrieval, routing, state, provenance, and verification, but locally successful components may disagree on shared state. We model this failure with a finite \emph{capability sheaf}: stalks encode typed behavior signatures, restriction maps retain shared fields, and accepted runs are useful global sections. An exact finite constraint-satisfaction problem (CSP) defines acceptance, while a linearized relative cohomology class provides a diagnostic and search feature.
  A controlled experiment over 20 task clusters introduces hidden interior mediators whose raw states are nuisance variables. Quotienting their coboundaries reduces the candidate budget from 2,000 to 1,000 per cluster; aligning the hidden state removes the gap. Exact CSP matches the quotient, so the result demonstrates invariance to stale representatives, not superiority over exact reasoning.
  We then test the method on a discovery split from the SWE-bench Multilingual pool of PatchFuseBench: 160 issues from 20 repositories, 875 real candidate patches, 2,579 source-aware edit atoms, and 153 newly executed patches. A first pool-level construction is constant because $[b-Dx]=[b]$ in $\operatorname{coker}D$ and therefore cannot rank configurations. A candidate-indexed repair is nontrivial on 848/875 candidates and varies within 120/160 issues. It resolves 118 issues versus 116 for a matched noncohomological selector, but the difference is not supported across repositories (exact sign-flip $p=0.75$). A leave-one-repository-out abstention gate reaches 127/160, tying the strong anchor and exceeding its matched gate by one issue ($p=1.0$). The discovery gate therefore fails and the confirmatory split remains sealed. The study supports the controlled invariance mechanism and an identifiability correction, but not a real-world cohomological advantage.

</details>


### [276] [vToken: Token-Level Virtualization for Reclaimable KV Caches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13263)
*Yuanhang Gao,Xiangrui Yang,Yuanfeng Chen,Hongjia Chen,Qianru Lv,Wenfei Wu,Dongsheng Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: vToken introduces a token-level virtualization layer to decouple logical token liveness from physical memory blocks in KV caches, mitigating fragmentation and reclaim inefficiency in PagedAttention-based LLM serving.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: KV caches for large language model serving grow with sequence length and batch size, and block-level memory management leads to intra-block fragmentation when evictions occur at token granularity.

Method: Implement a token-level virtualization layer (vToken) that uses token-table indirection to provide a stable logical view while asynchronously repacking live tokens to reclaim memory. This preserves existing PagedAttention kernels and CUDA Graph compatibility and is integrated into vLLM; evaluated on H2O, Random, and Scissorhands across models.

Result: Compared to a paired Naive-Evict baseline, vToken reduces retained KV blocks per request by 27.2%–72.3% and improves SLA-constrained throughput by up to 1.37×. Under a constrained active-KV budget, it doubles the maximum feasible concurrency and reduces per-policy integration footprint from 500+ lines to under 50.

Conclusion: vToken effectively mitigates intra-block fragmentation, enabling higher throughput and concurrency with simpler policy integration while preserving existing kernel compatibility.

Abstract: Large language model serving faces a critical memory bottleneck: the KV cache grows with sequence length and batch size. PagedAttention uses fixed-size memory blocks to reduce allocator-level fragmentation, but recent KV eviction algorithms operate at a token granularity finer than block-level management. This mismatch causes intra-block fragmentation, leaving a large fraction of allocated KV memory unreclaimable. We present vToken, a lightweight token-level virtualization layer that decouples logical token liveness from physical block placement. vToken maintains a stable logical token view through token-table indirection and realizes physical reclamation by repacking live tokens asynchronously. The design preserves PagedAttention kernels and CUDA Graph compatibility. We implement vToken in vLLM and evaluate it with H2O, Random, and Scissorhands across models. Compared with a paired Naive-Evict baseline, vToken reduces retained KV blocks per request by 27.2\%--72.3\% and improves SLA-constrained throughput by up to 1.37$\times$. Under a constrained active-KV budget, it extends the maximum feasible concurrency by up to 2$\times$, while reducing the per-policy integration footprint from 500+ lines to under 50.

</details>


### [277] [Sovereign by necessity? Frontier AI export controls, cyber security, and the limits of national AI capability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13272)
*Alan Woodward,Andrew Rogoyski*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Frontier AI is concentrated in a few states and treated as a national cyber-defense asset; licensing and access controls can restrict use but are costly and brittle. The paper links frontier-model proliferation to cyber-security economics, arguing sovereignty is partly feasible only for a few states and proposes a layered governance strategy including negotiated access, inference-level sovereignty, open-weight hedges, regional pooling, talent development, and cyber resilience—though deployment/containment risks dominate near-term concerns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how geopolitics, national security, and economics interact in frontier AI development and to assess what sovereignty can realistically mean for states of different sizes under new market dynamics.

Method: Conceptual policy analysis drawing on evidence about training costs, compute concentration, and national AI programs; synthesizes governance options and evaluates an open-weight hedge.

Result: Access to frontier AI is increasingly tied to national cyber defense and can be revoked; sovereign capability remains partly feasible and is most plausible for a few states. A layered strategy is proposed, including negotiated access guarantees, sovereignty at inference, open-weight hedges, pooled regional capability, talent development, and cyber resilience.

Conclusion: Near-term risk lies in how frontier models are deployed and contained rather than their technical performance. Effective governance will rely on layered strategies and sustained investment in resilience, with open-weight approaches both beneficial and politically risky.

Abstract: A small number of firms based in two states produce the most capable frontier AI models. The governments of those states have shown both the legal power and the political will to decide which other countries may use these systems. In June 2026 the United States required a leading developer to obtain licences before releasing its most advanced models to any foreign person, including foreign nationals resident in the United States. The affected models were withdrawn worldwide at short notice, partly because the restriction proved impractical to administer. This followed within months of the first documented case of a largely autonomous, AI-run cyber espionage campaign, and coincided with mounting evidence that frontier models alter the economics of both cyber attack and cyber defence. This article examines how these two developments interact, and situates them within the unusual market dynamics now driving large-scale AI development. It argues that access to frontier AI is becoming part of national cyber defence, that such access can be revoked, and that the obvious remedy of sovereign capability remains only partly feasible for all but a handful of states. Drawing on evidence about training costs, the concentration of computing power and the support offered by national AI programmes, it asks what sovereignty can realistically mean for small and middle powers, and for large powers as well. The article proposes a layered strategy: negotiated access guarantees, sovereignty at the level of inference, hedging with open-weight models, pooled regional capability, sustained talent development and continued investment in basic cyber resilience. The open-weight hedge proves at once more capable and more politically exposed than is commonly assumed. Much of the near-term risk lies in how capable models are deployed and contained rather than in their apparent performance.

</details>


### [278] [Towards Context-Aware Clinical Motion Understanding in Daily Living at Home: Freezing of Gait Detection with Egocentric Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13283)
*Vayalet Stefanova,Diwas Lamsal,Margot Genbrugge,Maxim Yudayev,Christian Schlenstedt,Moran Gilat,Bart Vanrumste,Benjamin Filtjens*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IMU-based TCN outperformed pretrained ego-video representations in FOG detection; egocentric video provides contextual signals that can complement IMU data; results support multimodal fusion for PD monitoring in daily living.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motion understanding in daily living requires context beyond kinematics; freezing of gait in Parkinson's is highly context-dependent; egocentric vision can provide task-related cues to help disambiguate motion patterns.

Method: Synchronized egocentric video, wearable IMUs, and expert-annotated FOG labels collected from 13 PD participants in their homes. Evaluated frozen representations from pretrained ego-video and time-series foundation models, alongside an IMU-based TCN trained from scratch, using leave-one-subject-out evaluation.

Result: IMU-based TCN achieved 42.3 F1 and 83.0 AUROC. Ego-video features achieved 32.6 F1 and 77.2 AUROC. Ego-video alone did not outperform IMU-based sensing but was above chance; qualitative analyses suggest egocentric vision captures FOG-relevant information independent of IMUs.

Conclusion: Pretrained ego-video representations can add contextual information to wearable-sensor-based clinical motion understanding in daily living; multimodal approaches leveraging both IMU and egocentric cues are warranted for improved PD FOG detection.

Abstract: Understanding motion in daily living requires context beyond kinematics, because similar inertial patterns during activities of daily living (ADLs) can reflect intentional stopping, object interaction, or pathological movement impairment. Egocentric vision provides task-related context that may help disambiguate these cases. We investigate this challenge through freezing of gait (FOG) detection in Parkinson's disease (PD), a symptom strongly influenced by contextual factors during ADLs. Using synchronized egocentric video, wearable IMUs, and expert-annotated FOG labels collected from 13 PD participants in their homes, we evaluate frozen representations from pretrained ego-video and time-series foundation models, alongside an IMU-based TCN trained from scratch, under leave-one-subject-out evaluation. The IMU-based TCN achieved the strongest event-detection performance, reaching 42.3 F1 and 83.0 AUROC, compared with 32.6 F1 and 77.2 AUROC for V-JEPA2 ego-video features. Although ego-video alone did not outperform IMU-based sensing, it showed above-chance discrimination, and qualitative analyses suggest that egocentric vision may capture FOG-relevant information independent of IMUs. Together, these results support the use of pretrained ego-video representations to add contextual information to wearable-sensor-based clinical motion understanding in daily living.

</details>


### [279] [NAS-Driven Hardware Accelerator Exploration for Edge AI and Quantization Effects on the Pareto Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13293)
*Eleftherios Mylonas,Angelos Kouprizas,Michael Birbas,Alexios Birbas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a 3-stage hardware-aware NAS+PTQ pipeline and analyzes INT4 PTQ impact on NAS-Bench-201 Pareto fronts; FP32 zero-shot surrogate outperforms INT4-trained surrogate in Pareto coverage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the need for accurate, efficient, hardware-deployable networks by decoupling architecture search from quantization, quantify PTQ perturbations on Pareto fronts, and enable automated hardware exploration on reconfigurable accelerators.

Method: Stage 1: hardware-agnostic Pareto surrogate frontend on NAS-Bench-201. Stage 2: quantization bridge with Pareto-aware filtering and feedback control. Stage 3: evolutionary Domain Space Exploration (DSE) backend on CGRA4ML for optimal hardware mapping. Empirical study across all 15,625 NAS-Bench-201 architectures to quantify INT4 PTQ effects using stability metrics; compare FP32 zero-shot surrogate versus INT4-trained surrogate across two standard search strategies.

Result: INT4 PTQ perturbs the NAS-Bench-201 Pareto space; FP32 zero-shot surrogate provides better Pareto-space coverage than a dedicated INT4-trained surrogate, and this advantage persists across two search strategies.

Conclusion: A modular, hardware-aware NAS pipeline with a post-search quantization bridge can effectively map quantized architectures to reconfigurable accelerators, and FP32 surrogates remain competitive for Pareto coverage despite PTQ perturbations.

Abstract: Edge AI deployment demands neural architectures that are simultaneously accurate, computationally efficient, and hardware-deployable - a challenge addressed by hardware-aware Neural Architecture Search (NAS). While recent works incorporate quantization directly into the NAS loop, these approaches expand search complexity and tightly couple architecture and quantization design. The simpler post-search quantization strategy has received little analytical attention: the effects of Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) on the NAS-discovered Pareto structure remain uncharacterised, and no framework combines quantized architecture mapping onto reconfigurable accelerators with automated hardware exploration. This paper addresses both gaps. First, a three-stage pipeline is proposed: a hardware-agnostic Pareto rank surrogate frontend on NAS-Bench-201, a quantization bridge with Pareto-aware filtering and feedback control, and an evolutionary Domain Space Exploration (DSE) backend on CGRA4ML for optimal hardware mapping. Second, an empirical study characterises how INT4 PTQ perturbs the NAS-Bench-201 Pareto space through formal stability metrics on ground-truth data for all 15,625 architectures, and demonstrates that an FP32 zero-shot surrogate outperforms a dedicated INT4-trained surrogate in Pareto space coverage across two standard search strategies.

</details>


### [280] [StateBridge: Training-free Hidden-state Alignment for Latent Communication in LLM Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13317)
*Yanwen Peng,Delvin Ce Zhang,Xi Wang,Nikolaos Aletras*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StateBridge: a training-free latent communication method for multi-agent LLMs that aligns sender final-layer hidden states to the receiver's input space via a closed-form orthogonal transform; prepends the aligned continuous prefix to the receiver input, with lightweight norm calibration and vocabulary anchoring. It delivers strong, portable performance across math reasoning, code generation, and QA tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Text-based token communication creates a discrete bottleneck that discards information in hidden states. Latent communication promises richer transfer but existing approaches rely on layer-wise memory augmentation or trained projectors, limiting portability. A training-free, model-agnostic alignment could enable direct, robust hidden-state transmission.

Method: Compute a closed-form orthogonal transformation that aligns the sender’s final-layer hidden states to the receiver’s input space. Apply lightweight norm calibration and vocabulary anchoring to maintain compatibility with pretrained input distributions. Prepend the aligned states as a continuous prefix to the receiver’s input. Evaluate on math reasoning, code generation, and QA using four models from two families.

Result: Empirical results show StateBridge achieves the best or tied-best on 22 of 26 model-task pairs, consistently outperforming the strongest baseline across math, code, and QA tasks.

Conclusion: A training-free latent-communication mechanism using an orthogonal alignment provides portable, effective hidden-state transfer for multi-agent LLMs, offering a strong alternative to discrete token bottlenecks and to memory-heavy or projector-based latent methods.

Abstract: Large language model based multi-agent systems usually communicate in text, i.e., using discrete tokens. However, text introduces a discrete bottleneck. Converting the sender's continuous hidden states into discrete tokens discards information that token identities alone cannot capture. Recent work proposes latent communication as an alternative, where agents transmit hidden representations directly without converting them to text. However, existing latent methods either inject working memory layer by layer across the transformers, or require trained projectors that limit portability. We propose StateBridge, a training-free latent communication approach that aligns the sender's final-layer hidden states to the receiver's input space via a closed-form orthogonal transformation. Lightweight norm calibration and vocabulary anchoring ensure compatibility with the pretrained input distribution. The aligned states are prepended to the input of the receiver agent as a continuous prefix. We evaluate StateBridge on math reasoning, code generation, and question answering with four models from two families. StateBridge achieves the best or tied-best score on 22 out of 26 model-task pairs, consistently outperforming the strongest baseline.

</details>


### [281] [LLM-Guided Graph Generation for Structure-Based Local Improvement Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13333)
*Hai Xia,Vaidyanathan Peruvemba Ramaswamy,Stefan Szeider*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-driven, problem-agnostic graph generator maps MiniZinc instances to uniform weighted graphs to guide a structure-based local improvement framework; improves algorithm selection over a Gurobi baseline, with further gains from ablations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve large neighborhood search by selecting informative variables via structural features extracted from problem-agnostic graphs, enabling better configuration and cross-problem transfer.

Method: Prompt an LLM with semantic guidelines to produce a graph generator that maps each problem instance to a uniform weighted graph; extract graph features for configuration; apply SLIM for variable selection; evaluate on 20 MiniZinc problems; compare against one-shot Gurobi and various configurations.

Result: Average problem-weighted win rate of 39.5% vs Gurobi baseline; best single configuration 19.3%; configuration and feature ablation raise to 44.0%.

Conclusion: LLM-based semantic generation enables effective automatic structure extraction and feature extraction for constraint optimization, enabling problem-agnostic graph representations and improved optimization performance.

Abstract: Large neighborhood search normally selects a random subset of decision variables for iterative optimization. For efficiently solving different problems, researchers tend to design variable selection strategies by taking into account structural features from different domains. In this paper, we build an automatic pipeline that is problem-agnostic to all problems in the MiniZinc format. By prompting an LLM with our semantic guidelines, we guide the LLM to produce a graph generator that maps any instance of a problem type to a uniform weighted graph, where nodes represent decision variables and edges represent constraint relationships. These problem-agnostic graphs guide our structure-based local improvement framework (SLIM) in variable selection. Meanwhile, the weighted graph enables all problem instances to share the same generic graph representation, from which the same graph features can be extracted and used for configuration selection. We evaluated our pipeline on instances across 20 MiniZinc competition problems, finding that algorithm selection achieves a 39.5% average problem-weighted win rate against a one-shot Gurobi baseline, more than doubling the best single configuration (19.3%). Configuration and feature ablation boost the performance further to 44.0%, demonstrating that LLM-based semantic generation enables effective automated structure extraction and feature extraction for constraint optimization.

</details>


### [282] [LongEarth-R1: Benchmarking and Aligning Vision-Language Models for Long-Horizon Earth Observation Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13344)
*Yupan Ding,Jing Xiao,Zhenyuan Zhang,Chaofeng Chen,Liang Liao,Gui-Song Xia,Mi Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A long-horizon remote sensing reasoning benchmark (LongEarth-Bench) with 120k QA samples from 117k images, 15–30 frame sequences across 12 tasks, plus a 30k-trace subset; paired with LongEarth-R1, a group-relative policy optimization approach using format, temporal, and spatial rewards, achieving state-of-the-art on all long-sequence tasks and competitive results on standard RS benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the limitation of existing remote sensing vision-language models that focus on isolated images or short sequences, hindering reliable grounding across frames and regions in long-horizon Earth observation tasks.

Method: Construct LongEarth-Bench with about 120k QA samples drawn from 117k unique images; sequences average 15.14 frames, extend to 30 frames; cover 12 tasks (evolution summarization, spatial reasoning, anomaly identification, logical prediction). Provide a 30k-sample subset with structured reasoning traces linking key frames and changed regions to final answers. Train with supervised fine-tuning using explicit sequence identifiers and structured chain-of-thought supervision. Develop LongEarth-R1 by applying group-relative policy optimization with rewards for format, temporal, and spatial aspects.

Result: LongEarth-R1 achieves the best results across all 12 long-sequence tasks and remains competitive on standard remote sensing benchmarks.

Conclusion: The work advances long-sequence reasoning in remote sensing by providing a large-scale, multi-task benchmark and a reinforcement-learning–augmented model that can reason over extended image sequences and grounded changes.

Abstract: Long-horizon Earth observation reasoning requires models to organize multi-stage geographic evolution, localize spatial changes, detect temporal anomalies, and infer future from extended image sequences. However, existing remote sensing vision-language models mainly focus on isolated images, image pairs, or short sequences, limiting reliable grounding in the relevant frames and regions. We introduce LongEarth-Bench, a benchmark containing approximately 120k question-answering samples derived from 117k unique images. Its sequences average 15.14 frames and extend to 30 frames, covering 12 tasks across evolution summarization, spatial reasoning, anomaly identification, and logical prediction. A 30k-sample subset further provides structured reasoning traces linking key frames and changed regions to final answers. We develop LongEarth through supervised fine-tuning with explicit sequence identifiers and structured chain-of-thought supervision. Building on LongEarth, LongEarth-R1 applies group relative policy optimization with format, temporal, and spatial rewards. LongEarth-R1 achieves the best results on all 12 long-sequence tasks while remaining competitive on standard remote sensing benchmarks.

</details>


### [283] [Rules or Character? Scaling Laws for AI Safety Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13345)
*Satoshi Takahashi,Nobuji Kouno,Masaaki Komatsu,Ryuji Hamamoto*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A stylized model analyzes the balance between character shaping (training-time shaping) and rule enforcement (inference-time filtering) in AI safety. It shows that as deployment scales, the optimal emphasis shifts weakly toward character shaping, dominated by baseline character fragility, with CVaR and expected-harm optima converging at large scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is limited formal analysis of how to optimally balance safety design choices (character shaping vs. rule enforcement) as deployment scale increases and in the presence of distributional shift, scale-dependent degradation, and fragility.

Method: Introduce a comparative-statics model parameterized by alpha in [0,1] representing the resource allocation between shaping and enforcement. Include scale-dependent filter degradation, common-mode failures, and character fragility. Use a multiplicative Pareto damage model to derive closed-form expected harm and perform CVaR analysis via Monte Carlo. Analyze three deployment scenarios (optimistic, moderate, pessimistic) to determine the optimal alpha* across scale T.

Result: Optimal alpha* is interior or at the rules-only boundary, shifting weakly toward character shaping with scale: Delta alpha* ranges from +0.01 (optimistic/moderate) to +0.21 (pessimistic). The dominant driver is the baseline character fragility rate p^(0)_frag, which shifts alpha* by about 0.50 across its range, much larger than effects of tail severity, filter quality, or common-mode failure probability. CVaR and expected-harm optima converge at large T.

Conclusion: Decisions about safety architecture depend more on the reliability of character shaping under distributional shift than on deployment scale alone; improving robustness of the shaped behavior to shifts can have a larger impact on optimal design than simply increasing scale.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) safety systems combine character shaping (e.g., Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback [RLHF], Constitutional AI), which modifies behavioral distributions at training time, with rule enforcement (e.g., output filters, safety classifiers), which blocks harmful outputs at inference time, yet little formal analysis exists on how their optimal balance should change as deployment scales increase. We introduce a stylized comparative-statics model that parameterizes safety design as a resource allocation alpha in [0,1] between these two approaches, incorporating scale-dependent filter degradation, common-mode failures, and character fragility -- the risk that shaped behavior degrades or collapses under novel conditions. Under a multiplicative Pareto damage model, we derive closed-form expected harm and supplement it with tail-risk (CVaR) analysis via Monte Carlo simulation. Across three scenarios (optimistic, moderate, pessimistic), the optimal alpha* is interior or at the rules-only boundary and shifts weakly toward character shaping as deployment scale T grows, from negligible (Delta alpha* = +0.01) to pronounced (Delta alpha* = +0.21) depending on scenario. The dominant parameter is the baseline character fragility rate p^(0)_frag, which shifts alpha* by 0.50 across its range -- far exceeding the effect of tail severity, filter quality, or common-mode failure probability. CVaR and expected-harm optima converge at large T. These results suggest that safety architecture decisions depend less on deployment scale per se than on the reliability of character shaping under distributional shift.

</details>


### [284] [TopoIntent: Compiling Security Intent into Executable, Compliance-Checked Network Topologies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13389)
*Xiaokang Qu,Jianliang Ma,Zao Fan,Tianshu Chu,Tianlong Fan,Linyuan Lü*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TopoIntent is a system that converts security intent into executable, CIS-compliant network topologies using schema constraints, template retrieval, staged fusion, and additive repairs, then exports to Mininet with iptables for tests; evaluated on constructed retrieval/held-out sets with notable CIS/ACL improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gap in NetOps automation: translating underspecified natural-language security requirements into structured, verifiable topologies that satisfy regulatory and controls requirements.

Method: Schema-constrained generation; dense-vector retrieval of reference templates; staged fusion for intent-template alignment and security completion; additive, schema-preserving repairs; export to Mininet with kernel-level iptables ACLs; compliance checks against CIS Controls v8.1.2; unresolved cases flagged for manual review; evaluation using a custom dataset with retrieval and held-out sets across finance/government scenarios.

Result: On held-out data, additive repairs improve topology-visible CIS satisfaction from 0.78 to 1.00 in <1.5 rounds; a single feedback round increases post-ACL policy pass rate from 0.78 to 0.88; enables executable reachability and allow/deny testing via Mininet+iptables.

Conclusion: TopoIntent demonstrates a workable pipeline from security intent to executable, compliant topology with automated repairs and evaluative evidence; due to lack of public benchmarks, evaluation relies on synthetic/constructed templates; promising for automation but requires broader benchmarking and scalability assessment.

Abstract: Enterprise security topology design requires translating business intent, regulatory requirements, and risk assumptions into zones, boundary devices, inter-zone paths, and access-control policies. Existing NetOps automation tools mainly operate after this design is fixed, providing limited support for generating structured security topologies from underspecified natural-language requirements. We present TopoIntent, a system that compiles security intent into executable, compliance-checked network topologies. It uses a schema contract to constrain generation, retrieves reference architectures from a curated template library via dense-vector search, and applies staged fusion for intent-template alignment and security completion. The generated topology is checked against CIS Controls v8.1.2 safeguards visible at the topology layer, while unresolved cases are marked for manual review. Structural gaps are repaired through additive schema-preserving edits. The final topology is exported to Mininet scripts with kernel-level iptables ACLs, enabling executable reachability and allow/deny tests. Because no public benchmark exists for this requirement-to-topology task, we construct an evaluation set from reference security architecture diagrams. The retrieval set contains 22 templates and 44 synthetic intents across five scenarios, while the held-out set contains 7 templates and 14 intents from finance and government scenarios excluded from retrieval. On the held-out set, additive repair improves topology-visible CIS satisfaction from 0.78 to 1.00 in fewer than 1.5 rounds on average, and one feedback round raises the post-ACL policy pass rate from 0.78 to 0.88.

</details>


### [285] [Jointly Predicting Courses and Grades Using a Transformer-Based Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13409)
*Paul Savala*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Jointly predicting a student's upcoming semester course selections and grades with TRACE yields large accuracy gains over predicting grades alone and over conventional architectures by modeling course concurrency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current predictive models in learning analytics treat student history as a simple sequence and ignore concurrent course enrollments within a semester, leading to poorer predictions for heavy or challenging course loads.

Method: Propose TRACE, a Transformer-based model that encodes courses on a per-semester basis to capture concurrency. It jointly predicts the set of courses for the upcoming semester and the grades for those courses, using a loss that combines course-set prediction with grade prediction. Trained on ten years of institutional data, TRACE is designed to be adaptable via retraining/calibration and can incorporate student attributes.

Result: TRACE reduces mean absolute error by ~50% compared to an identical architecture predicting grades alone. It outperforms LSTM-based sequential models and graph neural networks, and supports integration of student attributes. It is interpretable and transferable across institutions.

Conclusion: Modern neural architectures like TRACE can effectively model course concurrency and joint course-grade prediction, improving accuracy and enabling early detection systems. The approach demonstrates the importance of predicting courses taken during training and offers a viable path for institution-specific customization.

Abstract: Existing predictive models in learning analytics often treat student academic history as a simple sequence, overlooking the concurrent nature of courses taken within a semester. This simplification can lead to inaccurate performance predictions, particularly for students with heavy or challenging course loads. This paper introduces a TRansformer for Academic Course-grade Estimation (TRACE) that addresses this limitation by jointly predicting both the set of courses a student will take and their corresponding grades for an upcoming semester. Our approach encodes courses on a per-semester basis to capture the effects of course concurrency and utilizes a novel loss function combining course-set prediction with grade prediction. We demonstrate that predicting courses taken in addition to the grades in those courses leads to significant improvements in prediction quality. Trained on ten years of institutional data, our joint prediction model reduces mean absolute error by nearly 50% compared to an identical architecture that predicts grades alone. The model also outperforms traditional LSTM-based sequential models, as well as graph neural network-based approaches, and offers natural ways to incorporate student attribute data. This work demonstrates the utility of modern neural architectures for creating interpretable models that can be adapted to new institutions via retraining and recalibration, as well as the importance of key techniques, such as predicting courses taken during training. We discuss how this model could be incorporated into early detection systems at institutions of higher education.

</details>


### [286] [Who Speaks Matters: Authority-Aware Multi-View RAG over Italian Parliamentary Proceedings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13410)
*Mirko Tritella,Riccardo Pozzi,Matteo Palmonari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ParliamentRAG is a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) system for the Italian Chamber of Deputies that uses a topic-dependent authority model to ensure diverse, expertise-weighted, and accurately cited summaries across parliamentary groups; it retrieves relevant speech chunks, identifies topic-relevant experts, and generates synthesized summaries with quotations, evaluated against Google NotebookLM on 15 topics, outperforming on group coverage and quotation faithfulness, with NotebookLM stronger on prose.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parliamentary proceedings are voluminous and fragmented, complicating multi-perspective access for citizens, journalists, and researchers. RAG on political text risks dominance by frequent speakers, misweighting of topical expertise, and citation misattribution.

Method: Introduce a topic-dependent authority model that estimates each speaker's authority as a function of the current query, combining interpretable features such as profession, education, and prior interventions. For a given query, retrieve relevant speech chunks, identify topic-relevant experts across parliamentary groups, and generate a summary synthesizing their perspectives with supporting quotations. Evaluate ParliamentRAG against Google NotebookLM on 15 policy topics via a two-level protocol with automated metrics and blind A/B evaluation by six domain experts.

Result: ParliamentRAG achieves higher coverage across political groups (0.97 vs. 0.95) and perfect quotation faithfulness (1.00 vs. 0.95), and stronger expert preferences on source-related dimensions, though NotebookLM remains stronger on prose-oriented dimensions.

Conclusion: ParliamentRAG addresses three identified RAG risks (speaker dominance, misweighted expertise, citation misattribution) and improves multi-perspective access and quotation fidelity for parliamentary texts; the approach shows promise for broader parliamentary corpora while trade-offs in prose quality may exist.

Abstract: Parliamentary proceedings are a primary record of democratic deliberation, yet their volume and fragmentation make multi-perspective access difficult for citizens, journalists, and researchers. Applying Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) to parliamentary transcripts introduces three specific risks: dominance of the most frequent speakers, inability to weight speakers according to topical expertise, and citation misattribution in politically sensitive text. We present ParliamentRAG, a RAG system for the Italian Chamber of Deputies that addresses these risks jointly. Its core contribution is a topic-dependent authority model that estimates each speaker's authority as a function of the current query, combining interpretable components such as profession, education, and previous interventions. Given a user query, the system retrieves relevant speech chunks, identifies topic-relevant experts across parliamentary groups, and generates a summary synthesizing their perspectives, accompanied by supporting quotations. ParliamentRAG is evaluated against Google NotebookLM on 15 policy topics via a two-level protocol combining automated metrics and blind A/B human evaluation by six domain experts. The system achieves higher coverage across political groups (0.97 vs. 0.95), perfect quotation faithfulness (1.00 vs. 0.95), and stronger expert preferences on source-related dimensions, while NotebookLM remains stronger on prose-oriented dimensions.

</details>


### [287] [Beyond Final Scores: A Systematic Evaluation of Agents for Long-Horizon AI Research and Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13417)
*Yiwei Li,Wanli Yang,Hexiang Tan,Xiangzhou Huang,Zhengyu Chen,Ziran Li,Borun Chen,Shanglin Lei,Huaisheng Zhu,Hao Tian,Fei Sun,Xunliang Cai,Jingang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Systematic evaluation of frontier models on 36 long-horizon tasks using a rule-based framework (Solution Framing, Execution, Feedback Control) to analyze in-run behavior and experience reuse; findings show current agents act like engineering optimizers with limited novelty and varying performance across runs; bottlenecks and harness design influence stability; actionable directions for training, inference, experience management, and harness design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move beyond final scores and understand how and where long-horizon agents gain or lose progress, and whether accumulated experience improves later decisions.

Method: Rule-based metrics that characterize within-run behavior via Solution Framing, Execution, and Feedback Control; controlled comparisons of experience reuse within and across 36 tasks involving seven frontier models.

Result: Agents resemble engineering optimizers capable of practical solution generation but show substantial run-to-run variability; strongest solutions rely on adapting or combining established techniques; genuine methodological novelty is rare; performance shaped by process bottlenecks, experience reuse effects, and harness design affecting stability.

Conclusion: The study identifies concrete directions to improve model training, inference-time strategies, experience management, and harness design to enhance long-horizon planning performance and reliability.

Abstract: Autonomous agents are increasingly capable of improving models, systems, and other technical artifacts through long-horizon experimentation. To understand the current state of this capability, however, evaluation must go beyond final scores, which neither reveal where progress is gained or lost nor indicate whether accumulated experience improves later decisions. We therefore present a systematic evaluation of seven frontier models on 36 long-horizon tasks based on a new framework that uses rule-based metrics to characterize within-run behavior through Solution Framing, Execution, and Feedback Control and controlled comparisons to assess experience reuse within and across tasks. The results show that current agents operate more like engineering optimizers than fully autonomous researchers: they can formulate and implement practical solutions, but their performance varies substantially across runs, their strongest solutions mainly adapt or combine established techniques, and genuine methodological novelty remains rare. Detailed analysis reveals that observed performance is shaped by multiple factors, including distinct process bottlenecks behind similar final outcomes, experience reuse that can help or mislead subsequent decisions, and harness designs that affect performance stability. These findings suggest concrete directions for improving model training, inference-time strategies, experience management, and harness design.

</details>


### [288] [Enhancing Virtual Agents through SLMs and Edge-Computing: An Exploratory Evaluation of Think and Memory Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13420)
*Aimilios Hadjiliasi,Louis Nisiotis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Edge-driven Small Language Models enable Think and Memory components in CEAA-like embodied agents; demonstration on Jetson Orin NX with Qwen2.5; partial implementation validated via simulations on routing, memory reads, and latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable cognitively capable agents in immersive virtual worlds under edge constraints; reduce latency and preserve context by moving cognitive components to edge; leverage SLMs to support perception, memory, reasoning, planning, and action.

Method: Developed an edge-based gateway system; integrated Qwen2.5 SLMs of multiple sizes; deployed on NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX; evaluated on simulation experiments focusing on routing accuracy, memory-read performance, and latency; targeted CEAA components 'Think' and 'Memory'.

Result: Prototype demonstrates that SLMs can partially implement selected CEAA processes; capable of handling service requests and memory-driven conversations; memory read and latency metrics measured; shows feasibility of edge-based cognitive orchestration for virtual agents.

Conclusion: SLMs on edge can support cognitive brain-like functionality for embodied agents in immersive worlds; provides a scalable path leveraging edge computing; further work needed to complete full CEAA integration, optimize latency, memory management, and energy consumption, and test in more realistic interactive settings.

Abstract: Embodied intelligent virtual agents are expected to operate as persistent, adaptive, and context-aware entities within complex virtual and Metaverse worlds. However, implementing cognitively capable agents in such environments is conceptually and technologically challenging. Among a range of blueprints and development approaches, the Cognitive Embodied Agent Architecture (CEAA) has been developed as an implementation-oriented framework for architecting components of perception, memory, reasoning, planning, and embodied action. Considering the recent advances in edge computing and generative AI language models, this paper explores the use of Small Language Models (SLMs) to support edge-based operation of selected CEAA components, focusing on "Think" and "Memory" as processes central to cognitive orchestration and persistence of virtual agents in interactive virtual worlds. An edge-based virtual agent gateway system was developed and evaluated on an NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX using Qwen2.5 models of different sizes, exploring the system's capability to process service requests and handle memory-driven conversations. A series of simulation experiments evaluated routing accuracy, memory-read performance, and latency, demonstrating an SLM-driven prototype agent system that partially implements selected CEAA processes to support the development of embodied agents whose cognitive "brain" can operate efficiently and contextually for interactive experiences in immersive virtual worlds.

</details>


### [289] [RAIL: An Automatic Classifier of the Artificial Intelligence Readiness Level](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13428)
*Juan Irving Vasquez,Juan Terven,Laura-Ivoone Garay-Jimenez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Nine-level Unified AI Readiness Level (AIRL) with environmental evidence ladder and dimensional caps, plus RAIL: a panel of LLM-based experts to assess readiness via independent dimension agents and a deterministic rule, reviewed by a chief expert; tested on several research works showing consistency and reduced overestimation compared to monolithic LLM classifiers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address heterogeneity and inapplicability of existing AI readiness frameworks by providing a unified, decidable, and automatable scale that can be inferred from natural-language descriptions of work.

Method: Unify Technology Readiness Levels, ML Technology Readiness Levels, and AI/data readiness models into AIRL: a nine-level ordinal scale with an environmental evidence ladder and dimensional caps (spec: data existence, data quality, data legality, expert knowledge, algorithmic maturity) plus a generality-anchoring rule and explicit assignment disciplines. Implement RAIL: one evidence agent and six dimension agents (LLMs with narrowly scoped mandates) produce verdicts; a deterministic minimum rule aggregates and a chief expert reviews, with caps enforced and no lifting above caps.

Result: Applied to several research works, the approach showed consistency and avoided overestimation typical of monolithic LLM classifiers.

Conclusion: AIRL provides a decidable, scalable readiness metric; RAIL operationalizes the scale with expert oversight, yielding more reliable assessments across AI projects.

Abstract: Assessing the maturity of artificial intelligence technologies is essential for investment decisions, project management, and policy monitoring, yet the available readiness frameworks are heterogeneous and difficult to apply automatically: the adaptation of Technology Readiness Levels to AI lacks AI-specific gating criteria, the Machine Learning Technology Readiness Levels presuppose access to internal process artifacts, and AI/data readiness dimension models employ scales that resist direct comparison. This paper makes two contributions. First, we unify these three frameworks into the Unified AI Readiness Level (AIRL), a nine-level ordinal scale built on an environmental evidence ladder and complemented by dimensional caps (covering specification, data existence, data quality, data legality, expert knowledge, and algorithmic maturity) together with a generality-anchoring rule and explicit assignment disciplines, so that a readiness level becomes decidable from a natural-language description of the work alone. Second, we propose RAIL (Readiness Assessment via Independent LLM-experts), a panel-of-experts classifier that operationalizes the scale: one evidence agent and six independent dimension agents, each a large language model with a narrowly scoped mandate, deliver verdicts that a deterministic minimum rule aggregates and a chief expert reviews under asymmetric authority, confirming or lowering the panel's recommendation but never raising it above the caps. The method was tested in the analysis of several research works showing consistency and avoiding overestimation from monolithic LLM classifiers.

</details>


### [290] [Academic League of Artificial Intelligence - An Integrative Perspective of Teaching, Research, and Extension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13447)
*Alison R. Panisson,Maria Eduarda W. M. Vianna,Italo Firmino da Silva,Heitor Henrique da Silva,Rafaela Fernandes Savaris,Bernardo Pandolfi Costa,Martin Augusto Gagliotti Vigil,Jim Lau,Agenor Hentz,Andréa Sabedra Bordin,Alexandre Leopoldo Gonçalves,Roberto Rodrigues-Filho*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A replicable, student-centered, project-based framework uniting teaching, research, and extension in engineering/computing education through democratic governance and collaborative, project-based organization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To integrate teaching, research, and university extension within engineering and computing education by leveraging Academic Leagues to promote leadership, scientific production, and community engagement.

Method: Describe an organizational framework used by LIA/UFSC that combines democratic governance, collaborative learning, and dynamic project organization; implements competition teams, study groups, open lectures, knowledge repositories, and AI-powered social-impact applications.

Result: Illustration of initiatives within the common framework showing integration of activities; demonstrates flexibility and replicability for other academic leagues; enhances leadership, community engagement, and knowledge preservation; generates practical guidance.

Conclusion: The framework provides a flexible, replicable model for integrating the three university pillars into engineering and computing education, with practical guidance for student organizations.

Abstract: Academic leagues have become important mechanisms for promoting extracurricular education and strengthening the integration between universities and society. This paper presents the organizational framework adopted by the Academic League of Artificial Intelligence (LIA) at the Federal University of Santa Catarina (UFSC), designed to integrate teaching, research, and university extension through a student-centered, project-based approach. The framework combines democratic governance, collaborative learning, and dynamic project organization to foster both technical and transversal competencies. The framework is illustrated through representative initiatives, including competition teams, study groups, open lectures, knowledge repositories, and AI-powered applications with social impact. These projects demonstrate how diverse educational, scientific, and extension activities can be developed within a common organizational structure while promoting leadership, scientific production, community engagement, and knowledge preservation. The reported experience indicates that the proposed framework provides a flexible and replicable model for integrating the three university pillars into engineering and computing education, offering practical guidance for academic leagues and similar student organizations.

</details>


### [291] [A Unifying Perspective on Causal World Models: From Observations to Representations to Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13456)
*Avinash Kori,Fabrizio Russo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Formalizes Causal World Models (CWMs) that integrate causal structure and object-centric representations across perceptual and conceptual levels to support prediction, planning, and decision-making, with identifiability considerations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World Models should extend beyond pure generation to encode causal structure, entity properties, and interactions that govern dynamics, enabling robust reasoning and planning under distribution shifts.

Method: Proposes a formal definition of CWMs anchored in task support, and situates CWMs within the broader literatures of causal representation learning, object-centric learning, causal discovery, structural causal models, and model-based decision-making; analyzes identifiability and equivalence issues to determine when WM components can be recovered from data.

Result: A theoretical framework and definitions that connect world modeling with causality, providing a basis for identifiability analyses and future empirical instantiations.

Conclusion: CWMs ground World Models in representations and structures that support causal reasoning and informed decision-making.

Abstract: World Models (WM) are increasingly seen as a foundation for intelligent agents that can predict, plan, and act beyond their training distribution. In this paper, we study WMs from a causal perspective across multiple levels of abstraction, ranging from perceptual observations to building a conceptual representation of the structure governing the environment dynamics. We argue that useful WMs must go beyond generative capabilities alone: they should also capture entity properties, entity-to-entity interactions, and entity-to-environment interactions that determine and explain the dynamics of a system. We provide a formal definition of Causal WMs (CWMs) grounded in the tasks they are intended to support, connecting world modelling with existing work in causal representation learning, object-centric learning, causal discovery, structural causal models, and model-based decision-making. Finally, we relate CWMs to the literature on identifiability, clarifying when the components of a WM can be recovered from data and up to which equivalence. With this, we ground WMs in representations and structures that support causal reasoning and informed decision-making.

</details>


### [292] [MARC v1: An Open-Source Multi-Agent Framework for Clinical AI Reasoning and Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13476)
*Saisha Shetty,Satvik Tripathi,Austin Lin,Colin Zhao,Theodore Kim,Don Enwerem,Jacinta Arnold,Shahriar Faghani,Tessa S Cook*
